And here the doubt was not left standing: they were given a test any of them could have taken, at the price of one short passage of Arabic — and a whole nation of speech-makers, with everything to gain by succeeding, went silent. That silence is the argument the verse goes on to press: "and if you do it not — and you will never do it" (2:24) **[Ibn Kathīr]**.


## 2:24

*"And if you do it not — and you will never do it — then guard against the Fire whose fuel is men and stones, prepared for the disbelievers."*

**Meaning.** The challenge of 2:23 is now closed: you did not produce a sūrah, and you never will; so the alternative to arguing is a Fire that will not run out of fuel, since its wood is human beings and stones **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]**. The verse gives the disbelievers one route of escape — belief — and names what lies at the other end of the road **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Language.** "And you will never do it" (*lan tafʿalū*) is not a forecast among forecasts but a settled negation: *lan* negates forever, and with it the prediction became a fixed report of the unseen before the event — the disbelievers were provoked to try, and their failure was to be all the starker **[Qurṭubī]**. Ibn Kaysān: the words fix them in their own falsehood, for they claimed to know and could not produce a single sūrah **[Qurṭubī]**. The grammarians add that *lan* may be followed by the jazm in some dialects — Abū ʿUbaydah cites al-Nābigha's line, "and I will never expose myself to reproach with giving"; and Ibn ʿUmar's dream report, "it was said to me: you will not be terrified" **[Qurṭubī]**. On the pause: some exegetes read the verse as "call your witnesses... if you are truthful — and you will never do it — so if you do it not, guard against the Fire", which moves the stop to the end of 2:23; others stop at "truthful" **[Qurṭubī]**. *Waqūd* with a *fatḥah* is the fuel itself, the firewood; with a *ḍammah* it is the burning **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. The general reading has *waqūduhā*; al-Ḥasan, Mujāhid and Ṭalḥah b. Muṣarrif read *wuqūduhā*, and ʿUbayd b. ʿUmayr *waqīduhā* **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Context.** The stones: **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** report from Ibn Masʿūd that they are stones of sulfur — God created them when He created the heavens and the earth, and He has them stored in the lowest heaven, making them ready for the disbelievers. From al-Suddī and Ibn ʿAbbās: stones of black sulfur in the Fire, by which they are tortured along with it; and Ibn Jurayj heard ʿAmr b. Dīnār say they are harder and greater than the stones of this world **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the reason sulfur was singled out: it surpasses all other stones in five kinds of torment — it ignites fastest, it stinks worst, it smokes most, it clings hardest to the bodies, and its heat when heated is the most intense. Other exegetes take the stones to be the idols the polytheists worshipped, since 21:98 says, "You and all that you worship besides God are the firewood of Hell" — so that the human beings and the stone idols are together fuel, and the point is the size of the Fire, which burns stones as it burns men **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**. On the first reading, men are punished *by* the Fire and the stones together.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** Muslim records the Prophet's ﷺ words about Abū Ṭālib, when al-ʿAbbās asked whether his protection had benefited him: "Yes — I found him in the depths of the Fire, and I brought him out to a shallow place; and were it not for me, he would be in the lowest depth" **[Qurṭubī]**. Ibn Masʿūd said: we were with the Messenger of God ﷺ when he heard a thud, and he asked, "Do you know what this is?" — "God and His Messenger know best" — he said: "This is a stone that was thrown into the Fire seventy autumns ago; it has been falling in the Fire until now, when it reached its bottom" (Muslim) **[Qurṭubī]**. And the two Ṣaḥīḥs: the Fire and Paradise argued — the Fire said, "The mighty and the proud enter me," and Paradise said, "The weak and the poor enter me" — and God said to the first, "You are My punishment, by you I punish whom I will," and to the second, "You are My mercy, by you I show mercy to whom I will; and each of you shall be filled" **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Ibn Kathīr]** adds the greatest of miracles as the Prophet ﷺ described it: "Every prophet was given signs such that people believed on their like; what I was given is a revelation that God revealed to me, and I hope to be the one with the most followers on the Day of Resurrection" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim).

**Belief.** "Prepared for the disbelievers" is used by the people of truth as proof that the Fire is already created, against those who said it is not yet made — **[Qurṭubī]** cites the Andalusian judge Mundhir b. Saʿīd al-Ballūṭī as one who fell into that error. That the verse names the disbelievers does not mean no one else may enter it: the warnings against sinning believers, and the established reports of intercession, show otherwise; the Fire is *prepared* for the disbelievers in particular **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Saʿdī]** reads the same construction in the other direction: since the Fire is prepared for the disbelievers, the people of *tawḥīd* who commit some grave sins will not abide in it forever — otherwise it would not be prepared for the disbelievers alone, against the Khawārij and the Muʿtazila; and together with 2:24's companion verses it proves that Paradise and the Fire are both created and waiting. From the same verse he draws that punishment is earned through its causes — unbelief and sins of every kind.

**Cross-references.** 72:15, "the wrongdoers are firewood for Hell"; 21:98, "you and what you worship beside God are the firewood of Hell"; 66:6, "guard yourselves and your families against a Fire whose fuel is men and stones" **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**.

**Reflection.** **[Maʿārif]** records what the challenge did to its audience. The chiefs of Makkah feared the crowds of the pilgrimage and sought a label to hang on the Prophet ﷺ. Walīd b. al-Mughīra, the oldest and wisest among them, foretold that "lunatic" would not hold when men heard him speak, that "poet" would fail against a people with poetry in their blood, and that "soothsayer" would only turn the listeners against the accusers. Then he gave the testimony of a man who had listened: "By God, not one of you knows more of Arabic poetry than I. And by God, I find in this speech a sweetness and grace I have never found in the speech of any poet or any eloquent man." So they settled on "sorcerer". Others — Nadr b. al-Ḥārith, the brother of Abū Dharr, Asʿad b. Zurārah, Qays b. Naṣībah — said the like; and Abū Jahl, with Abu Sufyan and al-Akhans, crept by night to the Prophet's house to listen, unable to tear themselves away until dawn, and returned to their denial for a reason Abū Jahl confessed: the clan of ʿAbd Manāf had come up with a prophet, and they had nothing to match **[Maʿārif]**. This is what the verse does: it turns the silence of the eloquent into evidence, and then asks them to weigh the difference between losing an argument and losing themselves.


## 2:25

*"And give glad tidings to those who believe and do righteous deeds, that theirs are Gardens beneath which rivers flow. Whenever they are given a fruit from them as provision, they say, 'This is what we were given before' — and they are given it in resemblance. And therein they have purified spouses, and therein they abide forever."*

**Meaning.** The Fire of 2:24 was prepared for the disbelievers; now the contrast: good news for those who believe and work — Gardens, rivers, fruits that recall former fruits, spouses made pure, and an abiding that does not end **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Saʿdī]** notes the method: God pairs warning with encouragement so that the servant lives between hope and fear, wanting and wary.

**Context.** **[Ṭabarī]** explains *bashārah*: a report of what brings joy, given before any other can bring it; and the command to the Prophet ﷺ is to carry it to those who confirmed their faith with righteous deeds — not to those who professed with the tongue while denying in the heart. The Gardens are named for their trees, fruits and planted things, not for their dirt — which is why the rivers are said to flow *beneath them*: beneath the trees, not beneath the ground; water running under the earth gives no benefit to what grows above it. And from Masrūq b. al-Ajdaʿ: the palm trees of the Garden are arranged from root to branch, their fruit like great jars, and every time a fruit is taken another returns in its place — and its water runs without channels **[Ṭabarī]**.

**Language — "this is what we were given before".** **[Ṭabarī]** works through the readings. One group: "before, in this world" — the Suddī, Qatāda and Ibn Zayd report it, and the verse's plain sense supports it, for God says "whenever they are given" — at every giving, including the first, before any Garden fruit has preceded it; if the words meant "the like of what we were given before *in the Garden*", the first fruit they ever receive would be called a likeness of what they had not yet received. Others hold the reference is to the Garden's own fruits: they resemble one another so closely, since every fruit taken is replaced by its like, that the eater says, "This is what we were given before" — but **[Ṭabarī]** counts this against the apparent text, and reports Yaḥyā b. Abī Kathīr's scene as the middle position: one of them is brought a platter and eats, then another is brought and he says, "This is what we were brought before"; the angel answers, "Eat: the colour is one, the taste is different." And "they were given it in resemblance": resemblance between the fruits of the Garden and the fruits of the world — names familiar, reality different. **[Saʿdī]** collects three explanations and prefers the last: alike in name with different taste; one in colour, different in name; or alike in beauty, pleasure and delight — for there is no base fruit in the Garden and no moment empty of pleasure. **[Maʿārif]** puts the doctrine carefully: Paradise's fruits share names with earth's fruits but differ in nature, and the concrete language of the Hereafter is not thereby reduced to mere symbol — what is symbolized still exists, and the manner of it is left to God. **[Study Quran]** notes that the verse speaks from the point of view of one who dwells in Paradise remembering this world, whose bitterness is gone: "no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve" (2:62). On the "spouses made pure": free of injury, of menstruation and of what people find distasteful **[Study Quran]**, and — in **[Saʿdī]**'s wider reading — pure in character, speech and glance: loving toward their husbands, eyes and tongues kept from all that is ugly.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** draws three matters from the verse's opening words. First, the *bushrā* is used for good news by default; used of evil, it must be explicit — "give them tidings of a painful punishment" (84:24). Second, a juristic case: if a man says, "Whichever of my slaves brings me glad tidings of such a thing is free," the first is freed and not the second; but if he says "tells me", the Shāfiʿīs free each, since each is a reporter, while the Mālikīs free only the first, because the intent is a report that carries glad tidings, and custom fixes that to the first. Muḥammad b. al-Ḥasan distinguished *akhbaranī* from *ḥaddathanī*: the first covers a written message or a messenger, the second only face-to-face speech. Third: "and do righteous deeds" answers those who claimed faith by itself produces the acts of obedience — if that were so, the verse would not repeat it; the Garden is reached by faith and good work, and the levels within it are earned by works **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Maʿārif]** adds the balance: faith alone can save a man from remaining in Hell forever, and the Muslim who is a great sinner is finally taken out of it after punishment — but no one escapes it altogether without the good deeds defined by the Sharīʿah.

**Cross-references.** 47:15 (rivers of water, milk, wine and honey); 56:18–19; 4:57; 4:122; 72:23; 98:8; 11:108 **[Study Quran]**. And 3:7 for the wider sense of *mutashābih* **[Study Quran]**.

**Reflection.** **[Saʿdī]** counts the parts of the verse: the bringer of glad tidings — the Messenger ﷺ and whoever stands in his place; the gladdened — believers who do good; the tidings — the Gardens and their descriptions; and the cause that leads there — faith and righteous work. It is the greatest of all glad tidings, carried by the best of creation, by way of the best of means; and the first fruit of the tiding is the tiding itself — that a man is granted faith and good action at all. The rivers run without channels, the fruit tastes of what was known and better than what was known, and the joy renews at every moment — and it does not end. **[Maʿārif]** writes the last point as the difference between the two gardens: the joys of this world must be feared losing; the joys of the next need not be.

**[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise sets the Garden's promise against the preceding warning, making hope and fear work together in the believer's response.

## 2:26

*"God is not ashamed to strike a parable — even of a gnat or of what is above it. As for those who believe, they know it is the truth from their Lord; and as for those who disbelieve, they say, 'What did God intend by this parable?' He leads many astray by it and guides many by it — and He leads none astray by it except the iniquitous."*

**Meaning.** The parables of the two hypocrites — the fire-lighter and the cloudburst — drew mockery, and the mention of a fly and a spider elsewhere drew more: how could God speak of such mean creatures? The verse answers that God does not hold back from the truth, and strikes parables great and small as a test: the believer knows them for what they are, the disbeliever stumbles over them **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**.

**Context.** **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Ṭabarī]** collect the occasions. From Ibn ʿAbbās (via Abū Ṣāliḥ): when God struck the two parables for the hypocrites, they said, "God is greater and higher than to strike parables" — and this verse came down. From ʿAṭāʾ, from Ibn ʿAbbās: when God mentioned the idols — "if the fly snatches a thing from them, they cannot recover it from it" (22:73) — and made their plotting like a spider's house (29:41), they said, "What does He want with mentioning flies and spiders in what He sent down to Muḥammad?" From al-Ḥasan and Qatāda: the Jews laughed, saying this does not resemble God's speech. **[Ṭabarī]** prefers the report of Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās, because the verse follows the parables struck in *this* sūrah: what the hypocrites mocked was the fire-lighter and the rainstorm they had just heard, and the answer to a mockery fits the speech that occasioned it. Qatāda's gloss on the verse: God is not ashamed of the truth, that He should mention of it a little or much — and when He mentioned the fly and the spider, the people of error asked what God wanted by it. And on the gnat: it is the weakest of God's creatures **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**; so God named the least of things to show that He does not hold back from striking the smallest parable in the truth and the largest without limit.

**Language.** *Yastaḥyī* comes from *ḥayāʾ*; the form is heavy with weak letters — Ibn Muḥyiṣin and others read *yastaḥī* with a single *yāʾ*, the dialect of Tamīm and Bakr b. Wāʾil **[Qurṭubī]**. Among the exegetes, *lā yastaḥyī* here is glossed as "does not fear" (which **[Ṭabarī]** preferred — the Qurʾān itself pairs fear with shame in "you fear the people, and God is worthier of your fear", 33:37), or "does not leave", or "does not refrain"; the root of shame is drawing back from a thing out of fear of the ugly, and that cannot be ascribed to God **[Qurṭubī]**. The Prophet's ﷺ own words from Umm Sulaym — "O Messenger of God, God is not ashamed of the truth" — carry the same point: He neither commands shame about the truth nor refrains from mentioning it (Muslim) **[Qurṭubī]**. On the parsing of *baʿūḍatan*: it may be a substitute for "parable" with *mā* extra; *mā* may be an indefinite noun that *baʿūḍah* qualifies; the accusative may stand for a dropped *bayna* — "a parable between a gnat and what is above it"; or *yaḍrib* may mean "makes", making the gnat a second object; al-Ḍaḥḥāk, Ibrāhīm b. Abī ʿAblah and Ruʾba b. al-ʿAjjāj read it in the nominative **[Qurṭubī]**. And "what is above it" may mean what is greater or what is smaller still — beyond it in lowliness **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]**.

**Belief.** The verse sets out the same revelation producing two opposite effects, and ascribes both to God: He leads many astray by it and guides many by it **[Saʿdī]**. **[Saʿdī]** reads this against 9:124–125: when a sūrah is sent down, some say, "Which of you has this increased in faith?" — the believers are increased in faith and rejoice, while those with a disease in their hearts are increased in filth and die as disbelievers. So the greatest of blessings — the sending down of revelation — becomes for one people a mercy, and for another a trial and a step deeper into loss. And the misguidance falls on none but the *fāsiqīn*: those who have left obedience, whose transgression has become a fixed quality, who do not seek any replacement for it — their unfitness for guidance is what makes their misguidance just, while grace and wisdom guide those who bear faith and righteous deeds **[Saʿdī]**. **[Maʿārif]** adds the two uses of the word: in the Qurʾān *fisq* can name outright unbelief, while the jurists use *fāsiq* for the Muslim who persists in major sin or makes minor sins a habit — and one who sins openly and shamelessly is called a *kāfir* in some reports. **[Study Quran]** puts the mechanism starkly: the message is not neutralized by being denied, it places the denier further astray; a person's moral condition decides, in part, how much truth he can receive. Those described here are generally held to be the hypocrites **[Study Quran]**.

**Cross-references.** 22:73 (the fly); 29:41 (the spider's house); 7:172 (the covenant); 9:124–125; 6:44; and 2:27, which describes the same people **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**.

**Reflection.** **[Maʿārif]** draws two lessons from the occasion. First, when the aim is genuine guidance, there is no loss of dignity in using an image that common taste calls base — the Qurʾān, the Ḥadīth and the works of the scholars all use such comparisons when they serve the truth. Second, the mockery of the parables was itself a diagnosis: a heart that finds a gnat too small for God's speech has already stopped weighing the message and started weighing the messenger. The verse leaves the two audiences standing side by side, holding the same Book: "they know it is the truth from their Lord" — and "what did God intend by this parable?" The difference is not in the parable.


## 2:27

*"Those who break God's covenant after its ratification, and sever what God has commanded to be joined, and work corruption in the earth — it is they who are the losers."*

**Meaning.** The verse takes up the *fāsiqīn* of 2:26 and draws their portrait in three strokes: covenant-breakers, severers of ties, corrupters of the earth — and then fixes their name: the losers **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Saʿdī]** notes that the loss is not a part of loss but the whole of it: they are losers in the present life and in the next, since no good deed they do counts without faith, and their loss is beyond any profit.

**Context — what is the covenant?** **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** collect five readings. (1) The covenant of 7:172, taken from the descendants of Adam out of his loins: "Am I not your Lord? — They said: Yes." (2) God's charge to His creation: what He commanded and prohibited on the tongues of His messengers and in His Books; breaking it is abandoning it. (3) The very proofs of His oneness placed in the heavens and the earth — His signs stand as a covenant, and breaking it is refusing to look. (4) The covenant taken from the People of the Book: that they would make Muḥammad's prophethood clear and not conceal it — as 3:187 says, "And when God took the covenant of those who were given the Book: you shall make it clear to the people and not conceal it; then they cast it behind their backs." (5) Al-Zajjāj: the covenant taken from the prophets and those who followed them, that they would not disbelieve in him — "when God took the covenant of the prophets" (3:81). **[Qurṭubī]** observes that the second reading gathers the rest. **[Ṭabarī]** prefers the reading that these verses came down concerning the disbelieving rabbis around Madīnah and the hypocrites, while holding that the warning includes everyone who takes their path: as God sometimes speaks of them all together and sometimes distinguishes the two groups, so the ruling reaches all who share their description. **[Ibn Kathīr]** agrees, and adds the comparison with Sūrat al-Raʿd: "Those who fulfil the covenant of God and do not break the pact, and who join what God has commanded to be joined... and those who break God's covenant after ratifying it, and sever what God has commanded to be joined, and work corruption in the earth — for them is the curse and theirs the evil home" (13:20–25). **[Maʿārif]** and **[Study Quran]** add the primordial reading: the covenant of 7:172, along with 35:42 — the Arabs' own oath, "God their most solemn oaths that if a warner came they would be more rightly guided than any nation."

**Language.** *Naqḍ* is the undoing of what was made firm — a building, a rope, a covenant; *nuqāḍah* is what unravels from a hair-rope; *munāqaḍah* is speaking with two contradictory meanings; *naqīḍah* is the poem that undoes another **[Qurṭubī]**. *Mīthāq* is a covenant confirmed by oath, from *wathāqa*: firmness in binding and knotting; its plural is *mawāthīq*, and the *wāw* becomes *yāʾ* because of the vowel before it — hence *mayāthīq* and *mayāthīq* again **[Qurṭubī]**. "After its ratification" is the covenant made firm by God's own guarantee-taking **[Ṭabarī]**.

**Interpretations — what is severed.** On "what God has commanded to be joined": Qatāda says the womb and kinship — "would you then, if you turned away, work corruption in the earth and sever your kinships?" (47:22) **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]**. Others: joining speech to action, which they severed by saying what they did not do; joining belief in all the prophets, which they severed by accepting some and rejecting others; and the majority: every such God-commanded tie, of which kinship is one instance **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** adds that some say it means the Prophet ﷺ and the believers — the ties they cut with their own flesh and blood once they followed him; and, whether or not that is the original referent, the verse condemns every severer of what God commanded to be joined — kinship or anything else. **[Maʿārif]** widens it to all relations: between God and the servant, between a man and his parents and relatives, his neighbours and friends, one Muslim and another, one man and another; Islam itself means fulfilling the obligations of all these ties, and it is their severance that produces disorder among men. **[Study Quran]** cites 4:1, 8:75, 33:6 for the sanctity of blood ties, and notes that the Prophet had kinship with the Jews too, since Isaac and Ishmael were brothers.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** draws from the verse the obligation of keeping every covenant and every binding a person lays upon himself, whether it is with a Muslim or another: God censures the covenant-breaker, and He says, "Fulfil the contracts" (5:1); He told His Prophet ﷺ, "If you fear treachery from a people, throw their treaty back to them on equal terms" (8:58) — and forbade him treachery. So faithfulness to covenants is not a matter of convenience: a Muslim's word is a tie that may not be torn.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** Qatāda's warning: beware of breaking this covenant; God has hated its breaking and warned in it as He warned in no sin — "and I do not know of any sin for which God has given as much warning"; whoever gives God a covenant out of the purity of his heart should keep it **[Ṭabarī]**. And the report of the traits of the hypocrites: when they gain the upper hand they show six — they lie when they speak, break their promise, betray their trust, break God's covenant, cut what He commanded to be joined, and corrupt the earth; when the believers have the upper hand they show three: lying, breaking promises, betraying trusts **[Ṭabarī]**. Ibn Kathīr's note on the word *fāsiq* belongs here too: the date is *fasaqat* when it comes out of its skin, and the mouse is called *fuwaysiqah* because it leaves its hole to make mischief; and the Prophet ﷺ named five creatures *fawāsiq* to be killed in the sacred precinct or outside it: the crow, the kite, the scorpion, the mouse and the rabid dog (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from ʿĀʾisha) **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

**Belief.** "Corruption in the earth" is their worship of other than God and their running of affairs by their desires **[Qurṭubī]**; **[Ṭabarī]** puts it as their denial of the Prophet and what he brought. **[Saʿdī]** notes the two kinds of *fisq*: the transgression that exits the religion — the fisq meant here — and the transgression that does not exit it, as in "O you who believe, if a fāsiq brings you news, investigate" (49:6). The ones who break the pact do not merely commit a great sin: they have walked out of the circle of obedience altogether, and their ruin suits the disorder they spread.

**Reflection.** The three descriptions of the verse are one movement: a broken covenant, severed ties, a corrupted land — the damage begins with what a man says to God and ends in what he does to the world. **[Qurṭubī]**'s lexicography makes the point quietly: the root of *khusrān* is diminution — a man short in his own share. The losers of 2:27 did not lose some property; they lost themselves and their families on the Day of Resurrection, and were shut out of the Garden they had been equipped to reach **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Maʿārif]** closes the argument: real loss belongs to the next world; the loss of this world is too small a thing to be worth the name.


## 2:28

*"How can you disbelieve in God, when you were dead and He gave you life, then He will cause you to die, then He will give you life, and then to Him you shall be returned?"*

**Meaning.** An address of astonishment and rebuke: how can a man deny the One who drew him from death into life, and who will take that life, restore it, and bring him back to His own presence? **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Maʿārif]** notes the sharpness of the form: the disbelievers thought they were rejecting only a prophet, while the Qurʾān treats the rejection of Muḥammad ﷺ as a denial of God Himself — for refusing the Book is to withhold belief in the One who sent it.

**Context — the four stages.** **[Ṭabarī]** lists the reports on "you were dead". Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and their students: you were nothing, then He created and gave you life, then He will kill you, then raise you on the Day of Resurrection. Qatāda: you were lifeless sperm in your fathers' loins, then He gave you life, then death, then the resurrection — two lives and two deaths, like 40:11, "Our Lord, You caused us to die twice and gave us life twice." Mujāhid, Abū al-ʿĀliyah and al-Ḍaḥḥāk give the same. Abū Ṣāliḥ: the life is the life of the grave, after which comes the death. **[Qurṭubī]** lists the interpretations with their tallies: two lives and two deaths (the view of the majority — nonexistence, life in the plain sense, death, resurrection); three and three (including the life and death within the grave for the questioning); four and four (adding the existence and death of the descendants of Adam taken from his back); five and five (adding a state before Adam like scattered dust), to which he adds a sixth death for the sinners of the community who enter the Fire — on the strength of the ḥadīth of Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī in Muslim: the permanent people of the Fire do not die in it nor live, but people whom the Fire has touched for their sins are caused by God to die a true death (so Qurṭubī, noting the emphatic verbal noun) until they become coals; then intercession is allowed, and they are brought back in groups, cast upon the rivers of the Garden, and told, "O people of the Garden, pour over them" — and they sprout like seeds carried by a torrent. And a man among the listeners said that the Messenger of God ﷺ had grazed livestock in the desert — that is, he knew such growth from it. **[Ṭabarī]** also records the subtle reading he prefers: the Arabs call a thing whose trace is effaced and whose mention is gone *dead* — so a man with no fame is "dead", and poetry: "You gave my mention life, though I was not obscure." On this reading, the first death is the faintness of being unremembered, the first life is being made a known and remembered creature; then the taking of the soul; then the raising of the body. And he rejects Ibn Zayd's refinement, which has them created in Adam's back, then killed, then created in the womb — for that makes three lives and three deaths while 40:11 says two.

**Language.** "How" is a question about a state, set in the accusative by "you disbelieve"; it is built upon the *fatḥah* although it should be still, since its interrogative sense here is wonder **[Qurṭubī]**. And the question is not a real question: it is a rebuke and a reproach, a fixing of the hearers before their own case — how can you disbelieve in One whose might is this? **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]**. The *wāw* of "and you were dead" is the *wāw* of circumstance, with "already" understood — "and you were, before that, dead"; **[Qurṭubī]** mentions that a recension has *tarjiʿūna* beside the standard *turjaʿūna* — the same verb with two vowels, both attested. And "to Him you shall be returned" — your return is to His reckoning with you for your disbelief **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Cross-references.** 40:11 (two deaths and two lives — cited by Ibn Kathīr and the exegetes as the sister verse); 52:35–36, "Or were they created from nothing, or are they the creators?"; 76:1, "Has there come upon man a time when he was not a thing remembered?" **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]**. And **[Ibn Kathīr]** draws out what these verses settle: that God is the Creator and Sustainer, with authority over His servants — so how can anyone deny Him or set up a rival beside Him?

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** draws two lessons from the verse's order. First, the chief of all blessings is life itself, for without it no other blessing can be enjoyed; and the one who is made to live and to die and to live again is in no position to claim independence of the One at whose order all this happens. Second, death also is a blessing — it is the door into the life that does not end; and **[Saʿdī]** adds the practical conclusion: if you are in His grasp in creation and death and resurrection and reward, then it is not for you to disbelieve; it is for you to fear Him, thank Him, believe in Him, dread His punishment and hope for His reward. **[Study Quran]** notes how the teachers read the first death: either the lifelessness of the stuff out of which we were made, or the condition of being unremembered — "not a thing mentioned" — which al-Ṭabarī records as the usage of the Arab tongue; and Ibn ʿArabī's deeper reading, well known among the Sufis, of a state of non-manifestation in God's knowledge. In every reading the point stands: you did not bring yourself into being, and you will not keep yourself there.

**Reflection.** The verse is one of the Qurʾān's quiet chains: death, life, death, life, return. The first link is already behind every man, the last awaits him, and the whole length of the chain lies in the hands of the One being denied. A man who walks that line and still argues against the One who laid it is not making a case; he is answering a gift with a quarrel **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**.


## 2:29

*"He it is who created for you all that is in the earth; then He turned to the heaven and made them seven heavens — and He knows all things."*

**Meaning.** The blessing is repeated as an argument: the whole earth and what it holds were made *for you* — so how can you be ungrateful to the Maker? — and the same hand that spread the earth for man raised over him seven heavens, arranged and ordered **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**. **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes the pair of proofs: what man sees in himself (2:28) and what he sees around him (2:29).

**Language — "then He turned to the heaven".** The word *istawā* is the crux, and **[Ṭabarī]** works it as the Arabs use it: it may mean a man's youth reaching its full strength; a matter settling after crookedness — al-Ṭirimāḥ's "time passed on the trace of Mahdad and it was effaced, and its ground grew level over it"; turning to a thing; taking possession — "so-and-so rose over the kingdom"; or rising and elevation — "so-and-so rose over his throne". The meaning he prefers for this verse is the last: He rose above the heavens and was high over them, and He arranged them by His power. Then he takes on those who read *istawā* as "turned": if you say He turned to the heaven, they retort, you imply He had been turned away from it; if you answer that it was a turning of management, not of movement, then say the same of rising — a rising of dominion and authority, not a rising of transfer and change; and every answer you give here can be turned against you there. He also settles the timing: the turning came after the heaven was created as smoke, and before it was made into seven heavens, as 41:11 says, "Then He turned to the heaven when it was smoke, and said to it and to the earth: Come, willingly or unwillingly" **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** records the same range, and rejects Abū ʿUbaydah's claim that *idh* in such verses is a mere extra letter.

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** draws out the doctrine the sūrah has been building: "He created for you all that is in the earth" means that nothing in the universe is without benefit for man — food and medicine he uses, things useful without his knowing, even poisons and dangers serve some good, even what is forbidden in one respect is beneficial in another, and all of it can teach him. It reports the saying of Ibn ʿAṭāʾ: God created the universe for you that it may serve you, and created you to serve Him; the wise man knows he will certainly receive what was created for him, and does not, in worrying over it, forget the One for whose sake he himself was created. This is the verse that some jurists used to argue that things are lawful until forbidden, and others to argue the opposite; **[Maʿārif]** sides with the mediation of Ibn Ḥayyān: the *lām* says "for your sake", which is a statement of causation, not a ruling of law — the rulings come from the Sharīʿah, and it is binding to follow them. And **[Ibn Kathīr]** settles the order of creation: the earth came first, then the heaven was made into seven; when Ibn ʿAbbās was asked, he said the earth was created before the heaven and only *spread out* after it, which is what 79:30 means — "and the earth, after that, He spread out" — bringing out its water and pasture and fixing the mountains **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

**Cross-references.** 41:9–12 (the earth in two days, its sustenance measured, then the seven heavens in two days); 79:27–33; 21:32; 67:14, "Does He not know who created?" **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**.

**Reflection.** The verse joins two things that modern eyes hold apart: a universe described as *made for man*, and a God described as *knowing all things*. The first is a gift and the second is a warning — the same knowledge that fitted the world to human life sees exactly what human hands do with it. And **[Maʿārif]** has one more quiet observation on the heavens: the ancients counted nine spheres from their astronomy, but the Book says seven; the conjecture of the philosophers is one thing and the revelation another. The pattern of 2:28–29 is thus a single argument with two halves: the life you were given, and the world you were given — both borrowed.

The benefit-language is not a license to use creation without measure. **[Saʿdī]** treats provision as mercy and a sign that should turn its recipient toward gratitude and worship. **[Maʿārif]** cautions that “created for you” is not by itself a legal ruling that every object is permitted: benefit can exist in one respect while a revealed prohibition governs another. **[Qurṭubī]** records the range of meanings for *istawā* without erasing its grammatical force, while **[Ṭabarī]** reasons from Arabic usage and the sequence of creation. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the order here to the later spreading of the earth; **[Study Quran]** and **[Jalālayn]** give concise readings of the making of the heavens. The closing words, “He knows all things,” prevent the world from being treated as an unobserved resource: it is an ordered gift under the knowledge of its Creator. **[Maʿārif]**

## 2:30

*"And when your Lord said to the angels: I am placing a deputy on the earth — they said: Will You place on it one who will work corruption there and shed blood, while we glorify You with praise and sanctify You? He said: I know what you do not know."*

**Meaning.** After the blessings, the history: God announced to the angels the creation of Adam and the appointment of mankind as His deputies on the earth; the angels asked how such a being could be fit for it, given what they understood of his nature; and God answered only that He knew what they did not **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The ten verses that open here are the story of the father of mankind, which is also the story of the honour granted to his children **[Maʿārif]**.

**Context — the "when".** **[Ṭabarī]** begins with grammar, because a claim had been made that *idh* is a redundant particle that should be dropped. He rejects it: *idh* is a word that indicates a definite point of time, and it is not permissible to void a word that carries meaning — the poets' lines lose their sense if it is removed, and the same argument would let any reader cancel whatever he disliked. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the grammarians' anger at Abū ʿUbaydah for the view: al-Naḥḥās called it an error, since *idh* is a time-adverb and is not among the letters that are added; al-Zajjāj called it a transgression, and supplied the ellipsis it presupposes: "and He began your creation when He said to the angels". And **[Ṭabarī]** explains the flow of the passage: the address of 2:28–29 was a reproach to the disbelievers while God recounted His favours to them and their forebears; the mention of Adam then comes as the example of a father's honour — remember, says the sense of the passage, what I did with your father Adam when I said to the angels that I was placing a deputy in the earth.

**The angels' question.** **[Maʿārif]** rules out the misreading of the scene: God did not consult the angels, for He is not questioned about what He does (21:23) and needs no counsel; He gave His decree the form of a conversation to teach men the value of consultation, and to bring out what the angels had been thinking — that no creature would be made greater than they. Their question, then, was not an objection, for angels do not rebel, but a request for the reason. And their reasoning was not baseless: a being with a nature that can tend to corruption and bloodshed seemed unsuited to the office, while they themselves were sinless in their obedience. God's first answer was by authority: *I know what you do not know* — no creature can weigh the needs of a deputation it was not made for. And the second answer would be by demonstration, when Adam was taught the names (2:31).

**Interpretations of "deputy".** **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Ibn Kathīr]** record the readings: a *khalīfah* who succeeds another over the earth — Adam replacing those who came before him; a *khalīfah* who stands in God's rule over the earth, executing His commands; and the report of Ibn ʿAbbās that a race of jinn had inhabited the earth before, shedding blood, so that Adam succeeded them — which is also offered as the source of the angels' knowledge of what such creatures do. Others say God had told them, or that they inferred it from the name and nature of the creature to be made. The word itself is the centre of the story: man is appointed to act *under* God, never in His place — the honour is real and so is the accountability **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

**Rulings.** **[Ibn Kathīr]**, citing al-Qurṭubī among others, draws a political consequence the exegetes took from this verse: since God set a deputy over the earth to judge between men, help the oppressed against the oppressor, carry out the penalties and forbid evil, the community is obliged to appoint an imam by whom these duties are fulfilled — whatever an obligation requires is itself obligatory. The office is established by a predecessor's appointment, as Abū Bakr was named and ʿUmar was named by Abū Bakr; or by leaving it to a council of the righteous, as ʿUmar did; or by the pledge of the people of authority. The imam must be a responsible Muslim male, able to make legal judgments, sound of body, just, knowing war and politics; from Quraysh according to the correct view, though not necessarily from the Prophet's clan, and not immune from error as the Rāfiḍa claim. The wrongdoer among rulers is not deposed on mere sin — "unless you see open disbelief for which you have proof from God" — and the majority do not permit two imams at once, citing the Prophet's ﷺ command concerning the one who comes to divide a united community.

**Reflection.** The scene is the first council in the Qurʾān — a question asked, an answer given, and a demonstration promised. The angels measured worth by sinlessness — no blood, no corruption — and God measured it by something the angels had no capacity for: knowledge of the names, and the long, difficult work of living as a deputy in a world where choices are real. The verse that follows begins to answer the question with the names; the sūrah will not finish answering it until the story of Adam's fall and restoration is done. What is already clear is that the answer is not "no blood will be shed". The answer is that God knows what the angels do not: the blood is the price of a creature who can choose, and the same creature can be taught the names, prostrate before no one but God, and be forgiven.

The angels' question is framed as asking for wisdom, not challenging God's authority. **[Maʿārif]** stresses that He did not seek their counsel; the dialogue discloses their inference and teaches the human listener. **[Ṭabarī]** links the announcement to the prior reminder of God's favor in creating humanity; **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Ibn Kathīr]** discuss *khalīfah* as succession and transmit reports about jinn before Adam. The title therefore combines dignity with delegated responsibility: a steward remains answerable to the One who appointed him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** details how exegetes derive public responsibility from the appointment, while **[Saʿdī]** reads the scene as showing God's wisdom and knowledge. The angels confess their limit when they say they know only what God taught them. **[Study Quran]** and **[Jalālayn]** summarize the announcement and answer, while **[Maʿārif]** reads the exchange as a lesson in divine purpose rather than consultation.

## 2:31

*"And He taught Adam the names, all of them; then He presented them to the angels and said: Tell Me the names of these, if you are truthful."*

**Meaning.** God answered the angels' question not with an argument but with a demonstration: He taught Adam the names of all things, set the named things before the angels, and challenged them to name them — establishing by knowledge, where they had reasoned by nature, who was fit for the deputation **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**.

**Context.** **[Ṭabarī]** gathers the reports on Adam's creation and name. From Ibn ʿAbbās: God sent the angel of death to take a handful from the surface of the earth — its sweet and its salt — and Adam was created from it, and from this he was named *Ādam*, for he was made from the *adīm* (surface) of the earth. From Saʿīd b. Jubayr, twice: he was named *Ādam* because he was created from the earth's surface. From the Suddī and Ibn Masʿūd's circle: the angel took from the face of the earth, mixed it, took not from one place but from red, white and black soil — and so the children of Adam came out different. And a sound ḥadīth from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī: the Prophet ﷺ said, "God created Adam from a handful taken from all of the earth; so the children of Adam came out according to the earth — red and white and black and in between; the easy and the rough, the bad and the good" (Aḥmad, Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī) **[Ṭabarī]**. *(weak)* From ʿAlī: Adam was created from the surface of the earth, in which are the good and the corrupt — and you see both in his children; the chain runs through ʿAmr b. Thābit, whom the critics abandoned **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the linguistic debate: *Ādam* may come from *adīm*, the earth's face, or from *udmah* — a complexion — with al-Ḍaḥḥāk saying it means brownness and al-Naḍr saying it means whiteness, since a white she-camel is called *admāʾ*; its plural is *ādim* or *awādim*.

**The names.** **[Ṭabarī]** reports the interpretations. Ibn ʿAbbās (via al-Ḍaḥḥāk): the names people use among themselves — man, animal, earth, plain, sea, mountain, donkey, and the like of the nations and other things. Mujāhid: the name of everything. Saʿīd b. Jubayr: the name of everything, down to the camel, the cow and the sheep. Ibn ʿAbbās again, asked whether he was taught the names of the bowl and the pot: "Yes — and even the terms for breaking wind." Qatāda: he named each kind of creature and assigned it to its genus. Al-Rabīʿ b. Anas holds that the names were those of the angels; others that they were the names of Adam's own descendants — and **[Study Quran]** notes that al-Ṭabarī leans to the last, since the plural pronoun used is the masculine plural of persons. **[Study Quran]** also records al-Rāzī's conclusion that Adam was taught all the languages of the earth, and al-Ṭabrisī's note that a similar dispersal of tongues would have occurred after the flood; and Ibn ʿArabī's reading, in which the angels are like the faculties of a soul, each veiled within its own function, while Adam alone was given the synthesis by which all the Divine Names could be known. **[Qurṭubī]** offers the Sufi comment: Adam knew the names by God's teaching and kept them by God's protection — and then forgot what was entrusted to him (20:115), because God left him to himself; the lesson being that whoever is left to himself is lost, and whoever is helped keeps what he has.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** In al-Bukhārī's Book of Tafsīr, from Anas: the Messenger of God ﷺ described the believers' search for intercession on the Day of Resurrection — they come to Adam and say, "You are the father of mankind; God created you with His own hand, made His angels prostrate to you, and taught you the names of everything — intercede for us with your Lord" — and Adam, remembering his error, sends them on to Nūḥ, and so from prophet to prophet until Muḥammad ﷺ **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The honour of the names is thus written into the memory of the Day itself.

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** draws a doctrinal point from the episode: since God "taught Adam the names", language as such is created by God and taught, not invented by man; the many tongues of mankind arose later among his descendants. It also finds a subtle instruction in the pronouns: God said to the angels *anbiʾūnī*, "Tell Me", but to Adam *anbiʾhum*, "Tell them" — Adam was given the rank of teacher and the angels that of pupils, a sign of his superiority. And it observes that the angels' knowledge increased through Adam: even they can learn.

**Reflection.** The first lesson given to mankind is a lesson about knowledge: the creature made from the earth's surface is raised above the angels not by power or by purity, but by what he is taught and can carry. Every title man is given afterwards — deputy, honoured, prostrated to — hangs on this verse. And the challenge in it, "if you are truthful", will echo through the sūrah: the claim that a being is unfit for an office is answered, in God's court, by showing what the being can learn.

The word “all” is treated by the commentators at several scales. **[Ṭabarī]** preserves explanations ranging from the names of ordinary things and species to the names of angels or descendants, rather than reducing the teaching to a single vocabulary list. **[Qurṭubī]** notes the linguistic possibilities in Adam's own name and its relation to the earth; **[Ibn Kathīr]** stresses that God taught names, characteristics, and what things do. **[Study Quran]** records broader readings about languages and the relation of the names to the Divine Names, while **[Maʿārif]** underlines the rank of Adam as teacher. **[Saʿdī]** reads the test as displaying a real gift, not an inherent claim to divinity. **[Jalālayn]** keeps the focus on the named objects and the wisdom of their creation. **[Qurṭubī]** reminds the reader that every explanation remains downstream of God's teaching. The episode therefore grounds human learning in dependence: the ability to name and understand is a bestowed trust, and knowledge is honored because it equips a creature to answer a divine charge.

## 2:32

*"They said: Glory be to You! We have no knowledge except what You have taught us. Truly You are the Knowing, the Wise."*

**Meaning.** The angels' answer is the model of surrender: they declare God free of any rival in knowledge, disavow what they were not given, and attribute all they know to the One who taught them **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** reads the scene as one of the great lessons of the sūrah: the angels returned to their Lord, handed back what was not theirs, and testified of Him what He is — *al-ʿAlīm*, knowing without being taught; *al-Ḥakīm*, complete in wisdom, as Ibn ʿAbbās said, complete in knowledge and in governance.

**Language.** *Subḥānaka* is an infinitive with no verb to govern it — the sense being "we glorify You, declare You free, clear You of the implication that any but You knows the unseen" **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. "We have no knowledge except what You have taught us": the *mā* may be a relative — "except that which You taught us" — or a verbal noun — "except Your teaching of us" **[Qurṭubī]**. The verse's own proof, says **[Ṭabarī]**, is that every claimant of hidden knowledge has been refuted here: the soothsayer, the diviner, the reader of birds, the astrologer — whoever speaks of the unseen without a revelation or a proof has invented a claim that deserves punishment.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** draws one of the most important practical rulings in the whole sūrah from this verse: one who is asked about knowledge and does not have it must say "God knows best" or "I do not know" — in imitation of the angels, the prophets and the virtuous scholars. He records the warning that with the death of the scholars knowledge is lifted and ignorant people remain, who are consulted and give fatwa by their opinion, going astray and leading others astray. Then the examples: the Prophet ﷺ, asked which places are worst, said, "I do not know until I ask Jibrīl," and Jibrīl said the same until he asked Mīkāʾīl — and the answer came: the best of places are the mosques, the worst the markets. Abū Bakr told the grandmother who asked about her inheritance, "Go back until I ask the people." ʿAlī said the coolness of a man's liver — three times — is that when he is asked about what he does not know, he says, "God knows best." Ibn ʿUmar, asked a question, said, "I have no knowledge of it," and then remarked at how good his own answer had been. Yaḥyā b. Saʿīd upbraided al-Qāsim b. ʿUbayd Allāh — son of two rightly-guided imams — that it was ugly for the like of him to be asked about religion and have nothing; al-Qāsim answered: uglier still before God is to speak without knowledge or take from one unworthy of trust. And Mālik b. Anas was asked forty-eight questions and said "I do not know" to thirty-two of them; Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr comments that fairness is among the blessings of knowledge, and Mālik said, "There is nothing rarer in our time than fairness" — and Qurṭubī adds: that was Mālik's time; what of ours?

**Reflection.** The angels knew that they existed to worship, and they knew the limits of what they had been taught; their greatness appears precisely in the sentence *we have no knowledge except what You have taught us*. The scholars of this community took that sentence as a charter: it licenses "I do not know" as a form of knowledge. The danger the verse measures is not the man who lacks information but the man who lacks the modesty to say so — for the second will supply from himself what he owes to God, and will call it knowledge.

The reply does not compete with Adam's knowledge; it models the right response when knowledge reaches its limit. **[Ṭabarī]** reads *subḥānaka* as declaring God free of any rival in knowledge and the angels' answer as returning all credit to their Teacher. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that the grammar allows “except what You taught us” to point either to the things known or to God's act of teaching. In both readings, the source of knowledge is not independent. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains that no creature knows except what God makes known, and that divine wisdom governs both what is taught and what is withheld. **[Jalālayn]** glosses the divine names as showing that nothing escapes God's knowledge and wisdom; **[Saʿdī]** says His perfect wisdom means that creation and command are never without wise purpose. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** draws attention to the limitation of the angels' knowledge in this verse. **[Qurṭubī]** derives a practical rule: a scholar asked about something unknown should say “God knows best,” not invent an answer. **[Ṭabarī]** uses the angels' confession to reject unsupported claims to hidden knowledge. This is a discipline of epistemic humility, not a rejection of learning. **[Ibn Kathīr]** points out the contrast with God's complete knowledge of creation; **[Saʿdī]** likewise links recognition of one's limits to submission. **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:33

*"He said: O Adam, tell them their names. And when he had told them their names, He said: Did I not tell you that I know the unseen of the heavens and the earth, and that I know what you reveal and what you conceal?"*

**Meaning.** The demonstration is completed: Adam is made the teacher of the angels, names the things before them, and then God turns to the angels with the reminder that He had told them He knows what they do not — not only the hidden things of the heavens and the earth, but the words they had spoken and the thoughts they had kept back **[Jalālayn]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** notes what the scene sealed: Adam was made prostrated to, taught, then made a teacher of the very angels — the rank of the office, and the knowledge that fitted him for it.

**Context — what was concealed.** **[Qurṭubī]** reports the readings. Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and Saʿīd b. Jubayr: "what you conceal" is what Iblīs hid in himself of pride and disobedience — his inward refusal to prostrate, brought out soon enough. Al-Ḥasan, asked what the angels had concealed, said: when God created Adam, the angels saw a wondrous creature, and something entered them; they turned to one another and confided that this creature did not concern them — God had created nothing but they were nobler in His eyes than it. And on the other side, some say "what you reveal" is their spoken question — "will You place there one who corrupts?" — and "what you conceal" is what none had said aloud. **[Study Quran]** records both possibilities: the pride of Iblīs, or the belief reported of the angels that God never creates anything but that they are nobler than it — and observes the interpretive wrinkle that if the concealed thing was the angels' own sense of nobility, they accepted Adam's excellence without objection, so what they had concealed was not pride but their own standing. **[Ṭabarī]** argues at length for the Iblīs reading and answers the obvious objection — that the plural is used although one being concealed it. The Arabs do this constantly: "the army was routed" when a part was routed; "those who call you from behind the chambers — most of them do not understand" (49:4), when the caller was a single man. What was hidden in a group may be announced of the group, precisely to bring it into the open.

**Rulings & beliefs.** From this verse **[Qurṭubī]** takes up the long-debated question: which is nobler, the angels or the believers among mankind? The two positions are reported. Those who favour the angels argue from "honoured servants who do not outrun Him in speech" (21:27), "they do not disobey God in what He commands" (66:6), from 4:172, from 6:50, and from the ḥadīth, "Whoever remembers Me in an assembly, I remember him in a better assembly than his." Those who favour mankind argue from 98:7, "those who believe and do righteous works are the best of creatures", from the angels spreading their wings for the seeker of knowledge, and from God's boasting of the people of ʿArafah to the angels. **[Qurṭubī]** reports the scholars' caution: there is no decisive proof on either side, for the matter rests on revelation or consensus and neither is present; the Qadariyya and al-Qāḍī Abū Bakr held the angels nobler, and the argument that Adam must be nobler because the angels were commanded to prostrate to him is unsound: the Kaʿbah is prostrated toward, and everyone agrees the prophets are better than the Kaʿbah; prostration is worship of God alone, and a direction is not made noble by those facing it. He adds the second conclusion: this verse proves that none knows the unseen except by God's teaching — prophet taught, or taught by one whom God taught — so the astrologers and the soothsayers are liars.

**Reflection.** The episode that began with a question — "will You place there one who corrupts?" — closes with a demonstration and a reminder. The angels were answered not by being silenced but by being shown: the creature they doubted out-named them, and their Lord told them plainly that He had heard both what they said and what they had not said. **[Maʿārif]**'s observation on the wording belongs here: God said to Adam *tell them*, not *tell Me* — the teacher's seat was Adam's. And man's first appearance in the Qurʾān is, precisely, as the student who was taught and who then taught the angels: the honour of the deputy is not his body, which is earth, but what he can receive and what he can pass on.

The scene therefore does more than prove Adam's vocabulary: it places knowledge within God's awareness of public speech and private thought. **[Ṭabarī]** includes both the angels' outward question and their hidden assumptions among what God knows. **[Qurṭubī]** transmits readings that the concealed matter was Iblīs's pride, or the angels' thought that no later creature could be more noble than they. **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise links the hidden matter to pride while affirming God's knowledge of the seen and unseen. **[Jalālayn]** reads “what you reveal” as their question and “what you conceal” as their private claim. **[Saʿdī]** stresses that God knows what is outward and inward; **[Maʿārif]** notices that Adam is told to “tell them,” placing him in the teacher's role. **[Study Quran]** notes that the precise referent of the concealed matter is disputed, so the wording can encompass more than one transmitted interpretation. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** The account thus teaches God's complete knowledge without requiring certainty about every unreported motive. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:34

*"And when We said to the angels: Prostrate to Adam — they prostrated, except Iblīs; he refused, was arrogant, and became one of the disbelievers."*

**Meaning.** The superiority that was established by knowledge is now made visible: God commanded the angels to bow before Adam in honour, they all obeyed — and one of those present refused, out of pride, and so entered disbelief **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**. **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse as the continuation of the speech to the Children of Israel: remember, he says, that I created for you what is in the earth, that I said I would place a deputy on it, that I honoured your father with knowledge, and that I commanded My angels to prostrate to him and they did — and the exception of Iblīs from the angels shows that he had been among those commanded, as 7:11–12 states plainly: "except Iblīs — he was not of those who prostrated. He said: What prevented you from prostrating when I commanded you?"

**Who was Iblīs?** **[Ṭabarī]** collects a long dispute. From Ibn ʿAbbās: he was of a tribe of the angels called al-Ḥinn, created from the fire of the *samūm*; his name was al-Ḥārith, and he was one of the keepers of the Garden — while the other angels were created from light, and the jinn mentioned in the Qurʾān from *mārij*, the tongue of flame. In another report from Ibn ʿAbbās, before his sin he was with the angels and named ʿAzāzīl, a dweller of the earth, the most diligent in worship and the most knowledgeable among the angels — and that is precisely what raised him into pride; he belonged to a clan called Jinn. Al-Suddī: he was given the kingdom of the lowest heaven, of a tribe of angels named Jinn because they were the Garden's keepers. Ibn ʿAbbās again: he was among the noblest of the angels, the head of his clan, keeper of the gardens, holding authority in the lowest heaven and on the earth; and 18:50's "he was of the jinn" means, on this reading, "of the keepers of the Garden", as a man is called Makkī or Madanī. Ibn Isḥāq: the Arabs call *jinn* everything that is veiled from sight — the angels are veiled — and cites al-Aʿshā's verse on Solomon: "and He subjected to him nine of the angels' jinn". Against all of these stands al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī: Iblīs was never of the angels for the blink of an eye; he is the origin of the jinn as Adam is the origin of mankind **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Ibn Kathīr]** leans the same way: Iblīs was not an angel, though he was included in the command because he imitated the angels and lived among them — hence he was rebuked with them. **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Study Quran]** all cross-refer the fuller accounts to 7:11–27, 15:31–40, 17:61–65 and 18:50; and the reports identifying him as ʿAzāzīl and the angel-chiefs belong to the material the scholars transmit from earlier scriptures, to be weighed, not relied on.

**The prostration.** **[Qurṭubī]** gives the root: *sujūd* in Arabic is lowliness and submission — the poet describes small mountains as "prostrate to the hooves", and a "sagging eye" is *sājidah*. But what did the command require here? Qatāda's answer: the obedience was to God, and the prostration was *before* Adam — God honoured Adam and commanded the angels to bow toward him **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Yusuf's dream-vision in 12:100 — "and they fell down before him prostrate" — shows the same: such prostration was a greeting of honour, not worship; worship of other than God is disbelief in every law, and no Sharīʿah permits it **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**. And that practice was closed: Muʿādh returned from Syria saying, "I found them prostrating to their priests and monks, and you are more deserving of prostration, O Messenger of God" — and the Prophet ﷺ said: "No. If I were to command a human being to prostrate to a human being, I would command the wife to prostrate to her husband, because of the greatness of his right over her" **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Maʿārif]** sets out al-Jaṣṣāṣ's reasoning: prostration of respect was permitted in earlier laws, but since men slid from such gestures into worship, the final Sharīʿah forbade every act that had served as its doorway — prostration, deep bowing, the placement of one hand on the other as in prayer, even times of prayer chosen to match the disbelievers' — and left only the greeting and the handshake. The same principle banned depictions of living things after they had been permitted: "they made for him whatever he wished — places of worship and images" (34:13).

**Iblīs's sin.** He envied Adam because God had honoured him, and reasoned from his own material: "I was created from fire and he from clay" **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Qatāda's judgment: the first error ever committed was arrogance **[Ibn Kathīr]**. And the Prophet's ﷺ word from the Ṣaḥīḥ settles the danger of the root: "No one who has in his heart the weight of a mustard seed of arrogance will enter Paradise" **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Then the verse's last clause: "and he became one of the disbelievers" — the refusal and the pride came first, and disbelief named the state they produced.

**Reflection.** **[Saʿdī]** draws out the lessons of the episode: God made Adam's superiority known through knowledge, and knowledge is the best quality a servant can hold; God commanded the prostration to honour him once his merit was clear; the one tested who fails only sharpens the merit of the one who succeeds; and the whole account is a lesson in the state of the two fathers — Adam and Iblīs — in God's favour toward the one and the enmity of the other. **[Maʿārif]** adds that the refusal was not of Adam but of God's command as it touched Adam — the first recorded instance of a creature preferring its own judgment to its Lord's. And the Qurʾān tells it here, immediately after the honour, as the sūrah's permanent warning: the same species that was raised above the angels by knowledge can be dragged below them by pride. What is forbidden is not standing tall before men; it is standing tall before God's command.


## 2:35

*"And We said: O Adam, dwell — you and your wife — in the Garden, and eat from it freely wherever you both wish; but do not approach this tree, or you will be among the wrongdoers."*

**Meaning.** Iblīs is gone; the favour is completed by a home and a companion: Adam and Ḥawwāʾ in the Garden with wide and pleasant provision, and one limit placed in the middle of it — not to come near a single tree, on pain of becoming wrongdoers **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Ṭabarī]** notes a point the verse settles: Iblīs was cursed and cast out *before* Adam was settled in the Garden, and the tempter who will appear in 2:36 was already outside, coming at the pair he had promised to ruin. Al-Suddī's report: he swore by God's might to lead Adam and his descendants astray — except the sincerely devoted — after he was cursed and driven out, and before he was sent down to the earth.

**Context — Ḥawwāʾ.** Two reports are given on when she was created. From Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd (in al-Suddī's chain): Adam entered the Garden and walked in it alone, with no wife to take comfort in; he slept, and woke to find a woman sitting at his head, whom God had created from his rib. He asked who she was, and she said, "A woman"; he asked why she was made, and she said, "That you may find rest in me"; and when the angels — watching to see how much knowledge he had — asked him her name, he said *Ḥawwāʾ*, because she was created from something living **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. From Ibn Isḥāq, the other order: she was made from his left rib while he slept, before the command to dwell **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Qurṭubī]** tells it with a tenderness the reader does not expect: God took the rib without Adam feeling it — otherwise no man would ever be gentle to his wife — and made her a woman "that he might find rest in her" (7:189). And the angels, testing his knowledge, asked whether he loved her; he said yes, and asked her whether she loved him; she said no — though in her heart was many times what was in his. And, adds the report, if any woman were ever to be truthful about her love for her husband, it would have been Ḥawwāʾ. On the naming: she is called *marʾah* because she was taken from the *marʾ* (the man); Ḥawwāʾ because she was created from a living one **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* The rib, the sleep and the dialogue come from the reports of the People of the Book and from the exegetes' traditions; **[Study Quran]** notes that some of these details are not strongly attested in the Ḥadīth and that some of what al-Ṭabarī registers does not rise above folklore — while the Qurʾān's own account, which gives fewer details, remains the foundation.

**Language.** *Zawj* and *zawjah* — the Qurʾān uses her as *zawj* without the *hāʾ*, which is the more common form in the Arabs' speech; the poet's "I said, when she came forward, and Zahr came walking with her" **[Ṭabarī]**. *Raghadan* is a wide, pleasant living that tires no one: Imruʾ al-Qays' "while a man is seen in comfort, safe from troubles in a life of ease"; al-Suddī: pleasant; Ibn ʿAbbās: breadth of living; and Mujāhid's sharp reading — "no reckoning upon them" **[Ṭabarī]**. *Shajar* in Arabic is whatever stands on a trunk — "the star and the trees prostrate" (55:6) **[Ṭabarī]**. Which tree it was, **[Ṭabarī]** reports at length and refuses to settle: wheat, a vine, a fig; Abū al-Jald wrote to Ibn ʿAbbās that the tree they were forbidden was wheat, and the tree at which Adam repented the olive. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gives the principle he attributes to Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī: God forbade *a* tree in the Garden and they ate from it; the Qurʾān and the sound Sunnah do not name it, and while it may have been one of those named, this is knowledge that brings no benefit and ignorance of it no harm. **[Maʿārif]** says the same and points out that even the Bible does not name the tree — the apple is a popular misunderstanding, born of the Latin *malum*, which means both "apple" and "evil."

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** takes a lesson in property law from a single word: he was told *dwell* (*uskun*), not *possess* — and dwelling is a grant for a term, not ownership. Some of the knowers said: they entered the Garden as lodgers, not as owners; and that supports what the majority hold — one who is given a house to live in does not thereby own it, and the owner may take it back when the term ends. From this root come the rulings of *suknā*, *ʿumrā* and *ruqbā*, and the ḥadīths on them: "ʿUmrā is valid for the one to whom it is given, and ruqbā is valid for the one to whom it is given" (Jābir, in Ibn Mājah); "There is no ruqbā; whoever is given something as ruqbā, it is his for his life and his death" (Ibn ʿUmar); and the Prophet's ﷺ saying: "The loan must be returned, the gift of milk-animals returned, the debt repaid, and the guarantor is liable" (Abū Umāmah, al-Tirmidhī — he judges it sound) **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Maʿārif]** draws from the same word that Adam's stay was never meant to be permanent; and from the address *you and your wife*: the husband is responsible for providing the dwelling, and the wife lives where he lives — a legal principle taken straight from the grammar of the verse.

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** notes what the prohibition shows about the nature of the offence: Satan "caused them to slip" — the error of Adam and Ḥawwāʾ was not the technical sin of a rebel, but a lapse produced by a deceiver's cunning. And it distinguishes the names: Iblīs is a proper name, from *balasa*, one who has despaired; *Shayṭān* is a genus-word, from *shaṭana*, one far removed from God's mercy — it applies to Iblīs when the Qurʾān says *al-Shayṭān*, and to jinn and men in the plural. **[Study Quran]** records the long dispute whether this Garden was the Garden of Reward — the promise of eternal life in 7:20 and 20:120 would be pointless if Adam already possessed it, and 15:48 says those who enter will never be expelled; Qurṭubī, holding the common view, notes that 78:35 says its people hear no idle talk or lying, and yet Iblīs lied to them. The consensus is that the two Gardens are one; al-Rāzī's conclusion is the honest one: the proofs on all sides are weak and conflicting, so one should stop and refuse to be categorical — for none of this is central to the account.

**Reflection.** **[Saʿdī]** reads the giving before the forbidding: God created Adam, favoured him, then completed the favour with a wife to find rest in, a Garden to dwell in, and food to eat without labour — and only then, inside all that generosity, set one tree. The prohibition was itself meant to be a blessing: an opportunity to obey in the middle of abundance. And 20:118–19 has the promise God made with the gift: "You shall not go hungry in it nor go naked, nor shall you thirst nor be scorched by the sun." The first home of man was provision without exhaustion and shade without morning — and the first limit was a single tree. Man would prove unable to leave even that one alone.


## 2:36

*"Then Satan caused them to slip from it, and brought them out of what they were in; and We said: Go down, some of you an enemy to others. And for you on the earth is a dwelling place and provision for a time."*

**Meaning.** The serpent's work is done before the reader has turned the page: the one limit given in 2:35 is crossed, and the pair are out of the Garden into enmity and a temporary earth **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the sharp irony that the tempter did not get what he wanted: Iblīs aimed at Adam's fall and removal, and his fall became God's appointment — Adam left the Garden as a neighbour of God and entered the earth as His deputy. "How far between the deputy and the neighbour!"

**Readings.** **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** set out the two readings: the majority read *fa-azzalahumā* — with a doubled *lām* — "caused them to stumble", the *zallah* that is the fault; Ḥamza read *fa-azālahumā* — "removed them". **[Ṭabarī]** prefers the first, because 2:36 continues "and brought them out of what they were in" — if the verb already meant removal, the second clause would repeat it; rather, the devil *tripped* them and the bringing out followed. **[Qurṭubī]** agrees that the majority reading is more fitting, since the Qurʾān itself says "Satan only caused them to stumble by some of what they earned" (3:155), and whispering is entering someone into error, not moving him from place to place — the devil has no power over anyone's location.

**Stories & occasions.** *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* The reports on how the tempters got in come from the transmitted narratives, and **[Ṭabarī]** sets out the fullest of them from Wahb b. Munabbih: the forbidden tree had branches interwoven and a fruit the angels ate for their immortality; when Iblīs wanted to trip the pair he entered the belly of the serpent — which had four legs, like a young camel, the finest animal God created — came out inside the Garden, took from the tree, brought it to Ḥawwāʾ saying, "Look at this tree, how sweet its scent and taste and how beautiful its colour" — and she ate, then brought it to Adam, and he ate, and their nakedness showed. Adam hid inside the tree, and his Lord called him: "Where are you?" — "Here I am, Lord." — "Will you not come out?" — "I am ashamed before You." — and the sentence fell on the earth, on Ḥawwāʾ, and on the serpent **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. Al-Suddī's report from Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and others: Iblīs was barred from the Garden by its guardians, so he spoke to the serpent and had it carry him in its mouth past them; he called to Adam, "Shall I point you to the tree of immortality and a kingdom that does not decay?" (20:120) — meaning, if you eat you will be kings like God or live forever; and he swore to them that he was a sincere adviser **[Ṭabarī]**. Al-Rabīʿ from Abū al-ʿĀliyah: he came to Ḥawwāʾ first, and reminded her of what God had forbidden — "He forbade you only that you not become angels or immortals" (7:20) — and she ate, then ordered Adam and he ate; and the tree was one whose eater suffered a bodily change, which is not fitting in the Garden **[Ṭabarī]**. Ibn Isḥāq: his first move was to wail over them until they asked what grieved him, and he said, "I weep for you — you will die and leave this blessing"; the suggestion took hold, then he whispered **[Ṭabarī]**. Saʿīd b. al-Musayyib swore that Adam did not eat knowingly: Ḥawwāʾ gave him wine until he was drunk, then led him to the tree **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the same cycle with the sequel: the serpent lost its legs, enmity was set between it and the children of Adam, and so we are commanded to kill it; Ibn ʿAbbās used to say of it that Iblīs had said to the serpent, "Let me in and you are under my protection" — so, "break Iblīs's covenant." **[Study Quran]** is candid about this material: many such details come from Jewish, Christian and Arab traditions, some are not strongly attested in the Ḥadīth, and some that al-Ṭabarī registers do not rise above folklore.

**Language.** *Azzala* is from *zalla*, to slip; *zallah* is the fault; and Imruʾ al-Qays uses *yuzillu* of a light rider slipping from a horse's back and of the smooth rock from which a man slides **[Qurṭubī]**. "Go down" (*ihbiṭū*) is plural, and **[Study Quran]** notes that the plural can apply to Adam and Eve or to Iblīs as well; the most fitting reading takes in Adam, Eve and all their progeny, with Iblīs and his progeny sometimes included — Adam and Eve standing for mankind. *Mustaqarr* is a place of settling, as the earth is called a *qarār* in 40:64; some read it as the graves, but the Qurʾān's other uses (6:98; 25:24; 36:38) point to the dwelling **[Study Quran]**. "For a time" is literally "until a moment" — until death, or the Day of Resurrection, or the term appointed **[Study Quran]**.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** takes a legal chapter from the enmity with the serpent. The Prophet ﷺ commanded killing it; from Ibn ʿUmar, "five are killed by one in iḥrām", the serpent among them; from Sākinah bint al-Jaʿd, from Sarrāʾ bint Nabhān al-Ghanawiyyah: "Kill the snakes, small and great, black and white — whoever kills one has it as a ransom from the Fire, and whoever it kills is a martyr." The scholars explain the ransom: the serpent shares with Iblīs in the harm done to Adam and his children, so killing it is like killing an unbeliever. Ibn Masʿūd's report: at Minā a serpent fled into a stone hole, and the Prophet ﷺ said, "Bring a palm frond and fire, and burn it over it" — a report the legal scholars used in the case of something harmful that cannot be reached otherwise; they note that ʿAbd Allāh b. Masʿūd also reported the serpent that escaped in the cave, when the Prophet ﷺ said, "God protected it from your evil as He protected you from its evil." The jurists weighed the two: the command to kill is guidance toward repelling a feared harm, and the means are as the situation allows, while burning is a last resort, disliked by Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī as a form of mutilation, and justified here because no other means could reach it **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** addresses the puzzle of how Satan entered the Garden after his expulsion: perhaps he never met them and planted the suggestion from afar — which is precisely what he can do — or perhaps he took the form of a snake or another creature, and this disguise is why Adam did not remember God's warning about his enemy; 7:21, where he swears he is a well-wisher, suggests he did meet and speak to them.
