Then God showed them favour with complete deliverance and the drowning of their enemy while they watched, so that their eyes might be comforted. *And in that was a great trial from your Lord* — trial here, he says, means kindness: it obliges you to thank Him and stand by His commands.

**Context.** The address is to the living while the events befell their forefathers: *idh* is attached to "remember My favour", and the deliverance of the fathers is reckoned to the sons because it is the kind of blessing whose benefit descends — as 69:11 says, "when the water overflowed, We carried you in the ship", meaning your fathers **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Ṭabarī]** justifies the usage with a line of poetry: al-Akhṭal reviles Jarīr's tribe with a battle at which Jarīr was not even born — "al-Hudhayl rose against you and reached you at Irāb" — because it was the deed of his people; so God attributes to the present generation what He did to their ancestors.
*(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* The reports on why the slaughter began: **[Ibn Kathīr]** — Pharaoh saw in a dream a fire coming out of Bayt al-Maqdis and entering the houses of the Egyptians while passing over the Israelites, meaning his kingdom would be toppled by a man of the Children of Israel; others say his courtiers told him the Israelites were expecting a man to arise among them and establish a state. **[Jalālayn]** — some of their priests told him, "a child born among the Israelites will bring about the end of your rule." **[Ṭabarī]** gives Ibn ʿAbbās's fuller account: Pharaoh and his court recalled God's promise to Abraham of prophets and kings in his progeny, and agreed to send men with knives through the land, killing every male newborn; when they saw the elders dying in their time and the infants being killed, they feared the Israelites would be exhausted as a workforce — so they killed the male children one year and left them the next. Hārūn was born in the year of mercy; Mūsā the year after. And through ʿIkrimah from Ibn ʿAbbās: the soothsayers told Pharaoh that a child would be born that year who would take his kingdom, so he placed a hundred men over every thousand women. **[Study Quran]** observes that the Qurʾān itself gives no reason for the killing and, noting that Pharaoh repeats the same threat when Mūsā is grown (7:127; 40:25), reads it as collective punishment and control rather than the fulfilment of a predicted fate — though it records that Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr pass on the forecast of soothsayers or astrologers. The killer detail **[Ṭabarī]** preserves from Ibn Isḥāq is the machinery of it: Pharaoh made the Israelites servants and slaves, sorting them into those who built, those who ploughed, those who sowed for him, and those who served — and whoever among them was in none of these was charged the *jizyah*; al-Suddī says he put them to filthy work.

**Language.** *Āl* is *ahl*, "people": the *hāʾ* was exchanged for an *alif* — the way *māh* becomes *māʾ* — and it returns to the *hāʾ* in the diminutive, *uhayl* **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *Āl Firʿawn* are his people, his followers, his religion — not his blood only, and **[Qurṭubī]** builds the point: the *āl* of the Prophet ﷺ in this usage are those upon his religion in his time and every age, kin or not; Abū Lahab and Abū Jahal are not of his *āl* or his *ahl* however close the kinship — as an-Nūḥ's son was told, "he is not of your family" (11:46). And the proof from 40:46, "Enter, house of Pharaoh, the severest punishment" — *āli dīnihi*, since the man had no son, daughter, father, uncle or brother at all. *Firʿawn*: **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Ibn Kathīr]** — it was the title of the Amalekite kings of Egypt, like Qayṣar for Rome, Kisrā for Persia, Najāshī for Abyssinia, Tubbaʿ for Yemen; **[Qurṭubī]** adds the name of Mūsā's Pharaoh: al-Walīd b. Muṣʿab b. al-Rayyān, of the line of ʿImlīq, kunya Abū Murrah; in the People of the Book's account, Qābūs. And every tyrant is a *Firʿawn*. *Yasūmūnakum*: Abū ʿUbaydah — "they humiliate you" or "they impose on you", from *sāma ḫuṭṭata kh asf*, as ʿAmr b. Kulthūm says, "when the king imposes humiliation on a people"; others — the *sāma* is what is continuous, as the freely grazing flock continues to graze **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. *Sawʾ al-ʿadhāb*: **[Ṭabarī]** refines it, against the common gloss "the worst": if it meant worst it would have been *aswaʾ*; it means the punishment that *grieves* them — which is named exactly in the rest of the verse.

**Rulings.** **[Ṭabarī]** draws a legal conclusion from the wording — that God attributes the killing to the *house* of Pharaoh rather than to Pharaoh himself, though they did it by his order and his power — because the one who personally carries out the killing or the torture is the one to whom the deed belongs. Whoever himself kills a soul unjustly is the slayer against whom retaliation is due, even if he did it under the compulsion of another's command, whether that commander be an authority, a marauder or a usurper. **[Qurṭubī]**, in his treatment of *āl*, gives the doctrinal ruling on the family of the Prophet ﷺ that follows from the same usage, including the ḥadīth from ʿAmr b. al-ʿĀṣ in Muslim — heard openly, not in secret — that the family of Abū so-and-so are not his allies: "my ally is God and the righteous believers." And he records the word of Abū Ḥumayd al-Sāʿidī, taught by the Prophet ﷺ for the blessing of prayer: "O God, bless Muḥammad and his wives and his offspring, as You blessed the family of Ibrāhīm."

**Belief & reflection.** The verse closes on *balāʾ* — a word that **[Study Quran]** notes is lexically a testing and finding out, here usually tied to the sufferings but understood by some (Ṭabarī among them) as favour, since 2:47 used the same sense of blessing; and the Qurʾān uses one word because the two faces are one thing: "We try you with evil and with good as a trial" (21:35), and "We tried them with good things and with evil things" (7:168) **[Study Quran]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. What the verse is asking of its hearers is that both kinds of *balāʾ* should produce the same result — the man drowning in hard labour and the man walking out of the sea are being asked the same question. **[Saʿdī]** puts the obligation at the end, and it stands for the whole passage: mercy remembered is meant to become gratitude, and gratitude means standing by the commands of the One who gave the mercy.


## 2:50

*"And when We parted the sea for you, and delivered you, and drowned the house of Pharaoh while you looked on."*

**Meaning.** The second panel of the same deliverance: the water opened, Israel crossed, and the army behind them went under — with the eye open the whole time **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Saʿdī]** makes the observation at the end of the previous verse do its work here: the deliverance was completed and the enemy drowned *while they watched*, so that their eyes might be comforted — that is, the mercy was not merely rescue but vindication seen.

**Language.** *Faraqnā* is to separate and divide, as one parts the hair; from the same root come *al-Furqān*, because it separates truth from falsehood, "the day of *al-furqān*" (8:41) at Badr, and "a Qurʾān which We have *faraqnāhu*" (17:106) — We set it out and established it. Al-Zuhrī read *farraqnā* with a doubled *rāʾ*: We made it into parts. And the *bāʾ* in *bikum*: "for you", or, keeping its own force, "We parted the sea *by means of* you" — the parting happened by their entering it, so the sea stood as two walls with them between, which is borne out by "and it split" (26:63) **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**. **[Ṭabarī]** rejects the Baṣran gloss that it means "We separated between you and the water", since God said He parted the sea *by* the people, not between them and it — and what that parting was, he says, is what the reports describe: twelve tribes, twelve roads, a tribe to each path. *Al-bahr* is the salt sea, named for its vastness; a horse is *baḥr* when its running is wide; and the Prophet ﷺ said of Mandūb, Abū Ṭalḥah's horse, "we found him a sea" **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Context — how it happened.** From Ibn Isḥāq by way of Muḥammad b. Kaʿb al-Quraẓī and ʿAbd Allāh b. Shaddād: Pharaoh set out after Mūsā with seventy thousand dark horses besides the piebalds; Mūsā reached the sea with nowhere to turn, and when the two hosts saw one another the companions of Mūsā cried, "We are surely overtaken," and Mūsā said, "No — my Lord is with me; He will guide me" (26:61–62), meaning to deliverance: "He has promised me, and His promise is not broken." The sea had been told: when Mūsā strikes you, split — and it spent the night churning, out of fear of God and waiting for His command; then Mūsā struck it with the staff in which God had placed His authority, and "each part was like a great mountain" — a hill standing up out of the ground **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** also gives, without isnād to a Companion, the account of the crossing's end: when Israel had all entered and none remained, Pharaoh came to the sea's edge on his horse, which shied from entering; Jibrīl appeared before it on a mare in season, and the stallion followed her in, and the army followed Pharaoh; Jibrīl went out ahead of them, Mīkāʾīl drove them from behind saying, "Catch up with your master," and when the last of Israel was across and the last of Egypt was in, the sea closed over them; then Pharaoh, seeing God's power and knowing his own humiliation, cried, "I believe that there is no god but the One the Children of Israel believe in, and I am of those who submit" (10:90) **[Ṭabarī]**.

**Stories & occasions.** *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* The fullest narrative in the corpus is ʿAbd Allāh b. Masʿūd's, through ʿAmr b. Maymūn: when Mūsā left with Israel, Pharaoh said, "Let none follow them until the rooster crows" — and no rooster crowed that night; he called for a sheep to be slaughtered and said, "I shall not be done with its liver until six hundred thousand Copts have gathered to me," and he was not done with it before six hundred thousand had gathered. At the sea, Yūshaʿ b. Nūn asked Mūsā, "Where has your Lord commanded you?" — "In front of you," pointing to the sea. Yūshaʿ drove his horse in until it was at the depth, was carried off, and came back; he did that three times, saying, "By God, you have not lied and you have not been lied to." Then God revealed, "Strike the sea with your staff", and the sea, which had spent the night trembling, not knowing from which side it would be struck, opened into twelve paths, one for each tribe, each like a great mountain; the tribes complained they could not see one another, so Mūsā struck the water-walls with his staff and there were windows in them through which they could see each other, until they came out. Then Pharaoh entered with his host, on a long-tailed black stallion that feared to plunge, and Jibrīl again drew it in with a mare; the last of the people of Mūsā cleared the far side, the last of the people of Pharaoh entered, and the sea closed **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Saʿdī]** keeps the whole narration of the passage in one sweep — the warning that they would be followed, the drowning of the enemy before their eyes — and includes the report that 12,000 of Israel, or by another count 600,000 fighting men, faced Pharaoh's 1,100,000 horses **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Ḥadīth & āthār — ʿĀshūrāʾ.** Neither the Qurʾān nor these verses name the day; the day is fixed in the Sunnah. **Muslim**, al-Bukhārī and others report from Ibn ʿAbbās: the Prophet ﷺ came to Madīnah and found the Jews fasting ʿĀshūrāʾ; he asked what it was, and they said, "A great day on which God delivered Mūsā and his people and drowned Pharaoh and his people; Mūsā fasted it in thanks." The Prophet ﷺ said, "We have more right to Mūsā than you" — and fasted it and commanded its fasting **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Qurṭubī]** raises the objection to reading this as mere imitation: ʿĀʾishah reported that ʿĀshūrāʾ was a day Quraysh fasted in the Age of Ignorance and the Prophet ﷺ fasted it then; when he came to Madīnah he fasted it and commanded it, and when Ramaḍān was prescribed the obligation shifted and ʿĀshūrāʾ was left as one may choose. The virtue of it remains in the reports: it expiates the year before it.

**Belief & rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** draws the doctrinal point the verse is often used to defend: the splitting of the sea proves that miracles (*muʿjizāt*) do occur at the hands of prophets — the point directed at those of Westernized education among Muslims who have tried to deny them. And **[Study Quran]** notes the scene is retold in the Sūrah of Provisions of Mercy: drowning as a punishment appears again in the story of Nūḥ, in 17:69 and 36:43, and most authorities identify this unnamed sea as the Red Sea.

**Reflection.** The two halves of the verse are the two ends of one mercy: God saved by opening a road where none existed, and destroyed by closing the one that had just been used. The detail that matters most is at the end — "while you looked on." God did not merely take them out of Egypt; He let them watch the end of the thing that had owned them. For a people leaving generations of slavery, seeing is part of the cure, and the same scene is the reason the fast of ʿĀshūrāʾ has stayed alive among people who never saw it.

## 2:51

*"And when We appointed for Mūsā forty nights — then you took the calf in his absence, and you were wrongdoers."*

**Meaning.** The favour of the Book is set beside the infidelity of the receivers: while Mūsā was on the mountain for the appointment that would bring them the Torah, they took a calf as a god — and God names them by what they were: *wrongdoers* **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the point of the reminder: God is telling the Jews of the Prophet's day about the deed of their fathers — their denial of their prophets in the midst of favours that came one after another — so that they might recognise themselves as walking the same road, and be warned that what fell on the first deniers may fall on them.

**Language — the reading.** *Wāʿadnā* or *waʿadnā*: **[Ṭabarī]** sets out the two positions. Those who read *wāʿadnā* argued that every *mutual* appointment for a meeting is an appointment from both sides, each of them promising the other the encounter; those who read *waʿadnā* argued that *muwāʿadah* belongs to human equals, whereas God is alone in promise and threat, and the Qurʾān everywhere says "God promised you" (14:22), "God has promised those who believe" (24:55), "when God promised you one of the two parties" (8:7). **[Ṭabarī]**'s own verdict: both readings came down from the community and neither invalidates the other. The apparent addition in one is not a difference in meaning, because when God promised Mūsā the meeting at the mountain, Mūsā's acceptance of it was itself a promise — God did not appoint the mountain except with Mūsā's willing response. **[Qurṭubī]** records the same debate with the readers' names — Abū ʿAmr and Abū Ḥātim preferred *waʿadnā*, al-Naḥḥās called *wāʿadnā* the better, and it was the reading of Mujāhid, al-Aʿraj, Ibn Kathīr, Nāfiʿ, Ḥamzah, al-Kisāʾī and al-Aʿmash; and al-Zajjāj's reasoning: Mūsā's obedience and acceptance stand in the place of a promise from his side. And *Mūsā*: in Coptic, *mū* is water and *shā* is tree — he was found in the ark at a place of water and trees by Pharaoh's house, so he was named for the place where he was found; it is a foreign name, and does not take the genitive **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. His lineage, per Ibn Isḥāq: Mūsā b. ʿImrān b. Yaṣhar b. Qāhith b. Lāwī b. Yaʿqūb — Israel — b. Isḥāq b. Ibrāhīm **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *Ittakhadhtum* is *iftāʿaltum* from *akhdh*, the second hamzah eased away for the collision of two hamzahs; *al-ʿijl* is the calf — and **[Qurṭubī]** notes a suggested reason for the name: they named it so from the haste (*ʿajalah*) with which they took to its worship.

**Context — the forty nights.** Abū al-ʿĀliyyah: Dhū al-Qaʿdah and ten from Dhū al-Ḥijjah; Mūsā left his people, appointed Hārūn over them, stayed on the mountain forty nights, and the Torah was sent down to him in the Tablets — which were of hail-stone, per the report **[Ṭabarī]**. Ibn Isḥāq: God promised thirty nights and completed them with ten, and Mūsā went out to his Lord eagerly, asking that his brother succeed him among the people and not follow the way of the corrupters **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Ibn Kathīr]** joins it to 7:142, "We appointed for Mūsā thirty nights and completed them with ten." **[Qurṭubī]** gives the sequence that produced the calf: Mūsā went out to the mountain with seventy of the best of Israel, and the appointment was forty nights; the people counted twenty days and twenty nights and said, "He has broken his promise to us," so they took the calf, with al-Sāmirī telling them, "This is your god and the god of Mūsā" (20:88) — and they settled into his word, though Hārūn forbade them and said, "O my people, you have only been tried by it; your Lord is the Compassionate; so follow me and obey my command" (20:90). They answered, "We will not cease to be its devotees until Mūsā returns to us."

**Rulings — why nights, not days.** **[Qurṭubī]** raises the question and gives the answer: the night precedes the day in rank, and by it dates are reckoned — the nights open the months and the days follow. And he records al-Naqqāsh's inference, that the verse points to the joining of fasts, since had God mentioned the days one might have thought Mūsā broke his fast at night; specifying the *nights* carries the force that he continued forty days with their nights. From this, he says, the Sufis took their evidence for continuous fasting, and held the best of it forty days.

**Stories & occasions.** *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* The narrative of how al-Sāmirī obtained the dust is told at length by Ibn ʿAbbās through ʿIkrimah: he recognised Jibrīl when he saw him at the sea, driving Pharaoh's horse into the water, and took a handful from beneath the hoof of the horse; and Ibn Masʿūd is reported to have read 20:96 as *"a handful from the trace of the horse of the messenger."* Al-Sāmirī's mother had left him in a cave as an infant, and Jibrīl came and fed him from his fingers — milk from one, honey from another, butter from another — until he grew **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Maʿārif]** tells the outcome: the calf, made of gold or silver and animated by the dust, acquired life, and the ignorant among them worshipped it. **[Qurṭubī]** also records the detail that Hārūn was followed in refusing it by only twelve thousand, while the rest — more than two million, by that report — fell into its worship. These narratives belong to the Israelite reports the commentators transmit; the Qurʾān's own account is the summary at 7:148–155 and 20:83–98, and **[Maʿārif]** keeps that distance: what the verse itself says is that a man named al-Sāmirī fashioned the figure and the people who had been given so much fell to worshipping it.

**Reflection.** The word the verse ends on is the one that explains the rest: *wrongdoers* — **[Maʿārif]** defines the term exactly, that injustice lies in putting a thing where it does not belong, and that is what idolatry is at the root. The scene has a hard symmetry worth noticing: a people who had just been carried through the sea, whose God had drowned an empire for them, made a god out of a metal calf in the weeks while their prophet was away receiving their Book. The distance between a delivered people and a calf is not as long as it looks; it is the length of a wait that grew inconvenient.

The sequence keeps the delay in view. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the forty nights to the thirty plus ten of 7:142; **[Ṭabarī]** preserves both the “We promised” and “We appointed” readings without forcing a difference in the event. **[Qurṭubī]** records how the people mistook Mūsā's absence for a broken promise and joined al-Sāmirī's claim, while **[Saʿdī]** treats their impatience before the appointment was complete as part of their culpability. The verse itself does not recount the making of the calf in detail. **[Study Quran]** directs readers to 7:148–154 and 20:83–98 for fuller accounts; **[Maʿārif]** cautions that transmitted details about the calf's material and animation go beyond the concise wording here. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The address to later Israelites therefore makes inherited history a warning, not a substitute for present response. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:52

*"Then We pardoned you after that, so that you might give thanks."*

**Meaning.** The sentence runs directly into the next: the pardon of 2:52 came after the repentance described in 2:54 — "and when We said, 'Strike him with a part of it'" — and here the pardoning is stated plainly, followed by its aim: gratitude **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]**. **[Qurṭubī]** presses the grammar of the aim: *laʿalla* expresses expectation, and it is not that God was in doubt — what it means is that when people receive a pardon, onlookers may expect them to feel grateful.

**Language — ʿafw and shukr.** *Al-ʿafw* is God's passing over His creatures; **[Qurṭubī]** distinguishes it from *maghfirah*: pardon may come before punishment or after it, whereas forgiveness is never accompanied by punishment at all. Whoever deserved a punishment and had it left to him has been pardoned: so *ʿafw* is the erasure of the sin, from the wind that *ʿafā* the trace — blew it away. And the same root carries the opposite sense, increase, as in 7:95, "until they *ʿafaw*" (multiplied) — it is among the words that are their own opposites. *Shukr* in the language is a showing forth: a beast is *shakūr* when it displays fat beyond the feed it is given; the reality of it is praise of a benefactor for a good he has done you **[Qurṭubī]**. Al-Jawharī: "I thank him" and "I thank *to* him" — the latter more eloquent; *shukrān* is the opposite of *kufrān*. And the ḥadīth of Tirmidhī and Abū Dāwūd from Abū Hurayrah: "He who does not thank people does not thank God." Al-Khaṭṭābī explains it two ways: one whose nature is to deny the kindness of people and leave their good unacknowledged is the sort whose habit is to deny God's favour likewise; or, God will not accept a servant's thanks for His goodness while he refuses to thank the goodness of other men, since the two matters are joined.

**Belief — what gratitude is.** **[Qurṭubī]** collects the sayings of the masters. Sahl b. ʿAbd Allāh: gratitude is striving in obedience and avoiding disobedience, in secret and in public. Some: gratitude is the acknowledgment of one's shortcoming in gratitude itself — which is why God said, "Work, family of Dāwūd, in gratitude" (34:13), and Dāwūd asked, "How can I thank You, my Lord, when the gratitude is itself a favour from You?" and God answered, "Now you have known Me and thanked Me." He asked to be shown the most hidden of God's blessings on him, and God said, "Dāwūd — breathe": and Dāwūd breathed, and God said, "Who can count this blessing of nights and days?" And Mūsā said, "How can I thank You, when the smallest of Your blessings on my hand cannot be repaid by my whole life of work?" and God revealed, "Mūsā, now you have thanked Me." Al-Junayd: the reality of gratitude is the inability to give thanks — and he tells that at seven years old, sitting before al-Sarī al-Saqaṭī while the company spoke of gratitude, he was asked what gratitude is and said, "That God not be disobeyed by means of His blessings," and al-Sarī said to him, "I fear your share of God will be your tongue." Al-Junayd said he never stopped weeping at those words. Shiblī: gratitude is humility, guarding one's good deeds, opposing appetite, giving obedience, and watching the Compeller of earth and heaven. And Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī: gratitude toward those above you is obedience, toward your equals requital, and toward those below you kindness and generosity.

**Cross-references.** 2:51, 2:53, 2:54; 7:142–155; 20:83–98; 4:153; 34:13; 7:95; 14:22; 24:55; 8:7 **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Reflection.** The verse places the pardon before the thanks and makes the thanks the purpose of the pardon — which is the whole architecture of mercy in the Qurʾān: forgiveness is not the closing of an account but the opening of a relationship. **[Study Quran]** notes that 4:153 mentions this same pardon as a sign of God's mercy and power that passes human understanding. And the fact that the pardon follows a sin as large as worship of a calf is not a softening of the sin; it is the measure of what gratitude is for. A people who had been forgiven something unforgivable owed God a life; the verse tells them what the debt looks like, and the answer it settles on is not sacrifice or payment, but thanks.

Pardon is not approval of calf worship, nor does the verse detach forgiveness from return. **[Maʿārif]** states that the Israelites were pardoned only after the repentance described at 2:54; **[Saʿdī]** describes the command to repent and the pardon that followed. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the forgiveness to their worship of the calf and to the appointed period; **[Ṭabarī]** reads repentance as returning from what God dislikes to obedience He accepts. **[Qurṭubī]** distinguishes *ʿafw*, the passing over or erasure of a sin, from *maghfirah*, and explains the expectation in *laʿalla* as something people may have of a pardoned community. **[Maʿārif]** The stated goal, gratitude, is therefore ethical: **[Saʿdī]** relates it to keeping God's commands, while **[Qurṭubī]** gathers accounts of gratitude as obedience and restraint from sin. **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** connect this reprieve to the mercy remembered elsewhere; **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]**. The purpose clause is an invitation and expectation, not a declaration that every hearer will express thanks. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:53

*"And when We gave Mūsā the Book and the Criterion, that you might be guided."*

**Meaning.** Another item in the same list of favours: the Scripture itself, and with it the criterion that sorts true from false — given not as a burden but as guidance **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Jalālayn]** takes *al-Furqān* here as an explicative of "the Book": it is the mode by which one discriminates between truth and falsehood, and between the lawful and the unlawful. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the whole as a favour to the People of Israel in the persons of their ancestors: God gave Mūsā the Torah which He wrote for him in the Tablets, and by which He separated the true from the untrue, "so that you might be guided" — meaning, that you might follow the truth in it.

**Language — what the Furqān is.** The noun means the separating and the distinction, from the same root as *farq*, parting **[Qurṭubī]**. The interpretations:
- "The Book" is the Torah by consensus of the exegetes; and on *al-Furqān* Abū al-ʿĀliyyah and Mujāhid (through three chains) said: that by which God separates between truth and falsehood — Mujāhid adding that the Book *is* the Furqān. **[Ṭabarī]** prefers this: *furqān* is a description of the Torah, so that the verse means "We gave Mūsā the Torah, which We wrote for him in the Tablets and by which We separated the true from the false"; "the Book" stands in place of the Torah as its description, and "the Criterion" joins it as another description of the same thing.
- Ibn ʿAbbās: *al-Furqān* is a comprehensive name covering the Torah, the Gospel, the Psalms and the Qurʾān — the scripture as such, in every dispensation, is a criterion **[Ṭabarī]**.
- Ibn Zayd: the *furqān* given Mūsā was the parting of the sea — God separated between them and gave him victory, just as at Badr He gave the Prophet ﷺ victory and destroyed Abū Jahal on the day of the Furqān (8:41) **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** records the same and adds another reading: the *furqān* was the deliverance from distress, since they had been enslaved with the Copts — and 8:29 bears that sense, "if you are mindful of God, He will grant you a *furqān*", that is, a way out.
- Others: it is the proof (*al-ḥujjah*) and the clear demonstration (Ibn Baḥr); or the *wāw* is a link, and the meaning is "the Book, the Criterion", adjectives on one noun **[Qurṭubī]**.
- **[Qurṭubī]** also records and rejects a strange gloss attributed to al-Farrāʾ and Quṭrub — "We gave Mūsā the Torah, and Muḥammad the Furqān." al-Naḥḥās rejected it on two grounds: grammatically, a word joined by *wa* must belong to the same class as what it is joined to; and in meaning, God said of Mūsā *and* Hārūn, "We gave them the Book, the Criterion" (21:48). Al-Zajjāj's alternative: the two names are the same thing repeated for confirmation, as in the poetry where "falsehood" stands beside "lying" and "far-ness" beside "distance" **[Qurṭubī]**.
- **[Study Quran]** frames the question the same way: "the Book" may be all of the Torah, part of it, or something alongside it; and the Criterion may be a description of the Book or the power of discrimination given to Mūsā and Hārūn in 21:48 — noting that the Prophet ﷺ is given "the Book and Wisdom" (2:151; 3:164), which suggests *furqān*, like wisdom, can be an attribute bestowed on a prophet rather than a written text.
- **[Maʿārif]** gives the same three options in the form of a definition: *al-Furqān* in the Qurʾānic vocabulary is whatever separates truth from falsehood — so here it is (a) the Sharīʿah injunctions in the Torah, which resolve the differences that arise in doctrine and practice; or (b) the miracles, which decide between a true and a false claim palpably; or (c) the Torah itself, which holds the twin qualities of being God's Book and being the instrument of distinction.

**Belief & reflection.** The verse's last words are the point of the whole apparatus: "that you might be guided." God does not give a Book to be admired or even only to be obeyed — the aim is a road found and walked. And the fact that the thing given is called a *criterion* tells us what the community was meant to do with it. A people who can no longer tell the true from the untrue has lost more than an argument; and the cure God sent was not a new power or a new empire but a text with edges. **[Saʿdī]** lists the Book among the favours whose memory obliges gratitude and action — the guidance is the gift, and the people who receive a criterion are being asked to use it.

The paired nouns admit two compatible reading strategies. **[Ṭabarī]** reports that some treat the *Furqān* as the separation of truth and falsehood effected by the Torah, while Mujāhid identifies the Book itself with the *Furqān*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise glosses it as that by which truth and falsehood are distinguished. **[Maʿārif]** gives the Torah's rulings, its signs and miracles, or the Torah itself as readings; **[Qurṭubī]** tracks the same semantic range. The verse therefore need not posit two separate scriptures: a revealed Book may be a *kitāb* and a *furqān* in function. **[Study Quran]** notes the term's wider Qurʾānic use for a criterion, while **[Saʿdī]** treats scripture as guidance that clarifies religious matters. **[Jalālayn]** places the gift in Israel's history; **[Ṭabarī]** returns to the audience's duty of remembrance. The purpose, “that you may be guided,” is tied to receiving and following the revelation, not merely possessing it. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:54

*"And when Mūsā said to his people, 'O my people, you have wronged yourselves by taking the calf; so turn to your Maker and slay yourselves — that is better for you with your Maker.' Then He turned to you; He is the Ever-Turning, the Merciful."*

**Meaning.** The prescribed repentance for the worst thing the Children of Israel ever did: the innocent among them were to execute the guilty, and it was to be better for them with their Creator than the crime they had committed **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Ṭabarī]** explains *ẓulm* here as exact: the wrong they did was to themselves, since every doer of a deed that earns God's punishment has brought the punishment on his own soul — and the deed was their relapse into a calf-lord after Mūsā had left them. He defines *tawbah* as it has been defined before: the return from what God hates to what He approves.

**Language.** *Al-qawm* is the company of men, women not included — the Qurʾān pairs it against *nisāʾ* in 49:11, and Zuheir asks whether the *qawm* of Ḥiṣn's family are men or women; yet it may cover men and women together, since every prophet is sent to both **[Qurṭubī]**. *Yā qawmi* elides the *yāʾ* and the *kasrah* stands in its place; in other than the Qurʾān it may be restored, and the vocative may also take a *hāʾ* or an *alif* **[Qurṭubī]**. *Bāriʾ* — the Originator, who fashions by bringing into being — differs from *khāliq*, the One who measures out and moves a thing from state to state; both are from *barʾ*, the separation of a thing from a thing: the creation was cut out of non-existence into existence, and a man *baraʾa* from illness when he is cut free from it. *Barāʾah* is the dissolution of a covenant; *mubāraʾah* is the wife's separation by mutual release **[Qurṭubī]**. Abū ʿAmr read *bāriʾkum* with the *hamzah* made still in the connection; the old grammarians permitted it in poetry, al-Mubarrad called it an error, and al-Naḥḥās defended it with verses like Imruʾ al-Qays's *ashrab ghaira mustaḥqib*. The ending names God as *al-Tawwāb*, the One who turns to His servants again and again, and *al-Raḥīm* **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Stories & āthār.** Mūsā ordered them on God's command: those who had not worshipped the calf were to stand holding knives and put to death those who had **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān: they took to the daggers and began stabbing one another **[Ṭabarī]**. Saʿīd b. Jubayr and Mujāhid: they rose against one another with knives, not a man pitying either close or distant kin, until Mūsā waved his garment and they threw down what was in their hands — and the toll was seventy thousand; and God revealed to Mūsā, "It is enough — I am satisfied" **[Ṭabarī]**. Ibn ʿAbbās: those who had clung to the calf sat down and those who had not stood with the daggers, and a violent darkness fell over them as they killed one another; when the darkness lifted, seventy thousand lay dead — and every one of the slain had a repentance, and every one of the survivors had a repentance **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. al-Zuhrī's account: they went out with Mūsā and struck at one another with swords and thrust with daggers while Mūsā raised his hands in prayer, until when his hands grew tired, some of them came and propped his arms up; when God accepted their repentance He held their hands back from one another and they dropped their weapons; and Mūsā and the Israelites grieved at the dead, until God revealed to Mūsā: "Why do you grieve? As for those of you who were killed — a living one with Me, provided for; and as for those who remain — their repentance is accepted" **[Ṭabarī]**. al-Suddī: they formed two ranks and smote each other with swords until Mūsā and Hārūn cried, "O our Lord, the Children of Israel are perishing! Spare the remnant!" — and God commanded them to lay down arms and accepted their repentance: the killed were martyrs and the survivors had their sin atoned **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Ibn Kathīr]** adds that al-Ḥasan said the remorse began when 7:149 was fulfilled on them — "when they bit their hands in regret and saw that they had gone astray, they said, 'If our Lord does not have mercy on us and forgive us, we shall surely be among the losers.'"

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** records the reports — some said the seventy who had gone up the mountain with Mūsā executed the worshippers; al-Naḥḥās reports that Yūshaʿ b. Nūn came out against them as they sat and said, "Cursed be he who loosens his wrap, or stretches out a hand or foot to defend himself or to ward off his killer" — and not one loosened it until those who were killed had been killed. And **[Qurṭubī]** draws the reason why those who had *not* worshipped were also punished on the first view: they had not changed the wrong when it happened; they withdrew instead of fighting it, while what was due of them was to fight those who worshipped. This, he says, is God's way among His servants: when a wrong spreads and is not changed, the whole people is punished — with the ḥadīth of Jarīr from Ibn Mājah: "There is no people among whom acts of disobedience are committed while there is one stronger and mightier than they who does not change it, except that God envelopes them all in punishment." And **[Maʿārif]** notes the legal principle the event illustrates: certain major sins entail a capital penalty even after repentance — as blood for blood in intentional homicide, and stoning in adultery established by proof. On the other side, **[Qurṭubī]** rejects the inward reading he quotes from the people of spiritual reflections (the *arbāb al-khawāṭir*), that "kill yourselves" means "humble your souls with obedience and hold them back from appetites"; the sound meaning is the literal one, as the Qurʾān itself speaks of what they did. **[Study Quran]** sets out both: with Exodus 32:27 as a parallel, the best reading is that the guilty were executed by their fellows — the severity making the punishment more terrible — while some commentators hold that all were forgiven, the killed counted as martyrs, so that the episode became a collective expiation rather than an execution; and it notes that some read "slay yourselves" as the inward slaying of the ego in opposing the passions.

**Belief & reflection.** Sufyān b. ʿUyaynah says: repentance is a blessing God gave this community and not the communities before it — the repentance of the Children of Israel was killing **[Qurṭubī]**. That observation is the verse's real edge for the reader. The Children of Israel committed one sin and it cost seventy thousand of them their lives; the Muslim says the words of *tawbah* and walks away clean. The mercy is real, and it should be understood as mercy — which is why the verse ends not on the slaughter but on *al-Tawwāb al-Raḥīm*: the fire came down on a single generation, and the door it closed on them stands open for everyone after.

This command describes a specific repentance demanded of Israel after idolatry. **[Ṭabarī]** defines their sin as wronging themselves by earning punishment, and repentance as returning from what God dislikes to the obedience He accepts. **[Maʿārif]** says the command marked the form of repentance prescribed for that episode: those who had not worshipped the calf executed those who had. **[Saʿdī]** stresses that they complied and that God then pardoned them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** transmits accounts of the people taking weapons and of Mūsā intervening when enough had been done; **[Qurṭubī]** gathers the reports and their legal reflections. The episode is narrated as a particular command in Israel's history, not a general invitation to violence. The closing names place mercy after repentance: **[Ṭabarī]** reads “He turned toward you” as accepting their return; **[Saʿdī]** reads it as pardon and compassion; **[Maʿārif]** marks this as a special mode for that community and setting. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** In narrating the terrible cost of idolatry, the text moves immediately to the possibility of return, not despair. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:55

*"And when you said, 'O Mūsā, we will not believe you until we see God openly' — and the thunderbolt seized you as you looked on."*

**Meaning.** Another of the remembered scenes: a demand made not as a question but as a condition of faith — belief withheld until God be seen with the eye — and the answer given: a blast that killed them **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports Ibn Jurayj's framing: this was among the favours shown them, because they were raised after being struck — and the sin was that they asked to see God directly, which neither they nor anyone else can bear. **[Jalālayn]** fixes the occasion: they had gone out with Mūsā to apologise before God for the calf, had heard what God said to them, and then said this.

**Context — whose words were they?** **[Qurṭubī]** gives the report that they were the seventy whom Mūsā chose: when God had made them hear His speech, they said afterwards, "We will not believe you" — though belief in the prophets is obligatory once their miracles appear; so God sent down fire from the heaven which burned them, and Mūsā called on his Lord and they were raised, as 2:56 states. Ibn Fūrak adds a possibility: their punishment was for trying to reach the vision by a way that was not theirs to take — "Show us God openly" (4:153) — which was not within Mūsā's power. **[Maʿārif]** tells it in sequence: Mūsā brought the Torah from Sinai and presented it to them as the book of Allah; some of them said insolently that they would not believe until Allah Himself told them in so many words; with God's permission Mūsā answered that the condition could be met if they came to the mountain; they chose seventy men; there they heard the words of Allah with their own ears — and then they invented a new trick: hearing is not enough, they said, for they could not be sure it was Allah who spoke or someone else; they would be convinced only by seeing God with their own eyes. **[Study Quran]** notes the disagreement about identity: Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī identify the event with the one in 7:155, where Mūsā went up the mountain with seventy of the choicest Israelites from among those who had worshipped the calf, they asked for the vision, the thunderbolt struck them dead, and Mūsā prayed and God revived them; al-Jalālayn treat 2:55 and 7:155 as separate incidents; others take no firm position, since the two words used — *ṣāʿiqah* and *rajfah* — cover overlapping ranges and might refer to the same event.

**Language.** *Jahratan* means openly, unveiled — **[Ṭabarī]** derives it from the well whose water is covered by mud until it is cleared and shines: they asked to see God without a screen, with no veil between them and Him, looking at Him with their eyes. Ibn ʿAbbās glossed it "publicly", al-Rabīʿ "with the eye", Ibn Zayd "until He comes out to us", Qatāda "plainly". The root *jahr* is showing forth: *jahr* in recitation is to make it visible, *mujāharah* with sins is to display them; "I saw the commander *jihāran*" is without anything covering him **[Qurṭubī]**. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have read *jaharat an* with the *hāʾ* opened, like *zahrah* and *zaharah*. And **[Qurṭubī]** records the grammatical split: *jahratan* may characterise their utterance — with a transposition, "when you said openly, O Mūsā" — or characterise what they asked to see, keeping the sentence in its order and adding "openly" to distinguish seeing with the eye from seeing in a dream. *Al-ṣāʿiqah* is broader than a bolt: **[Ṭabarī]** defines it as every overwhelming matter a man sees, witnesses or is struck by, until from its terror he is brought to death, or to a loss of reason — a sound, a fire, a convulsion, a tremor. Qatāda: they died. al-Rabīʿ: they heard a sound and swooned — that is, they died. al-Suddī: the *ṣāʿiqah* is fire. Ibn Isḥāq: the tremor took them, and it was the *ṣāʿiqah*, and they all died. And that it can mean swooning rather than dying is shown by 7:143, "Mūsā fell down *ṣaʿiqan*" — unconscious; hence the reading attributed to ʿUmar, ʿUthmān and ʿAlī in this verse: *al-ṣaʿqah* — the swoon **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Rulings — the vision of God.** **[Qurṭubī]** turns the verse into a doctrinal chapter: the Muʿtazilah and most innovators held that God cannot be seen either in this world or the next; Ahl al-Sunnah and the Salaf held that the vision is possible in both and will occur in the Hereafter — so what they asked was not, in itself, an impossibility. What made it a crime was its manner: the demand, the setting of a condition of unbelief, and the attempt at it in this world through an avenue not given. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports the same dispute from another angle — al-Māwardī on whether responsibility for the law continues for those brought back to life after having witnessed such things, one view holding that the compulsion of what they saw removed the burden of choice, the other that obedience remained required, since no responsible adult is free of duties; and **[Qurṭubī]** calls the second the correct one, supporting it with the fact that the Israelites saw the mountain suspended over them and the fire surrounding them, which compelled them to believe, and still the obligation stood on them: "and they are like the people of Yūnus — it is impossible that they should not have been under obligation." And **[Study Quran]** records the echo of the word: *ṣāʿiqah* is kin to Mūsā's swoon in 7:143, and to the "swoon" of the heavens and earth at the blast of the trumpet (39:68); it is also the punishment of ʿĀd and Thamūd (41:13), where Thamūd, too, were seized while they looked on.

**Reflection.** Two words document the disease. The first is the *lammā* of condition: "we will *not* believe *until*." Faith is put on hold while God is given a test to pass — and the test is of the sort that the more we understand the more absurd it becomes, since the request is not for more evidence but for a different mode of being: the God of all things made an object of the eyes. The second is "while you looked on" — their punishment happened in front of them, so that the very sense they wanted to rely on was the one made to watch them die. And **[Ṭabarī]** makes the wider point that the verse is doing for the Prophet's contemporaries what it did for the first readers: this is the same people whose ancestors asked for an idol, took a calf, set conditions on faith, refused the order to fight, and, when told to say *ḥiṭṭah*, answered "wheat in barley" and entered by their backsides — a long ledger of stubbornness, laid out so that a later generation might recognise the family likeness in itself.

“Until we see God plainly” records a demand for direct sight as their condition for belief. **[Ṭabarī]** explains *jahratan* as seeing with the eyes after a covering is removed; **[Ibn Kathīr]** transmits Ibn ʿAbbās's explanation, “publicly” and “so that we gaze at God.” **[Qurṭubī]** explores the wording; **[Jalālayn]** links the bolt to their challenge, and **[Saʿdī]** calls the demand audacious toward God and His messenger. **[Maʿārif]** situates the request after the signs they had witnessed, and **[Study Quran]** compares the episode with 4:153 and 7:155. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** Their visible presence when the bolt struck magnifies the lesson: a sign does not ensure humility where a person insists on conditions of his own choosing. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**

## 2:56

*"Then We raised you up after your death, so that you might give thanks."*

**Meaning.** The favour that closes the scene: they died under the punishment and were brought back, and the purpose named is the same purpose that closed the pardon of 2:52 — gratitude **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** states the sequence plainly: God did this so that they would thank Him for the life He restored after the thunderbolt had killed them — a reprieve granted so that they might return to repentance from the enormity of their sin, after the punishment had already come down on them.

**Context.** **[Maʿārif]** explains why the revival was necessary and not simply a display of power: Mūsā feared the accusation. He had taken the seventy men out to a solitary place and they had died there; the Israelites had always mistrusted him, and he feared they would suspect him of having led the men away and had them slaughtered. So he prayed to God to save him from such a vile charge, and God answered and gave them new life. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gives al-Suddī's scene: Mūsā stood weeping and supplicating — "O Lord, what shall I say to the Children of Israel when I return to them after You destroyed the best of them? Had You willed, You could have destroyed them and me before. Would You destroy us for what the fools among us did?" (7:155) — and God revealed to him that the seventy were among those who had worshipped the calf. Then God brought them back to life one man at a time while the rest of the people watched how He was reviving them — which is why the verse says, "then We raised you up after your death, that you might give thanks." **[Qurṭubī]** reports the same through Qatāda — they died and their souls departed, and afterwards they wished to complete the terms of their lives — and through others: it was a death of extinction, so that others might take the lesson, after which they were sent back. And al-Naḥḥās draws the argumentative force of the verse: it stands as proof against the Quraysh who denied the raising of the dead, and against the People of the Book, to whom this event was told and who acknowledge it.

**Language.** *Al-baʿth* at root is the stirring of a thing from its place: a man *baʿatha* his riding camel when he roused it from where it knelt, as the poet describes driving on a she-camel fattened on a year's grazing, firm as a mountain's flank; from the same root a man is *sent* on an errand — set up from where he was to set out on it — and the Day of Resurrection is the Day of *Baʿth*, because it is the day people are stirred out of their graves for the standing of the reckoning **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** records one other reading of the verse: al-Suddī said *baʿathnākum* means "We sent you as prophets" — and he placed the verse out of order, treating "then We raised you up" as preceding the thunderbolt in time. **[Ṭabarī]** rejects it decisively: the apparent wording contradicts it and the exegetes agree that it is wrong, since the verse plainly reports a raising *after* death, and God's speech is not to be turned from its evident sense without proof. **[Qurṭubī]** records a similar figurative gloss — "We raised you" meaning "We taught you after your ignorance" — and calls the literal reading sounder, since the principle is the literal and the death here was a punishment. **[Study Quran]** notes that the primary sense is a resurrection to life, though some read it as the raising up of prophets among the Israelites, since the verb *baʿatha* carries the sense of rousing and is used of sending prophets (as in 2:213) — and from the same root comes *ʿĪd al-mabʿath*, the day many Muslims mark as the Prophet's commissioning.

**Belief & rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** carries over the consequences already raised under the previous verse: those brought back after they had witnessed such overwhelming events were not thereby released from obligation — al-Māwardī's two opinions, and Qurṭubī's verdict that responsibility remained, because no responsible person is free of the duty of worship, and the Israelites had already seen the mountain suspended and the fire surrounding them and were still bound. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports the same dispute and cites ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Zayd's continuation — that after being restored they were told, "Take the Book of God," and refused three times, saying, "We died and came back to life," until God sent angels who made the mountain topple over them — which shows, in that reading, that the commandments were still required of them after their revival.

**Reflection.** The verse names the resurrection as a *favour*, and that is theologically exact: for a people who had just been destroyed for demanding to see God, being brought back at all was pure gift. Also worth keeping in view is how the two great favours of this stretch of the Sūrah keep ending in the same word: pardon ends "that you might give thanks" (2:52), and resurrection ends "that you might give thanks" (2:56). The Qurʾān's picture of the human problem is not mainly that men need information — it is that they receive mercy upon mercy and still do not turn. And the event itself settles an argument that was live among the Prophet's hearers: a people who were actually killed and actually raised again were shown, in their own history, that the raising of the dead is not a metaphor. Their ancestors had a demonstration of the Resurrection standing on its feet in front of them, which is why the verse can use it as an argument against those who denied it **[Qurṭubī]**.

Here *baʿathnā* means that God raised them to life after the death caused by the thunderbolt. **[Ṭabarī]** derives “raise” from stirring an object from its place and applies it to their return to life; **[Ibn Kathīr]** calls it resurrection following the lightning strike. **[Qurṭubī]** records the transmitted reports about their death and revival, while **[Jalālayn]** keeps the plain sequence. **[Saʿdī]** reads the revival as a renewed favour after a grave transgression; **[Maʿārif]** emphasizes the divine mercy rather than any human claim on life. The clause “so that you might give thanks” turns survival into an obligation: **[Ṭabarī]** understands it as a call to thank God for the favour, and **[Saʿdī]** repeatedly connects blessing with obedience. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** relate the event to the account of the demand in 2:55. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** The order—death, revival, thanks—shows that being spared does not settle the past; it opens a chance to respond differently. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:57

*"And We shaded you with the cloud, and sent down on you the manna and the quails: 'Eat of the good things We have provided you.' And they did not wrong Us, but themselves they were wronging."*

**Meaning.** The favours turn outward — shade, food, and a command to eat freely — and the verse ends by placing the wrong on the wrongdoers' own account: God is not injured by disobedience **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Qurṭubī]** fills in what the concision leaves out: before "and they did not wrong Us" stands an implied "so they disobeyed and did not meet the favours with thanks" — and their wronging of themselves was exactly that, answering blessings with acts of disobedience.

**Context — the wilderness.** **[Maʿārif]** sets the scene: the Israelites belonged to Syria and had gone down to Egypt in Yūsuf's time; when Pharaoh drowned and the road home opened, God commanded them to march against the Amalekites who held their land. They set out, but on learning the enemy's strength their courage failed and they refused the fight — at which Mūsā was told of the coming punishment, and for forty years they wandered in the wilderness (al-tīh), a strip of some ten miles between Egypt and Syria, unable to reach either home or Egypt: they would march a whole day towards Egypt, and wake to find themselves exactly where they had started. The wilderness was bare of tree or building, food or clothing; and in answer to Mūsā's prayer God provided everything they lacked. **[Qurṭubī]** makes the same point a lesson in dependence: when they said, "Who will give us food?" God sent down the manna and the quails; "Who will shelter us from the sun's heat?" He shaded them with the cloud; "How shall we have light?" He set a pillar of light in the middle of their camp; "Who will give us water?" Mūsā was commanded to strike the rock; "What of our clothing?" — they were given garments that never wore out, never soiled, never rotted, and the clothes of their children grew with them.

**Language.** *Al-ghamām* is the plural of *ghamāmah*, as *saḥāb* is of *saḥābah*; the root *ghamma* means to cover — anything covered is *maghmūm*, a mind veiled is *maghmūm*, the moon is *ghamma* when cloud hides it, and from the same root comes the Prophet's ﷺ word, "my heart is *yughān* [covered]." al-Suddī: it is the white cloud **[Qurṭubī]**. But Mujāhid said twice that it *was not cloud* — it is the *ghamām* in which God comes on the Day of Resurrection (2:210), which belonged to no one else; and Ibn ʿAbbās: it was a cloud cooler and finer than this one, the very cloud of 2:210, the one in which the angels came at Badr **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** concludes that since *ghamām* is whatever covers the face of the sky, there is no reason to make it only cloud rather than anything else that wraps the sky. As for *al-mann*, the reports differ widely: a gum (*ṣamghah*); a sweet gum, the *tarranjabīn*; honey; a sweet drink that comes like honey and is mixed with water; thin bread like flatbread, per Wahb b. Munabbih; sap falling on the ginger tree, per al-Suddī; the thing that falls from the sky onto the trees and is eaten by people — this last being the majority report from Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Ḍaḥḥāk, al-Shaʿbī and others **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. And Amīyah b. Abī al-Ṣalt described it in verse as honey that drips and water sweeter than fresh **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** records the decisive gloss: *al-mann* is a verbal noun covering everything God grants His servants without toil, seed or watering — which is why the Prophet ﷺ said, "The truffle (*kamʾah*) is of the *mann* that God sent down on the Children of Israel, and its water is a healing for the eye" (Muslim). Abū ʿUbayd explains the analogy: the truffle needs no sowing, no irrigation, no cultivation — it belongs to the same class as the manna in coming without cost. *Al-salwā*: Ibn ʿAṭiyyah said all the exegetes agree it is the bird, the quail; Ibn ʿAbbās said it resembles the quail, and ʿIkrimah that it was a bird of Paradise the size of a sparrow. **[Qurṭubī]** corrects the claim of consensus: al-Mu'arrij and al-Jawharī held that in the dialect of Kinānah *salwā* means honey, citing the Hudhalī's verse, "sweeter than *salwā* when we uncover it" — and the word means whatever brings consolation (*sulwān*) to the grieving. On its grammar: al-Akhfash said it is a collective with no singular from its own form, like *khayr* and *sharr*; al-Khalīl made its singular *salwāh*; al-Kisāʾī made its plural *salāwī* **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** On the manna's manner: it fell from dawn until sunrise, white as snow and sweeter than honey, and a man gathered only what sufficed for the day — what was kept beyond that spoiled, except on Friday, when they gathered for Friday and the Sabbath together, since the Sabbath was a day of worship on which nothing descended **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. And from the Prophet's ﷺ words: "The *ʿajwah* date is from Paradise and in it is a cure for poison, and the truffle is of the *mann* and its water heals the eye" (al-Tirmidhī, from Abū Hurayrah) **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** pursues the truffle ḥadīth into medicine: some physicians held the water cures eye disease by cooling the heat in it, used alone or compounded, and Abū Hurayrah used it undiluted for every kind of eye ailment. On the command "Eat of the good things We have provided you": *ṭayyibāt* here joins the lawful and the delicious, and the verse carries an ellipsis — "and We said, eat" **[Qurṭubī]**. And **[Study Quran]** notes the wider Qurʾānic principle that hangs on the word: God repeatedly rebukes those who forbid the *ṭayyibāt* He has made lawful (4:160; 5:87; 7:32) — and it was precisely the Children of Israel who were most often rebuked for it.

**Belief & reflection.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** takes the occasion of this verse for an observation of his own: the Companions of Muḥammad ﷺ were superior to the companions of the other prophets in firmness, patience and freedom from arrogance. At Tabūk they went out with the Prophet ﷺ through fierce heat and hunger and *asked for no miracle*, though it would have been easy for the Prophet ﷺ by God's leave; when they were hungry they merely asked him to pray for increase in the little food they had — and every pot they owned came back full; and when they needed rain, he prayed and a cloud came, and when they looked around afterwards the rain had fallen only on their camp. He contrasts this with the Children of Israel, who saw signs and miracles beyond counting and still disobeyed. And the verse itself keeps the accounting straight: God gave shade in a land with no shade, food in a land with no food, and the only thing He asked was that they eat with thanks. Their rebellion cost Him nothing — "they did not wrong Us" — which is the reason the last words of the verse fall where they do: all sin is a wound a man gives himself, and the wilderness gave them the clearest possible view of the difference between what God had provided and what they did with it.

Provision here is described as abundant and unearned, but it does not make the people exempt from a test. **[Saʿdī]** glosses manna broadly as good provision received without toil and identifies quails as a small, pleasant bird; the verse's command permits enjoyment and calls for gratitude. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports the cloud's shade and the manna and quails in the wilderness, tying them to divine generosity. **[Ṭabarī]** transmits differing reports about the cloud and what the manna was, while **[Qurṭubī]** catalogs linguistic and transmitted views. **[Maʿārif]** sets the provision in the wider period of wandering. **[Study Quran]** notes the parallel account in 7:160. The closing declaration that they wronged themselves protects the point that sin does not harm God: **[Saʿdī]** says its consequences return to the disobedient, and **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise explains that the loss was theirs, not God's. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Qurṭubī]** The permission to eat therefore stands beside an ethical warning; provision is a gift to receive lawfully and gratefully, not a reason to forget the Giver. **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:58

*"And when We said, 'Enter this town and eat freely from it wherever you wish, and enter the gate prostrating, and say, "Relief" — We shall forgive you your sins, and We shall give more to the doers of good.'"*

**Meaning.** Another of the remembered mercies: a command that was itself a gift — a homeland, food without measure, and a door through which to be forgiven **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Saʿdī]** reads the verse as God's kindness after their disobedience: He ordered them into a town that would be their home and stronghold, with abundant provision, and He set the manner of entry — submission in deed, by bowing through the gate, and submission in word, by asking that their sins be lifted. The reward was two-tiered: forgiveness for what was past, and increase for those who did good.

**Context — which town, and when.** The majority: Bayt al-Maqdis, Jerusalem — Qatāda, al-Suddī, al-Rabīʿ and Abū Muslim al-Iṣfahānī **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Ibn Zayd and Ibn ʿAbbās in one report: Arīḥā, Jericho, near Jerusalem **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Qurṭubī]** adds more names: ʿUmar b. Shabbah said it was a capital and the seat of kings; Ibn Kaysān said Syria; al-Ḍaḥḥāk said al-Ramlah, Jordan, Palestine and Tadmor. **[Ibn Kathīr]** sets the setting: the command came after the forty years of wandering had ended, when God allowed the Children of Israel to conquer the Holy Land with Yūshaʿ b. Nūn, on the eve of a Friday, when the sun was held back from setting until the victory was complete — and the entry was to be a thank-offering, the people returning to their land and out of the wilderness. **[Study Quran]** notes the question of whether the passage concerns Mūsā or Yūshaʿ (Joshua), which bears on the identity of the city, since Mūsā never entered the Holy Land; al-Rāzī mentions the possibility that the town is Egypt itself, since the next verse is temporally subsequent and implies the plague fell before any entry into Jerusalem, and so within Mūsā's lifetime; others, says Ibn Kathīr's report, read it of the entry into Jericho after its conquest. **[Maʿārif]** gives the two views and then a principle for reading them: Shaykh ʿAbd al-Qādir held the command belonged to the episode of 2:61, when the people tired of manna and quails and asked for the food they were used to; the other view is that it belongs to the city they were ordered to fight for and conquered after the wandering — communicated through Yūshaʿ, the prophet among them at the time. And the apparent clash of chronology should not trouble the reader: the Qurʾān does not tell stories for entertainment but to draw lessons, and its arrangement of episodes follows the meaning a passage is built to convey.

**Language.** *Al-qaryah* is the town — so called because it *taqarrat*, gathered and settled; from the same root, "I *qaraytu* water in the pool" is I collected it, and the pool is a *miqrāh*; the large serving-platters are *maqārī* **[Qurṭubī]**. *Raghadan* is wide and plentiful, describing an implied verbal noun — "a free and ample eating" — or standing as a circumstantial state; the land was blessed and huge in yield, which is why the word is used **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *Sujjadan*: **[Ṭabarī]** gives the root as bowing down in reverence — anything bent in veneration is *sājid*, as Zayd al-Khayl describes hills bowing to hooves — and so Ibn ʿAbbās interpreted the word here as *bowing*, entering by a small gate, since someone bowing is bent though the prostrating bow is deeper. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the alternatives: bowing, or lowly and humble without a fixed posture. The gate is that of the *Ḥiṭṭah* in Bayt al-Maqdis, identified by Mujāhid and others; some said it was the gate of the dome towards which Mūsā and Israel prayed **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *Ḥiṭṭah* is *fiʿlah* from *ḥaṭṭa*: "God *ḥaṭṭa* your sins from you, He *yaḥuṭṭuhā ḥiṭṭatan*" — to set down and remove. The interpretations of what they were told to say: al-Ḥasan and Qatāda — "set down our sins from us"; Ibn Zayd and Ibn Jurayj and Ibn ʿAbbās — "God will set down by it your sin and your offence"; ʿIkrimah — "say *Lā ilāha illā Allāh*"; Ibn ʿAbbās in another report — "you are commanded to seek forgiveness"; al-Ḍaḥḥāk, from Ibn ʿAbbās — "say: this matter is true as it was said to you" **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** prefers the first cluster, on the reading that the *ḥiṭṭah* was the asking itself: "our entry bowing is a relief for our sins" — and he notes that if ʿIkrimah's gloss were right, the reading would have to be *ḥiṭṭatan* in the accusative, since *qūlū* would be taking an object, whereas every reciter agrees on the nominative *ḥiṭṭah* — a clear sign against that view. **[Qurṭubī]** records both the nominative (an implied subject: "our request is *ḥiṭṭah*", or quoted speech) and the accusative reading, and al-Naḥḥās's argument that the ḥadīth "say *Lā ilāha illā Allāh*" is a gloss explaining the sense, not the prescribed words.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Ibn Kathīr]** both record the ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah, in Muslim and al-Bukhārī: the Children of Israel were told to enter the gate bowing and to say *ḥiṭṭah* — but they entered on their backsides, dragging themselves, and said, "a grain in a hair." And **[Study Quran]**'s al-Zamakhsharī gives the grammatical shape of the prescribed word: "unburden us an unburdening" — a structure common in Arabic — meaning, "Say, 'Unburden us.'" The other versions of what they actually said: *ḥinṭah fī shaʿīrah*, "wheat in barley"; and a report that they said *hiṭāsamehāthā*, a Hebrew phrase meaning "red wheat" **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** draws from the incident a rule with wide reach: the change of an enjoined wording is the subject of the verse, and scholars have used it to discuss narration of ḥadīth by meaning. If a worship was prescribed by its exact wording, changing it is not permitted — God blames those who changed what He commanded them to say; if it was prescribed by its meaning, then narrating the meaning is a separate question. Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Abū Ḥanīfah with their companions held that a scholar who knows the contexts and the individual words may narrate hadith by meaning, on condition of exact correspondence; a large group — Ibn Sīrīn, al-Qāsim b. Muḥammad, Rajāʾ b. Ḥaywah — forbade it, and Mujāhid said, "Take away from the hadith if you wish, but do not add to it." Mālik was strict about the *tāʾ* and *yāʾ* of the Prophet's ﷺ words. And **[Qurṭubī]**'s verdict: the stricter course is the safer in religion and the more pious, though the majority allowed narration by meaning since the Companions reported the same incidents in different words, showing they attended to the meanings — but the safer path, he says, is to hold the words.

**Belief & reflection.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** draws the parallel the episode was clearly placed here to teach: when God's help comes and people enter His religion in crowds, the response is *tasbīḥ*, praise, and *istighfār* (110:1–3) — victory is met with humility, not swagger. The Children of Israel stood at the door of a land promised for forty years, at the end of a long punishment, and were asked for two small things: to walk in bent, and to say one word about mercy. They did neither with honesty — and the deeper point of the verse, for every community after them, is that the entry God asks for is not into a city: it is the lowering of the head on the way to what He has given.

The command to enter is paired with humility and a request for forgiveness. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** transmit accounts of the gate, the posture, and the wording; **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites reports about entering it humbly. **[Jalālayn]** reads *ḥiṭṭah* as a supplication for exoneration, while **[Saʿdī]** underlines obedience in both word and deed. **[Maʿārif]** distinguishes the town's identity as a matter of transmitted report, not the verse's central instruction. **[Study Quran]** notes parallels with 7:161. The promise of forgiveness is tied to good-doers, not to arrival in the town alone. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**

## 2:59

*"Then those who did wrong substituted a word other than the one that had been said to them; so We sent down upon those who did wrong a scourge from heaven, for the iniquity they were committing."*

**Meaning.** The command of 2:58 was met with mockery: they changed the word they had been told to say, and mocked the way they had been told to enter, so the punishment came from heaven for their transgression **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Saʿdī]** notes the precision of the wording: God says *those who did wrong* substituted, not "they substituted" — because not all of them did it, and the punishment falls on the doers. And he draws the escalation: if they substituted so light a thing as a *word*, how much more ready were they to substitute the *deed* — which is why they entered dragging themselves on their backsides; and since this insolence was the greatest cause of God's punishment falling on them, the verse names the reason: *for the iniquity they were committing*.

**Context & āthār.** What they actually said is preserved in the reports. The ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah, in Muslim, al-Bukhārī and elsewhere: the Children of Israel were told, "Enter the gate bowing and say *ḥiṭṭah*, and your sins will be forgiven" — and they substituted: they entered dragging themselves on their backsides, saying, "a grain in a hair" **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Ibn ʿAbbās, in several chains: they were commanded to enter bowing, by a small gate, and to say *ḥiṭṭah* — and they entered from their backsides, heads raised, mocking, saying "wheat" — and some said, "red wheat with a black barley in it." Ibn Masʿūd, in a report: they said a Hebrew-sounding phrase, "grain of red wheat pierced with black barley." Mujāhid: Mūsā ordered them to enter bowing and to say *ḥiṭṭah*; the gate was made low for them so that they would lower their heads, and they did not prostrate but entered on their backs, and said "wheat"; and in another report, they entered on their backsides toward the mountain — the mountain upon which God had revealed Himself to Mūsā **[Ṭabarī]**. al-Rabīʿ: the prostration of one of them was on his cheek. And Ibn Zayd gives the tone of it: they mocked Mūsā and said, "What does Mūsā want but to play with us? *Ḥiṭṭah, ḥiṭṭah!* What is *ḥiṭṭah*?" and some said to others, "Wheat" **[Ṭabarī]**.

**Language.** *Badala* is to change: "I changed it" means I altered it without removing its very self, while *abdala* means to remove its self and put another in its place — hence "perhaps our Lord will *replace* for us" (68:32) can be read on either pattern **[Qurṭubī]**. *Al-rijz*, in the language of the Arabs, is the punishment — different from *al-rujz* with a ḍammah, which is an idol they used to worship ("and the *rujz*, shun it", 74:5); and it differs from *al-rijz* meaning filth, though al-Farrāʾ and Abū ʿUbayd held that *rijs* and *rijz* are one, like *sudgh* and *zudgh* in the exchange of letters. *Al-rajz* with the *rāʾ* and the *jīm* opened is a metre of poetry — which al-Khalīl refused to call poetry at all — derived from *rajaz*, a disease that strikes camels in the hindquarters, so that when they rise their thighs tremble **[Qurṭubī]**. And *mā kānū yafsuqūn*: *fisq* is going out — and what they went out of was obedience; Ibn Waththāb and al-Nakhaʿī read it with the *sīn* opened **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** explains God's repetition of "those who did wrong" in place of a pronoun: it is a deliberate returning to the name, to magnify the offence.

**Ḥadīth — what the punishment was.** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**: al-Ḍaḥḥāk from Ibn ʿAbbās — every *rijz* in the Book of God is a punishment; Mujāhid, Abū Mālik and others — torment; and it was the plague, which killed seventy thousand of them in a short time. And the Prophet ﷺ used the same word of an epidemic among his own people: from Usāmah b. Zayd, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said — "This ailment, or sickness, is a *rijz* with which some nations before you were punished" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim in meaning, with ʿĀʾishah's report also in the two Ṣaḥīḥs: "If you hear of the plague in a land, do not enter it"); from Saʿd b. Mālik, Usāmah b. Zayd and Khuzaymah b. Thābit, that the Prophet ﷺ said, "The plague is a *rijz*, a punishment with which those before you were punished" **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

**Rulings — changing what God has said.** **[Qurṭubī]** draws the doctrine from the verse in its starkest form. They had been told to say *ḥiṭṭah*, a word expressing repentance, and they said *ḥinṭah*, adding a single letter — and that one letter brought down on them what it brought. So consider what changing something belonging to the attributes of the One worshipped must mean; and the whole blame here falls on speech, which is lighter than deed — so what of changing and altering in *acts*? The lesson he states in a phrase: this shows that adding to religion and innovating in the law is of grave consequence and severe harm — the incident is God's own demonstration against *bidʿah*, before any jurist wrote about it. **[Maʿārif]** gives the juristic settlement: by the consensus of jurists, changing the words of the Qurʾān, of a ḥadīth, or of a divine command in a way that invents or distorts the meaning is impermissible. As for changing words where the meaning is preserved: al-Qurṭubī reports that Abū Ḥanīfah, Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī held it permissible to narrate a ḥadīth by meaning, on condition that the narrator knows the language fully and understands the hadith's context; while Ibn Sīrīn, al-Qāsim b. Muḥammad and other masters of hadith forbade it entirely, insisting on the exact wording. And on prescribed worship, the words are as much intended as the meaning: the wording of the *adhān*, of the prayers and their formulas, may not be replaced even if the sense is retained — for the merit of reciting the Qurʾān attaches to the revealed words themselves, ten rewards for each letter, and no translation or paraphrase, however accurate, counts as recitation; the Qurʾān is the meanings *in* those words, and the two cannot be separated.

**Belief & reflection.** Three words, one letter, and seventy thousand dead — the arithmetic of the story is meant to be shocking, and it is the shock that teaches. The Children of Israel did not renounce the command; they entered the gate, and they said a word. What they did was to convert an act of worship into an act of mockery with a pun, and the difference between *ḥiṭṭah* and *ḥinṭah* is one dot of ink. **[Qurṭubī]**'s inference from that fact is the verse's enduring force against a religion of private improvisation: if a word as small as that was not the worshipper's to change, then neither are the acts of worship his to remodel. And **[Saʿdī]**'s note completes it — mockery of a light command is the seed of mockery of the heavy one; a people who joke at the door will curl their way through it, and after the curling comes the message from heaven, saying nothing new, only the thing they had already been told.

The next verse makes the consequence proportional to deliberate distortion. **[Ṭabarī]** glosses *fabbadala* as replacing the command with its opposite; reports describe them entering while crawling and saying “a grain in a hair” instead of seeking forgiveness. **[Ibn Kathīr]** preserves the prophetic report describing plague as a *rijz*, a punishment that afflicted earlier peoples. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the word, the reports, and the prohibition on altering revealed commands; **[Maʿārif]** highlights that changing the word to mock the instruction incurred punishment. **[Study Quran]** notes the account's parallels with 7:161–162. **[Jalālayn]** offers the concise identification of their transgression; **[Saʿdī]** relates their contempt for an instruction to the severity of the response. **[Ṭabarī]** distinguishes the narrative detail from the verse's clear assertion: the wrongdoers changed what they had been told. **[Qurṭubī]** relates the reports about plague and sin; **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites prophetic reports describing plague as punishment. The episode warns that mockery of a small command can disclose a larger refusal. **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:60

*"And when Mūsā sought water for his people, We said, 'Strike the rock with your staff.' Then twelve springs burst from it; each people knew its drinking place. 'Eat and drink of God's provision, and do not spread corruption in the earth.'"*

**Meaning.** The next provision in the wilderness: water from a stone, divided exactly — and a warning appended to the gift **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reads the whole verse as a favour recalled: God answered Mūsā's supplication when he asked for water for the people, made it come out of a rock, one designated spring for each of their tribes; they ate the manna and quails and drank what God provided without effort or hardship — so worship the One who did this for you. **[Saʿdī]** notes the division's purpose: each people knew the place where it would drink, so that no group pressed on another, and they drank at ease rather than in contention.

**Context & āthār.** The incident belongs to the wilderness: **[Ṭabarī]** reports from Qatāda that they complained to their prophet of thirst, and were commanded to use a stone from the Ṭūr which they carried with them — when they halted, Mūsā struck it and twelve springs gushed out, a known and abundant spring for each tribe. Ibn ʿAbbās's account adds the detail that a square stone was placed among them, three springs on each of its four sides, one for each tribe, and they never moved camp without finding that stone with them at the new station **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**.
