Ibn Zayd: a stone the size of a sheep's head which they put in the side of the provision-bag, struck by Mūsā at each halt until the springs flowed, and when they moved on the springs held back **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** gathers the descriptions: a cube-shaped stone from the Ṭūr, carried in the fold of a pack; or a stone that travelled with them and was found at every stage — which is greater as a sign; or the verse's definite article means a stone Mūsā would know, or any stone he chose, or the particular rock he had placed his garment on when he washed, out of which God vindicated him from what his people had accused him of; ʿAṭāʾ: the stone had four faces, three springs from each, one to a tribe, with none mixing with another. **[Jalālayn]** names the stone as the one that "ran off with his robe," describing it as a light cube like a man's head. **[Saʿdī]** notes the ambiguity in God's word "the rock": it may be one specific known stone or a generic noun — either way the sign stands.

**Language.** *Istasqā* is built on the *sīn* of asking — as in *istaʿlama*, *istakhbara*, *istanṣara*: he asked and sought water for his people. *Saqā* and *asqā* are two dialects with one meaning, as Labīd: "my people gave drink to the sons of Majd, and gave drink to Numayr" **[Qurṭubī]**. *Al-ʿaṣā* is a feminine noun whose *alif* turns back to a *wāw*; its plural is *ʿuṣiyy* and *ʿiṣiyy*, and al-Farrāʾ said the first solecism heard in Iraq was "this is my *ʿaṣātī*." The word also carries the sense of gathering and parting: "they split the staff of the Muslims" is they broke their unity, and "the staff was split" is that dissent occurred; "do not lift your staff from your family" means the discipline of them **[Qurṭubī]**. *Infajarat* is bursting open — from *fajr*, the splitting of the dawn — with an ellipsis in the verse: "so he struck, and it burst." **[Qurṭubī]** observes that God was able to make the water flow and the rock split without a blow, but He willed to bind effects to causes, as wisdom for His servants in how they reach what they intend, and so that reward and punishment in the Hereafter may be ordered upon it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** makes a fine distinction between the two accounts: 7:160 says *fanbajasat* — gushed out — describing the water as it first began, while here the word is *infajarat*, describing it when it broke out in full force; the one passage narrates and the other addresses, which suits al-Aʿrāf being addressed to the Prophet ﷺ about Israel and this Sūrah speaking to Israel directly. *ʿAthā* — "do not spread corruption" — is the extreme of corruption, from *ʿathiya*; Ruʾbah's line "and a lawbreaker ran riot among us, making lawful what was not his" **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**. And **[Study Quran]** notes that "eat and drink" is spoken either by God or by Mūsā, and that the commentators generally take each knowing its drinking place to mean each of the twelve tribes had its own fountain.

**Rulings — the prayer for rain.** **[Qurṭubī]** opens the legal chapter from this verse: *istisqāʾ* belongs to times of drought and withheld rain, and the ruling then is the display of servanthood, poverty and lowliness, together with sincere repentance. The Prophet ﷺ prayed for rain himself — going out to the prayer-ground humble, lowly, still, and imploring. And what of us, says Qurṭubī, with our stubbornness and our contravention of the Lord of servants — how shall we be given drink? He cites the ḥadīth of Ibn ʿUmar, "They did not withhold the *zakāh* of their wealth except that rain from the sky was withheld from them; and were it not for the beasts, they would not be rained upon." The Sunnah of *istisqāʾ*, on the majority view, is to go out to the *muṣallā* in that manner, with the *khuṭbah* and the prayer; Abū Ḥanīfah held that there is no prayer and no going out, and that it is supplication only, adducing the authentic ḥadīth of Anas — and **[Qurṭubī]** answers that this was a supplication answered at once, so he sufficed with it and did not intend to legislate a rite; when he did intend to teach it, he taught it by his act, as ʿAbd Allāh b. Zayd al-Māzinī reported: "the Messenger of God ﷺ went out to the *muṣallā*, asked for rain, turned his cloak, and prayed two *rakʿahs*" (Muslim). **[Maʿārif]** adds the alternative report from al-Bukhārī and Muslim through Anas — the Prophet ﷺ asking for rain during the Friday *khuṭbah* — and concludes that whatever form the prayer takes, the scholars agree it cannot be effective without repentance, the confession of one's powerlessness, sincere humility and the affirmation of servitude; God may of His mercy answer without these, but the servant has no right to expect it while persisting in sin.

**Belief — the sign and its denial.** **[Maʿārif]** brings al-Qāḍī al-Bayḍāwī's argument against those who deny miracles on rational grounds: God gave a certain stone the property of drawing iron to itself; it is therefore not rationally impossible that He should give another stone the property of drawing water from the earth and releasing it — and even that is an explanation for those who look at the surface only, since it is in no way impossible that God should produce water *within* the stone itself. Those who call it impossible have not understood what the word *impossible* means. And **[Qurṭubī]** adds a comparison of signs: what our Prophet ﷺ was given — water flowing from between his fingers — is greater, for the water of the Children of Israel came out of stone, while this came out from between flesh and blood: the report of ʿAbd Allāh, "we were with the Prophet ﷺ and found no water, and a vessel was brought to him, and he put his hand in it — I saw the water bursting out from between his fingers — and he said, 'Come to the purification'" — and Jābir, asked how many they were that day, said fifteen hundred **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Study Quran]** records the verse's spiritual reading by Ibn ʿAjībah: the rock is the heart, from which the water of spiritual life flows; the staff is the spiritual will, which strikes the ego and brings out blessings of wisdom and unveiling — an exegesis with an anchor in the Sūrah itself, where hearts are compared to stones from which rivers gush (2:74).

**Reflection.** The verse gives a people in a desert two words of instruction and one of prohibition, and the prohibition is placed exactly where gratitude was owed: *eat and drink* — *and do not spread corruption*. Provision, in the Qurʾān's accounting, never arrives without a test attached, and the test of abundance is not whether one enjoys it but what one does on the ground while enjoying it. And the stone itself carries the lesson of means: God did not need a stone or a staff; He bound the water to a blow so that His servants would learn that effects come from Him through causes — and so that their reward and their punishment would have something to attach to **[Qurṭubī]**. The same hands that strike the rock in obedience can turn to corruption in the earth; between the two there is only the interval of a grateful or an ungrateful heart.

The twelve springs answer thirst in an orderly way: each group knows its place, so provision does not turn into rivalry. **[Saʿdī]** draws precisely that social benefit from the verse; **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Ṭabarī]** describe one spring for each tribe. **[Qurṭubī]** notes the staff, the rock, and God's choice to connect an extraordinary result with a commanded means. **[Study Quran]** records uncertainty about whether “eat and drink” is spoken by God or by Mūsā. **[Maʿārif]** treats the event as a miracle and rejects the claim that such a sign is impossible. **[Jalālayn]** summarizes the flowing water. The final command not to spread corruption keeps the blessing linked to conduct: **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]**. The narrative calls for gratitude, but no theory of the stone should displace the verse's central points: divine provision, fair access, and a prohibition against harming the earth. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:61

*"And when you said, 'O Mūsā, we will not endure one food; so call upon your Lord for us, that He bring out for us some of what the earth grows — its herbs, its cucumbers, its garlic, its lentils, its onions.' He said, 'Would you exchange what is lower for what is better? Go down to a town — you will have what you asked for.' And humiliation and misery were stamped upon them, and they drew on themselves wrath from God. That was because they used to disbelieve in the signs of God and kill the prophets wrongly. That was because they disobeyed and used to transgress."*

**Meaning.** The complaint that ends the list of wilderness favours, and the sentence that follows it — the verdict on a people who grew weary of what God chose for them **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Saʿdī]** frames the request precisely: they spoke out of boredom with God's blessings and contempt for them — "we will not endure one food," meaning one kind, even though it came in flavours — and when Mūsā put it to them, "would you exchange what is lower for what is better?", he was telling them the exchange was unfitting: the foods they asked for could be had in any town they entered, whereas what God had given them was the finest and most honourable of foods. And because their conduct showed little patience and a low view of God's commands and gifts, God repaid them in kind: humiliation visible on their bodies, misery within their souls — no honour in their self-regard and no ambition that rose — and the only spoil they carried away was God's anger.

**Context.** The setting is the wilderness: they had eaten manna and quails every day, and they remembered the diet of Egypt — **[Qurṭubī]** gives al-Ḥasan's account of the craving: they had been, in Egypt, "a people of leeks and onions and lentils," and they reverted to their bad habit as instinct reverted to what it had always known. **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes the sense of "one food": it was one kind of provision though it came in two forms, because it never changed and was eaten day after day — **[Qurṭubī]** adds that the two things are called one because they ate one with the other, as a man who keeps to fasting and prayer and recitation is said to be "on one thing." And **[Ibn Kathīr]** concurs with the plain logic: their request was born of boredom and arrogance, and fulfilling it was unnecessary, so it was refused.

**Language.** *Lan naṣbira* is from *ṣabr*, the holding back of the soul; "we will not bear" is that they could no longer hold themselves to one food **[Ṭabarī]**. *Al-baql* is every plant with no trunk — the tree being what has a trunk. *Al-qiththāʾ* is the cucumber, known; its *qāf* is also read with a ḍammah, as Yaḥyā b. Waththāb and Ṭalḥah b. Muṣarrif read, and the opening is the more common **[Qurṭubī]**. ***Al-fūm*** is the crux of the verse's vocabulary. The reports split: *garlic* — the reading attributed to Ibn Masʿūd as *thūmihā* with *thāʾ*, and the view of al-Kisāʾī and al-Naḍr b. Shumayl, supported by the interchange of *thāʾ* and *fāʾ* in Arabic (as *maghāthīr* for *maghāfīr*, *jadath* for *jadf*), and by the fact that garlic matches onion in the list; or *wheat and bread* — from ʿAṭāʾ, Mujāhid, Qatāda, al-Ḥasan, Abū Mālik, al-Suddī and Ibn ʿAbbās, who supported it with Uh ayhah b. al-Julāḥ's verse, "I was the wealthiest of men, one who came to Madīnah from the planting of *fūm*" **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** reports al-Naḥḥās's verdict for wheat: its reports are sound whereas Juwaybir, who carried the garlic report, is not a match for them; and al-Zajjāj's objection — how could a people ask for food with no wheat in it, when wheat is the foundation of nourishment? **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes that al-Bukhārī said some held *fūm* to include all grains or seeds that are eaten, and that the old language had the expression *fūmū lanā*, "bake for us." *Adnā* is lower, meaner in worth and rank, from *danāʾah*; *istibdāl* is to leave a thing for another in its place **[Ṭabarī]**. And the *min* in "some of what the earth grows": **[Ṭabarī]** reads it as partitive, answering for the ellipsis of the object — they asked for a *portion* of the earth's produce, not all of it; others said it was redundant, which a group of grammarians denied for all contexts, since it is never brought except for a meaning. **[Qurṭubī]**'s summary: the first *min* is partitive, the second for specification, and *min baqlihā* stands in apposition to "what."

**Rulings — humiliation and its cause.** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**: al-Ḥasan said God humiliated them and they have no protector; He placed them under the feet of the Muslims, who came at a time when even the Magians were taking the *jizyah* from the Jews. Abū al-ʿĀliyyah, al-Rabīʿ and al-Suddī: *al-maskanah* is poverty; ʿAṭiyyah al-ʿAwfī: the payment of the land-tax. **[Ibn Kathīr]** stresses that the humiliation is stated as settled and continuing — they are despised by whoever deals with them, and carry the disgrace inwardly. **[Maʿārif]** explains why the punishment fits the crime: transgression of divine commands and outright denial were habitual with them, and they had slain a number of prophets; through persistent, willful misdemeanour they drew God's anger and disgrace settled on them forever — no respect in the eyes of others, no magnanimity in themselves. He cites Ibn Kathīr's summary: however wealthy they grow, they will always be despised, and whoever gets hold of them will humiliate them and attach to them the marks of servitude; and al-Ḍaḥḥāk's report from Ibn ʿAbbās, that they will always remain under the domination of others, paying taxes and tribute. Then the qualification from 3:112 — "humiliation is stamped on them wherever they are found, unless by a rope from God and a rope from men": the rope from God covers those whom His commandment protects (women, children, the devoted); the rope from men covers a treaty of peace with the Muslims or permission to live under Muslim rule on payment of the *jizyah* — and since the verse says "from men," not "from Muslims," a political arrangement with non-Muslim powers is also possible — that is, a condition of protection, not sovereignty. *(digression)* **[Maʿārif]** then applies this to the modern state of Israel, arguing that it is a client stronghold of Western powers living "by a rope from men," not a sovereign state in the sense the verse denies; we flag this as a modern political reading, and carry only the exegetical point that the exception in 3:112 is about terms of protection, not the removal of the humiliation.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** ties the phrase "kill the prophets wrongly" to the ḥadīth: from ʿAbd Allāh b. Masʿūd, the Messenger of God ﷺ said, "The people most severely tormented on the Day of Resurrection are: a man whom a prophet killed or who killed a prophet; an imam of misguidance; and a mutilator of the dead" (Aḥmad). And he cites the definition of the root sin behind all of it, in the two Ṣaḥīḥs: "Pride is the rejection of the truth and the contempt of people." **[Saʿdī]** explains why "without right" is added, though killing prophets is never with right: it removes any excuse of ignorance — they knew. And he draws the chain of consequence that closes the verse: "that was because they disobeyed and used to transgress" — disobedience drags disobedience; negligence produces a small sin, the small sin grows into the great, and from the great grow innovations and disbelief. Then he states a principle for the whole passage: the address is to the Israel of the Prophet's own time, about the acts of their forebears, and the acts are attributed to them for several reasons — they used to boast of their superiority, so God exposed the record of their ancestors; blessings given to the forebears are blessings on the descendants; and a community bound by one religion is as one body, so their early and their late are as a single generation, the deed of some returning as benefit or harm to all.

**Reflection.** The verse's core is a single exchange, and Mūsā's question is the one every generation is asked in its own currency: would you trade down for what you can see? They had bread from heaven and water from stone, and asked to go back to onions. What God gives is measured by what it does for the soul; what they wanted was measured by what it did for the tongue. And the tragedy of the last third of the verse is that the punishment was not merely hunger or thirst — it was the permanent loss of standing: a people who had been carried through a sea now had no honour in their own eyes, and the reason given is not the salad they asked for but the prophets they had killed. **[Maʿārif]**'s reminder is the warning that lifts the verse out of history: "that was because they disobeyed and used to transgress" — the road from complaining about manna to killing prophets is a road, and it is walked one step at a time.

The distinction between food as gift and food as appetite is central. **[Ibn Kathīr]** describes the manna and quails as pure, beneficial provisions that came without toil. **[Saʿdī]** reads the request as boredom with a blessing rather than a response to deprivation. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** preserve the disagreement over *fūm*—garlic or a grain used for bread—and **[Study Quran]** notes the same lexical range. **[Jalālayn]** understands “a town” broadly, and **[Ibn Kathīr]** prefers any town rather than insisting on Egypt. The later humiliation in the verse is linked to persistent disbelief and transgression, not simply to a preference for vegetables. **[Qurṭubī]** reports differing glosses of *maskanah*, including poverty and imposed tribute, while **[Ṭabarī]** keeps the two stated causes in view: disbelief in God's signs and the wrongful killing of prophets. **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:62

*"Those who believe, and those who are Jews, and the Christians, and the Sabians — whoever believes in God and the Last Day and does righteous work, their wage is with their Lord; no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve."*

**Meaning.** After the record of the Children of Israel's offenses, the verse states the general rule: acceptance with God is not a matter of lineage or label but of faith and righteous work **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Saʿdī]** explains why the verse appears exactly here: when the crimes of the Israelites were recited, it might have settled into a hearer's mind that the blame covered all of them, and when the blame was aimed at the Israelites it might have seemed that the ruling belonged to them alone. So God set down a judgement that covers all communities — the believers of this *ummah*, the Jews, the Christians, the Sabians — so that the truth would be clear and the confusion lifted. And he holds that the verse speaks of these communities *as they were in themselves*, before the mission of Muḥammad ﷺ: whoever among them believed in God and the Last Day and affirmed his messenger has a great reward and security; whoever disbelieved has fear and grief instead.

**Language.** *Hādū* is from *hāda*, "he repented" — the poet's "I am a man *hāʾid* out of love," that is, repentant; and 7:156, "we have *hudnā* to You," means we returned. On this view the name *Yahūd* comes from their own word of return; others take it from Yahūdhā, the eldest son of Yaʿqūb, with the *dhāl* Arabized to *dāl* **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. *Naṣārā* is the plural of *naṣrān* — as *sukārā* is of *sakrān* — with the more current singular *naṣrānī*; the feminine is *naṣrānah*, as the poet describes a she-camel's lowered head "as a *naṣrānah* bows who has not turned ḥanīf"; and the poets also use *anṣār* for them, which supports the derivation from mutual help, while Ibn ʿAbbās, Qatāda and Ibn Jurayj derive it from the village of Nāṣirah where ʿĪsā lived; a third view ties it to "Who are my helpers unto God?" (61:14) **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. *Al-ṣābiʾūn* is the plural of *ṣābiʾ* — in the language, one who leaves his religion for another, like an apostate; the Arabs called anyone who left a religion for another *ṣābiʾ*, and the idolaters called the Prophet's companions "the Sabians" for that reason, and "the stars *ṣabaʾat*" is that they rose **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the linguistic split that follows from the *hamzah*: those who read it with *hamzah* (the majority, except Nāfiʿ) take it from the rising of stars or the sprouting of a boy's tooth; those who read it without take it from *ṣabā*, to incline — so the *ṣābiʾ* is the one who has gone out and inclined from one religion to another. On the grammar of *man āmana*: it stands as an appositive to the preceding nouns, and the plural pronoun "their wage" after a singular *man* is proper, because *man* covers one and two and many: the Arabs may follow it with a singular verb for its wording or a plural for its meaning, as 10:42 has "among them are those who *listen* to you" with the plural, and the poet addresses two men, "come by Salmā — if you two pass by — and say to her, incline to those who *remained*," carrying the verb to the meaning; and **[Qurṭubī]** adds the rule of thumb — that once what follows *man* is carried on its meaning, it may not afterwards revert to its wording, since confusion would enter the speech.

**Context — who the parties are.** "Those who believe" is taken by most to mean the believers in Muḥammad ﷺ; Sufyān al-Thawrī said the reference is to the hypocrites — those who believed outwardly — which is why they are listed alongside the Jews, Christians and Sabians, after which the ruling is stated for whoever among them all believes truly **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Study Quran]** notes al-Zamakhsharī's version of that reading and that it would make this the only place in the Qurʾān where "those who believe" is used ironically of the hypocrites, and al-Rāzī's objection from 4:136, where "O you who believe, believe" shows a believer may be called to further belief. As for the Sabians: Mujāhid said they are neither Jews nor Christians and have no religion; others that they are a sect between the Magians and the Jews, so that neither their slaughter is eaten nor their women married; Abū al-ʿĀliyyah and al-Suddī that they are a sect of the People of the Book who read the Psalms; al-Ḥasan and Qatāda that they worship angels, pray toward the qiblah, read the Psalms and pray five times — and Ziyād b. Abī Sufyān, seeing this, moved to lift the *jizyah* from them, then learned they worshipped angels; Ibn Zayd that they are a people of a religion in the region of Mosul who say *Lā ilāha illā Allāh* and have no work, no book and no prophet; and **[Qurṭubī]** reports the summary of what was settled about their doctrine — that they are monotheists who believe the stars have an influence and are effectual, which is why Abū Saʿīd al-Iṣṭakhrī gave the caliph al-Qādir a fatwa of their disbelief. **[Ibn Kathīr]** prefers Mujāhid's and Wahb b. Munabbih's view: they were neither Jews nor Christians nor Magians nor polytheists, but a people without a fixed religion of their own, and God knows best.

**Rulings & the abrogation question.** Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said that 3:85 — "whoever seeks a religion other than submission, it shall not be accepted of him" — came after this verse; and **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains the sense: God accepts no deed from anyone unless it conforms to the law of Muḥammad ﷺ after he was sent — before that, whoever followed the guidance of his own prophet was on the right path and was saved. **[Qurṭubī]** reports both positions and, with the majority, holds the verse not abrogated: it speaks of those who held to their faith in their own prophet and remained on it. The decisive word for the community after the Prophet ﷺ is in the ḥadīth he cites: "No one of this *ummah* — Jew or Christian — hears of me and then does not believe in what I was sent with, except that he is among the people of the Fire." **[Maʿārif]** states the consequence: after the revelation of the Qurʾān, the last message, perfect obedience to God can only mean accepting Islam and following the final Prophet ﷺ; the verse, in its context, is an invitation to Islam extended to Jews, Christians, Sabians and, in principle, to the followers of any religion — and mentioning the Muslims alongside them shows that God's favour follows obedience itself, not partiality to a party, not that salvation is available without accepting Islam. He also closes the door opened by the verse's brevity: mentioning faith in God and the Last Day alone does not reduce the creed to two articles — the first and the last are named and all between them are entailed, since no one can believe in God and the Last Day without believing in His prophets, His books and His angels, through whom alone that knowledge comes. **[Study Quran]** presents the wide range of readings — al-Qushayrī's "the differences of paths with the oneness of origin do not hinder the beauty of acceptance"; al-Ghazālī's argument in *Fayṣal al-tafriqah* that those who never truly heard of the Prophet, or heard only the opposite of his attributes, are covered by God's mercy; the view that the verse concerns those who acknowledged the Prophet when he came, such as Salmān al-Fārsī and Waraqah b. Nawfal — and its own conclusion: the plain sense is that faith and righteous work, not the label, carry salvation, while 3:85 remains for those who deny after the truth has reached them, and only God knows who has truly been reached.

**Belief & reflection.** The verse's last words are the Qurʾān's summary of salvation: no fear for what lies ahead, no grief over what is past. And its first words are a warning against the oldest religious disease — the belief that a group owns God. The Children of Israel were just told what their ancestors did; the next sentence tells everyone, including them, that the door is open, its price is faith and work, and the register is kept by no community. **[Study Quran]** notes the same promise in 2:38, 2:112, 6:48, 10:62 and 46:13 — the Qurʾān keeps returning to it, and the reason is clear: men prefer a claim of privilege to the discipline of obedience, and this verse removes the claim while leaving the door standing open.

The exegetical tradition preserves several readings of the scope and time of this promise rather than one uncontested formulation. **[Saʿdī]** explains its placement as a correction to the impression that every Israelite is blameworthy and that the promise belongs only to Israel. **[Ibn Kathīr]** states that after Muḥammad's mission, accepted deeds must conform to the guidance brought by him, while before it the followers of their own prophets could be rightly guided. **[Qurṭubī]** reports the argument that 3:85 abrogates this verse and the majority response that a descriptive promise is not a legal command to be abrogated. **[Study Quran]** surveys readings that distinguish the communities before and after the final Prophet, alongside arguments about the verse's plain language. **[Maʿārif]** also treats faith and the Last Day as real commitments, not merely inherited labels. **[Ṭabarī]** transmits the general wording; **[Jalālayn]** keeps the promise attached to belief and righteous action. The commentary should therefore distinguish the verse's universal form from the different explanations of how its conditions apply across prophetic history. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:63

*"And when We took your covenant and raised the Mount above you: 'Take what We have given you with strength, and remember what is in it, that you may be reverent.'"*

**Meaning.** The covenant is recalled with the circumstance that gave it its force: the mountain held over them, and the charge to receive the Book seriously and keep it in mind **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Saʿdī]** notes why the scene is called a heavy, confirmed covenant: it was sworn under threat — the Mount lifted above them — and the command was to take the Torah with effort and patience upon the commands of God, to recite it and learn it, so that they might become people of reverence or be spared God's anger.

**Language.** *Al-mīthāq* is *mifʿāl* from *wathīqah* — a binding, whether by oath, by covenant or by anything else that secures **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *Al-ṭūr* in Arabic speech is the mountain; al-ʿAjjāj's line has a hawk folding its wings close as it drops from the *ṭūr* **[Ṭabarī]**. It is also said to be Syriac for mountain, which is how Mujāhid and Abū al-ʿĀliyyah explained it, while Ibn ʿAbbās in one report said it is the mountain on which God spoke to Mūsā and sent down the Torah, and al-Ḍaḥḥāk from him that the *ṭūr* is only those mountains on which vegetation grows **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Qurṭubī]** adds al-Bakrī's odd opinion that it was named after Ṭūr b. Ismāʿīl. "Take what We have given you *with strength*": Ibn ʿAbbās, Qatāda and al-Suddī — with effort and diligence; others — with intention and sincerity; Mujāhid — strength means acting upon what is in it; another — with much study and repetition **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. And "remember what is in it": ponder it, preserve its commands and warnings, do not forget or waste it **[Qurṭubī]**. In the grammar, the verse carries an ellipsis — "and We said: take" **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Context & āthār.** Ibn Zayd's account of how it came to this: when Mūsā returned from his Lord with the Tablets, he told his people that in them was God's Book, His command and His prohibition — and they said, "Who will take it on your word? By God, not until we see God openly, until God looks out at us and says, 'This is My Book, take it' — why does He not speak to us as He spoke to you?" Then a wrath from God came, and a thunderbolt struck them and they all died; and God brought them back to life, and Mūsā said, "Take the Book of God" — "No." — "What has happened to you?" — "We died and came back to life." — "Take the Book of God" — "No." So God sent His angels and uprooted the mountain above them and it was said, "Do you know what this is?" "Yes, this is the Ṭūr." "Take the Book or We will drop it on you." And so they took it with the covenant — and Ibn Zayd adds the point: had they taken it the first time, they would have taken it without a covenant at all **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the fuller picture: God ordered the angels and they uprooted a mountain of Palestine — a *farsakh* by a *farsakh*, the size of their camp — set it over them like a canopy, brought a sea behind them and fire in front of them, and said: take it, with the covenant that you will not waste it, or the mountain falls on you. They prostrated in repentance and took the Torah under the covenant, and their prostration was on one side, because they were watching the mountain out of fear; and when God showed them mercy, they said: there is no prostration better than one God accepted and by which He had mercy on His servants — and so they passed the practice of prostrating on one side down among themselves. al-Ḥasan: God raised the mountain above their heads so that they would listen **[Ibn Kathīr]**. And Mujāhid: the mountain was lifted above them like a cloud and it was said, "Either you believe or it falls on you" — and they believed **[Ṭabarī]**.

**Belief & rulings.** The scene raises the question of compulsion, and the commentators answer it on different levels. Ibn ʿAṭiyyah: what is sound and nothing besides it is that God created faith in their hearts at the time of that prostration, not that they believed under duress with hearts unsettled **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Maʿārif]** answers the objection in legal terms: the Qurʾān elsewhere forbids compulsion in religion, but here no one is being forced to change his religion — they had already accepted Mūsā as a prophet and had willingly covenanted to act on whatever Book God gave them; they were now rebels against an acknowledged law, and a rebel is dealt with as a state deals with rebels: submit or face the penalty. That, he says, is precisely why the Islamic law prescribes capital punishment for the apostate and not for the one who was never a believer — the covenant, not the belief, is what was broken.

**Rulings — the Book and its purpose.** **[Qurṭubī]** turns the phrase "remember what is in it" into a chapter of its own. The purpose of the revealed Books, he says, is to be acted upon, not to be recited with the tongue in place of obedience — to recite and neglect is to throw the Book away, as al-Shaʿbī and Sufyān b. ʿUyaynah said of those who "cast off" the Book (2:101). He cites the ḥadīth of Abū Dardāʾ in al-Tirmidhī, warning that knowledge would be snatched from people, and when Ziyād b. Labīd protested that they read the Qurʾān and taught it to their women and children, the Prophet ﷺ said: "Woe to you, Ziyād! These are the Torah and the Gospel with the Jews and the Christians — what do they avail them?" And he cites Ibn Masʿūd's word in the *Muwaṭṭaʾ*: you are in a time whose jurists are many and reciters few, when the limits of the Qurʾān are preserved and its letters are lost — a time will come when its jurists are few and its reciters many, when the letters are preserved and its limits are lost. The letter without the limit is the exact form of the sin this verse forbids. **[Study Quran]** notes the exegetes' general agreement that the mountain was physically uprooted and held over them to frighten them, while allowing that the phrasing may mean it loomed above them terrifyingly — the way a mountain looms — which fits Exodus 19, where Sinai is wrapped in smoke and shakes violently and the people are warned not to touch its edge; and it reads "take hold with strength" as taking it earnestly, obediently, with resolve to act.

**Reflection.** The verse is one of the clearest pictures in the Qurʾān of a Book received without the desire to receive it: the mountain held overhead, the people prostrate on one side so they could keep an eye on it, the words taken because the alternative was being crushed. **[Ṭabarī]**'s report carries the whole tragedy in a sentence God added through the narrative: had they taken it the first time, they would have taken it without a covenant. Every generation that receives revelation faces the same choice at a smaller scale — the Book with a heart at ease, or the Book with a mountain over it. And **[Qurṭubī]**'s warning about letters preserved and limits lost is this verse arriving at our own doorstep: it is possible to hold the text exactly and keep none of it.

“Take it with strength” is explained as serious commitment, not merely physical force. **[Saʿdī]** expands it as effort, patience, recitation, learning, and acting on God's commands; **[Ibn Kathīr]** similarly cites explanations that the Torah was to be held firmly and implemented. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** discuss the covenant and the mountain in the transmitted accounts, while **[Study Quran]** notes that interpreters differ on whether the mountain physically hung over the people or loomed above them in a terrifying scene. **[Maʿārif]** distinguishes this pledge from converting someone to a religion against conscience: the people were being held to an obligation they had already undertaken. **[Jalālayn]** reads remembrance as acting according to what the Torah contains. **[Qurṭubī]** warns that recitation without obedience fails the purpose of revelation; **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]** The verse thus joins reverence for scripture to the daily work of learning and living by it. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:64

*"Then you turned away after that; and were it not for the favour of God upon you and His mercy, you would have been among the losers."*

**Meaning.** The verb is the verdict on the whole preceding scene: after the covenant, after the mountain, they turned their backs **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Saʿdī]** keeps the contrast sharp: after so emphatic a confirmation they turned away and refused, and that deserved the gravest of punishments — but for God's favour on them and His mercy they would have been of the losers; the turning away was real, and so was the reprieve. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reads the exception as covering both forgiveness and the sending of prophets and messengers, and "the losers" as losing in this world and the next through the breach.

**Language.** *Tawallā* is *tafaʿʿala* from *wallā*, whose base sense is turning the back on a thing with the body — a man "turned his back on him" and left him behind his back — then used, by extension and metaphor, for turning away from commands, religions and beliefs **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** illustrates the extension with Abū Khirāsh's lament: "It is not as the covenant of the abode, Umm Mālik — but chains have encircled the necks"; that is, Islam, in preventing what they used to do in the age of ignorance, became like chains on their necks. And "after that" — after the proof: the taking of the covenant and the raising of the mountain **[Qurṭubī]**. In the grammar of *lawlā faḍlu Allāh*, Sibawayh holds *faḍl* is raised as the subject of an implied predicate that may not be expressed, the Arabs having dispensed with it **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Belief — two kinds of mercy.** **[Maʿārif]** draws the theological distinction the verse implies. God's mercy is of two kinds: a general mercy that reaches believer and disbeliever alike and shows itself as worldly well-being and prosperity, and a special mercy belonging to believers alone, appearing in the Hereafter as salvation and nearness to God; and he notes that the last phrase is addressed to the Jews who were the Prophet's contemporaries, since faith in him was itself part of the covenant, so that they too stand among those who broke it — the verse asks them to recognize that it is by God's mercy alone that the catastrophic punishments which used to descend on earlier traitors have not come down on them in this world. Some commentators, he says, on the strength of authentic hadiths that the sending of the Prophet ﷺ holds back such general destruction, identified this particular favour with the sending of Muḥammad ﷺ itself. **[Ṭabarī]** similarly reads *faḍl* as the favour of repentance granted after they broke the covenant, and mercy as what carried them through to obedience; and he states the principle for the whole passage: the address is to the People of the Book living among the Prophet's emigrants, but the report is about their forebears, put in the form of address to them — as one Arab tribe boasts or reproaches another with the deeds of its ancestors and attributes past deeds to the present.

**Reflection.** Two verses, and a people's whole history in miniature: a covenant taken under a lifted mountain, then backs turned. What makes the close of the verse so heavy is that the mercy is named almost reluctantly — *were it not for* — as though the reader is meant to feel the weight that would have fallen if it had not been there. **[Maʿārif]**'s distinction between mercies is worth keeping for everything that follows in the Sūrah: the general mercy that lets a rebellious people eat and trade and live on is not approval; it is time. The special mercy is another thing entirely, and it is asked for, not presumed.

“After that” makes the turning away more striking: it follows a covenant ratified under the sign of the Mount. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reads it as a breach of the pledge after a firm undertaking; **[Saʿdī]** says the refusal deserved severe punishment. Yet the verse does not end with that deserved outcome. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that God's favour and mercy kept them from being among the losers, and **[Ibn Kathīr]** identifies that mercy with forgiveness and the continued sending of prophets and messengers. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** likewise read the exception as a real saving favour, not as a denial that the covenant had been broken. The order matters: wrongdoing remains wrongdoing, while pardon remains God's gift. **[Saʿdī]** does not treat mercy as permission to disregard the Book; rather, the reprieve makes return possible. **[Jalālayn]** gives the concise sense of divine bounty and mercy, and **[Ṭabarī]** relates the verse to the prior command to remember and obey the covenant. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** The statement also keeps the account from becoming a simple tale of condemnation: a people may turn away and still be called back through revelation and forgiveness. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The conditional “were it not for” preserves the hierarchy between obligation and mercy. **[Ibn Kathīr]** says the saving favour included forgiveness and the renewed sending of prophets; **[Saʿdī]** states that turning away deserved punishment, which makes their rescue a gift rather than an entitlement. **[Ṭabarī]** keeps the broken pledge in view; **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Maʿārif]** likewise treat grace as decisive. The passage therefore offers no license to treat a covenant as a shield for neglect. **[Jalālayn]** reads the clause as bounty and mercy; **[Saʿdī]** makes the proper response gratitude and obedience. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** Later generations who hear the account inherit its admonition, not an automatic claim to safety. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:65

*"And you have indeed known those among you who transgressed in the matter of the Sabbath, and We said to them: 'Be apes, despised.'"*

**Meaning.** From the broken covenant the Sūrah moves to a case that proves what breaking it looks like in practice — a town that turned a day of rest into a trap **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Jalālayn]** identifies them as the inhabitants of Aylah, who fished on the Sabbath when it had been forbidden them, and were told to become apes, rejected — and became so, dying three days later. **[Saʿdī]** points the hearer back to the fuller account in 7:163, where the town by the sea is asked about, the fish coming to them openly on their Sabbath and not coming on the day they did not observe it, as a trial for their rebellion: that great sin required God's anger, and He made them *apes, despised* — abject and humiliated.

**Context.** The story is Sūrah al-Aʿrāf's in detail and this verse's in summary. **[Ibn Kathīr]** describes the transgression: they used deceitful means to avoid honouring the Sabbath — setting nets, ropes and artificial pools before the Sabbath, so that when the fish came in abundance on Saturday as usual they were caught and held through the day, and then collected after the Sabbath ended; their deeds looked lawful on the surface but were in reality wicked, which is why the punishment fitted the crime — they were changed into the animals closest to human form. **[Maʿārif]** supplies the social detail from al-Qurṭubī: the Israelites had first invented clever pretences for catching fish on the Sabbath and gradually began doing it openly; a division grew between the transgressors and those scholars and pious men who tried to dissuade them; when the sinners paid no heed, the pious broke away and lived in a separate part of the town. One day no sound came from the other quarter; they went to look, and found every transgressor changed into an ape — Qatāda adding that the young had become apes and the old swine; the apes recognized their relatives, approached them weeping out of remorse and seeking their help, and then all died after three days.

**Stories & occasions — what they actually did.** **[Maʿārif]** spells out the ruse: on Saturday they would tie one end of a cord to a fish's tail and the other to something on the shore, leaving the fish in the water, and on Sunday they would take it out and eat it. So the offence was not an open breach of the letter but the use of tricks that necessarily cancelled the command — and, worse, mocked it. That, he says, is why they were counted disobedient and rebellious and visited with so dire a punishment.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** records from al-Nasāʾī (and al-Tirmidhī, who graded it *ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ*) the report of Ṣafwān b. ʿAssāl: a Jew said to his companion, "Let us go to this prophet" — "Do not say *prophet*, lest he hear you; he has four eyes." They came and asked him about nine clear signs, and he listed them: do not associate anything with God; do not steal; do not commit adultery; do not kill the life God has forbidden except by right; do not walk with a slanderer to a ruler; do not practise sorcery; do not consume usury; do not slander a chaste woman; do not turn your back on the day of battle — "and upon you especially, O Jews, that you do not transgress the Sabbath." They kissed his hands and his feet and said, "We bear witness that you are a prophet." He asked what kept them from following him; they said that Dāwūd had prayed that prophethood never leave his descendants, and they feared that if they followed him the Jews would kill them. And the reports agree that the punishment distinguished among them: those who prohibited the sin openly and withdrew from it were saved, while the rest perished — **[Qurṭubī]** follows al-Aʿrāf's account in holding that the town divided into three parties, the transgressors, those who forbade them, and those who kept silent, and that this is sounder than the view that there were only two.

**Belief — did the transformed leave offspring?** The scholars differed. al-Zajjāj and al-Qāḍī Abū Bakr b. al-ʿArabī held it possible that the apes and swine alive now descend from them; the majority held that a metamorphosed people does not breed — apes and swine existed before this event, and those God transformed perished, no transformed creature ever living beyond three days, neither eating nor drinking nor begetting — Ibn ʿAbbās said it, and Ibn ʿAṭiyyah said it is established from the Prophet ﷺ **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**. **[Qurṭubī]** sides with the majority and rejects the proofs Ibn ʿArabī adduced for the other view: the ḥadīth in Muslim from Abū Hurayrah that a tribe of the Children of Israel was lost and he supposes it to be the mouse — "it does not drink the milk of camels, but drinks the milk of sheep"; the ḍabb ḥadīth in Muslim, "I do not know, perhaps it is from the generations that were metamorphosed"; and ʿAmr b. Maymūn's account of stoning a monkey in the age of ignorance. His verdict: there is no proof in any of it. The general lesson, however, is Ibn Kathīr's: the punishment of that village was made an example for those around it in its own time and a reminder preserved for those who came after — which is why the Prophet ﷺ in the ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah warned, "Do not commit what the Jews committed, making lawful what God has forbidden by the meanest of tricks."

**Rulings — stratagems and the law.** **[Maʿārif]** uses the verse for a delicate juristic point: the charge that the master jurists invented *ḥiyal* to help the powerful circumvent the Sharīʿah is a calumny resting on a confusion of meanings — in its technical legal sense *ḥilah* is a device for conforming to the law in a case of necessity or human weakness, not for by-passing it. He gives the Prophet's own example: bartering a measure of good dates for two of bad is a form of usury, so the Prophet ﷺ taught the device of selling the bad dates for cash and buying the good with the cash; the intention there is to conform, and the law is neither negated nor mocked. The Jews of the verse did the opposite: their ruse negated the command and made a mockery of the day. The distinction between a device that preserves the law and a device that mocks it is the verse's own line. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gives the ḥadīth that states it in the Prophet's words, reported by Ibn Baṭṭah with a good chain from Abū Hurayrah: "Do not commit what the Jews committed, making lawful what God has forbidden by the meanest of tricks."

**Belief — was the change real?** The commentators treat the metamorphosis as a literal act of God. Ibn ʿAbbās, in al-ʿAwfī's report, said God changed their bodies into apes and swine — the young to apes, the old to swine; Qatāda described them as howling monkeys with tails, after having been men and women **[Ibn Kathīr]**. As for the apes and swine alive today, the ḥadīth answers the question: Muslim records from Ibn Masʿūd that people asked the Prophet ﷺ whether the apes and swine were descendants of the metamorphosed Jews, and he told them that apes and swine existed in the world before that, and that when God sends this particular punishment on a people, their line ends with it and they do not breed **[Maʿārif]**. On the same principle, Ibn ʿAbbās said those who violated the Sabbath were turned into monkeys and then perished without offspring, no transformed person living on the earth more than three days, neither eating nor drinking nor begetting **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Maʿārif]** adds a firm note against those who read the verse as a purely moral transformation — that they merely took on the traits of apes and swine — pointing out that they had already displayed those traits before the punishment, so that such a reading empties the verse of its meaning as a punishment for them and a warning for others; and he observes that God's power to change a form is no harder to accept than the descent of man from apes, which the same people swallow without evidence. **[Study Quran]** notes al-Qurṭubī's comparison to 17:50, "Be stone or iron," where the command is rhetorical — as if to say, "Be apes, as you have chosen to be" — and Mujāhid's view that their hearts were transformed though they did not become apes, comparing them to the ass carrying books (62:5); it also collects the Qurʾānic passages where human beings are likened to animals, and the aḥādīth about people being resurrected in the forms their inner natures had taken.

**Language.** *Al-sabt* is rest and stillness, with ease; a sleeping man is *masbūt* for the stillness of his body, and "We made your sleep a *subāt*" (78:9) is rest for the bodies. It is said the day was named for this because God finished the creation on the day before it **[Ṭabarī]**. *Khāsiʾīn* is from *khasāʾa*: the outcast, driven away — as a dog is driven off with *ikhsaʾ*; the rhyme has it, "like the dog — say to it *ikhsaʾ* and it slinks away" — meaning, if you drive it off it goes off humbled and small. So the verse's *khāsiʾīn* is "driven away from good, abject, humbled": Mujāhid and Qatāda said *ṣāghirīn*, humbled; al-Rabīʿ, "lowly and humbled"; Ibn ʿAbbās, "humiliated" **[Ṭabarī]**.

**Reflection.** This is the first of the Sūrah's metamorphosis stories, and its edge is aimed less at the ancient village than at the reader: they did not break the Sabbath, they *kept* it — with a cord tied to a fish's tail. A law can be honoured in letter and murdered in intent, and the verse calls that transgression. Which is why **[Maʿārif]**'s defence of *ḥiyal* matters as much as his condemnation of them: the question is never whether one may be clever with the law, but whether the cleverness is in service of obedience or in mockery of it. Their fault was not intelligence; it was contempt wearing the clothes of compliance.

The Sabbath transgression is described as a breach concealed beneath apparent technical compliance. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates how nets and pools were set before the Sabbath so fish arriving that day could be collected afterward; **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** preserve reports about the coastal town and its people. **[Saʿdī]** presents the transformation as humiliation following a grave sin, and **[Jalālayn]** gives the compact reading of the punishment. **[Study Quran]** notes that interpreters differ over whether the language is a literal transformation or a rhetorical likeness, while Ibn Kathīr reports the bodily transformation as the prevalent account. **[Maʿārif]** cautions readers to separate the Qur'anic statement from details found only in transmitted stories. The warning is not against lawful interpretation but against using clever devices to defeat a command's purpose. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:66

*"So We made it an exemplary punishment for what was before it and what was after it, and an admonition for the reverent."*

**Meaning.** The punishment of the Sabbath-breakers was not left as a private event in one village's history; God made it a public lesson **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Saʿdī]** states the twofold purpose: as a deterrent for the communities present at the event and those who came after, and as a proof of God's justice against His servants, so that people would hold back from disobedience — with the sober qualification that such warnings profit no one but the reverent. **[Jalālayn]** notes that the reverent are singled out here precisely because they, unlike others, are the ones who benefit from the lesson.

**Language.** *Nakāl* is an infinitive from *nakkala bi-fulān*: it is punishment, as ʿAdī b. Zayd's line uses *nakāl* for penalty; and the root carries the sense of binding back, since *al-nikl* and *al-ankāl* are fetters — named because by them a man is *yunkal*, held back; a heavy bridle is called *nakl* because it restrains the animal, and *nakala ʿan al-amr* is to refuse and refrain. *Al-tankīl* is to visit an enemy with a punishment that *deters* those behind them — that makes them shrink back **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *Mawʿiẓah*, on the measure *mafʿilah*, is from *al-ittiʿāẓ*, taking heed and stopping short; al-Khalīl said *al-waʿẓ* is reminding of good in a way that softens the heart, and *al-ʿiẓah* is the noun — and **[Study Quran]** observes that *mawʿiẓah* can lean either way in the language, counsel and exhortation as in 2:275, 3:138 and 5:46, or reprimand and admonition. In the grammar, *nakalan* is the second object of "We made," and *mawʿiẓah* is joined to it **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Context — what "it" refers to.** **[Ṭabarī]** lays out five positions. From Ibn ʿAbbās, two: that the pronoun stands for the *maskh*, the transformation itself — God made His punishment of them by metamorphosis a deterrent; or that it stands for *the fish*, though no fish had yet been mentioned, the context pointing to it. Others: the village whose people transgressed; the apes themselves; or the community that broke the Sabbath. And "what was before it and what was after it" divides similarly: Ibn ʿAbbās — those who came after, to be warned by God's punishment, and those who remained with them; al-Rabīʿ and Qatāda in one report — the sins that had gone before them and the fish they took; Ibn ʿAbbās in ʿIkrimah's report — the villages around; Mujāhid — their past sins and the sins that destroyed them; and in the fuller reading al-Zajjāj and Ibn ʿAṭiyyah preferred: the transformation was made a warning for what had gone before of their sins and for what would be done after it, so that people would fear the same fate. al-Naḥḥās chose Ibn ʿAbbās's "those present and those who come after" as closest to the meaning **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**. **[Ibn Kathīr]** takes the same sense: the village was made an example for the villages around it, and the story was preserved in revelation as a reminder to those who came later — as 46:27 says, "We destroyed the towns around you and turned the signs in various ways, that they might return."

**Belief & reflection.** **[Qurṭubī]** records al-Māwardī's answer to a natural question: the warning is for the world, so why were the reverent named? Because they alone take it, as against the obstinate disbeliever who passes by the sign and does not move; and al-Zajjāj read the clause as aimed at the community of Muḥammad ﷺ — that they should not tear down what God has made sacred, lest there fall on them what fell on the people of the Sabbath when they violated God's sanctity on their day. Ibn ʿAṭiyyah's point is worth carrying, that the wording includes every reverent person of every community **[Qurṭubī]**. The verse thus closes the story with a general law: God's punishments in history are signs for those who can read them, and what makes the difference is not intelligence but reverence. Two people pass the ruined town; one walks on, one stops and changes direction.

The punishment is also made into a public lesson. **[Ibn Kathīr]** understands “those before it and those after it” as communities in the same region and later peoples who hear the story; **[Saʿdī]** similarly says the report reaches contemporaries and later generations as a proof against repeating the sin. **[Jalālayn]** emphasizes its deterrent purpose, and **[Study Quran]** records several possible referents for the phrase, including those present and those who came later. **[Ṭabarī]** transmits the account as a warning preserved beyond the town itself. The qualification “for the reverent” is not because the warning is hidden from everyone else, but because the God-conscious take admonition from it. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]** The verse moves from a specific punishment to a general discipline: a rule cannot be evaded by arranging circumstances so that its wording seems technically untouched. **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]**

The word *nakāl* underscores a consequence that restrains by example. **[Ibn Kathīr]** compares it with other punishments that deter people from repeating a transgression; **[Ṭabarī]** presents the transformed community as a sign preserved for observers. **[Qurṭubī]** records the different explanations of “those before it and those after it,” while **[Study Quran]** notes that the phrase can refer to the local audience, later peoples, or other temporal relations. The closing word *mawʿiẓah* can carry the sense of counsel as well as warning. **[Jalālayn]** says the reverent are singled out because they are the ones who benefit; **[Saʿdī]** makes the same distinction between hearing a sign and taking admonition from it. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** The account is thus more than an episode of punishment: its preserved memory is meant to restrain later readers from repeating the deception. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:67

*"And when Mūsā said to his people, 'God commands you to slaughter a cow,' they said, 'Do you take us in mockery?' He said, 'I seek refuge in God from being among the ignorant.'"*

**Meaning.** The scene returns to the wilderness generation, this time for a command that tested far more than their appetites **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Saʿdī]** supplies the frame: a man had been killed among them, and they fell to disputing and accusing one another over his killer until the matter nearly produced a great evil; to expose the killer, Mūsā told them to slaughter a cow — and the duty at that moment was to obey at once without objection. They refused anything but objection: "Do you take us in mockery?" And the prophet answered that the ignorant man is the one who speaks words with no benefit in them and mocks people; the wise man counts mockery of another as one of the greatest defects, disgracing both religion and reason, and if one man has been favoured over another, the favour obliges gratitude to God and mercy to His servants.

**Context — the murder behind the command.** The reports vary in detail but agree on the shape. ʿUbaydah al-Salmānī: a man in Israel was childless, with substantial wealth and only a nephew to inherit him, so the nephew killed him and moved the body at night to another tribe's quarter; fighting nearly broke out, and their leaders said, "Will you kill one another while God's Messenger is among you?" They came to Mūsā, the command to slaughter a cow came down, and only after their repeated questions did they find the particular cow; the dead man was struck with a part of it and rose and named his killer — after which no killer was ever allowed to inherit **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]**. Abū al-ʿĀliyyah: a wealthy man with no son, killed by a relative who stood to inherit, then thrown at the crossroads; the murderer himself came to Mūsā asking him to ask God, and when the answer came they were astonished: "Do you take us in mockery?" **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Maʿārif]** records the version in *Mirqāt*: a man of Israel sought a girl in marriage, her father refused, and the suitor killed him and disappeared — and notes al-Kalbī's remark that no law on manslaughter had yet been revealed, which would place the incident before the Torah. **[Study Quran]** summarizes the same background from al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr, notes the resemblance to Numbers 19's red heifer — "without defect, in which there is no blemish and on which no yoke has been laid" — and observes that the Israelites' impertinent counter-questioning has no parallel in the Biblical account.

**Language.** *Huzuwan* is play and mockery; the rhyme "Umm Ṭaysalah has mocked me, saying she sees me penniless with nothing" — that is, she scoffed and made sport **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Saʿdī]** presses the word's weight: mockery is what the *jāhil* does. **[Study Quran]** explains the Qurʾānic sense of *jahl*: to be *jāhil* is not a mere lack of knowledge but a disposition against it — a pathological adherence to one's own way, the same vision that underlies the term *jāhiliyyah* for the age before Islam — and it cites 6:35, 11:46 and 12:33, where being "among the ignorant" is a state one fears and a warning one is given. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that "a cow" is the female noun, the male being *thawr*, like *nāqah* and *jamal* — though some hold *baqarah* to cover both sexes; and the root sense is splitting, since the cow splits the earth with ploughing; he also records Abū ʿAmr's reading *yaʾmurkum* with the *rāʾ*'s vowel lightened, and al-Mubarrad's rejection of a fully vowelless reading, since the *rāʾ* is a letter of inflection.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** gathers the juristic matters the verse raises. The scholars agree that *dhabḥ* (slaughter at the throat) is preferable for sheep and goats, *naḥr* (the stab at the chest) for camels, and either is acceptable for cattle — though Mālik disapproved of the interchange without prohibiting it; Ibn al-Mundhir said he knew of no one who declared forbidden either manner of killing for the animal assigned to the other. And al-Māwardī gives the reason a cow was chosen rather than another animal: it was of the same kind as the calf they had worshipped, so that they would come to think little of what they had once magnified, and their obedience would expose what had truly been in their hearts. He adds a second wisdom: the living should be made to speak by the killing of a living creature, so that God's power would be shown more clearly in bringing things out from their opposites.

**Belief & reflection.** **[Ṭabarī]** draws the doctrine from the rebuke itself: mockery and play are not possible in a prophet when he conveys a command or a prohibition from God; their suspicion was itself a sin, since Mūsā was telling them that God had ordered it. So his answer is not a denial of the command but a disavowal of the character imputed to him — "I seek refuge with God from being among the ignorant," the fools who attribute to God what is false. And the verse marks the beginning of a pattern that will run through the cow's entire story: a people who could have obeyed with any cow, and who will instead question their way into a uniquely expensive animal — as the report puts it, had they taken the nearest cow it would have sufficed them. What looks like carefulness is, in the Qurʾān's reading, evasion in search of a way out.

The command's connection to the murder becomes clear only when the story unfolds: the slaughter is the means by which the killer will be identified. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the report of a wealthy man killed by his heir and the quarrel that nearly became a wider fight. **[Ṭabarī]** preserves related accounts; **[Study Quran]** notes that the fuller resolution belongs to 2:72–73 and compares the cow with Numbers 19. **[Saʿdī]** says that Mūsā's answer was a genuine divine command, not a joke, and that a wise person does not mock another person. **[Jalālayn]** likewise reads the prophet's refuge as a rejection of ignorant mockery. **[Qurṭubī]** treats the command and the people's response within the wider story, while **[Saʿdī]** cautions against treating later Israelite reports as certain exegesis when they are not authenticated. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The opening rebuke is therefore about their suspicion of the prophet as much as their reluctance to obey. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:68

*"They said, 'Call upon your Lord for us to make clear to us what she is.' He said, 'He says she is a cow neither old nor young, middling between that; so do what you are commanded.'"*

**Meaning.** The first of the three counter-questions, and the first narrowing of the command **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Saʿdī]** reads the question as asking about her age and the answer as closing the door on further haggling: neither a grande dame nor a heifer, but between the two — *so do what you are commanded*; leave off severity and needless prying. **[Ibn Kathīr]** states the law of the passage: God had mentioned the stubbornness of the Children of Israel and their excessive questioning of their prophets, so when they made the matter hard, God made it harder for them; had they slaughtered any cow it would have sufficed, as Ibn ʿAbbās and ʿUbaydah said.

**Context — why this is rebuke, not diligence.** **[Ṭabarī]** explains that by now they knew and were settled in the fact that the command came from God, not from Mūsā's own initiative. God had commanded them to slaughter *a cow* — any cow they wished, without restricting them to a type or a class — and they asked for a description God had already spared them the burden of, out of roughness of character, coarseness of constitution and poor understanding, imposing on themselves what God had waived — posing riddles to the Prophet ﷺ. Ibn ʿAbbās put it in one word: they said it *testing him* (yataʿannatūnahu). And because they had also offended by suspecting Mūsā of mockery, God requited them by restricting what He had left open: "she is a cow neither old nor young." **[Qurṭubī]** agrees with the diagnosis and gives the criterion: this is *taʿnīt* — vexatious quibbling — and want of obedience; had they simply complied and slaughtered any cow, the purpose would have been achieved; they tightened the rope on themselves, so God tightened it on them, and al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī reported the like from the Prophet ﷺ.

**Language.** *Fāriḍ* is the aged female — *faraḍat tafriḍu furūḍan*, she grew old — the same root used of old rancour that never cools, as the verse "many a man of ancient grudge against me" **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *Bakr*, with the *bāʾ* vowelled *kasrah*, is a female of the cattle or of human beings that has not been covered by a male; with a *fatḥah* it is a young camel **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *ʿAwān* is the middle-aged one that has borne a belly or two, the strongest and finest of cattle — unlike horses, where middle age is past its best; the plural is *ʿūn* and *ʿuwun*, and the word is used of a second war after a first, "when a middling war conceives" **[Qurṭubī]**. In the grammar, *lā fāriḍun wa-lā bakrun* may be read as adjectives of *baqarah*, or as the predicate of an implied subject with the force of "she is not old," and the like pattern follows in *lā dhalūlun* and *lā tasqī al-ḥarth* **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Rulings — the command and the law of abrogation.** **[Qurṭubī]** finds in the verse a juristic and a legal-theoretical point. As to the theory: the first command, "slaughter a cow," implied any cow at all; when the description was added, the first ruling was superseded before the act — a demonstration that abrogation may come before the time of performance, just as the alms due on thirty camels might first be a yearling she-camel and then be replaced by a two-year-old. As to the law of commands: *so do what you are commanded* is a renewal and reinforcement of the order, and a warning against the very thing they did not abandon; it shows that a command in itself carries obligation — the position of the jurists — and, on the majority view, that it requires immediate performance, since God later reproached them with "they nearly did not do it." Ibn Khuwayz Mindād argued the contrary, that obeying a command admits delay, since God did not rebuke them for the delay and the questioning. **[Ibn Kathīr]** collects the agreement on the description: every reported authority — Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, ʿIkrimah, Abū al-ʿĀliyyah, al-Suddī, al-Ḥasan, Qatāda, Wahb b. Munabbih and others — held her to be neither aged nor below the age of breeding; al-Ḍaḥḥāk from Ibn ʿAbbās said she was at the age when a cow is strongest and fittest.

**Reflection.** Every question they asked was plausible, and every answer cost them something. Had they gone out and slaughtered the first cow they met, the dead man would have spoken and the command would have been behind them; instead they asked what she was, and the answer took away the young and the old — and then the colour, and then the yoke. **[Study Quran]** records Ibn ʿAjībah's use of precisely this feature: the soul must be slaughtered so that the spiritual soul may live, and the best moment is when it is neither too old, its habits too deeply set for change, nor too young, feeling immortal and needing no change. The Qurʾān does not condemn asking questions as such; it condemns questions offered in place of obedience, the kind whose real content is "not yet."

The first question asks the age of the cow, a detail that the original command had left open. **[Ibn Kathīr]** says any cow would have sufficed before the questions began; the added description then narrowed the choice. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** explain the terms for an old cow, a young one, and the middle age between them. **[Saʿdī]** hears the answer as a rebuke to needless strictness: stop pressing and do what you were commanded. **[Jalālayn]** gives the same middle-age sense. The verse does not condemn sincere clarification in every circumstance; its setting is repeated questioning after a plain instruction. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]** The admonition is proportionate to this sequence: when a person seeks extra conditions instead of acting, the task may become harder rather than clearer. **[Study Quran]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:69

*"They said, 'Call upon your Lord for us to make clear to us what her colour is.' He said, 'He says she is a yellow cow, bright is her colour, pleasing to the beholders.'"*

**Meaning.** The second question, and the second restriction. **[Ṭabarī]** is explicit that this too was *taʿannut* — a fresh round of quibbling after the first: God had narrowed them to a type but had not bound them to a colour, and they insisted on asking what they had no need to know, and were then bound to a colour as well. The answer names the animal exactly: a yellow cow, whose colour is *fāqiʿ*, delighting those who look at her.

**Language.** In *mā lawnuhā*, the *mā* is interrogative and the subject, which is why *lawn* is nominative; Ṭabarī explains at length that the verb "make clear to us" cannot govern the interrogative directly, any more than it can govern *ayy*, since *mā* and *ayy* gather up the scattered parts of a question — as when one says, "make clear to us whether this cow is black or yellow" **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *Fāqiʿ* is intense and pure: the Arabs say *aṣfar fāqiʿ* for a concentrated yellow, as they say *aswad ḥālik*, *aḥmar qāniʾ*, *abyaḍ nāṣiʿ*, *akhḍar nāḍir*; al-Kisāʾī said *faqaʿa lawnuhā* is that its yellowness became unclouded. And the word wanders usefully: *al-ifqāʿ* is a bad state, *fawāqiʿ al-dahr* are its calamities, and *tafqīʿ* is cracking the fingers until the joints snap — which Ibn ʿAbbās forbade in prayer **[Qurṭubī]**. *Ṣafrāʾ* takes no *tanwīn* in either definite or indefinite use because of the inseparable *alif* of femininity, unlike forms ending in *hāʾ* **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Reading — what yellow meant.** The majority of exegetes took it plainly: yellow in colour, and some added that even the horns and the hooves were yellow **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. al-Ḥasan and Ibn Jubayr limited it to the horns and hooves alone. And al-Ḥasan in one report said *ṣafrāʾ* here means *black* — supporting it with the poet's "those are my horses and those my riding camels; they are *ṣufr*, their young like raisins," and with 77:33, "as though they were yellow camels" — because the blackness of a black camel carries a yellow cast. **[Qurṭubī]** rejects it: the apparent sense is the soundest, and the fact that God reinforced the colour with *fāqiʿ* settles it, since *fāqiʿ* is a description specific to yellowness and the Arabs do not say *aswad fāqiʿ*. Ibn ʿAbbās, in al-ʿAwfī's report, described it as a deep yellowish white **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

**"Pleasing to the beholders."** Wahb b. Munabbih said that if you looked at her skin you would think the sun's rays were coming out of it; Abū al-ʿĀliyyah, Qatāda and al-Rabīʿ said she pleased those who saw her — Abū al-ʿĀliyyah adding that it was in her bearing and her appearance, not her colour alone **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** notes Ibn ʿAbbās's word that yellowness pleases the soul, and how far the early authorities carried the remark: he encouraged yellow sandals, and ʿAlī is reported to have said that one who wears yellow leather sandals has little worry, since God said *yellow, bright is her colour, pleasing to the beholders*; Ibn al-Zubrayr and Muḥammad b. Abī Kathīr disliked black sandals for the opposite reason, that they bring gloom. *(digression)* This is a mild example of a habit worth naming: a Qurʾānic description of a cow's colour is quoted as a sanction for footwear. The verse's subject is a specific animal in a specific command; the sandal reports show the tradition's ease with taking a word's pleasantness as a general sign, not a rule of dress. **[Ibn Kathīr]** closes with a comparison to the Torah's account of a red heifer in Numbers 19 and comments that the description there may be an error or that she was so deeply yellow as to appear dark or reddish — either way, God knows best.

**Reflection.** Two questions have now cost them a colour, and the third will cost them the yoke. The remarkable thing is that the answers are beautiful: whatever God chose was worth looking at, *pleasing to the beholders*, in a scene that ends with a corpse rising to name its killer. The command they were treating with such caution was the raw material of a resurrection. **[Ṭabarī]**'s diagnosis is the one to keep: the second question was also *taʿannut* — not a search for guidance, but the itch of a people who had heard the answer and wanted another one.

The second question adds colour to the description. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** discuss *ṣafrāʾ fāqiʿ*, a vivid or intense yellow, and the readings that clarify its force. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gathers reports describing a bright, pleasing colour, while **[Jalālayn]** renders it as a deep golden yellow. **[Saʿdī]** focuses on the beauty that delights those who look at it. The commentators transmit some divergent explanations of precisely how yellow appeared, but the verse's plain point is a clear and distinctive colour. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** The sequence is important: each new question adds a constraint that had not been part of the first command. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Ṭabarī]** Their request for clarification may sound reasonable in isolation, but the narrative places it after an instruction they could already have obeyed. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**

No feature in the first instruction had required them to seek the most beautiful cow; the colour is supplied in answer to their own question. **[Ibn Kathīr]** observes that each added inquiry narrowed what had initially been left open. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** parse the adjective and its intensity, and **[Saʿdī]** reads “pleasing to the beholders” as a description of beauty. **[Jalālayn]** similarly explains that the vivid hue delights those who look. The description is clear enough to guide them, yet it also makes the task harder than it had to be. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:70

*"They said, 'Call upon your Lord for us to make clear to us what she is — for cows are much alike to us — and we shall, if God wills, be guided.'"*

**Meaning.** The third question, and the first softening. **[Jalālayn]** renders the question as asking whether she grazes free or is used for labour, and reads *cows are much alike to us* as their confession that the species is numerous and they had not found the one described. **[Saʿdī]** hears in the closing clause their admission that they had not reached what Mūsā intended, and their new vow: "and we shall, if God wills, be guided." **[Ṭabarī]** counts it a third instance of their ignorance, and gives the arithmetic of it: when they were first commanded to slaughter a cow, any cow falling under the name would have sufficed, for they had not been burdened with a description; then they asked, and were restricted by age; and having been given the age, had they slaughtered the cheapest cow of that age it would have sufficed, for they had not been restricted by colour; then they asked again, and the colour was fixed. The rope they were pulling was tied to their own necks.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** sets beside the story the Prophet's ﷺ warning to his own community: "Leave me alone as long as I leave you. Those before you were destroyed only by their many questions and their disagreement with their prophets. When I command you something, do it as much as you can; when I forbid you something, abstain from it." Ibn ʿAbbās: had they taken the nearest cow it would have sufficed them — but they made it hard, so God made it hard on them; and ʿUbaydah al-Salmānī: had they taken the cheapest cow it would have served. ʿIkrimah adds the other half: if the Israelites had taken any cow it would have sufficed them, and had it not been for their saying "if God wills," they would never have found her at all **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Jalālayn]** reports the same as a hadith: had they not uttered the proviso, it would never have been made clear to them.

**Language & readings.** *Al-baqar* is a collective noun, which is why the verb is masculine, *tashābaha*; the plural forms are *bāqir*, *bāqūr*, *baqar* and *baqīr*, and the Arabs use the word as both masculine and feminine — which is what the variant readings reflect. al-Ḥasan and al-Aʿraj read *inna al-baqara tashshābaha* with the *tāʾ* assimilated into the *shīn* and the verb feminine; Mujāhid read it without the *alif* in the same form; Yaḥyā b. Yaʿmur read *inna al-bāqira yashshābahu*, masculine and assimilated; and the muṣḥaf of Ubayy has *tashābahat* with the *shīn* doubled, which Abū Ḥātim judged an error, since the *tāʾ* in this pattern is assimilated only in the imperfect **[Qurṭubī]**. As for the sense of the complaint, **Qurṭubī** gives a charming observation: the faces of cattle resemble one another — which is why the Prophet ﷺ described the trials of the end of time as coming "like the faces of cows," one looking like the next; and it was this very resemblance of the herd that the Israelites pleaded.

**Language — the exception.** *In shāʾa Allāh* is their exception, and **[Qurṭubī]** finds in it, coming at the last of their questions, a turning back and a measure of submission, and evidence of regret for not having simply complied.
