**Meaning.** The Sūrah now sets out the content of the covenant they were charged with and then broke **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Saʿdī]** opens with a striking observation about their character: every command they were given, they resisted, and would not accept it except under the heaviest oaths and the most binding covenants — here, the covenant was taken before such a programme that a person of insight would recognize it as God's kindness: worship of Him alone as the foundation without which no deed is accepted; then the rights of creation, beginning with the parents; then kin, orphans and the needy; then a universal good that a man can extend to every creature — good speech; then prayer and alms, prayer containing sincerity toward the One worshipped and alms containing goodness to the servants. And after all of it: "you turned away" — a turning of aversion, with no desire and no returning.

**Language.** *Al-mīthāq* is *mifʿāl* from binding oneself by oath and the like **[Ṭabarī]**. On "you shall worship none but God," the readings divide between the *tāʾ* and the *yāʾ* — both valid, because taking a covenant is a form of adjuration: as one says, "I adjured my brother — you shall surely stand," speaking of him as one present in the words, and "I adjured him, that he should stand," speaking as of an absent man; so with the covenant, the address may be reported in either person **[Ṭabarī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the grammar: the verb stands in the nominative because the implied *an* was dropped — "We took their covenant that they should not worship any but God, and that they should be good to parents, and not shed blood" — and Ubayy and Ibn Masʿūd read it *lā taʿbudū* as a plain prohibition, which is why the verse rolls on into commands: stand, say, establish, give. ***Wa-qūlū lil-nāsi ḥusnan*** — goodness itself; a variant reading is *ḥasanan*, and *ḥusn* is a verbal noun used as a hyperbolic description. In "and goodness to parents" there is no explicit verb; the sense is "and do goodness to parents," the verb understood **[Study Quran]**. And *khaṭīʾah*/*ḍalāl* aside, the closing *tawallaytum* carries more than mere departure: the one who turns away may still intend to return, but these had neither desire nor intention of return **[Saʿdī]**.

**Rulings & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** observes the ordering: God's own right comes first, for He created them for it, as every messenger without exception was sent with "there is no god but I, so worship Me" (21:25) and every community received a messenger with "worship God and shun false deities" (16:36). Then the rights of the created come next, and foremost the parents — which is why God so often joins gratitude to them with gratitude to Him: "give thanks to Me and to your parents; unto Me is the journeying" (31:14). He cites the ḥadīth in the two Ṣaḥīḥs from Ibn Masʿūd: "I asked, 'O Messenger of God, which deed is best?' He said, 'Prayer at its time.' 'Then what?' 'Kindness to parents.' 'Then what?' 'Jihād in God's cause.'" **[Qurṭubī]** gives the practical content of that kindness: living with them in what is right, humility before them, obeying them, praying for forgiveness for them after their death, and keeping ties with those they loved — the details of which belong to Sūrat al-Isrāʾ (17:23-26). He notes why the parents' right is joined here to *tawḥīd*: the first bringing-forth is from God, and the second bringing-forth, the raising of the child, is from the parents' side. **[Study Quran]** adds that these duties hold whether the parents are believers or not, citing the commentators' use of Ibrāhīm's restraint with his disbelieving father (19:42-48), and notes the ḥadīth honouring mothers especially. **[Maʿārif]** puts the social teaching of the verse in a wider frame: the basic elements of religion common to Islam and the earlier laws are *tawḥīd*, service to parents, kin, orphans and the needy, gentle speech with all human beings, and prayer and *zakāh*; and on the command to gentleness he quotes **[Qurṭubī]**'s account: Ṭalḥah b. ʿUmar told ʿAṭāʾ that he was short-tempered with people of unorthodox belief and dealt harshly with them; ʿAṭāʾ said, "Do not do that," recited this verse, and added, "God commanded us to speak to people courteously — and if Jews and Christians are to be treated so, how much more a Muslim, whatever kind of man he is?" He observes that God instructed Mūsā and Hārūn to speak gently to Pharaoh, and no one addressing another today is superior to Mūsā, nor is the one addressed viler than Pharaoh. **[Saʿdī]** draws the same line from the other direction: since a man cannot reach all people with his wealth, he was commanded to the good that he can extend to every creature — speech; and in the command is an implied prohibition of ugly speech to anyone, so that the believer is not obscene, not abusive, not a curser, not a quarreller, but of good character, patient with the harm people bring.

**Reflection.** This verse is a compact constitution, and its order is its argument: God first, then the people nearest to you, then the stranger, and speech as the one currency everyone can pay. **[Maʿārif]**'s story of ʿAṭāʾ is the verse's most practical test: the person hardest to address kindly is usually the one whose beliefs offend us, and the Qurʾān's command to "speak to people a good word" does not carry an exception for him. And the ending is the part of the verse that hurts: after a covenant so reasonable, "you turned away, all but a few of you." The few are mentioned, as **[Saʿdī]** says, so that no one should imagine the whole community turned — mercy keeps a remnant in view.

The covenant orders obligations from the foundation outward: exclusive worship of God, care for parents and vulnerable kin, good speech to people, then prayer and almsgiving. **[Ibn Kathīr]** calls worship without partners God's foremost right, followed by the rights of parents, relatives, orphans, and the poor. **[Saʿdī]** explains that this ethical core belongs to every revealed law because it serves people across times and places. **[Study Quran]** compares the list with other Qur'anic passages on universal commandments; **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** discuss its wording and the covenant's force. **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]** The instruction to speak well is broad: **[Ibn Kathīr]** includes gentle conduct, enjoining right, patience, and forgiving people, while **[Saʿdī]** includes teaching, greeting, kindness, and avoiding abuse even in debate. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** The exception “a few of you” matters: **[Saʿdī]** says the wording prevents readers from thinking every individual turned away, and **[Ṭabarī]** notes the continuing relevance of the covenant. The verse therefore gives a compact programme of worship and social responsibility, followed by an honest account of how many neglected it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:84

*"And when We took your covenant: you shall not shed your blood, nor expel your own selves from your homes — then you ratified it, and you bear witness."*

**Meaning.** The second article of the covenant, and the first place in the Sūrah where its violation is named as an event rather than a disposition **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Saʿdī]** gives the setting at Madīnah: al-Aws and al-Khazraj — the Anṣār — were idolaters before Islam, fighting each other on the old footing; the three Jewish groups, Banū Qurayẓah, Banū al-Naḍīr and Banū Qaynuqāʿ, arrived and each allied itself with one party of the city, so that when the two sides fought, the Jew would aid his ally against the other side's Jews — killing his fellow Jew and driving him out of his home when exile and plunder fell, only to ransom the captives after the fighting stopped. All three things had been made obligatory on them in the covenant: not to shed one another's blood, not to expel one another, and to ransom their captives. They did the last and abandoned the first two — which is the substance of the rebuke that follows in the next verse.

**Language — "your blood," "your selves."** **[Ṭabarī]** puts the question a reader naturally asks — did they use to kill themselves and expel themselves? — and answers: they were forbidden to kill one another, and a man's killing of his brother is his killing of himself, because one religion made them like a single person; he cites the Prophet's ﷺ saying, "The believers in their mutual compassion and affection are like one body: when one limb complains, the whole body answers with fever and sleeplessness." A second account: the man who kills exposes himself to retaliation, so he has caused his own death — one says to the culprit, "You have brought this on yourself." Qatāda: your own self, son of Adam, is the people of your religion and your call; so do not shed their blood. Abū al-ʿĀliyyah: none of you shall kill another nor expel another from his home **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the readings — *tusaffikūn* with the vowelled *tāʾ* and doubled *fāʾ*, and the reading with the *fāʾ* vowelled *ḍammah* — and the lexical notes: *al-nafs* comes from *al-nafāsah*, a man's soul being the most precious thing in him; *al-dār* is the dwelling a people settle in, even without buildings, so called because it encircles (*yadūru*) upon its residents as a wall does upon what it contains. *Aqrartum* — you ratified; *wa-antum tashhadūn* — either that you attest to it with your hearts, or that you were present at its taking.

**Rulings & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** reads the verse outward, and then applies it inward. Since the passage forbids a man shedding his own blood and exiling himself, Ibn Khuwayz Mindād allowed that the plain sense may be meant as well: a man should not kill himself under hardship and affliction, nor wander in the wilderness refusing to shelter in houses out of a perverse asceticism. And it was reported that ʿUthmān b. Maẓʿūn, with ten companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ, swore an oath to wear rough sackcloth, wander the desert without entering homes, eat no meat and abstain from women; when this reached the Prophet ﷺ he came to ʿUthmān's house, and not finding him, asked his wife about what he had heard — she, unwilling to reveal her husband's secret and unwilling to lie to the Messenger of God ﷺ, said, "If something reached you, it is as it reached you." The Prophet ﷺ said: "Say to ʿUthmān: is this a departure from my Sunnah, or from my religion? I pray and I sleep, I fast and I break the fast, I approach women, I shelter in houses, I eat meat. Whoever turns away from my Sunnah is not of me." And ʿUthmān and his companions returned from what they were on. There follows **[Qurṭubī]**'s own word on it — and it is the sentence that makes this old Israelite covenant sting: "All of this is to be honoured among us too, and all of it has come to pass among us through the tribulations. We belong to God, and to Him we return"; and he cites 6:65, "or He confounds you into sects, and makes you taste the violence of one another." **[Study Quran]** treats the verse as the entry to a longer question — how a community as such can be addressed for the deeds of its ancestors: one may be implicated passively, as children suffer for their parents' crimes; one may also take an active part in them; and where people glory in a group identity and accept its achievements as extensions of themselves, they expose themselves to its crimes as well. It notes the human reality that traits and practices are inherited and imitated across generations — the reason the Qurʾān keeps rebuking the argument "our fathers did so" — while a community may still be rescued from its darkness by an infusion of virtue. **[Maʿārif]** notes simply that this verse supplements the previous one: the pledge was explicit, not a matter of innuendo — as clear as a witness's testimony.

**Reflection.** The verse's phrase "your own selves" is the whole ethical argument in two words. A community bound by one religion is, in the Qurʾān's accounting, one body, and war within it is amputation. **[Qurṭubī]**'s aside about the tribulations among the Muslims, and the story of ʿUthmān b. Maẓʿūn's companions, together make the verse's double edge visible: it forbids the community to devour itself, and it forbids the individual to wage war on himself in the name of piety. Both are violations of the same covenant, and both have a cure in the same sentence: whoever turns away from the Sunnah is not of him.

“Your blood” and “yourselves” describe a shared community in which killing another member is treated as an injury to one's own people. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the command forbids Israelites from killing one another and from driving one another from home. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the wording to the covenant taken from them, and **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the collective sense of these terms. **[Maʿārif]** emphasizes that the pledge was explicit and willingly accepted. **[Saʿdī]** places it in Madīnah, where Jewish groups allied with opposing Arab factions and could end up fighting fellow Jews. **[Study Quran]** notes that the next verse describes the contradiction between that covenant and their conduct. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ṭabarī]** The final “you bear witness” is not a casual observation: **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Ibn Kathīr]** understand it as acknowledgment of the binding covenant, while **[Saʿdī]** treats it as making their later violation harder to excuse. **[Maʿārif]** The verse thus establishes the standard before recounting the breach; it is the people's own confirmed agreement that makes their conduct a contradiction. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:85

*"Then it is you, the very ones, who kill your own and expel a party of you from their homes, conspiring against them in sin and enmity. And if they come to you as captives, you ransom them — though their expulsion was forbidden you. Do you then believe in part of the Book and disbelieve in part? What is the requital of those of you who do that but disgrace in the life of this world, and on the Day of Resurrection they shall be consigned to the severest punishment. And God is not heedless of what you do."*

**Meaning.** The covenant is now held up against the conduct, and the absurdity of their practice is set out in a single sentence **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Jalālayn]** narrates the circle: Qurayẓah was allied with al-Aws and al-Naḍīr with al-Khazraj; when the two Arab sides fought, the Jews fought on both sides, killing and expelling fellow Jews and taking prisoners — and then ransomed the captives. Asked why they fought them, they said, "For fear that our allies be humiliated"; asked why they paid ransoms, they said, "Because we were commanded to ransom." God answers: what is the requital of those who do that but disgrace in this world — and it fell: Qurayẓah were killed, al-Naḍīr expelled to Syria and ordered to pay the *jizyah* — and on the Day of Resurrection, the worst of punishments. **[Saʿdī]** gives the doctrinal point beneath the story: three obligations had been laid on them — not to shed one another's blood, not to expel one another, and to ransom the captive; they kept the last and abandoned the first two, and God's rebuke is the verse's question: do you believe in part of the Book and reject part? And that, he notes, is proof that faith requires performing the commands and avoiding the prohibitions, and that the commanded acts belong to faith itself.

**Context.** Ibn ʿAbbās, in Ibn Isḥāq's report: the Jews went out with the idolaters, shedding their blood alongside them and expelling them alongside them; God reproached them for it, having forbidden the shedding of their blood in the Torah and made obligatory the ransoming of their captives; they made two parties — Banū Qaynuqāʿ allied to al-Khazraj, and al-Naḍīr and Qurayẓah to al-Aws — so that when war broke out, each party backed its allies against its brothers, spilling each other's blood with the Torah in their hands, knowing from it what was owed to them and what was upon them **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that this verse is addressed to those who were present and cannot be referred back to their forebears: it came down concerning Banū Qaynuqāʿ, Qurayẓah and al-Naḍīr, and the alliances ran across the community — al-Aws were allies of Qaynuqāʿ and al-Khazraj of Qurayẓah, while al-Naḍīr and Qurayẓah were brothers, until they split and fought; when the fighting stopped, they ransomed their prisoners, and God reproached them for it. **[Maʿārif]** adds the human detail of their excuses: they were asked why they were so diligent about ransoming and answered that releasing prisoners was obligatory for them; and when they were challenged over helping the non-Jews slaughter Jews, they said that failing their friends would be a real disgrace — even if the friends were not Jews. So the verse exposes the duplicity: the Qurʾān labels it *sin and enmity*, which measures two kinds of rights infringed at once — God's right by disobedience, and the creatures' right by the pain and loss inflicted.

**Language.** *Thumma antum hāʾulāʾi* admits two analyses in **[Ṭabarī]**'s account: either *hāʾulāʾi* stands where a vocative was intended — "then you, O these" — the *yāʾ* of address dropped as the context supplies it; or *antum* is the subject and *hāʾulāʾi* an interposed demonstrative with the predicate following, as the Arabs say "I am this one standing." One Baṣran held that *hāʾulāʾi* merely calls attention to and confirms *antum*, comparing "we are these who love them and they love not you" (3:119) and Khufāf b. Nudbah's "I am that one." **[Qurṭubī]** gives the grammarians' alternatives and the readings: the people of Madīnah and Makkah read *taẓẓāharūna* with the *tāʾ* assimilated into the *ẓāʾ*, its origin *tataẓāharūna*; the Kūfans read it lightly, dropping the second *tāʾ* since the first points to it; Qatāda read *taẓharūna*. The root is *al-ẓahr*: mutual backing, each strengthening the other so that he is as a back to him; the poet's line has the image of men who back each other like buttresses against one. *Al-ithm* is the deed that earns blame; *al-ʿudwān* is excess in wrong and overstepping it **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]**.

**Rulings — believing in part of the Book.** **[Maʿārif]** states the principle: a divine Book must be accepted as a whole; to reject a part of it is to reject it, and that is unbelief. He then faces a difficulty: if the verse rebukes them merely for not *acting* on certain injunctions, how is that called *kufr*, when a believer who accepts the lawfulness of what is lawful and the unlawfulness of what is unlawful in principle is not an unbeliever? His answer: when a sin is very grave, the idiom of the Sharīʿah sometimes calls it *kufr* to underline its seriousness. **[Study Quran]** puts the matter in the larger vocabulary of the Qurʾān: here the wider sense of *kufr* — rejection and denial of certain truths — comes to the fore, since half-belief is not a denial of God's existence but the behaviour of hypocrisy and immorality, which cannot be separated from some degree of denial; it records the view that the inconsistency lies precisely between the expulsion, which violated the Book, and the ransom, which conformed to it, and notes the exegesis that connects the partial unbelief with the prophecies of the Prophet ﷺ, though the context does not imply it.

**Reflection.** The verse is one of the Qurʾān's sharpest portraits of religion used as a menu. They kept the clause that cost them money and gave them prestige — ransoming captives, admired by all — and dropped the clauses that would have cost them tribal loyalty: not to kill, not to expel. God's question — *do you believe in part of the Book and disbelieve in part?* — is not a quibble about jurisprudence. It is the observation that a Book held on those terms is not a revelation at all; it is a mirror for one's preferences, and the tribe, not the Lord, is doing the choosing. And the penalty named is suited to the crime: those who chose the good opinion of their allies over the covenant received, in this world, the loss of standing they feared — Qurayẓah killed, al-Naḍīr exiled, and the *jizyah* imposed — and in the next, a punishment that will not be lifted.

The reproach is sharpened by the contrast between what they did and what they were willing to do afterward. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that they killed and expelled fellow Israelites despite having affirmed the covenant, then ransomed captives in accordance with another command of the Torah. **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Qurṭubī]** describe the same cycle: fighting on opposite sides, driving people from their homes, then raising money to secure the prisoners' release. **[Saʿdī]** calls this belief in part of the Book and rejection of another part; **[Jalālayn]** makes the contradiction explicit. **[Maʿārif]** notes that acting on the ransom command could not cancel the simultaneous prohibition against killing and exile. The rhetorical question is therefore about selective obedience, not about whether ransom is itself virtuous. **[Study Quran]** relates the passage to the covenant in 2:84 and the political divisions in Madīnah. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The consequences are named in both temporal horizons: disgrace in this life and severe punishment in the next. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** The closing statement that God is not heedless prevents a community from imagining that public observance of one rule can hide its violation of another. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:86

*"Those are they who have bought the life of this world at the price of the Hereafter; so the punishment shall not be lightened for them, nor shall they be helped."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse as the naming of the bargain the previous verses described: these are the ones who bought the cheap goods of this world — precedence over the weak and the ignorant of their own community, and coarse food — with the faith that would have brought them, in place of their disbelief, eternal dwelling in the Gardens; they were content with this world through their unbelief as the price of the Hereafter's bliss, and so had no share in it at all. Qatāda put it plainly: they preferred the little of this world to the much of the Hereafter. And the sentence about them follows from the bargain: whoever has no share in the Hereafter's bliss has only the punishment there, and for those there is no alleviation and no interval — because it is only the one who has some share in bliss for whom punishment might be lightened. As for "nor shall they be helped," **[Ṭabarī]** explains it as a denial of every kind of help: no one will fend off God's punishment from them, by power, by intercession, or by anything else **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]**. **[Jalālayn]** gives the verse's own definition of the exchange: they bought this world *by preferring it* to the next — the preference is the purchase.

**Rulings.** In **[Qurṭubī]**'s reading the passage yields a standing legal obligation. His scholars say: the ransoming of captives is obligatory even if not a single dirham remains. Ibn Khuwayz Mindād held that the verse contains the obligation of freeing prisoners; the reports from the Prophet ﷺ show that he freed captives and commanded their freeing; the practice of the Muslims ran on it and consensus settled upon it. It is owed from the public treasury, and if there is none, it is an obligation on all the Muslims collectively, which whoever carries it out discharges for the rest. And then he wrote the sentence that has kept this commentary alive: "By God, we too have turned away from all of it, through the tribulations — each of us backing himself with others against the others! Would that it had been against the disbelievers; but no — against the Muslims, indeed not the disbelievers! — until we left our brothers humiliated and abased, the rule of the polytheists running over them. There is no might and no power except with God, the Most High, the Mighty." **[Ibn Kathīr]** draws the standing conclusion the passage is aimed at: since these people implemented the Torah when it suited them and defied it when it did not — knowing the while that they were wrong — they can be trusted neither to preserve it nor to convey it; and they are not to be believed in what they relate of the description of the Messenger of God ﷺ, his coming, his expulsion from his land and his migration, all of which the earlier prophets had told them and which they hid.

**Reflection.** The verse gives the conduct of the previous passage its true name: not politics, not loyalty, not honour — a sale. And it is worth noticing what was on each side of the scale in **[Ṭabarī]**'s account, because he refuses to romanticize the motive: what they purchased was precedence over the weak-witted among their own people and coarse food. Rank over the vulnerable, and a meal, bought with the Gardens. Every generation of believers has the same market open in front of it, and the only mercy is that the terms are stated in advance: the price is this world, and the merchandise is the next.

The metaphor of buying makes the choice deliberate. **[Ṭabarī]** says they traded faith and the Hereafter for worldly leadership over weaker members of their community and for inferior material gains. **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Qurṭubī]** connect the bargain to the breach described in the preceding verses: they obeyed one part of scripture when it served them and rejected another. **[Saʿdī]** says they preferred the little of this world to the lasting good of the next; **[Maʿārif]** draws attention to the way short-term advantage can disguise a grave spiritual loss. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ṭabarī]** The two clauses at the end are complementary: punishment is not reduced, and no helper will rescue them from it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains that the punishment follows their persistent denial and disobedience; **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the force of “they shall not be helped.” **[Ṭabarī]** relates the verse to the correction of their claim that punishment would last only a few days, while **[Saʿdī]** returns to the false bargain itself. The verse does not describe a loss caused by lack of information: it portrays a preference for advantage despite a known covenant. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The word “price” is intentionally small beside the Hereafter. **[Ṭabarī]** says they accepted fleeting leadership and coarse provision in exchange for the faith that would have brought lasting reward. **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes that the trade concerns a real preference, not a literal purchase; **[Saʿdī]** likewise contrasts the little of this world with the great reward of the next. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Maʿārif]** keep the image tied to their deliberate refusal of God's command. The final negatives close off both imagined escape routes: their punishment is not reduced, and no ally can remove it. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:87

*"And indeed We gave Mūsā the Book, and We followed him with messengers. And We gave ʿĪsā son of Maryam the clear proofs and strengthened him with the Holy Spirit. Is it that whenever a messenger came to you with what your souls did not desire, you grew arrogant — some you denied and some you kill?"*

**Meaning.** The favours are recounted in a chain, and then the response, and the contrast is the whole verse **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**. **[Saʿdī]** frames it as a bounty being enumerated: God sent them Mūsā, His interlocutor, and gave him the Torah; then He followed him with messengers who judged by the Torah, closing their line of prophets with ʿĪsā, to whom He gave such clear demonstrations that human beings would believe on their like; then, despite these incalculable blessings, when a messenger came with something their souls disliked, they grew arrogant, denied some and killed some — preferring desire to guidance and this world to the next, in which there is a rebuke whose severity needs no explaining. **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes the order of the offence: the prophets commanded what went against Jewish desires and upheld the rulings of the Torah which the Jews had changed, and it was precisely this that made belief difficult for them — so that ʿĪsā, whose law differed in some particulars from the Torah, drew their envy, as he said, "and to make lawful for you part of what had been forbidden you" (3:50).

**Language.** *Qaffaynā* is from *al-qafā*, the back of the neck: to follow a man so closely that you are at his nape **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**; it means to send one after another. The messengers after Mūsā did not bring new religions: every prophet raised between Mūsā and ʿĪsā was sent to the Children of Israel commanding the establishment of the Torah and work according to it, and ʿĪsā followed the same line until God sent him with what He sent **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. *Huwā* is the soul's inclination toward a thing; it is called *hawā* because it hurls its owner down (*yahwī*) toward the Fire, which is why it is generally used only of what is not true and holds no good — this verse among such uses — though it may be used of what is good, as ʿUmar's report of the captives of Badr: "the Messenger of God ﷺ inclined to what Abū Bakr said, and did not incline to what I said," and ʿĀʾishah's words to the Prophet ﷺ, "By God, I see that your Lord hastens to fulfil your desire" **[Qurṭubī]**. *Istaḥbartum* is *istikbār*: to disdain answering, holding the messengers contemptible and the very idea of their mission remote **[Qurṭubī]**. And "some you kill" is present where it means past — as **[Jalālayn]** puts it, the tense is used to narrate what has happened as though it were happening now, and it is the more damning for it: the killing is being described as their habit.

**The Holy Spirit.** **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** gather the views: "the Holy Spirit" is Jibrīl — the view of Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn ʿAbbās, Muḥammad b. Kaʿb, al-Suddī, al-Rabīʿ b. Anas, Qatāda and others, and the preponderant (ẓāhir) reading of the phrase — so that God strengthened ʿĪsā with the angel who accompanied him wherever he went; al-Naḥḥās explained the naming by God's bringing Jibrīl into being a spirit without the birth of a father, which is also why ʿĪsā himself is called spirit. Others held it was the name by which ʿĪsā revived the dead, i.e. the Greatest Name of God (al-Ḍaḥḥāk, Saʿīd b. Jubayr, ʿUbayd b. ʿUmayr); al-Ḥasan said *al-qudus* is God Himself and His spirit is Jibrīl; and some said the Gospel is meant, called a spirit as the Qurʾān is called one — "and thus We have revealed to you a spirit from Our command" (42:52). **[Study Quran]** notes that these interpretations share the sense of giving life, whether bodily quickening or the nourishment of heart and intelligence, and that *qudus* is defined as blessing or purity, so that some read the phrase as "the spirit of the Holy One," identifying it with the spirit breathed into Ādam (15:29). **[Maʿārif]** then enumerates how Jibrīl supported ʿĪsā concretely: he was conceived by the angel's breath; he was protected from Satan's touch at birth; the angel accompanied him to defend him against the hostility of the Jews; and through him he was raised to the heavens. **[Ibn Kathīr]** adduces the lexicon's own confirmation: "the trustworthy spirit brought it down upon your heart" (26:193-4).

**āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** records from al-Bukhārī that ʿĀʾishah said the Messenger of God ﷺ set up a pulpit in the mosque upon which Ḥassān b. Thābit would defend him with his poetry, and the Prophet ﷺ said, "O God, aid Ḥassān with the Holy Spirit as he defends Your Prophet." **[Qurṭubī]** cites Ḥassān's line: "And Jibrīl, the Messenger of God, is among us, and the Holy Spirit — of him there is no concealment." On the naming of those denied and killed, **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Jalālayn]** agree in substance: among those denied, ʿĪsā and Muḥammad ﷺ; among those killed, Yaḥyā and Zakariyyā. **[Study Quran]** observes that on this outward address to the Jews the Sufis built an inward address to the Muslims: God raised up a succession of saints and sages to show right from wrong and guide to the straight path, and they too are called liars or opposed by people ruled by their passions — and it cites the Prophet's ﷺ saying, "You will follow the ways of those before you, span by span, cubit by cubit, so much so that if they entered a lizard's hole, you would enter it."

**Reflection.** The verse is a picture of what refusal looks like from the inside. It is not argued out; it is *hawā* meeting a messenger, and pride resolving the collision. Notice how **[Saʿdī]** reads the sequence: the bounty first, then the reaction — the more lavish the guidance, the more exposed the disdain becomes. And the tense of the last clause is the verse's true judgement: written as present, the crime of the ancestors reads as a living habit, available to every generation that finds God's command unsuited to its taste.

The verse recalls a chain of guidance: the Book given to Mūsā, later messengers who followed him, and the signs given to ʿĪsā, strengthened by the Holy Spirit. **[Ṭabarī]** explains *qaffaynā* as sending messengers one after another on the same path and law; **[Ibn Kathīr]** identifies the Book as the Torah and the Holy Spirit with Gabriel in the transmitted explanations. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Jalālayn]** discuss the clear proofs given to Jesus. **[Saʿdī]** sets these gifts against the response at the end of the verse: whenever a messenger brought what their desires opposed, they became arrogant, denied some, and killed others. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** The rhetorical question gathers a repeated history into one pattern, not a claim that every individual acted identically. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:88

*"And they say, 'Our hearts are encased.' No — God has cursed them for their disbelief; little do they believe."*

**Meaning.** The verse catches the claim and turns it inside out **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Saʿdī]** reads it as an excuse offered when the Prophet ﷺ called them: their hearts are wrapped and covered so that they do not understand what he says — as if they had an excuse for not knowing. But it is a lie, and so: "No — God has cursed them for their disbelief": they are expelled, accursed, because of their unbelief; few of them believe, or their faith is little, while their disbelief is much. **[Maʿārif]** hears the sarcasm in the claim: they congratulated themselves on being unshakeable in their religion, and the Qurʾān answers that this is not firmness of faith but a damnation — they deny a religion that is now true and cling to one that has been abrogated. *Bal* is the rebuttal, and what follows it is the diagnosis **[Jalālayn]** **[Qurṭubī]**: no defect in the organ of hearing, but a curse incurred by their unbelief — which is how Qurṭubī reads the sequence, God showing that the cause of their aversion to faith is that they were accursed for their earlier unbelief and temerity: the punishment for a sin being something greater than the sin itself.

**Language.** *Ghulf* is the plural of *aghlaf*: a thing enclosed in a covering, as a sword is *aghlaf* in its scabbard, or a man uncircumcised; *ghilāf* gives the plural *ghuluf* — thus the second reading, with the *lām* vowelled **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Ibn ʿAbbās, on that reading: "our hearts are vessels full of knowledge and have no need of the knowledge of Muḥammad ﷺ or anyone else"; hence also "our hearts are vessels for knowledge, so why do they not understand you, when we have gathered much knowledge?" — which is the claim the Qurʾān rejects most sharply **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Others glossed it with Mujāhid's "a covering over them," ʿIkrimah's "a seal upon them," Abū al-ʿĀliyyah's "they do not comprehend," and it stands beside the like of "our hearts are within coverings from that to which you call us" (41:5) **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]**. *Al-laʿn* in the Arabs' speech is expulsion and removal — a wolf thrown out is *laʿīn*; al-Shammākh's line compares the ambush-post of the outcast wolf to the outcast man — so "God has cursed them" means He has removed them from His mercy, or from His enabling guidance, or from every good, and that last is the most general **[Qurṭubī]**. On "little do they believe," **[Ṭabarī]** rules on the grammar against an opinion of Qatāda's: *fa-qalīlan* is accusative because it qualifies an omitted verbal noun — *fa-īmānan qalīlan mā yuʾminūn* — whereas if the sense were "only a few of them believe," the word would have to be nominative, since the *mā* would need a subject to raise. He likewise refuses to make the *mā* a meaningless filler, since adding what carries no meaning to God's speech is not permissible; the *mā* gathers, then the following words specify. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the reports: Maʿmar's reading — they believe only a little of what is in their hands and reject most of it; al-Wāqidī's — they do not believe a little or a great deal, as one says "how seldom he does such a thing" meaning he never does it at all; and al-Kisāʾī's parallel from the Arabs: "we passed through a land that hardly grows leeks and onions" — meaning it grows nothing.

**āthār & belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** records from Ḥudhayfah a description of the hearts: four in number, among them a heart wrapped and bound — and that is the heart of the disbeliever; the editor notes the report is stopped at Ḥudhayfah with a broken chain, while its sense comes through Aḥmad by way of Abū Saʿīd with a sound chain, *marfūʿ*. **[Maʿārif]** clarifies what "little faith" can mean: it was the faith in doctrines common to Islam and Judaism — belief in God, belief in the Day of Judgement — while they refused Muḥammad ﷺ and the Qurʾān; and though the verse uses the word *īmān* of it in the lexical sense (for *īmān* is total certitude, even if it happens to cover some things and not others), the Sharīʿah recognizes as valid only the faith that affirms with certitude everything the Sharīʿah requires one to affirm. **[Study Quran]** stays with the harder question the phrase raises: if the organ of faith is covered, is its owner responsible? It notes that in 4:155 the identical claim is answered not with a bare denial but with "God has set a seal upon them for their disbelief," so that the dismissal of the excuse and the affirmation of sealing stand side by side — and reasons that a heart genuinely sealed could not see that it is sealed; a soul that laments its sealing is not fully sealed, since one cannot be aware of, or believe in, God unless God allows it. The same sentence, "my heart is covered," is one thing on the lips of a cynic and quite another in the mouth of a person longing for God and dismayed by the distance.

**Reflection.** The claim in this verse is a permanent human move: to turn an incapacity into an identity, to be proud of the very thing that shuts one out. **[Qurṭubī]**'s note that the curse for unbelief yields a greater curse is the most disturbing line in the passage: hardness of heart can become its own punishment, and the covering they joked about becomes the covering God confirms. Against that, **[Study Quran]**'s observation is the door left open — the person who knows his heart is closed and grieves over it has already disproved the diagnosis, for the spiritually dead do not mourn their own death.

“Our hearts are encased” is presented as an excuse for not receiving the Prophet's message. **[Saʿdī]** reads it as a claim that their hearts were covered and could not understand; the answer, *bal*—“rather”—rejects that excuse and attributes their condition to disbelief. **[Ṭabarī]** discusses two readings of *ghulf*: covered hearts, or hearts imagined to be full of knowledge. **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Qurṭubī]** preserve these interpretations, while **[Jalālayn]** gives the direct sense of a barrier. **[Maʿārif]** hears irony in their supposed firmness: what they describe as being secure in faith is answered as a consequence of rejecting truth. **[Study Quran]** notes the same distinction between an asserted incapacity and a moral refusal. “Little do they believe” may refer to the small number who believe or to the meagreness of their belief in relation to their denial. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]** The verse shifts responsibility back to the hearer: a closed heart is not established by self-description alone. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:89

*"And when there came to them a Book from God, confirming what was with them — and before that they used to pray for victory over those who disbelieved — yet when there came to them what they recognized, they disbelieved in it. So the curse of God be upon the disbelievers!"*

**Meaning.** The Book is the Qurʾān, and its confirmation runs to what they already held: it verifies the Torah and the Gospel, telling of what is in them **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Ibn Kathīr]** place the sting in the middle clause: these same people, before the mission, used to *seek victory* through Muḥammad ﷺ — that is, to ask God for help against the polytheists of the Arabs, saying to them, "A prophet is about to be sent, whose time has drawn near; we will kill you with him as ʿĀd and Iram were killed." The victory they asked for arrived, and it was not the victory they wanted. **[Jalālayn]** adds the motive plainly: they disbelieved in what they recognized out of envy and for fear of losing leadership.

**Occasion & context.** The most detailed report in **[Ṭabarī]** comes from the Anṣār themselves: "By God, this story came down concerning us and them" — the Jews who were their neighbours saying, while the Anṣār were still idolaters and the Jews a people of the Book, that a prophet whose time had come would destroy them as ʿĀd and Iram had been destroyed. When God raised His Messenger from Quraysh and they followed him, the Jews disbelieved. In Ibn ʿAbbās's report **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**, Muʿādh b. Jabal and Bishr b. al-Barāʾ said to them, "O company of Jews, fear God and become Muslim: you used to seek victory through Muḥammad ﷺ while we were idolaters, telling us he was sent and describing him to us by his description" — and Salām b. Mishkam of Banū al-Naḍīr answered, "He has not brought us anything we recognize; he is not the one we used to mention to you." **[Qurṭubī]** gives Ibn ʿAbbās's account of the practice itself: the Jews of Khaybar fought Ghaṭafān, and when they met in battle they were routed; so they turned to this supplication — "we ask You by the right of the ummī prophet whom You promised to bring out in the last times, that You grant us victory over them" — and they won; but when the Prophet ﷺ was sent, they disbelieved, and God revealed that they used to seek victory *through you, O Muḥammad*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** preserves Abū al-ʿĀliyyah's version of the motive: they saw that he was not one of them, and they envied the Arabs though they knew he was the Messenger of God.

**Language.** *Al-istiftāḥ* is *al-istinṣār*: to seek help, from the sense of an opening, since victory opens what was closed — as in "perhaps God will bring the opening (*al-fatḥ*) or a command from Himself" (5:52) **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** notes the idiom from the ḥadīth that the Prophet ﷺ used to seek opening "by the poor of the emigrants" — that is, he sought victory through their supplication and their prayer. He also records the grammar of the long sentence: al-Farrāʾ held that the answer to the first *lammā* is the *fa* clause — *fa-lammā jāʾahum mā ʿarafū* — and the answer to the second is *kafarū bih*; al-Akhfash and al-Zajjāj held the first answer is suppressed because the hearer already knows it; al-Mubarrad held the first answer is *kafarū* and the second *lammā* was repeated only because the sentence had grown long — which has the rhetorical effect of confirming their guilt and doubling it. And in the reading recorded from Ubayy's codex, *muṣaddiqan* stands in the accusative, as a circumstantial clause rather than an adjective, which the Qurʾānic text leaves in the nominative **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Belief — knowledge is not faith.** **[Maʿārif]** asks the question a reader of this verse cannot avoid: why does the Qurʾān call them unbelievers when they recognized the truth as truth, which should make them believers? The answer is that *īmān* is not merely knowing the truth but accepting it and affirming it in deed — otherwise Satan, who knows the truth perfectly well, would have to be called a believer; and indeed his knowledge only intensifies the gravity of his unbelief. **[Saʿdī]** reads the passage with the previous ones: they knew, they were certain, and they disbelieved out of envy and denial that God should give His bounty to whom He wills; so God cursed them and was angry with them with anger upon anger for the abundance of their unbelief — and their portion in the next life is a humiliating punishment, painful and stinging: the burning of the Fire and the loss of the enduring bliss. **[Study Quran]** preserves the same fact from two angles: the Jews recited in their prayer "O God, send this prophet whom we find written of in our book, so we may punish the idolaters and slay them," and rejected him when he was not of the Israelites; some commentators hold that "what they recognized" means not a detailed description he matched, but the general marks of prophethood and truth that he displayed.

**āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** brings two reports on seeking help through the weak — a fitting comment on a verse about people who asked for victory and were given one they resented. From Saʿd, in al-Nasāʾī: the Prophet ﷺ said, "God aids this community only through its weak, by their supplication, their prayer and their sincerity." And from Abū al-Dardāʾ: "Seek out the weak for me, for you are provided for and given victory only through your weak ones."

**Reflection.** The verse is about a request that was answered. The Jews of Madīnah had asked God for a deliverer against their enemies, and the deliverer came and stood among them with a Book that confirmed their own, and they could not bear that he was not theirs. There is no more searching episode in the Sūrah, because the sin is not ignorance — the Qurʾān is emphatic that they *recognized* him — but the refusal to be saved by someone else's prophet. And the verse's final word is not argument but abandonment: the curse of God upon those who, having seen, reject.

The confirmation of earlier scripture makes the response especially pointed. **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the Book as the Qur'an, confirming the Torah and Gospel; **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Qurṭubī]** connect that confirmation with the descriptions of the coming Messenger. **[Study Quran]** notes the reports that the Jewish tribes had previously asked for help against the idolaters through the prophet they expected. **[Saʿdī]** says the promised help arrived, but they rejected the Messenger when he came. **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]** The phrase “what they recognized” is central: **[Ṭabarī]** explains that they knew the truth of the message, while **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports that some rejected it because the Prophet ﷺ was not from the Children of Israel. **[Qurṭubī]** collects related accounts but the verse itself states the recognition and denial plainly. **[Study Quran]** The closing prayer of condemnation is directed at those who persist in that rejection, not at a lineage as such. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**

## 2:90

*"Evil is that for which they sold their souls — that they should disbelieve in what God sent down, grudging that God should send down of His grace upon whom He wills of His servants. So they brought on themselves wrath upon wrath; and for the disbelievers there is a humiliating punishment."*

**Meaning.** The verse names the transaction and then the motive **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. *Biʾsa mā* is blame in its most complete form, as *niʿma* is the most complete praise; the language has four ways of pronouncing each — *biʾsa, baʾsa, baʾisa, biʾisa*, and *niʿma, naʿma, naʿima, niʿima* **[Qurṭubī]**. The thing blamed here is what they chose for themselves in place of belief: **[Ibn Kathīr]** records Mujāhid's summary — they sold truth for falsehood and hid the truth about Muḥammad ﷺ — and al-Suddī's blunt reading, "they sold themselves." The clause that follows is both the content of the sale and its motive: they disbelieved in what God sent down *out of insolent envy* (*baghyan*) that God should send down of His grace on whom He wills of His servants. **[Jalālayn]** notes that the accusation itself is the pivot: envy that the revelation should go to someone other than them. **[Study Quran]** puts it in the terms of the day: the belief that prophecy should belong only to the Children of Israel, so that when that dignity passed to the Arabs, they purchased the false comfort of tribal pride at the price of faith in the Prophet ﷺ — the attitude, it notes, in which *kufr* recovers its root sense of covering over a blessing one has received, the same charge as the *ghulf* of 2:88.

**Context — the ground of the envy.** **[Ṭabarī]** collects the reports: Qatāda, "these are the Jews; when God raised His prophet Muḥammad ﷺ and they saw that he was sent from other than them, they disbelieved in him, envying the Arabs, though they knew he was the Messenger of God, finding him written in the Torah"; and al-Suddī preserves the sentence they actually used: "the messengers were only from the Children of Israel, so what is this one from the Children of Ismāʿīl?" Ibn Isḥāq's report has *baghyan an yunzila Allāhu min faḍlihi ʿalā man yashāʾu min ʿibādihi* explained: that God placed it among others than them — and Ibn Isḥāq adds, in Abū Mālik's report, that they said, "Had it been a matter of merit, we would have had a stronger claim to it than anyone." **[Ṭabarī]** sets the whole scene beside 4:51-54, where the same people are described as envying *the people* for what God has given them of His bounty — and where the answer is that God granted the family of Ibrāhīm the Book, the wisdom and a mighty kingdom, so that His bounty is His own to place.

**Wrath upon wrath.** *Bāʾū* means they returned with it, and deserved it **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**: they came back from their boasting that they would fight the polytheists with the coming prophet, carrying anger piled on anger. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the doubling as their rejection of the prophet whom they had promised the nations, added to all that preceded it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports the interpretations: Ibn ʿAbbās — God became angry with them because they neglected part of the Torah and disbelieved in the Prophet He sent to them; Abū al-ʿĀliyyah, ʿIkrimah and Qatāda — one anger for their disbelief in the Gospel and ʿĪsā, and another for their disbelief in Muḥammad ﷺ and the Qurʾān. **[Study Quran]** catalogues the causes the exegetes assign: the calf, the failure to uphold parts of the Torah, the rejection of ʿĪsā, the rejection of Muḥammad — and renders *bāʾū* as "they became deserving," noting that *ʿibādihi*, God's servants, may mean all who worship Him, and even all creation.

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** draws the doctrinal point sharply: prophethood cannot be earned through one's own efforts or merits; it is a special grace that God bestows on whom He wills — and by denying the Prophet ﷺ out of spite, the Jews were not merely rejecting a man but questioning God's own will. Hence the twofold wrath: one for denying a true prophet, and one for the envy and malice that caused the denial. He then makes a distinction that the verse's last clause requires: the punishment is described as humiliating because humiliation is peculiar to unbelief — it is not the manner in which a Muslim is punished, whatever the greatness of his sins; the Muslim's punishment is for purification, not for disgrace. **[Ibn Kathīr]** grounds the point in a ḥadīth of Aḥmad from ʿAmr b. Shuʿayb, from his father, from his grandfather: "The arrogant will be gathered on the Day of Resurrection in the size of ants, in the form of men; everything will be above them because of the humiliation placed upon them, until they enter a prison in Hell called Bawlas, where the fire of fires covers them from above, and they are given to drink from the mire of the people of the Fire" — the *istiḥsāb* against 40:60, "those who scorn My worship shall enter Hell, humbled."

**Reflection.** The verse is a lesson in what envy costs. The Jews of Madīnah did not lose prophethood — it was never theirs to hold; they lost their own share in it by refusing a prophet who confirmed their book. **[Ṭabarī]**'s report of their own words — "the messengers were only from the Children of Israel, so what is this one from the Children of Ismāʿīl?" — is the whole tragedy in one sentence: a family quarrel over a gift that was never a family inheritance. God's grace is not a thing a community can hold under lock; the anger upon anger is the compounding interest of pretending otherwise.

The motive is named directly: envy that revelation had come to a servant whom God chose. **[Ṭabarī]** explains *baghyan* as transgression arising from envy; **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports that they resented the Qur'an's descent upon Muḥammad ﷺ rather than upon someone from their own people. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Study Quran]** relate the verse to the preceding account of a Messenger they had expected but then rejected. **[Maʿārif]** stresses that divine grace is God's to bestow as He wills; **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** The words “they sold themselves” recall the bargain of 2:86, but here the cause is made explicit: resentment led them to refuse what God sent down. **[Ṭabarī]** explains “wrath upon wrath” through successive grounds of anger, and **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects it with their earlier disbelief and their rejection of the Qur'an. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Saʿdī]** The passage diagnoses envy as a spiritual danger: it can make a person resent another's blessing until he rejects the truth attached to it. **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:91

*"And when it is said to them, 'Believe in what God has sent down,' they say, 'We believe in what was sent down to us' — and they disbelieve in what is beyond it, though it is the truth, confirming what is with them. Say: 'Why then did you slay the prophets of God aforetime, if you were believers?'"*

**Meaning.** The exchange is reported and then broken open **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**. Their answer is not a profession but a partition: revelation has been filed by recipient, and they recognize only the file with their name on it. **Saʿdī** draws the principle out: the duty is to believe in what God sent down — absolutely, whether He sent it to them or to others; and faith that benefits is faith in what God sent down to all His messengers. To separate the messengers and the Books, professing one and rejecting another, is not faith at all but unbelief itself, and he cites 4:150-151: "those who disbelieve in God and His messengers and wish to divide between God and His messengers, saying, 'We believe in some and disbelieve in others,' wishing to take a path in between — those are truly the disbelievers."

**The argument.** Both **[Saʿdī]** and **[Maʿārif]** show why the claim collapses, and their reasoning is worth setting out as the verse's own logic. First, the Book they refuse is *the truth* — true in all its reports, commands and prohibitions, and sent from their own Lord; to disbelieve in it is to disbelieve in God and in the truth He sent. Second, it *confirms what is with them*: it agrees with the Torah in everything of truth it indicates, and stands as a guardian over it. Why then believe the one and deny its like — unless it is partisanship and following desire rather than guidance? And here **[Saʿdī]** presses with an image: for the Qurʾān to confirm what is in their hands means it is itself the evidence that what they hold is true; theirs is the position of a man who stakes a claim supported by a single witness, and then turns on his own witness and discredits him. To reject the Qurʾān is therefore to reject what is in their own hands. **[Maʿārif]** puts the same three points in a row — the other Books are established by irrefutable proofs, so what does a flat refusal mean? The other Books confirm the Torah, so denying them denies it. And to slay a prophet is unbelief by the standard of every divine Book, and the prophets they killed were themselves Israelites teaching the Torah; they not only killed them but took the killers as their chiefs. Nothing they say survives any of the three.

**Language.** *Warāʾ* means what is beyond — "other than it," per al-Farrāʾ, or "after it," per Qatāda and Abū ʿUbaydah, the two senses coming to the same thing **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that *warāʾ* is among the Arabic words that carry opposite senses: it means behind, and it can mean in front, as in "and behind them (*warāʾahum*) was a king" (18:79) — meaning before them. Here it is an adverb of place in the accusative. On "why then did you slay the prophets of God aforetime," **[Qurṭubī]** gives the tense-logic: the address is to those standing before Muḥammad ﷺ while the meaning is their forefathers, and it reaches the children either because they took as allies those who did the killing — "had they believed in God and the Prophet and what was sent down to him, they would not have taken them as allies" (5:81) — or because they were pleased with the deed, and the deed is therefore attributed to them; the present tense is used of a past act because the phrase "aforetime" (*min qabl*) removes any ambiguity. He compares al-Ḥuṭayʾah's line, "al-Ḥuṭayʾah bears witness on the day he meets his Lord," where "bears witness" means "will bear witness." **[Ṭabarī]** reports the Baṣran analysis to the same effect, and adds that God is answering their claim by their own history: had they truly believed in what was sent down to them, they would not have killed the prophets the Torah commanded them to follow and obey.

**Belief.** **[Study Quran]** notes that the question is a rebuke precisely because those slain prophets were Israelites who commanded their people to follow the Torah: that they slew even them proves their refusal of the Prophet ﷺ sprang from something other than faith in the Torah, since those prophets likewise confirmed what was with them and their murder was forbidden in any case. **[Ibn Kathīr]** states the conclusion in the same form: if their claim to believe in what was revealed to them were true, why kill the prophets who came affirming the law of the Torah, whom they knew to be true? They killed them out of transgression, stubbornness and injustice toward God's messengers — following their own desires, opinions and caprice. And their last condition falsifies the claim by itself: the verse ends "if you were believers," and the answer to "if" is written into their record.

**Reflection.** This verse is the anatomy of a hedge. The Jews of Madīnah did not deny revelation; they rationed it — one Book for us, the rest for others — and the Qurʾān calls that rationing unbelief and then proves it from their own history, not from ours. A community that kills its own prophets while professing its own Book has already told you what its profession is worth. **[Ṭabarī]**'s point that a people can inherit a crime by approving it, or by allying with those who committed it, is the verse's quiet sting for every later reader: ancestry is not adjudicated by genealogy but by whom you stand with, and what you are pleased to see done.

The answer “we believe in what was sent down to us” is measured against their own history. **[Saʿdī]** says beneficial faith accepts everything God revealed, whether to one's own community or another; dividing the messengers and books is not genuine faith. **[Ibn Kathīr]** makes the same point through the prophets who came affirming the Torah and were nevertheless killed. **[Study Quran]** notes that *mā warāʾahu* can mean what came after the Torah or what is other than it. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** connect the Prophet's question to that contradiction; **[Jalālayn]** notes that the killing is attributed to the ancestors while the rebuke addresses their approving descendants. The Qur'an's confirmation of earlier revelation is therefore not an excuse to reject later guidance. **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** The test is consistency: if they truly followed the Torah, why did they reject the prophets who called them to it? **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:92

*"And indeed Mūsā came to you with the clear proofs; then you took the calf after him — and you were wrongdoers."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the proofs as the wonders worked at Mūsā's hands that demonstrated his truthfulness and the soundness of his prophethood: the staff that became a manifest serpent, the hand drawn out white to the onlookers, the sea split and its floor made a dry road, the locusts, the lice, the frogs and the rest. They are called *bayyināt* because they showed everyone who looked that they were miraculous, beyond any human power except by God's enabling. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the tally from 17:101, "We gave Mūsā nine clear signs": the staff, the years of drought, the hand, the blood, the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, and the splitting of the sea — while others said the *bayyināt* here means the Torah itself and the proofs contained in it. Then the sentence turns: *then you took the calf* — **[Qurṭubī]** notes that *thumma* is more emphatic than *wāw* for castigation, since it marks a gap of deliberation after the signs were given; and the calf was taken, in **[Ibn Kathīr]**'s phrase, while he was away — after Mūsā had gone up Mount Ṭūr to speak with his Lord, exactly as 7:148 describes: "and the people of Mūsā made, in his absence, out of their ornaments the image of a calf, with a lowing sound."

**Language.** The pronoun in *min baʿdihi* — "after him" — may refer to Mūsā himself, since they took the calf after he left them for his appointment, or to the coming of the clear proofs: "then you took the calf after the proofs had come" — as one says, "you came to me and I hated it," meaning I hated your coming **[Ṭabarī]**. And *ẓālimūn* is not a general verdict dropped into the verse; **[Ṭabarī]** spells out the charge: you did what was not yours to do, and worshipped other than the One you should have worshipped — worship being owed to God alone. The reproof is built on what they knew: they took as a god a calf that can bring them neither harm nor benefit, after seeing their Lord's wonders performed through Mūsā, things no creature could do — not Pharaoh with all his power and hordes — and this while the memory of those wonders was still fresh. The argument then steps into the present: if they did that so soon after the signs, how much swifter would they be to deny Muḥammad ﷺ, and to reject what their own books they claimed to believe in say about his description and attributes, across the far longer stretch since Mūsā's time.

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** states the charge as the mirror of their claim: these are people who profess faith while falling into *shirk* — which is a denial not merely of Mūsā but of God Himself. He then handles the difficulty that their contemporaries at Madīnah never worshiped a calf: they did not take part themselves, but they honoured those of their forefathers who had, and took their side — so that for all practical purposes they share the sin. And he draws the lesson the verse yields for the Prophet's ﷺ own audience: there is nothing surprising in a people whose fathers denied Mūsā now denying Muḥammad ﷺ. **[Ibn Kathīr]** closes the narrative with the sequel from Sūrat al-Aʿrāf: when it fell into their hands and they saw that they had gone astray, they said, "If our Lord does not have mercy upon us and forgive us, we shall surely be among the losers" (7:149) — the repentance that followed the calf, and the measure of what they had done.

**Reflection.** The verse is a chronology of forgetting. Signs are not the cure for a heart set on something else: the same people who watched the sea divide made a god out of jewellery within weeks. **[Ṭabarī]**'s observation is the sharp edge — the shorter the time since the miracle, the more damning the lapse; and the longer the time since Muḥammad ﷺ, the more room for the same excuse to sound reasonable. That is why the verse addresses the descendants as though they had done it themselves: the pattern, not the date, is what is being exposed.

The “clear proofs” are plural and cumulative. **[Ibn Kathīr]** lists the staff, the radiant hand, the sea, the plagues, and the wilderness provisions among Moses' signs; **[Qurṭubī]** relates the count of nine signs in 17:101. **[Ṭabarī]** calls them manifest proofs of Moses' prophethood, and **[Jalālayn]** names the staff, hand, and parting of the sea. **[Study Quran]** points readers to the longer account in 7:148–155. Yet after Moses left, they adopted the calf, and the verse calls that injustice rather than a shortage of evidence. **[Saʿdī]** says they had no excuse; **[Maʿārif]** connects the episode to the earlier reminder in 2:51. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The placement makes the contrast with 2:91 immediate: a claim to believe in revelation is followed by a reminder that clear signs had already reached them. **[Ṭabarī]** interprets “after him” as after Mūsā's departure to the appointment, and **[Jalālayn]** gives the same sequence. **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes that the calf episode is recounted elsewhere in fuller detail, while **[Study Quran]** points to 7:148–155 for that narrative. **[Saʿdī]** stresses that the wrongdoing had no excuse in the face of such proofs. The verse is therefore not simply a list of miracles: it asks what the people did when the messenger was absent from their sight. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:93

*"And when We took your covenant and raised the Mount above you: 'Take what We have given you with strength, and listen!' They said, 'We hear, and we disobey.' And they were made to drink the calf into their hearts because of their disbelief. Say: 'Evil is that which your faith commands you, if you are believers.'"*

**Meaning.** The covenant of 2:63 returns, now with its response attached, and the response is the verse's indictment **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**. "Take what We have given you with strength" — the Torah, with resolution and energy, working by what is in it and abstaining from what it forbids **[Ṭabarī]**; and "listen" does not mean merely to hear the words: it means to obey them and commit to them — as the Arabs say "God hears the one who praises Him," that is, He accepts and answers, and as the old couplet runs, "hearing and obedience and submission are better and more cleansing for the sons of Tamīm" **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. Then the answer, which is the whole tragedy in four words: *samīʿnā wa-ʿaṣaynā*.

**Language.** "We hear and we disobey" **[Qurṭubī]** raises the question whether they actually said it or whether their conduct said it for them, an action standing in place of speech — as the poet's pool, filled to the brim, "says: enough, gently — you have filled my belly." **[Jalālayn]** reads it simply: we hear your words and disobey your command. **[Maʿārif]** reconciles the two: out of sheer fright they affirmed with their tongues that they had heard and accepted the command, but there was no consent in their hearts, and their posture and attitude were as though they were saying they would not or could not obey. On *wa-ushribū fī qulūbihimu al-ʿijl* — "they were made to drink the calf into their hearts" — **[Ṭabarī]** reports two lines of interpretation: the majority — Qatāda, Abū al-ʿĀliyyah, al-Rabīʿ — take it as the love of the calf, drunk until it reached their hearts; and al-Suddī's narrative: when Mūsā returned, he slaughtered the calf, filed it down, scattered it in the sea, then told them to drink, and whoever loved it found the filings of gold upon his lips. **[Qurṭubī]** weighs the two: the scattering in the sea is confirmed by 20:97, "then We shall scatter it in the sea with a scattering," but the drinking of the water and the gold upon the lips is contradicted by the verse's own words — it says the calf was made to drink *into their hearts*, not into their stomachs — and God knows best; al-Qushayrī adds that none drank of it without going mad. He also explains the choice of drinking over eating: water permeates the limbs until it reaches their inward parts, while food merely lies alongside them; hence the metaphor for a love that soaks all the way in. And he supports it with a ḥadīth of Muslim: "Trials are presented to the hearts as a mat is woven, stick by stick; whichever heart drinks them in is marked with a black spot, and whichever heart rejects them is marked with a white spot; thus two hearts result: a white heart, which no trial harms as long as the heavens and earth endure, and a black, ashen heart" — the owner of which does not recognize right or deplore wrong except as his passions make him drink. **[Study Quran]** cites the same ḥadīth and reads the drinking as the love of the calf, the heart's orientation satiated by worldly desire, a soul filled with the world and emptied of guidance.

**The reductio.** "Say: evil is that which your faith commands you, if you are believers" **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**. **[Ṭabarī]** spells out the argument: if the faith you claim — your acceptance of the Book God sent down to you — commands you to kill God's prophets, to deny His Books and to reject what comes from Him, then evil indeed is the thing it commands; and God thereby clears the Torah of any such command and informs them that what is really commanding them is their own desire, and what carries them along is insolence and aggression. **[Jalālayn]** states the trap in full: if you are believers in the Torah as you claim, then you are not — for faith does not command the worship of the calf; and in the same way you do not believe in the Torah, because you have denied the prophethood of Muḥammad ﷺ, which faith in the Torah does not command you to reject. **[Ibn Kathīr]** adds the capstone: how can you claim faith while committing the evil of breaking God's covenant, disbelieving His signs and worshipping the calf instead of God — and, worst of all, disbelieving in the final Messenger sent to all mankind?

**Context.** **[Maʿārif]** supplies the moral psychology that makes the sequence intelligible. When the Israelites, delivered from Egypt and across the sea, asked Mūsā for a visible god like the idols of a people they passed, they realized their error and repented — but repentance has degrees, and theirs was not of a high order; the darkness of that error did not leave their hearts but continued to grow until it emerged as the worship of the calf. Some paid with their lives in penance, others were forgiven; and even those who had not worshiped the calf did not hate the deed as much as they should have, so their hearts too carried a trace of *shirk*. Whether from the weakness of their repentance or from a failure to loathe unbelief properly, their hearts grew indolent in religion — until God required them to pledge themselves to the Torah, and the Mount was held over their heads to threaten them.

**Reflection.** The verse is a study in the difference between a tongue and a heart. The people who "heard and disobeyed" were not ignorant of what was demanded; the mountain was over their heads. What they had was a religion of the tongue, held in place by threat, while the heart drank something else — and the Qurʾān's choice of verb is exact: what the heart drinks, it drinks in, all the way down. Then comes the question that ends the passage, and it is aimed not only at them: if your faith is commanding you to do that, then it is not faith. A claim to belief is measured by what it makes you do, and the verse offers the simplest test available: a faith that commands the worship of a calf is not a faith, and a faith that forbids a prophet cannot be faith in the Book that announced him.

The command “listen” is glossed as obedient acceptance, not merely hearing sound. **[Saʿdī]** explains it as receiving the command and responding; **[Ibn Kathīr]** reads the answer “we heard and disobeyed” as an admission of rebellion after the covenant. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** discuss the raised Mount and the seriousness of the pledge, while **[Jalālayn]** makes the contrast explicit in its concise paraphrase. The image that they were made to drink the calf is explained as love of the calf filling their hearts. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]** This is why the final rebuke is ironic: **[Saʿdī]** asks what kind of faith commands idolatry and rejection of messengers; **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** connect the question to their boast of belief in the Torah. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** The verse exposes a gap between words spoken under pressure and loyalties shown in action. **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:94

*"Say: 'If the Abode of the Hereafter with God is yours alone, to the exclusion of all other people, then long for death — if you are truthful.'"*

**Meaning.** The claim of exclusive salvation is put to a test that costs nothing but a sentence **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Ṭabarī]** introduces it as one of God's own proofs for His Prophet ﷺ against the Jews, exposing their rabbis and scholars: as God had summoned the Christians who disputed about ʿĪsā to a mutual invocation of curse (3:61), so He said to the Jewish party — if you are in the right, then wish for death; it cannot harm you if your claim of faith and nearness to God is true. Rather, if you are granted what you wish, you pass to rest from the world's weariness and its turbid living, and to the neighbourhood of God in His gardens — if the matter is as you claim: that the Abode of the Hereafter is yours alone to the exclusion of us. And if you are not granted it, the people will know that you are the ones in the wrong and we are the ones who are right, and the whole matter will have been made clear.

**Language.** **[Jalālayn]** notes the structure: the two conditions attach to the one verb — if you speak truly in claiming that it is yours, you will naturally incline to what belongs to you; and the road to it is death; so long for death. **[Qurṭubī]** adds why the test is fair: whoever believes himself to be of the people of the Garden finds death more desirable than life, because of the bliss he is moving to and the harm of the world he is leaving behind. He cites the claims the Qurʾān has already recorded — "the Fire will not touch us but for numbered days" (2:80), "none shall enter the Garden except one who is a Jew or a Christian" (2:111), "we are the sons of God and His beloved" (5:18) — and notes that Ibn ʿAbbās, in ʿIkrimah's report, understood *fa-tamannaw al-mawt* as: invoke death upon whichever of the two parties — ours or yours — is the more lying. And he answers a natural objection: a wish can be of the tongue or of the heart, so how can we know they did not wish it in their hearts? Because God has said so — *"they will never long for it, ever"* — and had they wished it in their hearts, they would have shown it on their tongues to refute the Prophet ﷺ and nullify his argument; it would have been the cheapest of victories, and they were not silent because they were patient.

**āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** records through Ibn ʿAbbās a report attributed to the Prophet ﷺ, its chain graded sound by the editor: "Had the Jews wished for death, they would have died and seen their seats in the Fire; and had those who invoke curses upon the Messenger of God ﷺ come forward, they would have returned finding neither family nor property." Ibn ʿAbbās is also reported (al-Aʿmash's chain, broken) as saying: had they wished for death, one of them would have choked on his own spittle; and ʿIkrimah's report has him say simply, "Had the Jews wished for death, they would have perished."

**Context — the mutual invocation.** **[Saʿdī]** calls the verse a *mubāhalah* between them and the Messenger of God ﷺ, and observes the trap's construction: after this pressure there remain only two options — to believe in God and His Messenger, or to invoke the curse upon what they hold, by a thing that is easy for them, the wish for death, which would carry them straight to the house they claim is theirs alone. They refused. And so everyone knew that they were at the furthest extreme of obduracy and of hostility to God and His Messenger, knowing all the while. **[Maʿārif]** explains why such an unusual test was needed: the normal way to settle a dispute is discussion, with each side presenting its arguments — and the Jews, knowing they could not win, shied away from that. So the Qurʾān proposed a method that calls for no learning and no reasoning, only a little effort of the tongue; and it predicted in advance that they would never dare. In their hearts they knew the Prophet ﷺ and the Muslims stood for the truth and that they themselves were clinging to an abrogated law — so they feared that the moment they wished for death it would come for them, and they would be sent to the Fire. Their recalcitrance is itself an argument for Islam. **[Maʿārif]** adds a necessary qualification: the challenge was directed to the Jews who were the Prophet's ﷺ contemporaries, who denied him out of envy despite recognizing him — not to the Jews of every age.

**The prohibition of wishing for death.** **[Study Quran]** faces the apparent conflict with the ḥadīth, "Let none of you long for death because of an evil that befalls you; if he must say something, let him say: O God, give me life so long as life is good for me, and make me pass away if passing away is good for me." The reconciliation lies in the logic of the challenge itself: death is the only door to the felicity the Jews claim is theirs alone, so a person certain of that claim should rush to the door. The Prophet's ﷺ conditional supplication, by contrast, expresses the uncertainty of one's standing before God that Islam keeps alive — in the manner of 75:14-15, "no, man is a witness against himself," and of the knowledge of one's own sins. Like the claim to a numbered stay in the Fire (2:80; 3:24), the claim to a guaranteed and exclusive entry to Paradise betrays an excess of confidence; and the same challenge stands in 62:6, addressed to those who claim to be God's friends apart from other people. Some exegetes, it notes, read the passage as a species of *mubāhalah*, in which death is invoked upon whichever side, Muslim or Jew, is lying.

**Reflection.** The verse is a masterstroke of argument because it converts a boast into a request. Anyone can say the Garden belongs to him; only a man who believes it will ask to be let in. The refusal recorded in the next verse is the answer to every claim of privilege the Sūrah has been dismantling: a community that will not risk a single sentence in defence of its certainty has already been answered by its own silence.
