And the Prophet's ﷺ reported word — that had they wished for death they would have died and seen their seats in the Fire — makes the matter sharper still: the test was real, the danger was real, and knowing that, they still chose the world.

The challenge follows from the claim of exclusive access to the Hereafter. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that if their claim were true, wishing for death would not harm them; it would bring them from the hardship of this world to the comfort they say is reserved for them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Qurṭubī]** describe the challenge as exposing the rabbis' claim, and **[Jalālayn]** keeps the condition plain: if the Hereafter is theirs alone, they should desire death. **[Study Quran]** relates the verse to the broader Qur'anic criticism of religious claims unsupported by conduct. **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]** The point is not a general command for believers to seek death; it is a specific test of the boast just reported. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The verse asks for a response that would be easy if their confidence were sincere, yet the next verse predicts that they will not make it. **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]**

## 2:95

*"But they will never long for it, ever — because of what their hands have sent ahead; and God knows the wrongdoers."*

**Meaning.** The prediction attached to the challenge is itself the proof **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Ṭabarī]** reads it as God's report of their hatred of death and their refusal to answer the call: they fear that if they were to wish it, the threat would fall on them and death would be upon them at once — and this because they know Muḥammad ﷺ is a messenger sent to them, and that he never told them anything that did not come true. They are on their guard against wishing for death precisely for fear that God's punishment should alight on them for the sins their hands have earned. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report from Ibn Isḥāq, had already given the challenge its plain sense — invoke death upon whichever of the two parties is more lying — and adds: they refused; and God says they will never wish it, *because of what their hands have sent ahead*: that is, because of the knowledge in their possession concerning him, and their disbelief in that knowledge. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk's report is blunter: they know that they are lying; had they been truthful they would have wished it, eager to hasten to God's honour. And Ibn Jurayj: the Jews were the swiftest of people in fleeing death, and would never wish for it.

**Language.** "What their hands have sent ahead" is a metaphor of the Arabs' speech: a man taken for a crime he committed is told, "this has come to you for what your hands have earned" — even if the offence was committed with his tongue or another limb; because most offences are committed by the hands, the whole class of them came to be attributed to the hands **[Ṭabarī]**. So here, what their hands sent ahead is their disbelief in God by defying His command to follow Muḥammad ﷺ, which they found written in the Torah and knew to be a prophet sent — and God attributes to their hands what their hearts harboured and their tongues uttered of envy toward Muḥammad ﷺ, aggression against him, denial of him and rejection of his mission, because the language of the Qurʾān is the language of the Arabs and knows no difficulty with it **[Ṭabarī]**. Ibn Jurayj's gloss gives the charge its simplest form: they knew that Muḥammad ﷺ was a prophet, and they concealed it. And "God knows the wrongdoers" is not a general conclusion but a specific one: God knows the wrong of the children of Ādam — Jew and Christian, and people of every other confession — and the wrong of the Jews in particular was their disbelief and their rejection of the messenger they had themselves been invoking for victory before he came **[Ṭabarī]**.

**Context — the parallel.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** sets the challenge beside its Christian counterpart: the Messenger of God ﷺ summoned the delegation of Najrān to mutual invocation of curse after refuting them in a debate they fought with stubbornness, saying, "Come, let us call our sons and your sons, our women and your women, and ourselves and yourselves, then let us pray and invoke the curse of God upon the liars" (3:61) — and when the Christians heard it, some said to one another, "By God, if you do that with this prophet, none of you will have an eye that blinks." They settled for peace and paid the *jizyah* in submission. He explains why such an invocation counts as a "wish": every just person wishes that God should destroy the unjust opponent who disputes with him, especially when he holds a clear proof for what he calls to; and the *mubāhalah* amounts to invoking death on the unjust party, because to disbelievers life is the greatest prize — the less so when they know the destination awaiting them. **[Study Quran]** makes the same point from the psychology of the verse: those who truly expected no destination but Paradise should long for death, and their awareness of their own sins — "no, man is a witness against himself" (75:14-15) — however deep or undetected, stops them.

**Reflection.** The verse treats silence as testimony. A test that requires nothing but a wish was refused, and the refusal is the verdict — **[Maʿārif]**'s added observation is decisive: if they had wished it in their hearts, they would have declared it with their tongues, since their victory lay precisely in such a declaration and it was the easiest way to refute the Prophet ﷺ; and the fact that no such declaration has come down to us, among a people whose opponents always outnumbered their adherents and would have trumpeted it, settles the matter. The Qurʾān's own word for their motive — what their hands sent ahead — is exact: they feared their own record, and the claim of exclusive Paradise came with an examination they knew they would fail.

The refusal is tied to what they have sent ahead, not to a failure to understand the challenge. **[Ṭabarī]** says they feared that death would bring God's threatened punishment because they knew the Prophet ﷺ was truthful and had rejected him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise relates their hesitation to their knowledge of their deeds and their denial of the Messenger. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Saʿdī]** read the prediction as exposing a contradiction between their claim of exclusive salvation and their aversion to the test. **[Jalālayn]** gives the direct sense: they will never wish for death because of their wrongdoing. **[Maʿārif]** The verse does not require readers to speculate about the private state of every individual; it answers the public boast described in 2:94. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]** The closing reminder that God knows wrongdoers turns the debate from a verbal wager to accountability before the One who knows what each person has done. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:96

*"And you will find them the greediest of people for life — and of those who associate others with God: one of them wishes he could be given a thousand years; and it will not remove him from the punishment that he should live long. And God sees what they do."*

**Meaning.** The verse turns from what they refuse to say to what they cannot hide **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]**. **[Ṭabarī]** reads it as God's oath-sworn report to His Prophet ﷺ: you will find them — the Jews — the most intense of people in greed for the life of this world and the most averse to death, *more so than the polytheists*, as one says "he is the bravest of people, and than ʿAntarah." The reasoning is exact: they know what has been prepared for them in the Hereafter for their disbelief, so they hate death more than those who do not believe in the raising at all. Ibn ʿAbbās, in Ibn Isḥāq's report: the polytheist has no hope of a resurrection after death, so he loves long life; but the Jew has known what awaits him of shame in the Hereafter for what he has squandered of the knowledge he had. **[Qurṭubī]** records both readings of the sentence — either the comparison is with the polytheists (and the pronoun in "one of them" returns to the Jews), or the sentence closes at "for life" and a separate report begins about the Magians, whose blessing to one who sneezes means "may you live a thousand years," which is why **[Study Quran]** notes the Persian phrase commentators quote; while al-Ḥasan held the idolaters are the Arab polytheists, who crave long life because they do not believe in the raising. **[Study Quran]** also reports al-Rāzī's preference: "a thousand" is an idiom for "many," and he dismisses the identification with the Zoroastrians as too specific.

**Language.** *Zahzaha* is removal and displacement: to push a thing away so that it moves off **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** — as in the poet's petition, "O you who seize the soul from a body when death comes, and forgive the sin: keep me away from the Fire," and the lover's complaint that the darkness "does not shift." On *wa-mā huwa bi-muzahzihihi min al-ʿadhābi an yuʿammar*, **[Ṭabarī]** weighs the analyses and prefers one: *huwa* is a separating pronoun (*ʿimād*), and the sense is "what is he — any one of them — a remover of him from punishment by being given long life," the pronoun standing like the *huwa* in "what is he, standing, ʿAmr." Others took it as the pronoun of "long life": what is that living-on but a thing that will not remove him — and the infinitive "that he should be given life" stands in apposition to it. **[Qurṭubī]** reports the same options and registers a doubt about the *ʿimād*: its proper place is between two mutually dependent things, as in "if this is the truth" and "but they were the wrongdoers" — and he gives the alternative readings: *huwa* as the pronoun of "the one" (no one of them is removed from punishment by being given life), or as the pronoun of the matter and the affair, which Ibn ʿAṭiyyah also judges remote since the preserved usage is to explain it by a clause free of a preposition. He adds the word's finer edge: the thousand is specified because it is the limit of the reckoning's decade. And on "God sees what they do" — *baṣīr* in the Arabs' language is not merely one who sees but one who knows a thing thoroughly, as one says a man is *baṣīr* in medicine, or in law, or in the ways of men.

**āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** brings a report from al-Nasāʾī on the opposite of this verse's verdict: from Abū Hurayrah, from the Prophet ﷺ — "Whoever fasts a day in the way of God, God will keep his face seventy autumns away from the Fire." Against the long life that removes no one from punishment, the Qurʾān sets a short act that does. **[Study Quran]** adds al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī's observation that a hypocrite is more covetous of the world than an idolater — perhaps because of a gnawing awareness of the consequences of his own actions; hypocrisy's certainty of salvation is a delusion whose underlying truth, that its owner's deeds preclude such certainty, is not quite hidden from him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes the maxim that this world is a prison for the believer and a paradise for the disbeliever, and draws the conclusion that the People of the Book wish to delay the Hereafter as long as possible — though they will surely meet what they flee.

**Reflection.** The verse is a test by appetite. A man's claim about the next world is measured by how tightly he holds this one; and the striking thing here is that those who believed least in the Hereafter clung to life less than those who insisted the Hereafter was theirs by right. **[Maʿārif]** puts the point exactly: the polytheists' love of life is no surprise, since for them physical life was the whole of the matter; but a people who acknowledge the next world, and imagine that its joys are reserved for them, and still wish above all others to remain where they are — their desire publishes what their doctrine denies; they knew, whatever they said aloud, what sort of account awaited them. And the closing clause leaves nothing outside the ledger: God sees what they do, and the thousand years for which they hoped could not have shifted them a hand's breadth from it.

The verse shifts from the death they refuse to the life they cling to. **[Ṭabarī]** reads the opening as an oath-sworn report about the Jews' intense desire for worldly life, even in comparison with idolaters. **[Ibn Kathīr]** transmits explanations that their fear of death was especially strong because they knew what they had done and what awaited them. **[Qurṭubī]** examines the comparative wording and the meaning of *ḥayāh*, while **[Jalālayn]** emphasizes their extraordinary greed for long life. **[Study Quran]** notes that wishing for a thousand years is an image of extreme attachment, not a calculation that longevity can secure salvation. **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]** The verse immediately denies that added years would remove punishment: time can extend worldly experience, but it cannot erase the moral account. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]** The final statement that God sees what they do connects their desire for life to deeds already known to Him. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:97

*"Say: 'Whoever is an enemy to Jibrīl — he it is who brought it down upon your heart by God's leave, confirming what was before it, a guidance and glad tidings for the believers.'"*

**Meaning.** The verse answers a complaint about the postman **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** notes the commentators' consensus that it came down in reply to the Jews who claimed Jibrīl was their enemy and Mīkāʾīl their friend. Their reasoning, in the report **[Qurṭubī]** takes from al-Tirmidhī: no prophet ever came but an angel brought him the message from his Lord — so who is your companion? "Jibrīl," he said. "That is the one who descends with war and killing; he is our enemy. Had you said Mīkāʾīl, who descends with rain and mercy, we would have followed you." **[Jalālayn]** condenses the reply to a single dismissive clause — whoever is an enemy to Jibrīl, let him die of his rage — and then states what Jibrīl did: he brought the Qurʾān down upon your heart by God's leave, confirming what came before it of scriptures, a guidance out of error and glad tidings of Paradise for the believers. **[Saʿdī]** lays bare the self-contradiction: Jibrīl is purely a messenger; the One who commanded and sent him is God; the Book he brought agrees with the earlier Books and contradicts none of them; it carries complete guidance out of every kind of error and good tidings for whoever believes. So enmity to such a Jibrīl is disbelief in God and His signs and enmity toward God, His messengers and His angels — and their enmity is not to Jibrīl himself but to the truth he brings down from God to God's messengers. It therefore contains unbelief toward the Sender, the one sent, what was sent, and the one sent to.

**The occasion in full.** **[Ṭabarī]** gives the exchange in the report of Ibn ʿAbbās (a chain the editor grades sound, also in Aḥmad): a group of Jews came to the Messenger of God ﷺ and said, "Abū al-Qāsim, tell us of certain matters no one knows but a prophet," taking from him an oath that if he told them something they recognized, they would follow him. They asked four things: which food Isrāʾīl forbade himself before the Torah was sent down (camel flesh and its milk, from a vow in sickness); how the fluid of the man and the fluid of the woman determine male or female (whichever overcomes the other); that this ummī prophet's eyes sleep while his heart does not; and — "who is your guardian among the angels? On that we will follow you or part from you." He said: "My guardian is Jibrīl, and God never sent a prophet but he was his guardian." They said: "Then we part from you. Had your guardian been another of the angels, we would have followed you." He asked what prevented them from believing him; they said, "He is our enemy" — and God sent down: *whoever is an enemy to Jibrīl...* **[Ibn Kathīr]** adds the parallel from al-Bukhārī: when ʿAbd Allāh b. Salām heard the Prophet ﷺ had arrived in Madīnah, he came and asked three things no one knows but a prophet — the first portent of the Hour, the first meal of the people of Paradise, and why a child resembles its father or its maternal uncle. The Prophet ﷺ said, "Jibrīl has just told me the answers," and ʿAbd Allāh said, "He, among all the angels, is the enemy of the Jews" — after which the Prophet ﷺ recited the verse, gave the answers (a fire driving the people from east to west; the caudate lobe of the fish's liver; whichever discharge precedes), and ʿAbd Allāh bore witness. He then asked that the Jews be asked about him before he announced his Islam — and when they were asked, they called him the best of them and their master, and when he came out and professed, they called him the worst, exactly as he had feared. **[Study Quran]** gives the second account: ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb visiting the Jews while they studied the Torah — he enjoyed the mutual confirmation of Torah and Qurʾān — and their telling him that they believed Muḥammad was a prophet but were "at peace" with some angels and not others; ʿUmar was astonished that one angel could be a friend and another a foe when both stood on either side of God, and when he returned to the Prophet ﷺ he was told the verse had already come down.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** notes that the pronoun in *fa-innahu* carries two senses: either "God sent Jibrīl down upon your heart," or "Jibrīl brought it down upon your heart." The heart is named specifically because it is the seat of reason and knowledge and the place where understanding is received — which is why the verse, he observes, indicates Jibrīl's rank and the blame due to his enemy. *Bi-idhni Allāh* is by His will and His knowledge. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports the etymology from al-Bukhārī: ʿIkrimah said *Jibr*, *Mīk* and *Isrāf* all mean "worshipper," while *īl* means "God" — the pattern of the theophoric names, in which one element stays constant and the divine name varies, as in ʿAbd Allāh, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, ʿAbd al-Malik.

**Rulings & āthār — the Book on the heart.** **[Maʿārif]** warns against a serious misreading of "upon your heart," refuting the notion that only the meanings of the Qurʾān were revealed while its wording was supplied by the Prophet ﷺ: many verses of the Qurʾān itself name the Arabic language as the vehicle of revelation, and the heart perceives words as much as meanings — indeed the heart is the perceiving agent, while the ears and other organs are its instruments. In the state of revelation the outward senses are suspended and the heart receives the very words without the mediation of the ear; the analogy, imperfect as it is, is that in sleep one hears words and remembers them afterwards though the sense of hearing is dormant. It is, he says, intellectual dishonesty to build a doctrine on conjecture against what the Book says of itself. **[Study Quran]** adds al-Rāzī's reading of the motive beneath the excuse: at least part of the Jews' hostility to Jibrīl came from the fact that Muḥammad was not of Israel, so that invoking Gabriel's severity was a pretext masking displeasure that an Arab had been chosen; and to fault the angel is to fault the One who sent him. It notes the Jewish background in which Michael is Israel's prince and advocate (Daniel 10), the angel of snow and mercy, while Gabriel is the angel of fire and severity, the destroyer of Sodom — the soil, it suggests, in which a tradition of Gabriel as the Jews' enemy could grow, though in Jewish literature as a whole Gabriel is seen positively. And it points to the Qurʾān's own parallel statements that the revelation came down upon the Prophet's heart (26:194; 53:10-11), since the heart in the Qurʾānic anthropology is the seat of knowledge and understanding, not merely of sentiment.

**Reflection.** The passage is a small anatomy of how a pretext works. The objection is not to the message — the Book confirms what they have and guides and gives glad tidings — but to the messenger who carried it, and behind that to the choice of the One who sent him. **Saʿdī**'s chain of consequence is the verse's argument: to hate the angel is to hate the revelation he brings, and therefore the God who sent it, the Prophet who received it, and the truth itself. **[Study Quran]**'s citation of al-Rāzī completes the picture: the quarrel with an angel is often a quarrel, in disguise, with the human being God chose — and it is worth noticing that the man who asked his questions honestly, ʿAbd Allāh b. Salām, heard the same name and believed.

The reply treats the identity of the angel as irrelevant to the truth of the revelation: Jibrīl brought it down only by God's leave. **[Ṭabarī]** says the verse answered the Jewish claim that Gabriel was an enemy and Michael a friend, though the reports differ about how that claim arose. **[Ibn Kathīr]** records their association of Gabriel with war and punishment, and **[Study Quran]** preserves the account in which they said they would have followed the Prophet if Michael had brought the message. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Jalālayn]** emphasize that Gabriel delivered what God commanded. **[Saʿdī]** notes that the Qur'an confirms earlier revelation, guides, and brings glad tidings to believers. The messenger's role is service to God's command, not independent choice. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:98

*"Whoever is an enemy to God, His angels, His messengers, and Jibrīl and Mīkāʾīl — then God is an enemy to the disbelievers."*

**Meaning.** The verse generalizes the preceding one and seals the matter **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]**. **[Ṭabarī]** sets out the logic: those named here are God's friends and the people of His obedience; whoever takes a friend of God as an enemy has taken God as an enemy and crossed into open war with Him — and whoever is at war with God is at war with everyone who serves and is loyal to Him. So He says to the Jews who claimed that Jibrīl was their enemy and Mīkāʾīl their friend: whoever is an enemy to God and His angels and His messengers, and Jibrīl and Mīkāʾīl — the enemy of Jibrīl is an enemy of Mīkāʾīl and of every friend of God. **[Qurṭubī]** spells out what the two opposing enmities consist in: the servant's enmity to God is his disobedience and his avoidance of obedience and his hostility to God's friends; God's enmity to the servant is his punishment and the bringing upon him of the marks of that enmity.

**Language — why the two angels named.** **[Qurṭubī]** answers the obvious question: God had already mentioned His angels, so why single out Jibrīl and Mīkāʾīl by name? Two reasons — first, to honour them, as in "therein are fruits, and palms and pomegranates" (55:68), where the particular is named after the general; second, and more to the point here, because the Jews had named them, and the verse came down on their account: naming them was required so that they could not say, "We did not become enemies of God and all His angels," and the explicit mention refutes their attempt to make the hostility specific. **[Study Quran]** makes the same grammatical point about the structure of the verse: naming Jibrīl and Mīkāʾīl after "His angels" does not imply that the two are not angels, any more than 55:68 implies that dates and pomegranates are not fruit; and angels are themselves called messengers in the Qurʾān (10:21; 11:77). **[Qurṭubī]** also records the lexicographers' variations for the two names — for Jibrīl, ten dialectal forms, the Ḥijāzī *Jibrīl* the one Ḥassān b. Thābit uses, *Jabrīl* with a vowelled *jīm* the reading of al-Ḥasan and Ibn Kathīr, and forms with *ʾīl* appended; and he preserves the report that Ibn Kathīr said, "I saw the Prophet ﷺ in a dream reciting *Jabrīl* and *Mīkāʾīl*, so I shall never read them otherwise."

**āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** reports that the Prophet ﷺ asked the Jews: "I ask you by your Book which you recite — do you find that ʿĪsā son of Maryam gave glad tidings of me, that a messenger named Aḥmad would come to you?" They answered, "By God, we have found you in our book, but we hated you, because you make lawful our property and shed blood" — and then God sent down, "Whoever is an enemy to God and His angels and His messengers..." (the chain is graded weak by the editor, who notes the report's provenance). He also gives the report from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Abī Laylā: a Jew met ʿUmar and said, "The Jibrīl your companion mentions is our enemy," and ʿUmar recited this very verse at him — and the report adds that it came down on ʿUmar's tongue (its chain is broken; ʿUmar lived after it could have been revealed). **[Ibn Kathīr]** brings the ḥadīth from al-Bukhārī, from Abū Hurayrah: God, exalted, said, "Whoever takes a friend of Mine as an enemy has declared war on Me" — and draws the consequence: whoever takes one messenger as an enemy has taken all the messengers as enemies, for one who believes in one messenger is required to believe in them all, and one who rejects one has rejected them all, as in 4:150, where those who believe in some and reject others are declared disbelievers outright. His explanation of the angels' position is precise: Jibrīl did not choose his missions on his own; he descends only by the command of his Lord, as in 19:64, and the Qurʾān describes the trustworthy spirit bringing it down upon the Prophet's heart (26:192-194) — so the hostility is to the command, not to the servant who carries it. **[Study Quran]** adds the ḥadīth's other form, "Whoever is an enemy of God's friend, God makes war on him," as the verse's practical complement.

**Belief.** **[Saʿdī]** draws out why the passage is about *kufr* rather than mere dislike: enmity to Jibrīl, in the light of what Jibrīl does, is unbelief in God and His signs and enmity to God, His messengers and His angels. Their quarrel was never with the angel's person but with the truth he brings down from God to God's messengers — and so the enmity gathers in the One who sent, the one sent, the message sent, and the one it was sent to; which is why the verse closes not with "God is their enemy" but with "God is an enemy to the disbelievers," naming their condition rather than their persons, as **[Jalālayn]** observes: the substitution of "the disbelievers" for "them" is there to declare their standing.

**Reflection.** This verse turns a grudge into a doctrine and then shows the doctrine for what it is. The Jews of Madīnah had drawn a line through the angelic host and chosen whose messenger they would accept — and the Qurʾān answers that revelation is not a market in which one picks the courier one prefers. **[Ibn Kathīr]**'s rule is the hinge: to take one messenger as an enemy is to take them all, since the message is one and its source is one. And the verse's closing word is chosen with care: God is the enemy not of the men but of the disbelief that has made them so.

The following verse broadens the warning: hostility to Gabriel is part of hostility to God, His angels, and His messengers. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that God's allies are the people of obedience, so opposing them is not a neutral preference. **[Qurṭubī]** says that enmity to a servant of God entails enmity to God, and explains why Gabriel and Michael are named after the general category of angels. **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Jalālayn]** connect the verse to the earlier dispute about the two angels; **[Study Quran]** notes that their specific mention answers that historical objection. **[Saʿdī]** treats the divine response as a warning to all who reject God's messengers. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** The final phrase “to the disbelievers” identifies the underlying state of such enmity: rejecting the messengers is rejection of the One who sent them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:99

*"And We have sent down to you clear signs, and none disbelieve in them except the iniquitous."*

**Meaning.** The charge that no evidence has come is answered with the evidence itself **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]**. **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the *āyāt bayyināt* concretely: the marks, clear and indicative of Muḥammad's ﷺ prophethood, contained in the Book God sent down to him — the hidden sciences of the Jews, the buried secrets of their reports and of their earliest forebears among the Children of Israel, and the news of what their own Books contained, which none knew but their rabbis and scholars, together with the rulings of their Torah that their ancestors and later generations had altered and substituted. God brought all of it out into the Book He revealed to His Prophet ﷺ; and in that there were clear signs for the one who dealt justly with himself and was not driven to destroy himself by envy and aggression. For it is in the nature of every sound disposition to affirm the truthfulness of one who brings what Muḥammad ﷺ brought — signs of that kind — without having learned them from any human being or taken any part of them from a man. Ibn ʿAbbās, in al-Ḍaḥḥāk's report: you recite it to them and inform them of it morning and evening, while you are in their eyes an unlettered man who has never read a book, and you tell them what is in their hands exactly as it stands — in that is a lesson and a clarification for them, and a proof against them, if they would only know.

**Occasion.** Ibn Ṣūriyā al-Fiṭyūnī said to the Messenger of God ﷺ, "Muḥammad, you have not brought us anything we recognize, and God has not sent down upon you any clear sign that we should follow you by it" — and God sent down this verse **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**. **[Qurṭubī]** notes simply that it is the answer to that man's words, and **[Jalālayn]** records the same occasion in brief and adds the sting of the ending: these men have indeed disbelieved in them — the verse is not merely a general statement but a verdict on the speakers.

**Belief & reflection.** **[Saʿdī]** frames the verse by its two effects: guidance for whoever seeks guidance, and the establishment of proof against whoever is obstinate — signs that have reached such a pitch of clarity and indication of the truth that no one refuses them but one who has left God's command behind, departed from His obedience, and grown arrogant with the furthest arrogance. **[Maʿārif]** makes the same point from the other direction: the objection that no recognizable sign had come is false on its face, since God had sent down clear signs of the prophethood which the Jews themselves understood perfectly well; therefore their denial was not a matter of insufficient recognition but of habitual disobedience — and it is a general rule that no one denies such evidence except those who are willful and stubborn in their disobedience. **[Study Quran]** pictures the signs in a broader way: the marks of Muḥammad's prophethood in his person, his qualities and the knowledge he possessed, which, the commentators say, ought to have been apparent to any believing Jew. The verse is thus not an argument but an exposure: the verdict is already given in what they are looking at, and the only thing left to explain is the eye that will not see it — which the word *fāsiqūn* supplies: not those who lack evidence, but those who have broken out of the command of their Lord.

The charge that revelation lacks proof is answered by the signs already sent down. **[Ṭabarī]** identifies these clear signs with evidence in the Qur'an that no ordinary human teacher could have supplied: hidden details about Israel's history, the contents of their own books, and the alterations made to earlier rulings. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the verse to the Prophet's signs and to the recognition of his truth by those who knew scripture. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** The evidences are not presented as obscure riddles; they are *bayyināt*, proofs made clear to those willing to judge fairly. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]**

The concluding description, “none disbelieve in them except the iniquitous,” does not blame the evidence for the refusal. **[Ṭabarī]** connects *fāsiqīn* with people who leave obedience and reject what they know; **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Saʿdī]** likewise read the verse as exposing persistent transgression. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that the word names those who depart from God's command, while **[Maʿārif]** distinguishes deliberate rejection from mere lack of information. **[Study Quran]** places the statement in the continuing discussion of scriptural recognition and denial. **[Jalālayn]** gives the concise contrast between clear signs and the disobedient response. The verse thus preserves both sides of the argument: revelation comes with signs, and people remain responsible for whether they respond to them. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]**

The wording “We sent down to you” also places the signs within the Qur'an's revealed form, not only in isolated events. **[Ṭabarī]** highlights information about Israel's scriptures and history that the Prophet ﷺ had not learned from their scholars; **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects that evidence to his prophethood. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Maʿārif]** note the signs' clarity for anyone who weighs them without prejudice. The verse does not imply that evidence compels every hearer; rather, **[Saʿdī]** says persistent transgression explains why some reject what has been made clear. **[Study Quran]** **[Jalālayn]** The close links back to the preceding accusations and insists that rejection is accountable. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:100

*"Is it not so that whenever they make a covenant, a party of them cast it aside? No — most of them do not believe."*

**Meaning.** The inquiry is a rebuke, and its form is the point **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**. **[Ṭabarī]** reads it as: is it their custom that every time they give their Lord a covenant — binding themselves again and again to work by what is in the Torah — a party of them throws it away and breaks it? He reproaches them for that, and through them reproaches their descendants, who walked their road in some of what had been taken from them of covenant and pledge concerning belief in Muḥammad ﷺ: they disbelieved and denied what the Torah contained of his description and attributes. **[Saʿdī]** puts the surprise in the foreground: the verse conveys astonishment at how many pledges they made and how little patience they had in keeping them — *kullamā* carries repetition, so that the very existence of a covenant was the occasion of its breach. Why? The answer is in the verse's own last words: because most of them do not believe; their lack of faith was what obliged them to break their pledges; had their faith been true they would have been like those of whom God says, "among the believers are men who were true to what they pledged to God" (33:23).

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** reports the grammarians' dispute over *a-wa-kullamā*: Sibawayh held the *wāw* is a conjunction with the interrogative *alif* placed before it — as the *alif* enters upon *fa* in "is it the judgement of the age of ignorance they seek?" (5:50) and upon *thumma* elsewhere; al-Akhfash said the *wāw* is superfluous; al-Kisāʾī held the word is *aw*, its *wāw* given a vowel for ease; and some read *aw* with a quiescent *wāw*, giving the sense of *bal* — as when a man threatens "I will strike you," and the other replies, "or God will suffice." Ibn ʿAṭiyyah called all this forced and endorsed Sibawayh; and **[Ṭabarī]** agrees, adding a principle he had already established: it is not permissible for a letter in God's Book to carry no meaning — which disposes of the view that the *wāw* or *fāʾ* of *a-wa-kullamā* and *a-fa-kullamā* are meaningless additions. *Kullamā* is an adverb of time here, and *nabadha* is, in the Arabs' tongue, to throw away — whence the foundling is called *al-manbūdh* **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**.

**Occasion.** **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Qurṭubī]** both give the report of Ibn ʿAbbās: when the Messenger of God ﷺ was sent, Mālik b. al-Ṣayf — also called Ibn al-Ḍayf — said, in the face of what had been taken from them of covenant and what God had charged them with concerning him: "By God, He made no covenant with us about Muḥammad ﷺ, and took no pledge from us for him!" And God sent down: *is it not so that whenever they make a covenant, a party of them cast it aside? No — most of them do not believe.* **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Study Quran]** give the other construction of the occasion: that the Jews had pledged that if Muḥammad came out they would believe in him and stand with him against the polytheists of the Arabs — and when he was sent, they disbelieved; and **[Study Quran]** adds that for others it refers to their betraying the Prophet ﷺ by breaking their alliance with him and siding with Quraysh, while quoting al-Rāzī's judgement that the meaning is not restricted to any particular incident.

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** distinguishes two offences in the passage and keeps them distinct: to go against a pledge is a sin and a transgression, but to have no faith at all in the pledge one gives to God is outright unbelief — and it is that second thing the verse's closing clause names. He also notes the precision of "a party of them": the Qurʾān does not say all the Jews broke the pledge, for some fulfilled it, so much so that many eventually accepted Muḥammad ﷺ as a true prophet and joined the Muslims. The door left open is part of the verse's meaning, not an accident of its wording.

**Reflection.** The verse is about a pattern rather than an incident, and the interrogative form exposes it: not "they broke a covenant" but "is this what they do *every time*?" A community whose word dissolves on contact with its interests has diagnosed itself, and **[Saʿdī]**'s reading is the one the verse invites: the cause is not weakness of memory but absence of faith — for the man who truly believes in the One to whom he has given his word does not weigh the pledge against his advantage. The line from 2:93, "we hear and we disobey," runs straight through this verse; and the remnant named at the end of the covenant passage — "all but a few of you" — is named again here, in the party that did not cast it aside.

The question expects recognition of a repeated pattern: whenever a covenant is made, some cast it aside. **[Ṭabarī]** reads the covenant in the immediate context as a pledge to follow the Torah and accept the Prophet whose description it contains. **[Maʿārif]** reports that the Jews denied having pledged to follow the coming Messenger, then notes the verse's wider criticism of repeated covenant-breaking. **[Study Quran]** records several occasions proposed by commentators, including the pledge concerning the expected prophet and later breaches of alliance. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** The text carefully says “a party” and then “most of them”; it does not flatten every person into the same act. **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Saʿdī]** note the qualification. The final clause explains that such repeated abandonment is not merely forgetfulness: it reflects disbelief in the obligation itself. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** The verse therefore looks backward to the covenants already recounted and forward to the continuing rejection of revelation. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:101
101
*“And when a messenger from God came to them, confirming what was with them, a party of those who were given the Book cast the Book of God behind their backs, as if they did not know.”*

**Meaning.** The verse takes the pattern of covenant-breaking from the previous line and gives it a concrete instance: the messenger arrives not as a stranger to Israel’s revelation but as one who confirms its truth. **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the messenger as Muḥammad ﷺ and the people in view as learned Jews; the confirmation is reciprocal in the sense that the Qur’an affirms the Torah’s original revelation while the Torah, as they had it, contained signs of the coming prophet. **[Maʿārif]** likewise stresses that accepting Muḥammad was not a departure from the Torah but a response to its own testimony. **[Study Quran]** notes that “those given the Book” can refer broadly to its adherents, while the immediate emphasis on learned custodians explains why the verse says “a party,” not every individual. **[Jalālayn]** reads the confirmation as the messenger’s agreement with what the Torah says about belief in him.

The verb “cast away” is deliberately vivid. It does not require that the Torah be physically thrown aside; it names a refusal to let its guidance govern conduct. **[Qurṭubī]** reports that the “Book of God” most directly means the Torah, although some exegetes allow the Qur’an as a secondary referent. He also preserves the distinction between possessing and acting upon scripture: people may keep a book, adorn it, and recite it yet still cast its commands behind them if they do not make lawful what it makes lawful or forbid what it forbids. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the image as turning away from something one had been charged to follow. **[Saʿdī]** calls the image especially forceful: the book is not merely neglected at one’s side but put behind one’s back, as though one wishes not to see it. **[Study Quran]** takes the Torah as the primary referent because the audience is described as people who received it, while noting that the moral image can apply to any revealed book that is disregarded.

**Context and reports.** The preceding verse describes a party that repeatedly breaks its covenant; this verse shows the breach in their treatment of revelation and the messenger. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the passage to the Jewish leaders’ dispute with the Prophet ﷺ: when the Torah and Qur’an supported one another, some abandoned that argument and reached instead for material that did not agree with the revelation. **[Ṭabarī]** transmits reports from al-Suddī about that abandonment and from Qatādah, who says that they knew but corrupted their knowledge, concealed the truth, and denied it. These reports present culpable refusal, not an innocent failure to understand. **[Maʿārif]** emphasizes the same point: the prophecy was sufficiently recognizable to those who knew the scripture, so rejecting the messenger implicated their claim to follow the Torah. **[Qurṭubī]** cautions, through the grammar and the image, that “as if they did not know” is a comparison to the behavior of the ignorant, not a declaration that they lacked access to knowledge.

**Language and belief.** “Confirming what was with them” gives the verse a criterion for continuity: a later messenger is not validated by simply echoing every later practice, but by confirming the divine source and truth carried by earlier revelation. **[Ṭabarī]** describes the Qur’an and Torah as agreeing about the messenger’s commission; **[Jalālayn]** makes the point specifically about the Torah’s prediction. **[Study Quran]** distinguishes the Book as a physical text from its testimony, which can remain present even when its readers ignore its demands. **[Saʿdī]** draws a severe consequence: rejecting the messenger who confirms one’s scripture becomes, in effect, rejection of the scripture itself. That argument is about this particular group’s response, not a license to make a careless judgment about every member of a religious community. **[Maʿārif]** preserves the verse’s own qualification, “a party,” and the preceding verse’s “most,” leaving room for those who did respond faithfully.

**Reflection.** The image of a book behind the back asks whether reverence for a text is measured by possession, display, or obedience. **[Qurṭubī]**’s example of a book treated as an ornament but not as a source of moral obligation makes that question practical; **[Ṭabarī]** locates the offence in knowingly abandoning a covenant. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects it with the next verse, where a rejected scriptural witness is replaced by an attractive but destructive pursuit. **[Saʿdī]** generalizes the pattern carefully: when a person turns away from what is beneficial and available, he may become absorbed in what harms him. **[Study Quran]** underscores that the verse is directed at a group’s selective rejection, not at scripture as such. The passage therefore warns readers of every revelation not to use inherited knowledge as a shield against the truth it requires them to follow. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]**

The verse also narrows the target by saying “a party.” The critique is directed at those who knowingly refused, while the preceding text says “most” rather than every person. **[Maʿārif]** notices this qualification, and **[Study Quran]** cautions that book-holders who do heed their scripture are not erased by the behavior of rejecters. Thus the rebuke keeps both moral precision and historical force: inherited privilege can increase responsibility, but it does not make guilt automatic for everyone sharing an ancestry. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:102
102
*“And they followed what the satans recited concerning the kingdom of Solomon. Solomon did not disbelieve, but the satans disbelieved, teaching people sorcery and that which was sent down to the two angels at Babylon, Hārūt and Mārūt. They would teach no one until they said, ‘We are only a trial, so do not disbelieve.’ And they learned from them that by which they could separate a man from his wife. Yet they harmed no one by it except by God’s leave. They learned what harmed them and did not benefit them, though they knew that whoever purchased it would have no share in the Hereafter. Evil indeed was that for which they sold themselves, if they only knew.”*

**Meaning.** The verse explains one consequence of throwing the revealed Book behind one’s back: people turn to an alternative that promises hidden power. **[Ṭabarī]** reads “they followed” as the Jewish scholars’ adoption of material attributed to the satans in Solomon’s time and by later generations; he records that *tatlū* can mean reciting, reporting, or following, so the verse condemns both transmission and adherence to false teaching. **[Ibn Kathīr]** recounts reports in which devils circulated writings about magic and falsely attached them to Solomon after his death. The Qur’anic correction is explicit: Solomon was not an unbeliever. His extraordinary authority was a gift from God, not proof that he practiced sorcery. **[Maʿārif]** emphasizes that the denial is placed in the verse precisely to clear a prophet whom some had accused of magic. **[Jalālayn]** likewise reads the satanic teaching as the thing rejected by the statement that Solomon did not disbelieve.

The allusion to Hārūt and Mārūt raises questions about the nature of the trial and about the phrase “the two angels.” **[Ṭabarī]** records different readings and transmitted accounts about whether the word is read as “angels” or “kings,” and about how the Babylon episode should be understood; his discussion does not make every circulating story part of the Qur’anic statement. **[Qurṭubī]** also preserves interpretive disagreements about the syntax and the identity of the two, alongside legal discussion of sorcery. **[Study Quran]** warns that commentaries on this verse have sometimes repeated elaborate legends, including the tale of Venus’s seduction of the angels, even though such accounts are not needed to understand the verse and are rejected by major exegetes. **[Maʿārif]** similarly says that the story of Zuhrah is not established by an authentic Islamic report and that the Qur’anic teaching stands without it. The text itself gives the essential safeguard: before teaching, the two say plainly that this is a test and that the learner must not disbelieve.

**Knowledge, harm, and responsibility.** The warning does not make the teaching an invitation to practice sorcery. **[Saʿdī]** explains that the warning identifies the moral danger and removes any excuse for treating the knowledge as innocent entertainment: the learner has been told its spiritual status before choosing it. **[Maʿārif]** describes the angels’ function as distinguishing sorcery from prophetic miracles, so that people would not mistake deceptive effects for divine authorization. **[Study Quran]** notes that the Arabic *siḥr* has a range of meanings, from illusion and sleight of hand to alleged manipulation of hidden forces; not every use of persuasive language or wonder is identical with the condemned practice. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses legal definitions and the question of when an act constitutes unbelief, a question that must be handled with care rather than applied indiscriminately to individuals. **[Ibn Kathīr]** treats the passage as a warning against learning a practice that harms religion and can be used to damage human relationships.

The verse names the separation of spouses as a particularly destructive use, not the only possible claim made for sorcery. **[Ṭabarī]** connects the wording to the harm people sought through magic; **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates it to satanic efforts to fracture marriage. **[Study Quran]** points out that the Qur’an attributes no independent power to such means: they harm no one except by God’s leave. **[Saʿdī]** draws from that qualification a principle about created causes generally: even a cause that appears potent is not sovereign or independent of divine decree. **[Jalālayn]** echoes the restriction to God’s permission. The language neither denies that people can suffer from malicious acts nor grants sorcery a rival power alongside God.

**Reflection.** The verse contrasts Solomon’s God-given kingship with the satanic promise of control. **[Ṭabarī]** shows how a false story about the prophet could be used to authorize what revelation condemns; **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise presents the denial of Solomon’s unbelief as a defense of prophethood. **[Maʿārif]** stresses that the verse is not dependent on sensational stories, while **[Study Quran]** counsels restraint about reports that exceed what the Qur’an affirms. The most consequential judgment arrives at the end: buyers of this teaching exchange their souls for something harmful and receive no share in the Hereafter. **[Saʿdī]** calls it a loss in both religion and worldly life; **[Qurṭubī]** treats the warning as a serious moral and legal boundary. The test is not whether a person can acquire hidden technique, but whether he will heed the warning and refuse a knowledge whose use is destructive. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]**

A further distinction is needed between recognizing that a harmful practice can have effects and attributing independent power to it. **[Saʿdī]** says the cause remains subject to divine decree, and **[Study Quran]** rejects the idea that Solomon’s granted command over wind and jinn made him a sorcerer. **[Ibn Kathīr]** uses the verse’s explicit denial to protect prophetic integrity, while **[Maʿārif]** views the episode as a lesson in distinguishing miracles from occult claims. A report’s presence in later commentaries should therefore not be confused with Qur’anic endorsement of every detail. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The warning attached to the two teachers also shapes the reader’s understanding of the whole episode. Before a student learns anything, the danger is stated openly: the knowledge is a trial, and choosing it may amount to disbelief. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** The verse thus does not portray the learner as an innocent bystander after an unavoidable exposure; it foregrounds a decision made with a warning in view. **[Saʿdī]** At the same time, the closing qualification that no harm occurs except by God’s leave prevents the episode from turning into a story of occult forces competing with divine power. **[Study Quran]** This combination—real moral danger, clear disclosure, and divine sovereignty—is why the passage can condemn sorcery without treating every unusual event as proof of it. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:103
103
*“Had they believed and been reverent, a reward from God would have been better, if only they knew.”*

**Meaning.** The verse gives a concise alternative to the exchange described at the end of 2:102. Those who spent their effort pursuing sorcery could instead have believed and guarded themselves from disobedience; the reward God gives would be better than the object they chose. **[Saʿdī]** reads the conditional as a rebuke and a lesson: faith and *taqwā* would have brought a reward from God superior to what they imagined they were buying with their souls. **[Study Quran]** makes the link explicit by saying that the divine recompense is better than what they purchased in the preceding verse. **[Jalālayn]** likewise takes the “reward” as the recompense for belief and reverence. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the statement to the warning that magic leaves its adherents without a share in the Hereafter; the better alternative is not another technique but a changed allegiance.

The verse does not contrast faith with knowledge as though ignorance were a virtue. The surrounding passage has already described people who “knew” the danger, yet acted against what they knew. **[Maʿārif]** draws attention to that rhetorical tension: the repeated “if they only knew” indicates that information without understanding deep enough to govern action is not beneficial knowledge. **[Ṭabarī]** places the verse within the indictment of those who abandoned the divine Book and followed the satanic teaching; the words “had they believed” expose the choice they refused to make. **[Qurṭubī]** explains the phrase in the context of recompense and the believer’s share in the Hereafter. **[Saʿdī]** points to the heart of the contrast: they preferred a fleeting and harmful gain over faith, obedience, and enduring reward.

**Belief and reverence.** The two terms belong together. Belief is not merely verbal assent to God’s existence; in this context it means accepting the messenger, the revealed warning, and the limits God sets. Reverence, or *taqwā*, is the practical protection that follows: avoiding forbidden action because one is conscious of God and of the reckoning. **[Saʿdī]** relates both to leaving the practice of sorcery and to obeying the messenger. **[Jalālayn]** reads the verse as a promise that the reward for faith and guarding oneself is better than the condemned pursuit. **[Ibn Kathīr]** draws the same contrast between believing in God and His Messenger and avoiding prohibitions. **[Maʿārif]** stresses that a person’s moral knowledge must become effective in conduct. **[Study Quran]** keeps the focus on the soul’s exchange: the reward is better because the alternative costs the learner his ultimate portion.

**Language and context.** The conditional construction makes the unchosen path imaginable: “had they believed and been reverent” frames a consequence that was available to them, not an abstract ideal unrelated to the scene. **[Ṭabarī]** ties the pronoun back to those who followed the satans’ recitations; **[Qurṭubī]** treats the promised recompense as a real reward from God rather than a merely worldly advantage. The final clause, “if only they knew,” echoes the previous verse’s “if they only knew,” forming a pair: they knew enough to be warned, but did not know in the sense of recognizing the value of what they rejected. **[Maʿārif]** makes that distinction especially clearly. **[Study Quran]** shows why the wording follows the catalogue of harm: the reader is invited to compare what was purchased with what could have been received from God.

**Reflection.** The verse asks what a person is willing to exchange for a promise of control, status, or secret advantage. Its answer is not that every worldly skill is condemned, but that knowledge pursued against a clear warning can become a transaction in which one gives away the most valuable thing. **[Saʿdī]** says that faith and reverence would have been better for them; **[Ibn Kathīr]** emphasizes the Hereafter’s reward; **[Qurṭubī]** keeps the statement tied to divine recompense. The brief verse therefore reverses the apparent calculation of the previous one: what looked like acquisition is loss, and what may look like restraint is gain. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** The close is also an invitation to the reader: knowledge becomes true discernment when it changes what one chooses. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]**

The contrast also explains why the verse names God as the giver of recompense rather than promising that faith always yields immediate comfort. **[Study Quran]** reads the promise in relation to the Hereafter; **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise places the reward beyond the temporary acquisition described before. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Jalālayn]** keep the statement tied to recompense, while **[Saʿdī]** stresses the better outcome for servants. The verse does not ask readers to calculate worship as a bargain; it exposes the poor bargain made by those who trade moral integrity for passing advantage. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** A useful implication is that the warning is paired with an open alternative: even after exposing their choice, the verse speaks in the language of possibility. **[Saʿdī]** reads it as pointing to a path still intelligible to the hearer, and **[Qurṭubī]** ties the reward to God’s favor. The close thus summons people to act on knowledge before a habit hardens into denial. **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]**

The closing “if only they knew” is not a claim that no warning reached the people, because the preceding verse explicitly says that the teachers warned them. **[Maʿārif]** Rather, the phrase exposes a failure to grasp the true value of the alternatives: a temporary gain cannot compare with the lasting reward God gives. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** This is why the verse calls faith and *taqwā* “better” without attaching the promise to immediate worldly advantage. **[Study Quran]** The invitation is practical: hear the warning, recognize the cost of the exchange, and choose the path that preserves one’s share in the Hereafter. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:104
104
*“O you who believe, do not say, ‘Attend to us’; rather say, ‘Regard us,’ and listen. And for the disbelievers is a painful punishment.”*

**Meaning.** The address turns from the conduct of earlier scripture communities to the manners of the believing community. The phrase *rāʿinā* could be used by an Arab speaker to ask someone to attend, wait, or lend an ear, but in the mouths of hostile hearers it could be twisted into an insult or made to resemble a word of contempt. **[Ṭabarī]** records several explanations: some early authorities gloss it as “do not say a word of abuse”; others understand it as “give us your attention”; he also reports that the word had ordinary usage among Arabs and that the problem lay in its ambiguity and the disrespectful use made of it. **[Qurṭubī]** likewise treats the prohibition as a safeguard against a word that could be used mockingly. **[Jalālayn]** explains that Jews exploited a similar-sounding expression in Hebrew, turning a request for attention into a taunt. **[Ibn Kathīr]** brings the parallel of 4:46, where twisted speech is explicitly connected with mockery and distortion.

The replacement, *unẓurnā*, asks the Prophet ﷺ to look toward or consider the speaker, and so preserves the believers’ practical request without the problematic ambiguity. **[Study Quran]** links the first word to an idiom such as “lend me your ear” and notes a further ethical echo in 24:63, where the Messenger must not be addressed as casually as people address one another. **[Saʿdī]** draws a legal and moral principle from the change: a permissible expression may be avoided when it becomes a means to something prohibited, and believers should choose clear words that carry only a good meaning when addressing the Prophet. **[Maʿārif]** also connects the instruction with the hostile use of speech, while **[Ṭabarī]** emphasizes that the commanded alternative accomplishes the same respectful purpose. The verse is therefore not a ban on asking for clarification; it is a direction about how to ask.

**“And listen.”** The imperative can mean listening attentively to revelation, but it also reaches beyond hearing sounds. **[Saʿdī]** notes that the object is left unstated so that the command includes listening to the Qur’an, the Prophet’s teaching, and the practical demand to respond. **[Jalālayn]** explains “listen” as an instruction to hear and obey what is commanded. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the verse among the manners by which the community distinguishes itself from people who use language to deceive. **[Study Quran]** similarly allows the sense of obedience in the command to listen. The pairing of “say” and “listen” is purposeful: respectful speech is not enough if the hearer refuses the instruction that follows.

**Context and language.** The reports differ over the precise historical route by which the word became problematic. **[Ṭabarī]** transmits that some Muslims had used it in a normal Arabic sense, while Jews also used a similar phrase contemptuously; he is cautious about assuming that believers knowingly adopted a foreign insult. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports a named Jewish man, Rifāʿah b. Zayd, who used “hear, without being heard” and related phrases, after which some Muslims misunderstood the expression as an honorific. **[Qurṭubī]** also places the episode in the wider context of distorted speech. **[Maʿārif]** focuses less on reconstructing the incident than on the rule the verse establishes: protect prophetic address from phrases that can be twisted. These explanations need not be forced into one exact chronology; they converge on the Qur’an’s practical instruction. **[Jalālayn]** notes the wordplay, while **[Study Quran]** cautions that the believers themselves are told to change their wording, so the command is intelligible without accusing every user of malicious intent.

**Reflection.** The verse asks a community to take responsibility for the meanings its words can carry. A phrase can be innocent in one speaker’s intention yet harmful in a setting where it becomes a vehicle for mockery. **[Saʿdī]** sees the command as a model of careful and gracious speech; **[Ṭabarī]** stresses reverence for the Messenger; **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Ibn Kathīr]** connect it to the danger of imitating hostile verbal habits. The conclusion about painful punishment reminds the reader that mockery of revelation is not a trivial game, even though the believers are instructed in an alternative. **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** The passage balances firmness and courtesy: believers should not mirror insult, but neither should they lose the ability to ask, learn, and listen. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**

This gives the wording an educational dimension too. **[Study Quran]** compares the respectful address with the warning against treating the Messenger’s calls like ordinary calls among people. **[Saʿdī]** makes the practical rule broader than this single historical word: select a clear expression when ambiguity can be exploited. Yet the believers’ original intention may have been simply to ask for attention, as **[Ṭabarī]** records, and the verse corrects the phrase rather than accusing every speaker of bad faith. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]**

The change of wording is a small example of a broader Qur’anic concern for clear and dignified address. The believers are not told to stop asking the Prophet for attention; they are told to use a phrase that cannot be repurposed as an insult. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** The replacement therefore retains the legitimate need while removing the opening for mockery. **[Saʿdī]** The instruction to listen then completes the scene: the community must not only choose respectful language when speaking but also receive revelation attentively when it is recited. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** This pairing makes the verse a lesson in both sides of communication—speech that honors the messenger and hearing that takes his teaching seriously. **[Maʿārif]** **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:105
105
*“Neither those who disbelieve among the People of the Book nor the idolaters wish that any good should be sent down to you from your Lord; but God singles out for His mercy whom He wills, and God is Possessed of great bounty.”*

**Meaning.** The verse names an obstacle faced by the believing community: some opponents resent that divine guidance has come to it. “Any good” is broad, though revelation and prophethood are central in the immediate context. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the disbelieving People of the Book and the idolaters do not wish for the Qur’an and the guidance given to Muḥammad ﷺ to descend upon the believers; their wish arises from envy and hostility. **[Jalālayn]** similarly identifies the “good” as revelation and reads the addition of the idolaters as a grammatical supplement that broadens the group described. **[Saʿdī]** says “any good” includes neither a little nor a great share of God’s favor, and specifically includes the Book sent to purify and teach the community. **[Maʿārif]** describes the claims of some Jews to be well-wishers of Muslims and contrasts those claims with the envy the verse exposes. **[Study Quran]** interprets the mercy as the prophethood bestowed on Muḥammad, while allowing the wording to point to revelation and its wisdom more generally.

The sentence does not say that every person who belongs to a community shares the same inward motive. Its grammatical subject is “those who disbelieve among the People of the Book” and “the idolaters,” and the discussion has already used qualified terms such as “a party.” **[Qurṭubī]** parses the syntax and notes the possible ways “the idolaters” can be joined to the sentence; **[Ibn Kathīr]** reads the verse as describing the enmity of disbelievers in the setting of the prophetic mission. The qualification matters: scripture’s criticism is directed at a response to revelation, not a license to attribute envy to every individual regardless of conduct. **[Ṭabarī]** describes the hidden resentment that can coexist with public speech; **[Study Quran]** locates the particular issue in the choice of a prophet from a community some opponents did not expect.

**Mercy and election.** The answer to resentment is not that God must distribute prophethood according to the preferences of observers. God “singles out” whom He wills for His mercy. **[Qurṭubī]** records interpretations of “mercy” as prophethood, the Qur’an, or the wider range of divine favors. **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the central gift here as the sending of a messenger and the granting of guidance, both given as grace rather than as something any group can claim to control. **[Jalālayn]** focuses on God’s choosing a prophet for His office. **[Maʿārif]** likewise connects the favor to the prophethood of Muḥammad ﷺ and the revelation sent with him. **[Saʿdī]** broadens the point to the teaching, purification, and knowledge that the Book provides. The closing description, “Possessed of great bounty,” emphasizes that divine generosity is not depleted when it reaches a people whom others envy.

**Language and context.** The indefinite “any good” gives the refusal a sweeping force: the opponents do not wish for even some good to descend, not merely for one particular command. **[Ṭabarī]** ties this to revelation and guidance; **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the syntax of “from your Lord” and the placement of the preposition; **[Jalālayn]** identifies the phrase with inspiration. **[Study Quran]** relates the verse to the preceding instruction not to use an ambiguous address: careful manners do not mean pretending that hostility is absent. The following verse turns to abrogation, a subject that some opponents used to challenge revelation; the sequence therefore moves from resentment of the recipient to a question about the freedom of the Sender. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the verse with the perfect law revealed to the Prophet ﷺ, while **[Saʿdī]** treats the revelation itself as a major favor for which the believers should be grateful.

**Reflection.** Envy becomes especially corrosive when it refuses another person’s good not because that good is harmful, but because someone else has received it. **[Saʿdī]** describes this as opposition to God’s favor; **[Maʿārif]** notes that a claim of goodwill cannot be judged apart from the wish and action it conceals. Yet the verse directs believers away from resentment in return: the gift is God’s mercy, not a possession secured by human rivalry. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** The last phrase is consoling as well as declarative: God’s bounty is great, and His generosity toward one community does not make guidance inaccessible to others. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The verse also prevents opponents’ feelings from becoming the measure of legitimate guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** presents revelation as a mercy granted by God, while **[Qurṭubī]** records the broader sense of mercy as the many forms of divine beneficence. **[Saʿdī]** calls the sending of the Book a favor because it teaches and purifies; **[Study Quran]** connects that favor to prophetic office. The believer is invited to receive guidance with gratitude, not defensiveness, while remembering that the source of the gift is the Lord rather than a human community’s approval. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]**

The verse’s description of God’s bounty also answers the emotional logic of envy. A person who treats revelation as a scarce possession may resent its arrival among another people, but divine mercy is not diminished by being bestowed on someone else. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** Prophethood is granted by God’s choice, not by the approval of those who believe themselves entitled to it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The wording “whom He wills” does not present the choice as arbitrary; the closing description of God’s great bounty points to generosity governed by divine wisdom. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** The proper response for believers is gratitude for guidance and fairness toward individuals, rather than mirroring the resentment the verse exposes. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:106
106
*“Whatever sign We abrogate or cause to be forgotten, We bring one better than it or like it. Do you not know that God has power over all things?”*

**Meaning.** This verse answers objections to change in revealed rulings. A command given for one stage can be replaced by a later command, and revelation may also be withdrawn from recitation or left in place while its legal force changes. **[Ṭabarī]** defines the central sense of *naskh* as transferring people from one ruling to another: a permitted thing may become prohibited, a prohibition may be lifted, and an obligation may be adjusted. He emphasizes that this concerns commands and prohibitions, not reports of past events or descriptions of God. **[Qurṭubī]** likewise treats the verse as a major principle for legal interpretation and says that knowledge of abrogation is necessary when distinguishing what remains binding from what no longer governs practice. **[Ibn Kathīr]** records early explanations that an earlier wording might remain in the Qur’an while its ruling changes, or that a verse could be removed from memory and recitation. **[Jalālayn]** notes these alternatives in its concise gloss; **[Study Quran]** surveys the lexical range of the word, from copying or transfer to erasure.

**Context and readings.** Commentators relate the verse to objections raised when the direction of prayer changed from Jerusalem to the Kaʿbah and when other rulings were changed. **[Qurṭubī]** reports that some Jews used such changes to claim that Muḥammad ﷺ was contradicting himself; **[Maʿārif]** explains that they argued that a later instruction could not be good if the earlier one had also been good. The Qur’an’s reply is that the two rulings may both be wise for their respective times. **[Study Quran]** explains that *naskh* is principally understood in the legal sciences as replacement of an earlier ruling by one revealed later, while commentators also discuss whether the verse can refer to a sign or miracle. **[Ṭabarī]** records variant readings and reports concerning *nunsi-hā* (“cause it to be forgotten”) and *nansaʾ-hā* (“defer it”), and distinguishes retaining a verse’s written form while changing its ruling from removing its recitation. **[Jalālayn]** likewise gives readings in which God abrogates the wording, the ruling, or delays a revelation. These are exegetical possibilities, not a license to call any disliked text abrogated.

The phrase “better than it or like it” does not mean that God first revealed something evil and then corrected a mistake. **[Maʿārif]** explicitly rejects that inference: the earlier command was good for its circumstances, and the later command is good for the circumstances God knows will follow. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains “better” as more beneficial or easier for the community, and “like it” as equal in goodness and reward. **[Saʿdī]** makes the same point: divine abrogation never gives people a lesser benefit than the earlier ruling; it reflects wisdom and mercy, including the facilitation of the law. **[Study Quran]** describes classical examples in which a later ruling brings ease in worldly practice or a greater opportunity for reward. The unit of comparison is the benefit and obligation for servants, not a supposed improvement in God’s knowledge.

**Belief and legal method.** The question “Do you not know?” is an affirmation of God’s power, not uncertainty. **[Ṭabarī]** connects it to the ability to change a ruling while retaining or withdrawing its recited wording. **[Qurṭubī]** warns by implication that one must know the chronology and scope of legal evidence before deriving a rule. **[Study Quran]** stresses that, in the mainstream definition, abrogation concerns legal commands and prohibitions rather than enduring truths about God, history, or moral reality; it also records minority interpretations that reject this technical sense or understand “sign” as miracle. **[Jalālayn]** presents the verse as a direct answer to those who deride abrogation. **[Ibn Kathīr]** adds that human obedience is tested by accepting the command in force, even when an earlier command differs. **[Maʿārif]** compares this to a wise lawgiver who knows from the outset that conditions will change; there is no discovery of error.

**Reflection.** The verse places revelation within the sovereignty already named in the next verse: God may guide a community through stages, and the community’s task is to follow the guidance actually given. **[Saʿdī]** sees in this a sign of mercy, not caprice. **[Qurṭubī]** cautions that claims of abrogation require knowledge; **[Study Quran]** notes that scholars have disagreed about the scope of the doctrine, so it should not be used as a slogan to erase difficult passages. The principle is narrower and more disciplined: where a later revelation truly replaces an earlier legal ruling, the change belongs to the same wise revelation. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]** The believer need not know every divine reason before obeying, but can trust the One whose knowledge encompasses the needs of every time. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The verse therefore requires precision whenever a later text is invoked against an earlier one. **[Qurṭubī]** treats the subject as a technical field because mistaken claims about what is abrogated can distort lawful and unlawful practice. **[Study Quran]** notes that some interpreters take “sign” more broadly, but the mainstream legal use does not make every apparent difference a repeal. The chronology, subject matter, and possibility of harmonizing the instructions all matter. **[Ṭabarī]** distinguishes a changed ruling from a report, which cannot be made false by a later command; **[Maʿārif]** stresses that divine legislation reflects known circumstances rather than ignorance. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:107
107
*“Do you not know that to God belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth, and that apart from God you have no protector or helper?”*

**Meaning.** The verse grounds the previous discussion of abrogation in God’s complete sovereignty. The One who owns the heavens and earth has authority over the revealed law, just as He has authority over creation. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the address first as directed to the Prophet ﷺ and, through him, to the hearers: God alone possesses the kingdom, so He alone determines what He commands, retains, or changes. **[Study Quran]** notes this shift from direct address to the Prophet toward a statement that embraces humanity generally. **[Jalālayn]** describes God as doing what He wills in His dominion, then explains that no guardian besides Him can protect people from His judgment and no helper can repel the consequence of disobedience. **[Saʿdī]** makes the connection explicit: as God directs events in creation, He also legislates with wisdom and mercy; no servant can veto His decree or object as if he were an equal owner.

**The logic of the sequence.** The question is not a new topic detached from 2:106. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places both verses together: God owns His creatures, tests them by commands, and may order or prohibit what He wills. The test is not that a ruling changes because the first was mistaken; rather, the servant demonstrates obedience by following the messenger in each stage. **[Maʿārif]** likewise says that the change of a command is not objectionable when the lawgiver knows the conditions and benefits of each stage. **[Qurṭubī]** draws attention to the legal setting of the previous verse and the force of this one as a response to those who challenge divine authority. **[Ṭabarī]** ties the declaration of sovereignty to the ability to abrogate or defer a sign. **[Jalālayn]** reads the paired questions as affirmations: the hearer is expected to recognize both God’s power and His ownership.

**Protector and helper.** The closing clause uses two related descriptions. A *walī* is a close guardian or one who takes charge of protection; a *naṣīr* is one who helps or gives support against an adversary. **[Jalālayn]** distinguishes the protector who safeguards a person from the helper who wards off punishment. **[Saʿdī]** develops the positive side of the image: God is the guardian who secures His servants’ interests and the helper who repels harm, and His legislation is part of that care. **[Study Quran]** remarks on the broadening of the address, so that the reminder is not limited to one dispute between Jews and Muslims. **[Ibn Kathīr]** stresses that no rival can oppose God’s judgment or call Him to account. **[Qurṭubī]** connects the words to the practical need for reliance upon God rather than defiance of His revealed direction.

The verse does not deny that human beings may assist one another in ordinary life.
