Its point is ultimate dependence: no creature can provide a protection that overrides God’s dominion or rescue anyone from a judgment He has decreed. **[Ṭabarī]** reads the negation as “other than God,” not as a denial of all created means. **[Saʿdī]** says that created causes remain under divine governance, and that a believer can use them without mistaking them for independent powers. **[Maʿārif]** draws the same comfort from God’s ownership: the believer’s security rests not on the approval of opponents but on the Lord who controls the outcome. **[Jalālayn]** underscores that no protector or helper can prevent His chastisement if it comes.

**Reflection.** In the surrounding passage, some object to a change in revealed instruction and others resent the gift of prophecy. This verse directs both the challenge and the believer’s anxiety to the same answer: ownership belongs to God. **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]** The Lord who has authority over creation is not bound to preserve one ruling for all circumstances, and His servants are not abandoned when a command becomes difficult or opponents object. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** The two questions also turn knowledge into responsibility. To “know” that God owns the heavens and earth is to accept that guidance comes from Him, not to treat revelation as a negotiation among equal parties. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** The concluding pair invites reliance: seek help through lawful means, but place ultimate trust in the One who alone can guard and deliver. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**

This is why the verse moves from a question about power to a reminder about the servant’s dependence. **[Maʿārif]** describes God as both guardian and helper: He legislates for the good of His servants and can protect them against opponents. **[Ibn Kathīr]** adds that no one can question God about His acts while every servant remains answerable. **[Study Quran]** observes that the address widens beyond the Prophet ﷺ, so the principle belongs to every generation hearing the Qur’an. The change of qiblah, already central to the surrounding dispute, becomes one example of a broader truth: the community follows God’s direction because He owns its present and its future. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The paired questions ask the hearer to recognize not only that God owns all things but that ownership carries authority. The power to give a ruling, alter a direction, or remove a protection belongs to the same Lord who governs the heavens and earth. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The statement does not imply that a servant may never seek help from another person; it denies that any created ally can overrule God or guarantee safety from His judgment. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]** Read after the discussion of abrogation, the verse asks the objector to trust the lawgiver’s wisdom rather than treat a changed command as evidence of weakness. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]**

The wording “protector or helper” also balances fear with reliance. A believer may be vulnerable to opposition in the world, yet no opponent has ultimate power over the divine decree. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** The verse therefore invites obedience without despair: God’s sovereignty is the reason His command must be followed and the reason His servants can turn to Him for protection. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:108
108
*“Or do you wish to question your messenger as Moses was questioned before? Whoever exchanges belief for disbelief has surely strayed from the right way.”*

**Meaning.** The verse warns against questions that are not asked to learn but to challenge, impose conditions, or refuse a messenger’s authority. **[Ṭabarī]** records several reports about the occasion: some say Rāfiʿ b. Ḥuraymila and Wahb b. Zayd demanded a book sent down from heaven and rivers flowing before they would follow; others relate the demand that God be shown openly, or that Ṣafā be turned to gold. **[Qurṭubī]** mentions the demand for a visible manifestation and reports the request for a heavenly book and rivers. **[Jalālayn]** links the verse to Meccans who asked for extraordinary signs, while also recalling that Moses’ people asked to see God openly. **[Maʿārif]** emphasizes that the problem is not a sincere request for guidance but an insolent demand whose purpose is to cast aspersions on the prophet or question divine wisdom. **[Study Quran]** also cites demands for a book, rivers, or a golden Ṣafā, while connecting the passage with other Qur’anic cautions against questions that create difficulty.

The reference to Moses evokes a recurring pattern in the history of revelation. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains that some Israelites asked Moses to show them their Lord openly and recounts the prophetic warning against excessive questioning and disputation. **[Ṭabarī]** preserves more than one account rather than reducing the verse to a single event. This range matters: the Qur’an’s rebuke is not dependent on deciding whether the immediate speakers asked for a written book, a miraculous sign, or a direct vision. Each report describes a demand made as a precondition for faith. **[Qurṭubī]** understands *am* here as a shift into a reproachful question; **[Jalālayn]** explains the question as a warning about exchanging faith for unbelief. The shared ethical point is clear even where the reports differ in detail.

**Good questions and bad questions.** The verse should not be used to condemn inquiry, learning, or requests for clarification. **[Saʿdī]** carefully distinguishes questions asked for instruction and sound guidance—which the Qur’an elsewhere approves—from questions of obstinacy and objection. He cites the contrast between asking the learned when one does not know and demanding unnecessary matters that may burden the community. **[Study Quran]** similarly notes that the Qur’an praises the Companions’ questions about charity, orphans, and other matters, while warning against inquiries that are made to dispute with prophets. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the hadith that excessive questioning and disagreement with messengers contributed to the ruin of earlier peoples, and the Prophet’s direction to obey commands as far as one can and avoid prohibitions. **[Ṭabarī]** includes a report contrasting the demanding questions of earlier communities with the easier path of repentance and prayer granted to this community. **[Qurṭubī]**’s linguistic note that the *am* is reproach supports the distinction: it is the posture of the questioner, not the faculty of questioning, that is being censured.

The warning that follows—whoever exchanges belief for disbelief has strayed from the right path—names the consequence of such a posture. **[Jalālayn]** says that the person abandons belief by failing to consider clear proofs and demanding something else instead. **[Maʿārif]** interprets the demand as a rejection of the proper relation between creature and Creator: people may ask for guidance, but they cannot dictate the precise manner in which God must act. **[Saʿdī]** says that these questions may lead their author into disbelief because they turn the messenger’s mission into a contest of conditions. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reads the “right way” as the path of faith and obedience, not mere compliance with a spectacle.

**Reflection.** The verse invites a distinction between seeking light and demanding control. **[Study Quran]** notes that other passages warn against questions whose answers would cause distress, yet also recount revelation prompted by sincere questions; context and intention matter. **[Saʿdī]**’s distinction prevents the warning from silencing study: ask in order to understand and obey, not to postpone obedience until every preferred sign appears. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** The memory of Moses’ community is a mirror rather than a taunt: believers too can turn a genuine need into a condition that God has not imposed. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The “middle” or “straight” way named at the end is not an absence of thought; it is a path that joins understanding with trust and action. **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The final phrase also explains why the warning is severe: the questioner can turn away from faith by treating revelation as acceptable only if it conforms to a demand of his own. **[Ṭabarī]** preserves reports with different requested signs, so no one report should be treated as the only possible occasion. **[Maʿārif]** identifies the shared fault as insolence rather than honest uncertainty. **[Study Quran]** balances the warning by recalling positive questions elsewhere in the Qur’an. The “right way” is thus not a ban on thought; it is a stable course in which inquiry serves recognition and obedience. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**

The “right way” in the final clause is not a compromise negotiated between revelation and an opponent’s preferred spectacle. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** The imagery of exchange makes clear that abandoning belief for unbelief is a choice with a cost, not a neutral delay while one waits for a more entertaining sign. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** The verse therefore leaves room for a learner to ask what a teaching means, while refusing to make a demanded miracle the price of hearing the messenger. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** Its comparison with Moses also gives the warning a historical depth: the same pattern can recur whenever people mistake control over the form of proof for a sincere desire to know. **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:109
109
*“Many of the People of the Book wish to turn you back into disbelievers after you have believed, out of envy from their own selves, even after the truth has become clear to them. So pardon and forbear until God brings His command. Truly God has power over all things.”*

**Meaning.** The verse names the motive behind an attempt to reverse the believers’ faith: envy of a favor granted by God, not an instruction in their own scripture. **[Ṭabarī]** explains “from their own selves” as a desire originating in them, and “after the truth became clear” as recognition of Muḥammad’s prophethood and the message he brought. **[Qurṭubī]** parses the phrase as emphasizing that this envy came from their own initiative, not from a command in a revealed book; he distinguishes blameworthy envy, which wishes that another lose a blessing, from commendable emulation, which wishes to share in good without taking it away. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports that some Jewish opponents knew the signs of the messenger but rejected him through envy and transgression, including because he was not from their own people. **[Study Quran]** records several proposed settings, including former Jewish companions being taunted after Uḥud, but also notes that commentators connect the “truth” especially with the recognition of the Prophet in earlier scripture. The wording “many” does not say every person among the People of the Book shared this intention.

The command “pardon and forbear” gives the believers a disciplined response. **[Jalālayn]** explains pardon as leaving retaliation and forbearance as holding back from aggression until God’s command arrives; **[Qurṭubī]** distinguishes *ʿafw* as not taking a person to account and *ṣafḥ* as removing the trace of resentment from the heart. **[Saʿdī]** reads the instruction within the believers’ vulnerable early situation: respond to grave provocation with restraint until a later command establishes how to address continued hostility. **[Maʿārif]** likewise connects the verse to a community that was then conscious of its weakness and was told that God’s power would make future guidance possible. **[Ibn Kathīr]** presents patience and pardon as qualities the Prophet and believers are to maintain while awaiting God’s aid. This is not a statement that injustice is good or that every harm must go unanswered; it is a command situated in a particular stage of revelation.

**Abrogation and ethical scope.** Some exegetes describe the instruction as later abrogated by commands concerning fighting or the status of hostile groups. **[Qurṭubī]** records the view attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās that 9:29 supersedes it, and **[Saʿdī]** notes that a later command to fight came after this period. **[Study Quran]**, however, reports al-Rāzī’s argument that pardon and forbearance as virtues need not be abrogated by a legal command to fight: the two can apply in different circumstances, and fighting itself does not cancel patience, justice, or restraint. **[Ibn Kathīr]** describes the sequence as forbearance until God grants aid. **[Maʿārif]** treats the wording “until God brings His command” as anticipation of a later legal direction. These interpretations should be kept distinct rather than compressed into the claim that the verse has no continuing ethical force.

**Language and belief.** “Hasadan min ʿindi anfusihim” locates the impulse in the self. **[Qurṭubī]** says the phrase intensifies the attribution: they wished the believers to lose their faith from their own envy, not because they had found a revealed warrant for that wish. **[Ṭabarī]** ties that envy to the resentment of a community receiving revelation. **[Jalālayn]** describes the truth as the Prophet’s status made clear in the Torah. **[Study Quran]** broadens “the truth” to revelation itself in some interpretations. The final sentence, that God is powerful over all things, balances the command to wait: restraint is not helplessness or surrender of justice, because the outcome is not beyond God’s power. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

**Reflection.** The verse condemns envy without making the believers’ response another form of envy. The proper answer is not to wish that opponents lose every good, but to remain grounded in the faith they would dislodge. **[Qurṭubī]**’s contrast between destructive envy and emulation clarifies that a person may seek comparable virtue without resenting its possessor. **[Study Quran]** points to patience under taunts; **[Saʿdī]** reads pardon as a command to govern anger until a just course is given. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** The words “until God brings His command” teach that restraint can be active: it protects the community from acting on provocation and leaves judgment to a wider moral and legal order. **[Maʿārif]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Saʿdī]**

The command to pardon also has a measured endpoint in the wording: “until God brings His command.” **[Ṭabarī]** treats the instruction as a real direction for the people addressed at that moment, not as a denial that later circumstances may require other rulings. **[Study Quran]** records the disagreement over abrogation and cautions that ethical virtues do not automatically disappear when legal policy changes. **[Qurṭubī]** distinguishes pardon from the inward release of resentment, while **[Saʿdī]** stresses patience until guidance clarifies the next step. Forgiveness here does not mean declaring the envy truthful, nor does restraint require trusting every hostile claim. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]**

The word “many” is an important limit on the accusation. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]** The verse identifies a substantial hostile group without turning every member of the People of the Book into a single moral subject; the Qur’an elsewhere acknowledges those who believe and act uprightly. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The timing “after the truth has become clear” also distinguishes the envy described here from honest disagreement or a person who has not yet understood the message. **[Study Quran]** **[Jalālayn]** What is condemned is the wish to reverse another’s faith despite recognizing the evidence, not the mere fact that communities differ. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** This precision keeps the verse’s rebuke tied to conduct and motive rather than ancestry. **[Maʿārif]**

## 2:110
110
*“And establish the prayer and give the alms. Whatever good you send forth for yourselves, you will find it with God. Truly God sees whatever you do.”*

**Meaning.** After discussing envy, challenging questions, and the command to pardon, the verse directs believers toward acts they can perform now: establish prayer, give alms, and send good ahead for their own account. **[Ṭabarī]** reads the first command as establishing the prescribed prayer and the second as giving the obligatory alms; he also sees the general expression “whatever good” as including other acts of obedience. **[Jalālayn]** mentions charity and maintaining kinship among the good deeds included. **[Saʿdī]** says the believers should occupy themselves with present duties and every form of nearness to God rather than being consumed by hostility. **[Ibn Kathīr]** describes the commands as a means of preserving righteous practice and connects the promise to the deeds that believers will find with God. **[Study Quran]** notes the close pairing of prayer and charity elsewhere in the Qur’an and reads the promise as one whose fruits are received on the Day of Judgment.

**“For yourselves.”** The phrase does not imply that an act of worship is a private investment detached from concern for others. It tells the worshipper that no good is lost: what is given to another in charity, or what is performed in obedience, is stored for the giver with God. **[Qurṭubī]** treats “whatever good” as a general expression and discusses the wording of the verse in relation to the reward preserved for the servant. **[Ṭabarī]** includes both public and hidden action in God’s knowledge and recompense. **[Maʿārif]** stresses that no particle of good is wasted and that believers should not think prayer and charity are spiritually insignificant while they await later developments. **[Saʿdī]** uses the promise to encourage every act of devotion, since its reward is kept in full. **[Jalālayn]** calls the future “finding” the discovery of one’s recompense with God. The image of sending something ahead turns each present act into a provision for a meeting still to come.

**Prayer and almsgiving.** Prayer orders the believer’s attention and regular obedience; almsgiving turns that obedience outward into a duty toward those with a claim upon wealth. **[Ibn Kathīr]** emphasizes that the believers must continue both practices even amid social pressure. **[Maʿārif]** says that these deeds retain their full value whether or not the community has yet received a further command concerning conflict. **[Saʿdī]** frames the pair as immediate work: establish prayer, pay zakāh, and do all the good deeds available in the present. **[Ṭabarī]** connects prayer with expiation for past mistakes and zakāh with purification; his explanation of “God sees” makes the warning and promise comprehensive, since neither public virtue nor hidden wrongdoing escapes the divine knowledge. **[Qurṭubī]** likewise links the general command to the range of good actions and their effects on the soul.

**The closing assurance.** “God sees whatever you do” is more than a statement that God observes outward movements. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the deeds are seen whether done openly or secretly, and that the statement carries both promise and warning: good is rewarded and wrongdoing is not concealed. **[Jalālayn]** says that God will requite the worshipper for the good he sends ahead. **[Study Quran]** preserves a report that the Prophet ﷺ would sometimes pause after reciting the verse to respond in prayer, a practice that reflects the verse’s invitation to make worship immediate. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the promise in the context of the believer’s future meeting with the record of deeds. **[Maʿārif]** emphasizes that every act will receive its full spiritual recompense.

**Reflection.** The transition from pardon to prayer is not an escape from the world but a way to act without being ruled by resentment. **[Saʿdī]** describes worship and service as the believer’s present responsibility; **[Study Quran]** makes the reward future-facing, yet the command itself is immediate. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** The verse also joins inward devotion and material responsibility: a prayer that does not shape conduct is incomplete, and wealth becomes a means of worship when it is given as God commands. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** Whatever the community’s circumstances, there is good it can send forward. **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** The assurance that God sees all action comforts the unnoticed giver and admonishes the person tempted to perform virtue only for an audience. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**

The word “good” leaves the door open to more than the two named acts, without making prayer and zakāh optional examples. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Ṭabarī]** read the wording generally; the specified acts provide the foundation, and other obedience can also be sent ahead. **[Saʿdī]** includes every act of nearness to God, while **[Jalālayn]** mentions charity and kinship. The verse’s promise encourages deeds whose immediate benefit may be hidden: a person gives, prays, or serves without seeing the full return in this life, yet the Lord sees and preserves it. **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The text therefore grounds hope in divine knowledge rather than public recognition. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The phrase “send forth” gives the present tense of worship an image of movement toward the future. A deed may leave the hand, the household, or the worshipper’s immediate notice, but it is not lost; it is preserved with God. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** The assurance makes the command especially relevant after the instruction to pardon: the believer can do good without requiring immediate recognition or repayment from the person who caused harm. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** Prayer and almsgiving remain concrete duties, while the open word “good” keeps the instruction from being confined to those two forms alone. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** The closing statement that God sees all action assures the unnoticed worshipper and warns anyone tempted to conceal wrongdoing. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:111
111111
*“And they say, ‘None shall enter the Garden unless he is a Jew or a Christian.’ Those are their hopes. Say, ‘Bring your proof, if you are truthful.’”*

**Meaning.** The verse rejects competing claims that salvation belongs exclusively to one community by inheritance or name. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the plural statement as two separate claims: the Jews said that only Jews would enter the Garden, and the Christians said the same of Christians. The wording does not mean that they agreed with one another; it compresses parallel assertions into one report. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the same interpretation and notes grammatical explanations of *hūdan* and *naṣārā*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the claim with earlier boasts that the Fire would touch the People of the Book for only a limited number of days, and with the claim “we are God’s children and beloved.” **[Maʿārif]** says each party had confused religion with communal or racial affiliation, leaving aside fidelity to revealed teaching and righteous action. **[Jalālayn]** situates the claims in the dispute between Jews of Madinah and Christians of Najrān. **[Study Quran]** links the verse to later passages in which groups claim special access to God’s favor without proof.

The phrase “those are their hopes” calls the claims wishes, not demonstrated doctrine. **[Saʿdī]** says that any assertion about admission to Paradise must be supported by evidence; if one party may make an unproved claim, another could assert its opposite with equal force. The demand “bring your proof” makes reasoning part of the Qur’anic response. **[Qurṭubī]** defines *burhān* as evidence that produces certainty and notes that the verse affirms the legitimacy of seeking proof. **[Ṭabarī]** describes the statement as an empty wish placed before God without warrant. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites early exegetes who gloss the command as “bring your evidence.” **[Maʿārif]** points out that the Books themselves could have tested the exclusivist claims, since revelation calls people to the truth rather than to a bare communal label. **[Study Quran]** notes that the same term for proof is used elsewhere in the Qur’an when claims about worship or divine authority are tested.

**Context and argument.** The verse is not a declaration that every person who ever belonged to a prior community is excluded, nor does it itself settle every later theological question about salvation. Its immediate target is the unsupported proposition that Paradise is guaranteed solely by group membership. **[Maʿārif]** says that those who followed their prophet faithfully in an earlier dispensation were not being condemned by the claim’s refutation; the following verse states the general principle of sincere submission and virtue. **[Study Quran]** likewise notes a range of interpretation on the relation of this passage to earlier communities. **[Ibn Kathīr]** emphasizes the false security of assuming that a name alone can overcome sin. **[Ṭabarī]** keeps the contrast focused on the rival assertions and the absence of proof. **[Jalālayn]** records that each group claimed the Garden for itself in the Prophet’s presence. The sequence therefore answers rhetoric with evidence and identity with ethical accountability.

**Language.** The expression *hūdan* may be understood as “Jews,” a plural related to *hāʾid*, or as a form tied to Jewish identity; **[Ṭabarī]** records alternative grammatical derivations, and **[Qurṭubī]** does likewise. The word *amānī* suggests wishes or fantasies—claims one hopes God will grant, not knowledge of what God has promised. **[Jalālayn]** reads it as false desire. **[Saʿdī]** draws a general rule from the imperative: whoever makes a claim bears responsibility for substantiating it. **[Study Quran]** points to other Qur’anic uses of *burhān* as decisive evidence. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the passage to the demand that opponents prove their assertion rather than rely on inherited confidence.

**Reflection.** The verse challenges religious complacency without dismissing the importance of religious belonging: a community’s name is not a substitute for truth, worship, and moral response. **[Maʿārif]** stresses authentic doctrine and good deeds; **[Saʿdī]** says the unproved claim collapses when tested by a demand for evidence. The challenge is also reciprocal. **[Qurṭubī]**’s account of proof rules out a standard applied only to opponents: believers must be willing to examine their own claims. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** The next verse supplies the alternative to exclusive boasting: submit sincerely to God and do good. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Qurṭubī]** The movement from wish to proof and then to conduct makes the passage’s standard practical rather than merely polemical. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]**

The challenge is a method for separating conviction from wishful certainty. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that a *burhān* is evidence that yields confidence, and **[Study Quran]** shows how the same Qur’anic term appears when claims about divine power and worship are tested. **[Saʿdī]** adds that an assertion without proof can be reversed just as easily by an opponent, leaving no way to distinguish truth from preference. The next verse supplies a positive criterion rather than merely negating the claim: submission to God joined with *iḥsān*. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**

The challenge to bring proof is an invitation to test the claim, not a demand that the speaker accept whatever answer the audience prefers. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** In the next verse, the argument is not left as a bare negation: the Qur’an names submission to God joined with excellence in action as the criterion of hope. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** This transition matters because it replaces exclusive boasting with a principle that can be examined in conduct. The claimed guarantee must be weighed against revelation, while the proposed alternative requires sincerity and virtue. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]** The verse thus asks its hearers to move from assertion to evidence and from identity to accountable practice. **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:112
112112
*“Nay, whoever submits his face to God while being virtuous shall have his reward with his Lord; no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve.”*

**Meaning.** The opening “Nay” rejects the exclusive claims just reported and replaces them with a standard that can be applied to any person: sincere orientation toward God joined to virtuous action. **[Ṭabarī]** interprets submitting the face as surrendering oneself and one’s deeds to God; the face stands for the person as a whole. **[Qurṭubī]** says the face may signify submission, sincerity, or a person’s purpose, and that it is singled out as the noblest visible part. **[Jalālayn]** similarly explains it as directing oneself to God and obeying His commands. **[Saʿdī]** describes the two conditions: sincere devotion to God and excellence in worship by following the divinely revealed way. **[Ibn Kathīr]** makes that pair explicit as sincerity for God and conformity to the Messenger’s teaching. **[Study Quran]** notes that some commentators understand the wording broadly as submitting one’s body and actions, while others give particular weight to sincerity and religious conformity.

The verse does not reduce religion to private feeling. “While being virtuous” qualifies the submission: an inward claim must be embodied in sound action. **[Saʿdī]** says that good intention alone does not authorize a person to invent forms of worship, and **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the principle that an action not conforming to the prophetic way is rejected. **[Maʿārif]** also presents obedience to God in belief and action as the essence of religion, while insisting that worship must be sincere rather than expedient. **[Qurṭubī]** records several interpretations of *muḥsin*: one who worships well, acts righteously, or submits in a state of excellence. These explanations converge on the joining of inward allegiance and outward conduct, even when commentators debate how the verse applies to earlier communities.

**Paradise and assurance.** Such a person’s reward is “with his Lord,” an expression of divine keeping and generosity rather than a wage that God is obliged to pay. **[Jalālayn]** identifies the reward as Paradise and the promised safety as belonging to the Hereafter. **[Ṭabarī]** understands the statement as assurance to those whose deeds are offered sincerely. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains that their reward is preserved with God and that fear and grief will be removed. **[Study Quran]** notes the force of the “face” metaphor in prayer, where prostration places the most honored part of the body on the ground before God. **[Qurṭubī]** adds that Arabic can use “face” for a person’s whole being or intention. The promise is not a guarantee of a life without hardship; “no fear” and “no grief” describe the final security God grants, not immunity from trials in this world.

**Context and scope.** The verse follows the challenge to produce proof for an exclusive claim. **[Saʿdī]** calls the principle clear and general: what matters is not an inherited label but devotion to God expressed in right action. **[Maʿārif]** explains the passage in the context of successive revealed laws and says that obedience to one’s prophet in his time is distinct from clinging to an earlier law after a later messenger has come. **[Study Quran]** records both broad readings and more restrictive readings offered by some commentators, including the question of how sincerity and conformity are assessed. This interpretive discussion should not be flattened into an assertion that the verse has no ethical breadth, nor into a claim that any self-defined spirituality meets its conditions. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** The text itself names submission and *iḥsān* together.

**Reflection.** The face is the part of a person most readily seen by others, but the verse points beyond appearance to the direction of the whole self. **[Study Quran]** connects the image to prostration; **[Qurṭubī]** connects it to intention; **[Saʿdī]** joins that intention to observance. The answer to communal boasting is therefore neither indifference to truth nor a new boast about one’s own group. It is accountability before God. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** A believer asks whether worship is directed to God alone and whether its manner follows revealed guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** The promise of no fear or grief offers hope to those who live by that standard, while leaving judgment and reward with the Lord. **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**

The two conditions also guard against opposite errors. Sincerity without sound guidance may turn personal preference into worship, while outward conformity without sincerity becomes display. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explicitly joins intention and conformity; **[Saʿdī]** says both must be present. **[Maʿārif]** describes the essence of religion as obedience in belief and action, not social identity. **[Study Quran]** acknowledges that commentators differ over the verse’s application to earlier communities, but the wording itself insists on a real response to God. Its promise is therefore both inclusive in its call and demanding in its content. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The promise is stated in the plural because the standard is open to every servant who meets it, not reserved for one lineage. **[Jalālayn]** reads the absence of fear and grief as the security of the final life; **[Study Quran]** connects it with the reward kept by God. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**

The image of submitting the face adds a bodily dimension to the principle: the face is turned toward God, and in prayer it is also the part lowered in prostration. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** The metaphor can therefore hold intention and embodied worship together without making outward gesture the whole of faith. **[Ṭabarī]** The condition of *iḥsān* keeps submission from being merely formal; the worshipper seeks excellence in the manner God has taught. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The promise of safety belongs to the final return to God, so it offers hope amid present trials without suggesting that believers will never experience fear or grief in this life. **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]**

## 2:113
113113
*“The Jews say, ‘The Christians stand on nothing,’ and the Christians say, ‘The Jews stand on nothing,’ though they recite the Book. Likewise did those who know not speak words like theirs. God will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that wherein they differed.”*

**Meaning.** The verse exposes reciprocal condemnation between two communities that both claim scriptural authority. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the Jews denied the Christians a share in divine reward and the Christians made the corresponding denial about the Jews; the two statements are reported together even though they contradict each other. **[Jalālayn]** notes that each party rejected a figure the other honored—Christians rejected Moses, and Jews rejected Jesus—while both recited scriptures that testified to earlier revelation. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the dispute in the context of a delegation from Najrān and the Jewish scholars, but also reads the verse as exposing the confusion of each party’s claim. **[Study Quran]** says the wording can mean “stand on nothing” or “follow nothing,” and records disagreement about whether the verse describes a particular encounter or a general tendency. **[Maʿārif]** sees the quarrel as the result of replacing fidelity to revelation with sectarian identity.

The clause “though they recite the Book” sharpens the rebuke. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the Torah contains testimony concerning Jesus and the Gospel affirms Moses and the Torah; recitation should have reminded each community of truths it shared with the other. **[Jalālayn]** makes the same point through the scriptural testimony of Moses and Jesus. **[Ibn Kathīr]** highlights the contradiction between reading scripture and denying its testimony. **[Qurṭubī]** treats the clause as a circumstantial qualification: their recitation should have restrained such blanket dismissal. **[Study Quran]** notes that some commentators understand “stand on nothing” as saying the other community has no valid foundation, while others link the phrase to the historical changes each group made to its religion. The verse condemns the polemical denial, not the act of distinguishing truth from falsehood by evidence.

The words “those who know not” widen the pattern beyond Jews and Christians. **[Ṭabarī]** identifies them in reports with the idolaters who had no revealed book, and **[Ibn Kathīr]** mentions the polytheists who also made claims about the other communities. **[Jalālayn]** understands the expression as ignorant Arabs and others who repeated the same kind of accusation. **[Study Quran]** records several identifications, including earlier peoples and the pagan opponents of Islam. **[Saʿdī]** reads the verse as a general human tendency: when desire and envy prevail, each group can pronounce the other misguided without weighing the evidence. The comparison is not an equation of all beliefs; it is an exposure of an argumentative habit that replaces proof with mutual dismissal.

**Divine judgment.** The final clause postpones the ultimate resolution of the disagreements to God’s judgment on the Day of Resurrection. **[Study Quran]** places this within a recurring Qur’anic theme: God will disclose the truth of religious disputes at the final judgment. **[Ṭabarī]** says He will judge between the disputants justly. **[Jalālayn]** explains that those who affirmed truth will be distinguished from those who falsified it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the promise to the return of all people to their Lord. **[Qurṭubī]** points to the grammatical force of the final statement and its eschatological timing. **[Maʿārif]** stresses that judgment is not decided by the competing slogans heard in the world.

**Reflection.** The verse does not tell the reader to abandon conviction; it warns against turning conviction into a license for baseless condemnation. **[Saʿdī]** says that salvation belongs to those who affirm God’s messengers and obey His commands, not to those who merely denounce others. **[Study Quran]** shows that the passage can be read across eras and communities. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** Reading a scripture should deepen humility before what it teaches, not supply a shield against the parts that challenge one’s own claims. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** The final judgment also frees believers from pretending to know every person’s final standing: they must speak truthfully and act justly, while leaving the full adjudication of disputed histories to God. **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The promise of a final judgment should not be misread as permission to avoid honest discussion in the present. **[Study Quran]** notes that the Qur’an repeatedly reserves the ultimate disclosure of religious disputes for God, while also sending prophets to clarify truth. **[Qurṭubī]**’s grammatical note that the scripture is being recited at the very time of accusation underscores the irony: the parties have evidence before them yet speak as if no one else has a foundation. **[Maʿārif]** describes this as a shift from shared divine teaching to group pride. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** A believer can therefore state convictions clearly while leaving ultimate judgment to God. **[Jalālayn]**

The repeated “they say” also exposes how easily rivalry turns theological language into a mirror of one’s own group. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]**

The added phrase “though they recite the Book” points to a failure of method as well as a failure of civility. The parties possess a source that could correct their claims, yet they use sweeping denial instead of attending to what it says. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The verse does not require readers to pretend that disagreements are unimportant; it warns against declaring an entire community groundless while ignoring shared scripture and the evidence within it. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** By adding “those who know not,” the verse also removes any comfort that the same polemical posture becomes acceptable when adopted by people without a book. **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]** Every group is responsible for what it says, whether it can appeal to a text or not. **[Saʿdī]**

## 2:114
114114
*“And who does greater wrong than one who bars God’s mosques, lest His Name be remembered therein, and strives for their ruin? Such people should not enter them except in fear. For them is disgrace in this world and a great punishment in the Hereafter.”*

**Meaning.** The verse describes a grave injustice: preventing worshippers from remembering God in His places of worship and actively seeking their ruin. **[Ṭabarī]** treats “ruin” as both physical destruction and the emptying of a mosque by preventing prayer and remembrance. **[Saʿdī]** likewise distinguishes material ruin—demolition, defilement, and neglect—from spiritual ruin, which occurs when people are barred from prayer and other acts of devotion. **[Jalālayn]** explains that the wrongdoer both keeps people from the mosques and works to destroy them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the verse with those who obstructed worship at the Sacred Mosque and with earlier attacks on Jerusalem. **[Study Quran]** records several proposed referents: the Temple in Jerusalem, the Kaʿbah at the time of Ḥudaybiyyah, and, more generally, anyone who prevents access to a house of worship. The wording “the mosques of God” is broad enough to state a general moral principle even when the historical occasion is disputed.

The opening question—“who does greater wrong?”—is a rebuke, not a request for information. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Jalālayn]** explain the rhetorical form as meaning that none is more unjust than such a person. **[Saʿdī]** says the description includes anyone who shares this trait, whether the episode concerns the people of the Elephant, Quraysh at Ḥudaybiyyah, or the Christians connected in reports with the desolation of Jerusalem. **[Maʿārif]** discusses several events that commentators associated with the passage and stresses that the Qur’an’s general wording establishes an enduring rule. **[Study Quran]** notes that some exegetes understand idolatrous use of the Kaʿbah as a kind of spiritual ruin, since a sanctuary dedicated to the One God has been filled with practices contrary to its purpose. These applications should be distinguished rather than collapsed into one certain occasion.

**“Except in fear.”** The phrase has been interpreted as a prediction of the humiliation of those who barred the mosques and as a command that they not enter arrogantly or without fear of God. **[Jalālayn]** explains it as entry under fear and interprets the following disgrace as defeat, captivity, or payment of tribute in the historical setting. **[Ṭabarī]** discusses the construction and associates the threatened fear with the fate of those who sought to obstruct worship. **[Qurṭubī]** surveys legal questions about non-Muslims entering mosques, while **[Saʿdī]** draws a ruling about not enabling those who actively work to prevent worship. The verse’s moral point is not that worshippers should act lawlessly; it is that those who weaponize power against worship will not enjoy a secure claim to sacred space. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the prediction to the eventual entry of Muslims into Makkah and the removal of idolatrous control.

The consequences are named in both worlds. **[Maʿārif]** says that the groups associated with obstructing mosques were later humiliated, while the final punishment belongs to God’s judgment. **[Study Quran]** cautions that “disgrace” can describe a broad public humiliation and should not be reduced to one political event. **[Saʿdī]** contrasts the obstruction of worship with the honor of building and maintaining mosques materially and spiritually. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the relevant legal implications; **[Jalālayn]** identifies the fire as the Hereafter’s punishment. The threat is directed at the described injustice, not at every disagreement over the use of a building.

**Reflection.** A sanctuary is not honored merely by its walls. **[Saʿdī]** says that preventing remembrance can ruin a mosque as surely as tearing it down. The verse therefore places access, safety, and worship at the center of the institution’s purpose. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Its universal phrasing also warns against treating a sacred place as property to be used against God’s worshippers. **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]** The punishment language teaches accountability, while the positive contrast is the work of making spaces where prayer, praise, and learning can flourish. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The broad language also makes the verse relevant beyond the named historical disputes. **[Study Quran]** records the view that anyone who stops people from entering a house of worship or obstructs prayer falls under the moral description. **[Saʿdī]** includes both the person who demolishes a building and the person who leaves it standing while barring remembrance inside. **[Maʿārif]** says the general wording lays down a lasting rule for desecrating mosques and hindering worship. At the same time, juristic discussions of who may enter a mosque are not identical to the verse’s accusation against those who strive to ruin it. **[Qurṭubī]** treats those as separate legal questions. The central test is whether conduct disables worship and remembrance. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]**

The contrast makes mosque maintenance more than architecture: it includes keeping a place available for prayer, praise, and learning. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**

The phrase “strives for their ruin” depicts intention and effort, not only the final condition of a building. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** A mosque may be ruined by dismantling its walls, but it can also be emptied of its purpose when people are driven away from remembrance and prayer. This reading makes the moral test broader while keeping the accusation specific to obstruction and destructive effort. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** It does not authorize private retaliation or erase the legal procedures discussed by the jurists; the verse condemns the act of barring worship and entrusts punishment to God. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** The lasting lesson is to protect access to worship and to maintain sacred spaces for the purpose for which they are dedicated. **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:115
115115
*“To God belong the East and the West; wherever you turn, there is the Face of God. Truly God is All-Encompassing, Knowing.”*

**Meaning.** The verse begins by declaring that every direction belongs to God. The East and West are named as the horizons of sunrise and sunset, but the ownership includes all the space between them. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the phrase answers objections raised when the qiblah changed: God may direct His worshippers toward whichever place He chooses, since all directions and all creation belong to Him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** recounts that the Prophet ﷺ first faced Jerusalem in Madinah and later was commanded to face the Kaʿbah; the change unsettled some observers, but the change of direction did not mean a change in the One worshipped. **[Qurṭubī]** records several occasions: a confused direction on a dark journey, voluntary prayer while mounted, the shift of qiblah, and other circumstances. **[Jalālayn]** likewise offers both the qiblah dispute and supererogatory prayer during travel. **[Saʿdī]** says the verse covers directions a worshipper faces under God’s command, through travel, uncertainty, or incapacity.

The phrase “there is the Face of God” has received several explanations. **[Ṭabarī]** reports interpretations that it means God’s direction, the qiblah He approves, or the place toward which the servant has been commanded to turn. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the grammar of *thamma* and the meaning of “wherever you turn,” then surveys the legal settings in which the phrase was applied. **[Study Quran]** presents both the practical question of prayer direction and the theological question of the Divine Face: some commentators gloss it as God’s direction or pleasure, while others understand it as an affirmation of an attribute in a manner unlike created faces. **[Saʿdī]** affirms the attribute as befits God, without likeness to creatures, and says God’s breadth of mercy accommodates worshippers who act under a valid command or excuse. These readings should not be confused with permission to ignore the prescribed qiblah when one knows it and is able to face it.

**Law and circumstance.** A general statement does not erase specific instructions. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the verse to the earlier and later commands concerning the qiblah; **[Maʿārif]** explains how the Jewish objections to the change were used to sow doubt about revelation. **[Qurṭubī]** reports the legal view that a traveler who sincerely investigates in darkness or cloud and later discovers an error may still have fulfilled the duty, with details differing among jurists. He also discusses optional prayers on a mount, for which a traveler may face the direction of travel. **[Jalālayn]** distinguishes these cases from the commanded orientation toward the Kaʿbah. **[Saʿdī]** summarizes the principle: the servant is excused or obedient when the turn is authorized by the circumstances, not when it is arbitrary. **[Study Quran]** adds a report about the Negus and the question of his prayer direction, illustrating that commentators connect the verse with more than one occasion.

**Theological dimension.** “All-Encompassing” (*wāsiʿ*) can point to God’s vast bounty and to the fact that no direction limits His knowledge or authority. **[Qurṭubī]** notes this lexical breadth; **[Study Quran]** connects the attribute with God’s presence and mercy while warning that theological interpretations vary. **[Ṭabarī]** emphasizes ownership and governance over the horizons. **[Maʿārif]** relates the verse to the preservation of prayer amid disputes about the qiblah. The declaration is not that God is a body spread across space; the commentaries explain the Divine Face in ways consistent with God’s transcendence. **[Saʿdī]** explicitly rejects likeness between God’s attribute and created faces. **[Ibn Kathīr]** preserves reports about direction and prayer without making the verse a license for anthropomorphism.

**Reflection.** The verse steadies worshippers when circumstances make direction uncertain and reassures them that obedience, not geography alone, is the measure of their prayer. **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** It also prevents a sacred direction from becoming an idol: the Kaʿbah is obeyed because God commanded it, while God is not confined to a compass point. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** The pair “All-Encompassing, Knowing” joins breadth with discernment: God knows whether a person turned in obedience, ignorance, or convenience. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The verse therefore holds together order and mercy—follow the prescribed direction when able, and trust that God knows and accepts the worship offered under genuine constraint. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]**

The verse consequently offers reassurance without erasing ritual order. **[Qurṭubī]** gives reports of the Prophet’s companions praying after seeking the qiblah in a dark journey, while **[Saʿdī]** says a mistaken direction after sincere investigation is not the same as turning away from a known command. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the verse to the transition from Bayt al-Maqdis to the Kaʿbah, and **[Maʿārif]** explains why opponents tried to use the transition to unsettle faith. **[Study Quran]** emphasizes that the phrase can also carry a theological meaning beyond prayer. The worshipper follows a direction because God commands it, not because God is confined to it. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The verse’s breadth is not a cancellation of the qiblah. It teaches that God owns every direction and accepts worship under the conditions He has authorized, while the revealed command still determines the ordinary direction when a person knows it and can follow it. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** The distinction is especially important because the verse appears in the context of dispute about a changed qiblah: the change concerns the worshipper’s obedience, not a movement of God from one place to another. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** The phrase “All-Encompassing, Knowing” joins reassurance with discernment—God’s mercy embraces the constrained worshipper, and His knowledge distinguishes sincere effort from convenience. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:116
116116
*“And they say, ‘God has taken a child.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth; all are devoutly obedient to Him.”*

**Meaning.** The verse answers claims of divine offspring by beginning with *subḥānahu*: God is exalted and dissociated from what is attributed to Him. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that God has no need of a child and that all beings belong to Him as created servants. **[Qurṭubī]** relates the statement to claims made by Christians about Jesus, by Jews about ʿUzayr in some reports, and by Arab polytheists who described angels as God’s daughters; the wording is intentionally broad enough to refute every claim of divine parenthood. **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise cites both the Christian and pagan forms of the assertion, connecting them to the Qur’anic insistence on God’s oneness. **[Jalālayn]** includes Jews, Christians, and those who call angels God’s daughters. **[Study Quran]** stresses that the argument comes from the Islamic understanding of God’s absolute oneness, transcendence, self-sufficiency, and uniqueness. **[Maʿārif]** interprets the verse as a refutation of attributing to God a relationship that belongs to created beings.

The rebuttal is not only “Glory be to Him,” but also “to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth.” **[Saʿdī]** draws the inference: if all creatures are His property and servants, dependent upon Him, none can be His child in the sense claimed by those who make the allegation. A child belongs to the order of generation and likeness, whereas God is the Creator and owner of that order. **[Study Quran]** describes the theological argument in terms of contingency and need: created offspring would share limitations and dependence that cannot be attributed to the Absolute. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that *mā* (“whatever”) embraces all that exists, rational and nonrational. **[Jalālayn]** says the universal ownership contradicts the claim of a child. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects it to other verses that deny divine sonship and affirm that Jesus himself was a servant of God.

**“All are devoutly obedient.”** The word *qānitūn* can carry senses of humble obedience, compliance, standing in worship, or silence before God. **[Study Quran]** lists these shades and notes that commentators differ on whether the verse speaks of the creatures’ present submission to divine governance, their worship, or their eventual obedience on the Day of Resurrection. **[Qurṭubī]** similarly records the lexical range. **[Saʿdī]** distinguishes a universal form of submission, in which all creation is subject to the Creator’s ordering, from the special submission of worship performed willingly by believers. **[Jalālayn]** emphasizes that all are subject to what is required of them. **[Ṭabarī]** treats the phrase as describing creation’s obedience and dependence, not as saying that every person freely worships God in this life. **[Maʿārif]** sees the word as another contradiction of parentage: the beings named as God’s children are in fact His servants.

**Belief.** The verse rejects likeness between the Creator and created family relations without demeaning Jesus, angels, or any other being. **[Ibn Kathīr]** stresses that those whom people call divine offspring are themselves devoted servants. **[Study Quran]** cites the Qur’anic reminder that the Messiah would not disdain to be a servant of God and that the angels brought near are servants too. **[Qurṭubī]** treats *subḥāna* as a declaration of God’s transcendence. **[Jalālayn]** makes ownership the immediate proof. **[Saʿdī]** adds that God is rich beyond need while all created things are poor and dependent; a child would imply a likeness and need that do not belong to Him.

**Reflection.** The verse moves from an accusation to a portrait of reality: the universe is not God’s family in a literal, biological sense, but His creation, sustained by His command. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]** The proper response is worship without assigning creaturely limits to the Creator. **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]** The word *qānitūn* also calls human beings to make willing what is already true of creation’s dependence: to serve God consciously rather than claim an independence no creature possesses. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** The verse therefore joins theology and devotion—God has no child because all are His servants, and the servant’s dignity is found in sincere obedience to Him. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The verse’s reasoning depends on the difference between ownership and kinship. **[Saʿdī]** argues that a child would belong to the same order of need and likeness as its parent, whereas God owns all things and is independent of them. **[Study Quran]** sets out related theological arguments: created beings are contingent and cannot be eternal partners in God’s essence; any literal offspring would imply parts or dependence. The Qur’an’s negation is therefore not a denial of honor to Jesus or the angels but a denial that honor makes them divine. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]** The people most elevated by revelation remain servants who worship the One who created them. **[Jalālayn]**

The term *qānit* helps keep the description from being reduced to a single kind of worship. **[Study Quran]** notes that it can mean compliance, humility, standing, or silence; **[Saʿdī]** distinguishes creation’s universal subjection from voluntary devotional obedience. In either sense, the phrase contradicts the idea that created beings are independent divine relatives. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The universal phrase “whatever is in the heavens and the earth” leaves no category of created being outside God’s ownership. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]** This is the force of the rebuttal: a child in the creaturely sense belongs to a relation of likeness, dependence, and generation, but all beings here are described as belonging to God and obeying Him. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]** The verse does not diminish those honored by revelation; it gives them the dignity of servants rather than the impossible burden of divinity. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** The declaration of God’s transcendence therefore protects both sides of the relationship: it preserves God’s uniqueness and the proper status of every created being. **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:117
117117
*“The Originator of the heavens and the earth: when He decrees a thing, He only says to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”*

**Meaning.** The verse continues the refutation of divine sonship by describing God as the Originator of the heavens and earth. **[Ṭabarī]** explains *badīʿ* as the One who brings creation into existence without a prior model. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the same sense of origination without precedent, and notes that the term conveys God’s creative distinction. **[Jalālayn]** says He brings the heavens and earth into being without an example already available. **[Saʿdī]** adds that He created them in a perfectly ordered and beautiful way. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the verse with the preceding denial of a child: the One who originates all things does not need a partner or offspring in order to create. **[Study Quran]** notes that “Be!” expresses the ease and power by which God creates, not a human-like utterance that requires effort or preparation.

The expression “when He decrees a thing” translates *qaḍā*, which may mean to decree, complete, accomplish, or judge. **[Study Quran]** explores this range and relates the phrase to other Qur’anic statements that creation comes by God’s command. **[Ṭabarī]** understands it as God’s will that a thing exist; **[Qurṭubī]** treats the command as an expression of the Creator’s power. **[Jalālayn]** notes a grammatical variant in the final verb, *fa-yakūnu* or *fa-yakūna*, the latter reflecting a subjunctive analysis of the response to the command. The recitational and grammatical discussion does not alter the central meaning: nothing frustrates God’s creative will.

**“Be, and it is.”** The phrase is not a description of God laboring over matter or negotiating with an independent force. **[Saʿdī]** emphasizes that a thing cannot resist the One who creates it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites parallel verses, including the creation of Jesus and Adam, to show that the formula describes divine power rather than biological generation. If Jesus’s creation without a human father were evidence of divinity, Adam’s creation without father or mother would be an even stronger claim; the Qur’an instead treats both as signs of God’s command. **[Study Quran]** records theologians’ discussions of whether “Be!” is sequential to the created thing or conveys the immediacy and sufficiency of divine will. Some argue that the wording is not a temporal conversation with nonexistence; it indicates that creation requires no preparation, practice, or external aid. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Maʿārif]** likewise use the verse to affirm God’s omnipotence and the dependence of creation.

**Context and doctrine.** The connection with 2:116 is decisive: everything belongs to God and is obedient to Him because He is its Originator. **[Jalālayn]** explicitly joins the two verses, explaining that all creation is subject to the One who brings it into existence. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** The verse therefore rejects any account in which God needs a consort, a child, or a material cause to exercise creative power. **[Ibn Kathīr]** uses Adam and Jesus as Qur’anic comparisons; **[Study Quran]** discusses the verse’s broader metaphysical implications, including divine knowledge of things before their creation. **[Qurṭubī]** preserves the theological reasoning without making the metaphor into a likeness between God’s speech and human speech. **[Maʿārif]** highlights the finality of God’s decree: what He wills comes to be.

**Reflection.** Human beings create by using what already exists, learning a method, and often failing. The Qur’anic Creator is not one maker among others. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** The simplicity of “Be” is a way of declaring that existence depends entirely on God’s knowledge, will, and power. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** It also humbles claims that any created person shares divine status: even the most honored messenger remains among those brought into being by God’s command. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]** The verse invites confidence in God’s ability to bring relief and accountability, while reminding the reader that creation is not a rival to its Creator but a sign of His command. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]** It therefore anchors hope in the same power that brought the world into being. **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]**

This matters especially because the preceding verse names claims about divine offspring. **[Ibn Kathīr]** compares Jesus’ creation with Adam’s: neither case requires that the created one be a child of God. **[Study Quran]** cites 3:59 and 19:35 as parallels and notes that “Be!” conveys effortless creative power rather than a sequence of physical labor. **[Qurṭubī]** explains *badīʿ* as bringing forth without prior model, while **[Saʿdī]** stresses that nothing resists the decree. The verse thus uses creation as evidence of divine self-sufficiency, not as an invitation to speculate beyond what revelation says about how God creates. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]**

The verb *qaḍā* also carries the sense of bringing a matter to completion, which reinforces the finality of God’s decree. **[Study Quran]** describes scholarly discussions of whether the imperative is temporal or a figure for the immediacy of creation; either way, the verse denies that a created thing can frustrate God. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]**

The formula “Be, and it is” expresses the dependence of every created thing on God’s will, not a process that requires matter, tools, or a model external to Him. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** The verse follows the declaration that all creation belongs to God: the One who brings it into being is not one member of its family or a being limited by its laws. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The comparison with Adam and Jesus in the Qur’an uses this same principle to reject the inference from unusual creation to divine sonship. **[Study Quran]** The phrase is not meant to satisfy curiosity about the mechanics of creation; it directs attention to God’s power and the creature’s dependence. **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]**

The title *badīʿ* likewise focuses on origination without precedent. **[Qurṭubī]** explains that the heavens and earth were brought forth without an earlier pattern; **[Jalālayn]** and **[Ṭabarī]** identify God as their Originator. The word therefore supports the surrounding argument: a Creator who does not need a model or partner cannot be understood through the rules of created parenthood. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:118
118118
*“Those who know not say, ‘Why does God not speak to us, or a sign come to us?’ Likewise did those before them speak words like theirs; their hearts are alike. We have made the signs clear for a people who are certain.”*

**Meaning.** The verse depicts a demand for direct speech or a sign, but the surrounding passage shows that such a demand may be made as a taunt rather than a genuine search for guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** reports that the speakers have been identified as pagan Arabs and, in other interpretations, as people of the Book; he connects the question with demands that God appear openly or that a particular miracle be produced. **[Jalālayn]** identifies the immediate speakers as the disbelievers of Makkah and explains that they requested God to speak to them or provide the sign they had prescribed. **[Saʿdī]** says the request is for a demanded sign shaped by their own opinions, not an inquiry meant to recognize truth. **[Study Quran]** similarly notes that some commentators read the question as a taunt and connects it to a sequence of incredulous demands in 17:90–95. **[Ibn Kathīr]** compares it with the earlier demand that Moses show God openly and with questions that burdened earlier communities.

The phrase “those who know not” is interpreted in more than one way. **[Ṭabarī]** transmits explanations that identify them with the Arabs who had no scripture, but also records broader readings. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the wording and the reports about those who demanded signs. **[Jalālayn]** calls them those lacking knowledge of God’s signs. **[Study Quran]** notes that the phrase can refer to pagans, Christians, Jews, or anyone whose heart repeats the same resistant posture. The important point is the likeness of the demand to earlier demands: the speakers seek a spectacle on their own terms. **[Maʿārif]** reads this as insolence toward prophets and a desire to question divine wisdom rather than submit to it. **[Saʿdī]** contrasts such demands with questions made to learn, as he did in the preceding verse about Moses.

**“Their hearts are alike.”** The continuity is moral, not ethnic. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains that earlier peoples made comparable requests and disobeyed their messengers. **[Ṭabarī]** records several historical parallels; **[Study Quran]** mentions the similar demands in other Qur’anic passages. **[Jalālayn]** says their hearts resemble one another in unbelief and stubbornness. The verse therefore warns that the same posture can recur in any community, including one that has received revelation. **[Saʿdī]** insists that the messengers had already brought signs sufficient for people capable of certainty. **[Qurṭubī]** treats the closing clause as an answer: signs have been clarified for people who are ready to recognize them.

**Certainty and evidence.** “We have made the signs clear for a people who are certain” locates the problem not in the absence of evidence but in the disposition of the audience. **[Saʿdī]** says that those who seek certainty already have enough of God’s manifest proofs to remove doubt. **[Jalālayn]** likewise says clear signs are enough for people who know and believe. **[Maʿārif]** argues that demanding an arbitrary additional sign can become a way to avoid the evidence already given. **[Ibn Kathīr]** draws the same distinction between a request for guidance and one made in obstinacy. **[Study Quran]** places the verse beside 4:153, where a similar demand is made of the Prophet. **[Ṭabarī]** preserves the range of historical identities without restricting the lesson to one group.

**Reflection.** The verse does not deny that God can speak or provide signs, nor does it condemn every question. It asks whether a person is willing to follow evidence once it is clear. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** Sincere uncertainty invites teaching; an ultimatum that demands a chosen miracle can conceal refusal. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The resemblance between hearts across generations is a warning to believers not to repeat the same posture under a different name. **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]** The final phrase leaves a path open: certainty is possible where people approach the signs with honesty. **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**

The plural “signs” includes the revelation already recited, the messenger’s character, and the signs in creation, rather than only the spectacular event a skeptic chooses. **[Saʿdī]** explicitly gathers the evidence into the messenger’s mission, his conduct, and the Qur’an and Sunnah. **[Study Quran]** points to similar demands elsewhere, while **[Jalālayn]** says the signs have been made clear to those who recognize them. This does not prevent believers from asking for clarification about a teaching; it distinguishes that need from an ultimatum that refuses all existing evidence. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The close also directs attention to the audience that can benefit: not those who insist on controlling the form of a sign, but those who seek certainty. **[Ṭabarī]** preserves different identifications of the speakers, and **[Study Quran]** leaves the phrase open to anyone who repeats their posture. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The question about direct divine speech must be read beside the final clause about clear signs. The verse does not say that revelation or miracles are absent; it says that signs have already been made clear for those seeking certainty. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** This helps distinguish an honest request for clarification from an ultimatum that chooses in advance what proof must look like. **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]** The historical identities of the speakers vary in the reports, but the repeated posture is stable: each demands an exceptional display rather than engaging with the evidence already presented. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** The reader is invited to ask whether more evidence is truly needed or whether the heart is postponing a response it already understands. **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:119
119119
*“Indeed, We have sent you with the truth, as a bearer of good tidings and a warner; and you will not be questioned about the inhabitants of the Blazing Fire.”*

**Meaning.** After describing people who demand signs as a condition of belief, the verse reaffirms the Prophet’s mission: he has been sent with the truth, to give glad tidings to those who respond and warning to those who reject. **[Ṭabarī]** explains “with the truth” as guidance from God and the message he is commanded to deliver. **[Jalālayn]** calls the truth guidance and identifies Paradise and the Fire as the contrasting outcomes announced by the bearer of glad tidings and the warner. **[Saʿdī]** expands the proof of prophethood across three matters: the fact of the messenger’s sending, his character and conduct, and the truth of the Qur’an and law he brings. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the verse among the consolations given to the Prophet when opponents refuse clear signs. **[Study Quran]** notes that “bearer of glad tidings” and “warner” are complementary descriptions used for prophets throughout the Qur’an. **[Maʿārif]** reads the verse as a reminder that the Prophet’s task is to convey, not compel.

The closing phrase, “you will not be questioned about the inhabitants of the Fire,” relieves the Prophet ﷺ of responsibility for those who persist in rejection. **[Ṭabarī]** explains it as assurance that he is not accountable for why they disbelieved after the message reached them; their deeds and judgment belong to God. **[Jalālayn]** gives the same sense: the messenger’s responsibility is delivery, not guaranteeing that every hearer accepts. **[Saʿdī]** says the Prophet is not answerable for them, while God will reckon with them. **[Study Quran]** notes an alternate reading that would make the clause an instruction not to ask about the people of Hell, though most prefer the reading that absolves the Prophet of responsibility. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the variant form of the verb and its grammatical implications. The interpretive variation does not erase the main reassurance in the preferred reading.

**The character of the message.** “Truth” here is not merely a set of propositions; it includes the messenger’s calling people to worship God and the guidance that clarifies their path. **[Saʿdī]** argues that the Prophet’s mission itself is evidence: humanity was in need of guidance, his character displayed the qualities of a prophet, and the Qur’an brought truthful reports and sound commands. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects his mission to the manifest signs already mentioned in 2:99. **[Ṭabarī]** emphasizes that the message came from God, rather than being devised by the Prophet. **[Maʿārif]** likewise says that rejection by some hearers does not invalidate the truth delivered. **[Study Quran]** explains the pairing of good news and warning as a recurring prophetic function, balancing hope and accountability. **[Jalālayn]** makes the recipients of each clear: those who respond receive glad tidings, and those who refuse are warned.

**Belief and responsibility.** The verse protects both sides of the relationship. The messenger must convey faithfully; the hearer remains responsible for response. **[Qurṭubī]** notes the grammatical variants without turning them into a denial of prophetic compassion. **[Saʿdī]** says the Prophet is not to exhaust himself grieving over every rejecter. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the statement to other passages that say the Prophet is not a guardian over people and is not tasked with their guidance. **[Study Quran]** cross-references those verses, including 2:272 and 88:22. **[Ṭabarī]** frames the message as a proof established against the hearers. **[Maʿārif]** stresses that God’s judgment is not transferred to the messenger merely because he brought the warning.

**Reflection.** The verse steadies the Prophet and every teacher who speaks truth: convey with care, offer hope, warn honestly, and do not claim control over another heart. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]** The fact that some become inhabitants of the Fire is grievous, but it does not make the messenger answerable for their choice. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]** The verse therefore balances compassion with limits. The Prophet is sent with truth for people’s benefit, while final judgment remains with God, who knows what each person received and how he responded. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]**

The paired titles also show that warning is not the whole of the Prophet’s message. **[Saʿdī]** explains that the bearer of good tidings gives hope to those who obey, while the warner makes the consequences of rejection clear; both are aspects of the same truthful call. **[Study Quran]** notes that these epithets are used for prophets throughout the Qur’an. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** The Prophet’s compassion does not require him to carry responsibility that belongs to each hearer and to God’s judgment. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The alternate reading discussed by commentators would make the final words an imperative not to ask about the people of Hell, rather than a statement that the Prophet is not accountable for them. **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Study Quran]** note the grammatical possibility; the majority reading fits the surrounding reassurance and parallels other verses absolving the Prophet of control over guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]**

The verse’s reassurance does not make the Prophet indifferent to the people who reject him. Other passages describe his grief and concern for them; this statement sets a boundary around responsibility rather than extinguishing compassion. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** He must convey the message truthfully and offer both hope and warning, but the hearers’ response and final judgment belong to God. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** This distinction also protects the message from being judged by its immediate acceptance: truth does not become false when someone refuses it. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** The alternative reading discussed by commentators further underscores the need to handle the final clause carefully, since its grammar admits another possibility even though the reassurance is the preferred explanation. **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:120
120120
*“Never will the Jews be content with you, nor the Christians, until you follow their creed. Say, ‘Truly God’s guidance is the guidance.’ If you were to follow their whims after the knowledge that has come to you, you would have against God neither protector nor helper.”*

**Meaning.** The verse warns the Prophet ﷺ not to measure the truth of revelation by whether opposing communities approve of it. **[Ṭabarī]** reads “their creed” as the religious way they call him to follow and connects the warning with the conflict over the qiblah and the new revelation. **[Jalālayn]** explains that the Jews and Christians would not be satisfied until he adopted their religion; the command is to declare that God’s guidance, Islam, is true guidance. **[Saʿdī]** says the two communities invite the Prophet to their own ways while claiming those ways are guidance, but the revealed knowledge has already clarified the truth. **[Maʿārif]** connects their dissatisfaction to the change of qiblah and the efforts to sow doubt. **[Study Quran]** explains *millah* as creed, community, or religious way, and notes that the “whims” are self-centered desires rather than the truth itself. **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise ties the verse to the People of the Book’s refusal to accept the Prophet unless he follows their direction.
