The declaration “God’s guidance is the guidance” is an answer of principle, not a negotiated compromise. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the guidance sent to Muḥammad ﷺ is the guidance to which people are called. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the concise form of the statement and the force of naming divine guidance as the true standard. **[Jalālayn]** contrasts it with error. **[Saʿdī]** says what others invite to, when it contradicts revelation, is *hawā*—desire—rather than guidance. **[Study Quran]** notes that the word *millah* appears elsewhere for the religious way of a community and that the verse’s wording recurs in 2:145. **[Maʿārif]** reads the verse as a warning against giving up revelation merely to win the approval of people who will remain dissatisfied until their own standard is adopted.

**The warning after knowledge.** The verse’s conditional threat is addressed first to the Prophet but also instructs the community through him. **[Saʿdī]** states that the lesson applies to his followers, since the meaning is general even though the immediate addressee is the Messenger. **[Ibn Kathīr]** emphasizes that the phrase “after the knowledge that has come to you” makes the danger particularly grave: deliberate abandonment after clarity is not the same as honest uncertainty. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that there would be no *walī* to protect and no helper to defend a person against God’s judgment. **[Jalālayn]** gives the same meaning of the final warning. **[Qurṭubī]** treats the oath-like construction as intensifying the hypothetical consequence. The verse does not suggest that the Prophet actually followed such whims; it states the seriousness of the principle by addressing him directly.

**Context and scope.** The surrounding passage contains the change of qiblah, criticism of communal exclusivism, and a warning against replacing evidence with demands. **[Maʿārif]** explains that the qiblah change disturbed the People of the Book and that some hoped the Prophet would return to their direction. **[Study Quran]** similarly notes a report that the change created resentment and that opponents might use a truce to press the Prophet toward their religious way. **[Ibn Kathīr]** keeps the focus on the People of the Book’s desire that he adopt their religion. These reports illuminate the setting but do not exhaust the verse’s range: the danger is any capitulation to desire after knowledge, whether the pressure comes from an individual or a community. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**

**Reflection.** The verse distinguishes respect for people from surrendering truth for approval. A believer can recognize the shared history of revelation and still refuse a demand that contradicts what God has made clear. **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]** Its stern warning is not a command to hostility; it is a call to guard the integrity of guidance and avoid being governed by others’ approval. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** The final pair—no protector and no helper—recalls 2:107, making the believer’s ultimate dependence on God explicit again. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** The right response to pressure is neither arrogance nor confusion, but fidelity to the knowledge received, coupled with justice and good conduct toward all. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The distinction between revelation and *hawā* is crucial. **[Study Quran]** says that “whims” here are self-centered desires, not every thought or preference a person may have; **[Saʿdī]** connects them to religious practices that oppose revealed knowledge. **[Maʿārif]** presents the qiblah dispute as a concrete pressure to return to an earlier direction, while **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes the broader call to adopt another community’s religion. The verse therefore does not prohibit courtesy or honest dialogue; it forbids changing a known divine direction merely to win approval. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** The warning’s direct address to the Prophet makes the principle weighty for every believer who has received clear knowledge. **[Study Quran]**

The verse does not predict that every member of the Jewish or Christian communities will always reject the Prophet; it describes the demand made by opponents who will not be satisfied until he adopts their religious way. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The distinction between “God’s guidance” and “their whims” keeps the dispute focused on the source of authority, not on a personal contest between communities. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]** The believer can acknowledge what earlier revelation shares with the Qur’an while still following the guidance God has made clear in the present. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** The warning against abandoning knowledge therefore concerns surrendering truth for approval, not refusing courtesy or justice toward those who disagree. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]**


## 2:121
121121
*“Those to whom We have given the Book and who recite it as it should be recited—they believe in it. And whoever disbelieves in it, those are the losers.”*

**Meaning.** The verse describes what truthful engagement with scripture looks like: a person does not merely possess or quote the Book but recites it in a way that follows its guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** records that *haqqa tilāwatihi* can mean reciting it properly and following it faithfully; to follow it is to observe what it makes lawful and unlawful. **[Saʿdī]** explicitly explains *tilāwah* here as following: the faithful uphold its clear commands, believe in its revealed truths, and do not divide the messengers by accepting one and rejecting another. **[Jalālayn]** takes “recite it as it should be recited” as reading it according to the revelation and not distorting it. **[Study Quran]** likewise notes that the verb *talā* can mean “follow,” and that some exegetes prefer this sense; it also records the alternative that proper recitation involves humility. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the verse with people who recognize and follow the signs given in their scripture. **[Maʿārif]** places it against selective acceptance—claiming faith in what was sent to one’s own group while rejecting what confirms the next messenger.

The identity of “those given the Book” has been explained in more than one way. **[Ṭabarī]** reports interpretations that include Jewish scholars who follow the Torah properly and believers who follow the Qur’an; **[Study Quran]** similarly says it may refer to Muslim believers or learned Jews. **[Jalālayn]** gives a particular occasion involving Ethiopians who came to the Prophet ﷺ and accepted Islam, while the verse’s general wording remains wider than that report. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the passage to the People of the Book who had signs of the Prophet in their scripture. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the phrase’s grammar and its relation to the verse’s conclusion. The point is not that every reader of scripture automatically believes, but that the person who truly follows the revelation’s guidance is led toward belief in the truth it contains.

**Recitation and action.** Recitation in the narrow sense and faithful following need not be opposed. A person can recite carefully, with attention and reverence, and allow that recitation to shape conduct. **[Study Quran]** mentions humility as one sense of proper recitation and cites passages where the People of the Book are commanded to uphold their scripture. **[Saʿdī]** names practical consequences: make lawful what revelation makes lawful, refrain from what it forbids, act upon its clear teaching, and accept what is not fully understood without rejecting it. **[Ṭabarī]** notes that true recitation is not distorted to evade the messenger whose coming the Book indicates. **[Maʿārif]** warns against saying “we believe what was sent down to us” while refusing the next revelation that confirms it. **[Jalālayn]** uses the same contrast between recitation and distortion. **[Ibn Kathīr]** sees the verse as an account of sincere scriptural recognition rather than mere verbal display.

**Belief and loss.** The final clause is uncompromising: whoever disbelieves in the Book is among the losers. The pronoun can refer to the Book under discussion or to the revelation as a whole; in the immediate context it includes rejecting the Qur’an after recognizing its truth. **[Ṭabarī]** links the loss to abandoning what one knows God has sent. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects it with denying the messenger foretold in earlier scripture. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the construction as a general warning. **[Jalālayn]** explains the loss as the punishment that follows distortion and denial. **[Maʿārif]** says a person cannot claim fidelity to a scripture while rejecting the divine truth it points toward.

**Reflection.** The verse does not praise reading as a display of memory or status. It asks whether the word recited has become a path followed. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** For Muslim readers, that standard applies to the Qur’an as well: reverence includes understanding, humility, and obedience. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** The verse also resists the idea that communities may divide revelation into convenient portions. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]** The true reader is one whose recitation does not become a shield against the truth, but a means of recognizing and living by it. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The verse can also be read as a test of the reader’s relation to earlier revelation. **[Study Quran]** notes that some commentators take the people given the Book as the learned Jews who follow the Torah, while others understand believers in the Qur’an; both readings converge on a general standard of faithful recitation. **[Ṭabarī]** emphasizes that following includes accepting the messenger whose description the scripture contains. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects this to the Prophet’s signs, and **[Maʿārif]** says that rejecting what a prior Book confirms is not true loyalty to that Book. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** The passage honors sincere readers across the history of revelation while warning against selective adherence. **[Saʿdī]**

A report about visitors from Abyssinia in **[Jalālayn]** illustrates how following scripture may lead a person to recognize a new messenger rather than cling to a communal boundary. **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]**

The verse’s sequence—reading or following the Book, believing in it, and warning of loss for rejection—makes faith a response to guidance, not a label attached to one community. **[Ṭabarī]** The faithful reader does not select only the passages that affirm a prior view; accepting the whole revelation can require recognizing a messenger whom one’s community has resisted. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** This also clarifies why the word *tilāwah* can carry both recitation and following: scripture is voiced, understood, and obeyed. **[Study Quran]** **[Saʿdī]** The report about the visitors from Abyssinia is an illustration rather than a limit on the verse’s audience. **[Jalālayn]** Its lesson is that honest engagement with revelation should lead toward truth even when that truth crosses communal expectations. **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:122
122122
*“O Children of Israel, remember My favor which I bestowed upon you and that I favored you above the worlds.”*

**Meaning.** The address returns to the Children of Israel after the discussion of scripture, religious claims, and the people who accept or reject revelation. **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the immediate hearers as Jews living in the Prophet’s community at Madinah, whom God calls to recall what He had done for their ancestors and to recognize the messenger now sent among them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** similarly connects this reminder with the description of the unlettered Prophet in earlier scriptures and says that the people should not let envy of an Arab messenger keep them from following him. The address is therefore not a detached statement about a distant past; it is an appeal to use inherited knowledge honestly in the present. **[Maʿārif]**

The “favor” includes both worldly rescue and religious guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** names deliverance from Pharaoh, provision of manna and quails during the wilderness years, establishment in the land, and the raising of prophets from among them. These blessings transformed a people who had been humiliated and oppressed into a community with scripture and prophetic guidance. **[Ibn Kathīr]** also recalls the many benefits God gave Israel in worldly affairs and religion, and stresses that the prophetic mission itself is a favor. **[Ṭabarī]** The verse’s wording gathers these different gifts under one term rather than reducing divine favor to political success or material abundance. **[Maʿārif]**

**Remembering the favor.** The imperative asks the hearers to keep the gift present in thought, but in this context recollection should produce a response. **[Ṭabarī]** links remembrance with obeying God and confirming the messenger; the history of guidance is a reason to trust the next messenger whose signs correspond to what they already know. **[Ibn Kathīr]** says the warning against envy is crucial: the fact that the final messenger is Arab should not lead the People of the Book to conceal or reject the truth. Remembering, then, is more than reciting ancestral stories. It requires acknowledging the source of the blessings and acting faithfully when revelation calls again. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The phrase “I favored you above the worlds” describes a real historical distinction, but it should be read in the terms of the verse’s address and its surrounding argument. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the preference in relation to the people among whom Israel lived, and connects it with their obedience and following the messenger sent to them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** emphasizes the prophets and signs granted to them. The wording therefore recalls a period of particular divine favor; it does not make every descendant immune from judgment or superior by ancestry alone. This limit is made explicit in the next verse, which warns that no soul can answer for another, and again in 2:124, where the covenant excludes wrongdoers. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]**

**The repeated address.** The wording recalls 2:47, where the same command to remember God’s favor begins the extended address to Israel. **[Jalālayn]** notes that this verse has already appeared in a similar form. **[Study Quran]** directs the reader back to 2:47–48 for the paired reminder and warning. **[Maʿārif]** explains the repetition as a frame around the preceding discussion: the opening statement names the blessings and warns of judgment, the intervening verses develop those principles through particular examples, and this closing statement brings the audience back to the central appeal. The repetition is thus structural as well as emphatic. It asks the hearers to understand the preceding debates in light of a simple choice: respond to the favor with fidelity, or face the consequences of rejecting guidance. **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]**

**Reflection.** Every community can learn from the way this verse treats its religious past. A history of revelation is a gift, not a possession that can be used to claim automatic virtue. The most faithful inheritance is to recognize the truth when it comes and to live by it. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** For the first audience, that meant following the messenger rather than allowing rivalry to overrule knowledge; for later readers, it means that memories of past guidance must deepen humility and obedience. **[Maʿārif]** The verse honors Israel’s history without turning honor into a substitute for present faith. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Its place immediately before the warning about judgment keeps gratitude and accountability together: divine favor is remembered most truly when it changes how one responds to God. **[Maʿārif]** **[Jalālayn]**

The sequence of gifts in the historical account has a movement of its own. Deliverance from Pharaoh first restores freedom; provision sustains the people in the wilderness; settlement gives them a place to live; and prophets and scripture provide direction for how to use that freedom and security. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Remembering the favor means holding together these material and spiritual dimensions instead of treating one as the whole story. **[Maʿārif]** The verse does not ask its audience simply to celebrate the fact of past rescue; it calls them to recognize the purpose of the guidance that accompanied it. **[Ṭabarī]**

The reminder also comes at a moment when the identity of the messenger is contested. **[Ibn Kathīr]** says the People of the Book knew descriptions of the final Prophet in their scriptures, and that envy of the Arabs should not prevent them from following him. That makes the appeal to memory pointed: earlier revelation is not invoked to freeze the community at an earlier stage, but to prepare it to recognize the continuation of God’s guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** The question implicit in the address is whether the hearers will let the record of divine favor lead them toward faithful recognition, or turn it into a reason to resist a new messenger. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**

## 2:123
123123
*“And be mindful of a day when no soul will avail another in any way; no ransom will be accepted from it, no intercession will benefit it, and they will not be helped.”*

**Meaning.** The verse moves from the reminder of Israel’s historical favor to the Day on which no community can rely on its past as a substitute for individual response. **[Ṭabarī]** addresses the warning to the Israelites who had altered revelation and denied the messenger, urging them not to persist in unbelief until death. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects this repeated warning with the call to follow the final messenger whose description the People of the Book knew. The history of favor, therefore, heightens responsibility rather than removing it. **[Maʿārif]**

The first clause says that one soul cannot “avail” another. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that no person can discharge another’s duty, remove the burden of that person’s sin, or supply help when God’s judgment arrives. **[Jalālayn]** understands the clause to mean that no soul can benefit another on that Day. This does not deny the ordinary duties of care and assistance in worldly life; it marks the limit of human help at the final accounting. The verse’s singular “soul” makes the warning personal even though the address is to a community. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]**

The following clauses name different hopes of escape. A ransom would provide payment or compensation; intercession would ask another to plead; being helped would mean receiving rescue from an outside supporter. **[Ṭabarī]** says that no payment will be accepted for what is due, no advocate can secure a benefit against God’s judgment, and no helper can prevent punishment. **[Jalālayn]** likewise explains that neither compensation nor intercession will avail those addressed, and that they will not be defended against God’s chastisement. The sequence makes the warning comprehensive: neither wealth, influence, nor a presumed advocate can take the place of sincere faith and obedience. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The statement about intercession should be read alongside the Qur’an’s teaching that intercession belongs to God and occurs only by His permission (2:255). The verse rejects any claim to an independent or guaranteed advocate; it does not place mercy beyond God’s power. Its purpose is to remove false security, not to deny divine mercy. **[Jalālayn]** focuses on the people addressed here, for whom intercession will not benefit; **[Ṭabarī]** similarly stresses that no intercessor can overturn the right God has established against a person. The distinction is between hope in what God may grant and entitlement to a rescue that no one can promise.

**Context and repetition.** This warning repeats the substance of 2:48, which follows the same reminder of favor in 2:47. **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes that the earlier passage is repeated here to emphasize following the Prophet, while **[Study Quran]** directs readers to its commentary on 2:47–48 for both verses. **[Maʿārif]** describes the pair as the closing frame of a long address: the blessings offered as a reason to return to guidance are balanced by a picture of the Day when those who reject it cannot rely on anyone else. The recurrence binds the surrounding passages together rather than introducing an unrelated warning. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The repeated admonition also answers the temptation to rely on inherited status. **[Ṭabarī]** speaks directly to those addressed as people who must not persist in denial; the warning is not a claim that ancestry itself is blameworthy, but a reminder that ancestry cannot answer for the individual. **[Maʿārif]** In the verses that follow, Abraham’s family is praised for tested obedience, while the covenant is denied to wrongdoers. The sequence gives a consistent standard: history matters as a source of guidance, but each person and community must respond to that guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]**

**Reflection.** The verse calls the hearer to prepare before the day of judgment, when payment, advocacy, and worldly support will not substitute for turning to God. It is a warning against complacency, not an instruction to despair. A believer may hope for God’s mercy, pray for others, and assist them in this life, while still recognizing that no one can guarantee another person’s final outcome. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ṭabarī]** Remembering the blessings of revelation should therefore lead to gratitude expressed in faith, repentance, and right action. **[Maʿārif]** The warning is especially pointed after the call to remember Israel’s favor: receiving scripture is a responsibility, and the proper answer to that gift is faithful obedience before the final accounting. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The four clauses also cover distinct imagined routes to escape. The first denies that another person can assume one’s account; the second denies payment as a substitute; the third denies a self-appointed advocate; the fourth denies rescue by allies or power. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** Taken together, they move from personal substitution to material compensation, then to pleading, and finally to outside force. The verse’s rhetoric leaves no loophole in which status, wealth, or connection can overrule the judgment of God. **[Maʿārif]**

The command *ittaqū*—“be mindful” or “guard yourselves”—turns the statement from a prediction into an instruction for the present. A hearer cannot change the terms of the final account, but can respond now through faith and obedience. **[Ṭabarī]** This explains why the warning follows the appeal to remember blessings: the history of revelation supplies guidance before judgment arrives. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The ensuing account of Abraham continues the point in positive form, showing an ancestor whose standing came through fulfilling God’s commands rather than merely belonging to a lineage. **[Maʿārif]** The warning and the example thus work together: the Day closes false exits, while Abraham’s story shows the faithful path that remains open. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:124
124124
*“And when his Lord tested Abraham with words, and he fulfilled them, He said, ‘I am making you an imam for mankind.’ He said, ‘And from my progeny?’ He said, ‘My covenant does not include the wrongdoers.’”*

**Meaning.** The passage turns to Abraham as a model of fidelity and as the ancestor invoked by Jews, Christians, and the Arabs. **[Qurṭubī]** notes the connection with the preceding discussion of the Kaʿbah and qiblah: the descendants of Abraham should not turn away from his religion. **[Ṭabarī]** explains *ibtalā* as testing and says the “words” were obligations God commanded Abraham to fulfill. **[Saʿdī]** likewise describes them as commands and prohibitions by which God tests sincerity, and says Abraham completed them fully. **[Ibn Kathīr]** presents him as an imam because he adhered to God’s commands and prohibitions and embodied *tawḥīd*. **[Study Quran]** notes that the Qur’an does not specify these “words” here; commentators propose ritual duties, acts of purification, pilgrimage rites, circumcision, sacrifice, or trials such as the fire into which Abraham was cast. **[Jalālayn]** lists a range of commands, including pilgrimage and bodily cleanliness. **[Maʿārif]** treats the test as a proof of Abraham’s obedience and the foundation of his leadership.

The variety of interpretations should not obscure the verse’s explicit judgment: Abraham fulfilled what was asked of him. **[Ṭabarī]** transmits reports that identify the words with thirty elements of the religion or with ten practices of purification, but the reports are explanations rather than a list supplied by the verse itself. **[Qurṭubī]** records similar views, including the virtues listed in passages such as 9:112 and 33:35. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects fulfillment with 53:37, “Abraham, who fulfilled,” and with his obedience in other Qur’anic passages. **[Saʿdī]** explains testing as a means by which truthful servants rise in rank and their actions are purified. The emphasis lies not in speculative reconstruction of every test but in steadfast completion.

**Imam and covenant.** God’s declaration that Abraham will be an imam for mankind is a grant of leadership and a lasting example. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that people are to follow his guidance; **[Jalālayn]** calls him a leader in religion. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the term’s possible meanings, including a religious leader or one who leads prayer. **[Saʿdī]** describes the honor as a high station, one whose followers guide others toward happiness. Abraham then asks about his descendants, a request that reflects care for their future and a desire that they share in guidance. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**

The answer limits the covenant: it does not reach wrongdoers. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that unjust descendants do not automatically inherit the promise by descent. **[Qurṭubī]** records interpretations that associate the covenant with leadership or prophethood and notes that wrongdoers are excluded. **[Saʿdī]** explains that religious leadership requires patience, certainty, faith, and good character; wrongdoing contradicts the station. **[Study Quran]** presents several classical readings: a wrongdoer may never be appointed imam, or may hold worldly leadership without receiving the covenant’s promise of the Hereafter, or the statement may simply mean that not all of Abraham’s descendants will be righteous. **[Ibn Kathīr]** emphasizes the exclusion of polytheists and wrongdoers. The verse thus separates Abrahamic descent from Abrahamic conduct.

**Reflection.** Abraham’s leadership is the fruit of tested obedience, not a hereditary entitlement. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** The request for descendants shows love for one’s family, while the divine answer makes clear that kinship cannot override justice. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** A community that claims Abraham must examine whether it follows his worship of God alone and his willingness to fulfill divine commands. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** The verse rebukes every attempt to convert ancestry into immunity: the covenant’s moral boundary is wrongdoing, and its promised leadership depends upon fidelity. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]**

The exegetes preserve several lists of the test’s “words.” **[Ṭabarī]** transmits a report that describes thirty elements of the religion and another that identifies ten practices of purification, divided between the head and body. **[Qurṭubī]** records these and other views; **[Jalālayn]** gives a list including trimming the moustache, rinsing the mouth, cleaning the teeth, clipping nails, circumcision, and washing. **[Study Quran]** adds pilgrimage rites and trials such as the sacrifice of his son or the fire from which God saved him. These reports illustrate the range of Abraham’s tested obedience, but the verse itself leaves the exact list unspecified. **[Ibn Kathīr]** stresses the result rather than a complete catalogue: Abraham fulfilled all the commands entrusted to him. **[Saʿdī]** says the test distinguishes steadfast sincerity and raises the rank of the one who completes it.

His request for his descendants is also an act of care, not an attempt to secure status for them regardless of character. **[Saʿdī]** reads it as a wish that guidance continue through his family; **[Maʿārif]** explains that the divine answer sets a moral condition. The covenant cannot be claimed by a wrongdoer simply because Abraham is an ancestor. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The word “imam” consequently names a responsibility to guide people by example, not merely a privilege to command them. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The exclusion of wrongdoers places justice inside the definition of legitimate religious leadership. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**

The covenant’s boundary is important to the chapter’s argument about sacred history. Abraham is honored because he fulfills God’s commands; his descendants are not declared leaders by blood alone. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The verse does not say that every wrongdoer is immediately removed from all worldly office; rather, it denies that injustice qualifies a person for the divine covenant in the sense under discussion. **[Study Quran]** records differing interpretations of whether the covenant concerns prophethood, religious leadership, or ultimate reward, while agreeing that wrongdoing cannot claim its promise. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** In the immediate context, that principle challenges any group that treats ancestral connection to Abraham as sufficient proof of right guidance. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:125
125125
*“And when We made the House a place of return for mankind and a sanctuary: ‘Take the station of Abraham as a place of prayer.’ And We covenanted with Abraham and Ishmael: ‘Purify My House for those who circumambulate it, those who remain there in devotion, and those who bow and prostrate.’”*

**Meaning.** “The House” is the Kaʿbah, made a *mathābah*—a place to which people return—and a place of security. **[Ṭabarī]** explains *mathābah* as a place of return and reports that worshippers come back repeatedly without exhausting their desire to visit. **[Qurṭubī]** similarly derives the term from returning and notes that the House continually draws visitors. **[Jalālayn]** describes people flocking to it from every direction and the sanctuary’s protection from violence. **[Saʿdī]** says it provides religious and worldly benefit and that people return to it without feeling they have had enough. **[Study Quran]** notes the Kaʿbah’s pre-Islamic status as a sanctuary and explains the continuity of its sacred protection. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the verse within Abraham and Ishmael’s role in building and purifying the House. **[Maʿārif]** connects the pilgrimage and qiblah to divine command, not merely to an Arab custom.

The sanctuary’s security is a blessing with legal and moral consequences. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses juristic disagreement about the treatment of a person who commits an offense and then seeks refuge in the sanctuary; he distinguishes the sanctity of the House from broader questions about enforcement in the whole precinct. **[Jalālayn]** describes the security in vivid terms: even an enemy might be encountered there without violence. **[Study Quran]** notes that commentators mention sanctuary status as part of the historical meaning. **[Saʿdī]** says the House remained honored even in pre-Islamic times, and Islam increased its sanctity. The verse’s point is not that wrongdoing is permitted there, but that the sanctuary is to be protected and respected.

**The station of Abraham.** The command “take the station of Abraham as a place of prayer” has been interpreted as the specific stone near the Kaʿbah associated with Abraham’s footprint and as the wider stations and rites of pilgrimage. **[Jalālayn]** identifies the familiar station and the two units of prayer after circumambulation. **[Study Quran]** records alternate locations and readings, including the possibility that “they took” describes earlier worshippers. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the grammar and legal status of prayer at the station. **[Saʿdī]** allows both the specific place and the broader Abrahamic rites, preferring the broader sense insofar as it includes the specific one. **[Ṭabarī]** links the verse to the change of qiblah and reports the station’s role in Abraham’s building of the House. **[Ibn Kathīr]** describes the prophetic instruction connected with the station.

**Purifying the House.** Abraham and Ishmael are charged to purify the House for those who circumambulate, make devotional retreat, bow, and prostrate. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains purification as removing idols, impurity, sexual misconduct, false witness, and sin. **[Maʿārif]** links the command with Abraham’s responsibility to maintain the sanctuary in a state of pure worship. **[Jalālayn]** identifies the groups as pilgrims, residents in retreat, and people at prayer. **[Qurṭubī]** examines the legal meanings of *ṭahhira* and the rites named; **[Ṭabarī]** relates the command to both the House’s physical preparation and the worship performed there. **[Study Quran]** explains *iʿtikāf* as remaining in a mosque in devotion, a practice later associated with retreat during Ramadan. **[Saʿdī]** notes that the House’s attribution to God calls for honoring and preserving it.

**Reflection.** The verse joins welcome, safety, and discipline. The House receives people from afar, but it is not a place for whatever practice its visitors choose; it is purified for worship of God. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** Its repeated return teaches continuity in devotion, while the station of Abraham reminds pilgrims that their rites are inherited through obedience. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** The order to purify includes the outer space and the moral life of those who worship there. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The sanctuary’s peace should therefore be matched by reverence, cleanliness, and protection of worshippers. **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]**

The groups named in the command describe the full range of worship at the sanctuary: the circumambulator moves around the House, the *ʿākif* remains devoted in the mosque, and those who bow and prostrate perform prayer. **[Jalālayn]** explains these as distinct forms of service; **[Study Quran]** connects *iʿtikāf* with the later practice of devotional retreat. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports that purification includes removing idols and sins as well as physical impurity. The House is therefore prepared not for a single ritual alone but for a community gathered in varied acts of worship. **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The station of Abraham may refer to the stone near the Kaʿbah, to the prayer after circumambulation, or more broadly to the rites associated with Abraham. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]** The specific and broad readings can coexist: the local station recalls the builder, while pilgrimage reenacts his wider acts of devotion. **[Maʿārif]** sees the connection as a reminder that Islamic practice renews Abraham’s way rather than inventing a separate sanctuary tradition. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The word “return” also describes more than a visitor’s physical journey. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** Pilgrims return to the sanctuary in recurring seasons, and worshippers may return to it throughout life as a center of prayer and remembrance. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** The House’s safety and the ordered rites make it a place for people to gather without turning the gathering into worship of the building itself. Abraham and Ishmael’s charge is to purify it for God’s worship, and the named acts all direct attention to God. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** The station of Abraham likewise recalls a servant’s obedience, whether understood narrowly as the stone or more broadly as the rites connected with him. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ṭabarī]** This preserves the verse’s balance between a concrete sanctuary and the spiritual purpose for which it is maintained. **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:126
126126
*“And when Abraham said, ‘My Lord, make this a secure land and provide its people with fruits—those of them who believe in God and the Last Day.’ He said, ‘And whoever disbelieves, I shall let him enjoy for a little while, then compel him to the punishment of the Fire. What an evil destination!’”*

**Meaning.** Abraham prays for the place of the House to be secure and for its people to receive provision. **[Ṭabarī]** explains security as safety from fear and provision as fruits and sustenance brought to a land without ordinary agriculture. **[Jalālayn]** notes that travelers came from distant regions with produce, while the valley itself was initially barren. **[Study Quran]** connects the request with 14:37, where Abraham describes the valley as uncultivated and asks God to incline people’s hearts toward it and provide for them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the prayer to Makkah’s inviolability, preserved by God as a sanctuary. **[Maʿārif]** reads the verse as showing Abraham’s care for the community that would gather around the House. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the meaning and legal implications of security in the sanctuary.

The prayer first asks for safety, then for material provision. **[Saʿdī]** says Abraham’s request for fruit shows the need for worldly sustenance as well as religious well-being. He initially asks broadly, then specifies believers when praying for provision; God’s response clarifies that unbelievers too may receive provision in this world, though their enjoyment is temporary. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that God grants worldly sustenance to both believer and disbeliever, while the ultimate outcome differs. **[Jalālayn]** notes that the disbeliever is given a little enjoyment during life and then driven to punishment. **[Ibn Kathīr]** compares this with other verses in which worldly benefit is not proof of divine approval. **[Study Quran]** underscores the distinction between the city’s security and the eternal security of the Hereafter.

**The limit of the request.** Abraham’s prayer is careful: he asks for the believers’ provision, and God tells him that the disbeliever will also be given a short enjoyment. **[Saʿdī]** interprets this as Abraham’s etiquette before God after he had learned that the covenant does not reach wrongdoers in 2:124. **[Qurṭubī]** records discussions about the scope of “its people” and whether the prayer includes believers specifically. **[Maʿārif]** says worldly provision is a general divine gift, not a sign that all recipients are accepted in the Hereafter. **[Ṭabarī]** likewise distinguishes provision from final salvation. The verse therefore avoids two errors: it does not treat material comfort as proof of righteousness, and it does not deny that God provides for those who disbelieve during their appointed lives.

**Security and sacred place.** The request for security is fulfilled in Makkah’s special status, though the sanctuary’s protection is itself part of God’s decree and law. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the prophetic statement that Makkah was made inviolable when God created the heavens and earth. **[Jalālayn]** describes the sanctuary’s protection from bloodshed and injustice. **[Study Quran]** connects the word *āmin* with the *amn* of the previous verse, which called the House a sanctuary. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the legal question of security for one who enters the precinct after committing an offense; scholars differ on the application, so the verse should not be reduced to a simplistic claim that wrongdoing is immune from justice.

**Reflection.** Abraham’s prayer integrates devotion and ordinary human need. He asks for a place where worship can flourish and for food that allows its inhabitants to live. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** The response teaches that worldly gifts are temporary and widely distributed, while faith and the Last Day determine the lasting outcome. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** The House’s sanctity thus points beyond itself: security in this life is a mercy, but the final destination depends on the response to God. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]**

The prayer also shows Abraham’s care for inhabitants, not merely for pilgrims who visit the sanctuary. **[Study Quran]** connects the request to the barren valley and to the provision mentioned in 14:37; **[Jalālayn]** describes produce arriving through travelers from far away. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the city’s security in the history of its divinely protected sanctuary. The response distinguishes worldly provision from spiritual approval: the disbeliever may enjoy the world briefly, but provision is not a verdict on faith. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** The believer uses sustenance in obedience and hopes for the lasting reward, while the unbeliever’s enjoyment ends at death and judgment. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** This contrast prevents either envy of the prosperous or contempt for those who lack worldly ease. **[Study Quran]**

The sanctuary’s security is therefore connected with service: pilgrims need safety, residents need provision, and worship needs freedom from threat. **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]** But worldly safety itself is not the final promise of salvation. The disbeliever may partake of the city’s benefits for a limited term, then face the consequence of rejecting God. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]** The two horizons—life in the sanctuary and the Last Day—keep the prayer from becoming merely political or economic. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]**

There is also a relationship between safety and the ability to worship. A sanctuary can welcome people from different lands only if its inviolability is respected; its inhabitants can maintain the rites only if they have the food and stability needed for life. **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** The prayer does not promise that every period of history will be free from danger or hardship, but it identifies security and provision as gifts to ask from God and protect through reverence for His sanctuary. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]** The mention of the Last Day in Abraham’s request also frames worldly prosperity: provision in Makkah is a mercy, whereas final safety belongs to the faithful. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** A person should therefore be grateful for material ease without confusing it with an endorsement of every belief or action. **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:127
127127
*“And when Abraham and Ishmael were raising the foundations of the House, ‘Our Lord, accept this from us. Truly You are the Hearing, the Knowing.’”*

**Meaning.** The verse presents Abraham and Ishmael working together to raise the foundations of the House. **[Ṭabarī]** explains *qawāʿid* as the bases or supporting foundations; the verb “raising” may describe building upon what was already there rather than creating a sanctuary without any prior foundation. **[Jalālayn]** glosses the phrase as raising the supports or walls of the House. **[Study Quran]** records traditions that the Kaʿbah had an earlier history extending to Adam, while noting that al-Ṭabarī recounts such stories without treating them all as reliable history. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates reports about Abraham and Ishmael uncovering the foundations. **[Maʿārif]** connects the construction to God’s command and to the later pilgrimage. The verse itself emphasizes their work and prayer, not the precise archaeology of the site.

The father and son’s collaboration is notable. **[Saʿdī]** says that both labored in this great act and continued with humility, asking God to accept it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the scene within the honor given to Abraham’s family, while **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the grammatical structure of the verse and how Ishmael is joined to Abraham as a participant. **[Jalālayn]** reads their words as a prayer that God receive the building. **[Study Quran]** notes that this act establishes a sacred center for worship that later revelation connects with pilgrimage. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]**

Their prayer follows immediately upon their work: “Our Lord, accept this from us.” **[Saʿdī]** reads this as fear and hope together; even an act commanded by God and performed by prophets is not treated as a reason for self-admiration. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the request seeks acceptance of their service. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that the utterance turns the building into worship by dedicating it to God. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the principle that a deed is not judged by its outward appearance alone; its acceptance belongs to God. **[Jalālayn]** distinguishes the two divine attributes in the closing prayer: God hears their words and knows their deeds. **[Study Quran]** links the verse with the wider Abrahamic history of the House.

**Hearing and knowing.** The names of God are not ornamental endings. **[Ṭabarī]** explains “Hearing” as hearing the prayer they utter and “Knowing” as knowing the sincerity and action behind it. **[Qurṭubī]** similarly notes that their petition is fully known to God, whether spoken aloud or hidden in the heart. **[Saʿdī]** draws reassurance from this: God hears the request and knows the intention and quality of the work. **[Ibn Kathīr]** relates the prayer to the divine acceptance of righteous deeds. **[Maʿārif]** highlights the humility of asking for acceptance even while performing a foundational act for worship.

**Reflection.** The verse joins labor with supplication. Abraham and Ishmael do not replace work with prayer, nor prayer with work; they raise the House and ask God to accept their effort. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]** This pattern challenges anyone who assumes that a public religious project guarantees its own spiritual worth. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** The House is sacred because it is dedicated to God, not because its builders seek fame. **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]** The believers’ work, however great, remains dependent on divine acceptance. **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The wording “raising the foundations” has prompted commentators to ask whether Abraham and Ishmael erected the House anew or raised an earlier base. **[Ṭabarī]** reports traditions of a pre-existing foundation; **[Study Quran]** describes stories connecting it to Adam but warns that not all such accounts are reliable history. **[Ibn Kathīr]** preserves reports about discovering or rebuilding the foundation. The Qur’anic point does not depend on resolving every story: father and son labor in obedience and ask for acceptance. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that the grammar names both as participants. **[Jalālayn]** calls the work construction of the House. Their prayer reminds builders that visible completion does not ensure spiritual acceptance. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**

The names “Hearing” and “Knowing” fit their request precisely. **[Ṭabarī]** says God hears the words of their supplication and knows their action. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** The builders ask that their service benefit worshippers, not simply that they be praised for the structure. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** The foundation thus carries a spiritual intention: a place dedicated to remembrance and pilgrimage. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**

The account also shows how a place can be sacred without the physical structure becoming an object of worship. **[Study Quran]** Abraham and Ishmael build the House as a direction and gathering place for service to God, and their own prayer is directed to God alone. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Saʿdī]** Their request for acceptance guards against confusing the sacred building with the One who makes it sacred. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** The work itself becomes a form of devotion because it answers a divine command. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The verse also makes the builders’ labor intergenerational. Ishmael’s presence beside Abraham turns the act into a shared duty, while their prayer that God hear and know them places the outcome beyond their control. **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]** Later worshippers inherit not only the structure but the posture of humility that accompanied its construction. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** A righteous deed is to be completed carefully, offered sincerely, and entrusted to God. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**

Their petition is not merely for the structure to stand but for God to accept their work and its continuing benefit. **[Saʿdī]** The House becomes a place of worship because it is built in obedience and remains oriented toward God. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** Their prayer invites later builders and caretakers to examine intention as well as craftsmanship: a sound building can serve a noble purpose, but the worshipper still asks for acceptance rather than boasting of the achievement. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** The divine names make their confidence neither self-reliance nor uncertainty; they trust that God hears and knows what people cannot fully see. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

## 2:128
128128
*“Our Lord, make us submissive to You, and from our progeny a community submissive to You. Show us our rites, and relent toward us. Truly You are the Relenting, the Merciful.”*

**Meaning.** The prayer continues from Abraham and Ishmael’s work on the House to the kind of community they hope will worship there. They ask to be *muslimayn*—people who submit to God—and ask for a portion of their descendants to become a community characterized by that submission. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the request as a prayer for themselves and their offspring to obey God and follow His religion. **[Jalālayn]** notes that “from our progeny” is partitive: they ask that some descendants form a people devoted to God, consistent with the earlier statement that the covenant excludes wrongdoers. **[Study Quran]** points out that the word *muslim* here is a participle describing submission to God, a universal sense of the word that predates the Qur’anic community. **[Saʿdī]** defines it as inward surrender that is reflected in the limbs and actions. **[Maʿārif]** treats the prayer as evidence that Abraham’s religion is submission to God rather than ethnic identity.

“Show us our rites” may refer especially to the pilgrimage practices connected with the House, or more broadly to acts of worship. **[Ṭabarī]** records interpretations that take *manāsik* as the rites of Hajj, sacrificial offerings, or worship more generally. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the word in its ritual sense and its connection to the pilgrimage. **[Jalālayn]** says “teach us our holy rites,” particularly ceremonies of worship or pilgrimage. **[Study Quran]** notes the term’s use for the rites appointed to different communities and connects it with 22:34 and 22:67. **[Saʿdī]** says that the request may mean teaching the rites by direct guidance and demonstration, and more generally asks for beneficial knowledge and righteous action. The prayer shows that even Abraham and Ishmael do not claim to invent worship from their own preferences; they ask God to show them how to serve Him.

They then ask God to “relent toward us.” **[Saʿdī]** observes that human beings remain in need of repentance and mercy, even when they perform a major act of obedience. **[Qurṭubī]** explains the reciprocity of repentance: servants turn back to God, and God turns toward them in mercy. **[Study Quran]** notes the same movement in the Arabic concept of *tawbah*. **[Jalālayn]** says they ask for this despite their moral excellence, out of humility and to teach their descendants. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the prayer to the prophetic model of seeking forgiveness and divine acceptance. **[Ṭabarī]** reads the names “the Relenting, the Merciful” as the grounds on which the prayer is made.

**Community and lineage.** The phrase “from our progeny” avoids claiming that every descendant will be faithful. **[Jalālayn]** explicitly notes the partitive meaning; **[Study Quran]** says commentators connect the request to the principle of special concern for one’s family. **[Saʿdī]** sees in the prayer a desire for righteous descendants who will pass on knowledge and practice. **[Maʿārif]** points out that Abraham’s lineage includes prophets and communities, but the relevant bond is submission, not ancestry alone. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The next verse asks for a messenger from among these descendants, showing that the requested community is to be taught, purified, and guided.

**Reflection.** The prayer balances personal devotion with concern for future generations. Abraham and Ishmael ask not only to build a sanctuary but to become servants who worship rightly and to see a community formed by revelation. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** Their petition for repentance after obedience teaches humility: the worshipper does not assume that good action eliminates the need for mercy. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Their request for the rites also teaches that worship is learned and transmitted under divine guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]** The verse therefore links faith, ritual knowledge, family responsibility, repentance, and mercy in a single supplication. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]**

The prayer for a community is not a request that every descendant inherit faith without effort. **[Jalālayn]** notes the partitive “from our progeny,” and **[Ṭabarī]** interprets it as some descendants who submit and obey. **[Study Quran]** connects the word *muslim* with the universal meaning of surrender to God, showing that the essential act of submission is not limited to one later historical name. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The request for rites is similarly practical: Abraham and Ishmael seek instruction in acts through which the community will worship. **[Saʿdī]** says this may include the full pilgrimage or every act of devotion, and in either case asks for sound knowledge and righteous action. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

Their request for repentance after building the House teaches that worship does not remove human need for mercy. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** The name *al-Tawwāb* points to God’s repeated acceptance of those who turn back; *al-Raḥīm* grounds that acceptance in mercy. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]** The prayer therefore gives future generations a pattern: ask for guidance, learn how to worship, and return to God when deficient. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]**

The grammar and sequence reinforce that the prayer is for a community shaped by worship rather than simply a population living near the sanctuary. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Abraham and Ishmael first ask for themselves to submit, then for a community from their progeny, then for knowledge of the rites that will organize its devotion. **[Study Quran]** **[Jalālayn]** The request for divine turning and mercy follows those petitions, reminding the community that its worship remains dependent on God’s guidance and forgiveness. **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]** The verse therefore refuses to separate religious identity from practice: submission is learned, enacted, and renewed through repentance. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** The prayer portrays the prophets as teachers of dependence upon God, not as people who presume that their prior service makes them self-sufficient. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Qurṭubī]**

## 2:129
129129
*“Our Lord, raise up among them a messenger from among them, who will recite to them Your signs, teach them the Book and Wisdom, and purify them. Truly You are the Mighty, the Wise.”*

**Meaning.** The prayer asks that a messenger arise among the descendants and community associated with the House. The request is for a messenger “from among them,” one who shares their language and social world and can be recognized by them. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the prayer as Abraham and Ishmael’s supplication for a messenger among their progeny. **[Jalālayn]** identifies its fulfillment with Muḥammad ﷺ, sent among the people of the House. **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise states that God answered the prayer by sending the Prophet from the descendants of Ishmael. **[Saʿdī]** says that a messenger from among them would be more readily known and followed by the people. **[Maʿārif]** reads the verse as a link between Abraham’s construction of the sanctuary and the prophethood that later restored its monotheistic purpose. **[Study Quran]** notes that the prayer’s fulfillment is described again in 2:151 with nearly the same wording.

The messenger is asked to perform four connected tasks. First, he recites God’s signs. **[Saʿdī]** explains this as recitation, memorization, and teaching of the revealed verses. **[Jalālayn]** identifies the signs with the Qur’an. **[Ṭabarī]** sees the recitation as a public conveyance of revelation. Second, he teaches the Book. **[Study Quran]** says this is usually understood as the Qur’an, while the pairing of Book and Wisdom also appears in relation to Jesus, Israel, and the House of Abraham. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gives the Qur’an as the Book. Third, he teaches Wisdom, commonly understood as the Prophet’s Sunnah or sound understanding of religion. **[Ṭabarī]** reports that the Wisdom may mean the prophetic practice and judgments. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the pairing and its interpretive range. **[Maʿārif]** connects the teaching with practical understanding rather than recitation alone.

The fourth task is purification. **[Saʿdī]** explains it as nurturing people in righteous deeds and cleansing them from blameworthy actions that corrupt the soul. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects it with removing idolatry and sin. **[Jalālayn]** says the messenger purifies them from polytheism. **[Study Quran]** describes purification as the moral transformation that accompanies revelation. **[Ṭabarī]** includes the cleansing of belief and conduct. These functions form a whole: recitation presents revelation, teaching explains it, wisdom guides its application, and purification shapes the people who receive it. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Maʿārif]**

**Divine attributes.** The prayer closes with “the Mighty, the Wise.” **[Ṭabarī]** explains that God’s might makes the sending of a messenger possible and His wisdom places the mission where it should be. **[Saʿdī]** says the messengers’ work cannot be prevented by any power, while God’s wisdom determines its timing and form. **[Jalālayn]** understands the attributes as victory and wise governance in creation. **[Ibn Kathīr]** sees the Prophet’s mission as the answer to Abraham’s prayer and a mercy extending beyond his descendants to the world. **[Study Quran]** notes that the formula links divine power to the fulfillment of the promise.

**Reflection.** The verse shows what prophetic guidance is for: a community does not merely need information but a messenger who recites, teaches, interprets, and purifies. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** Abraham’s prayer for a messenger also places the later community within a history of monotheistic supplication, not as an isolated people. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The wording “from among them” suggests closeness and accessibility, while the messenger’s mission reaches beyond kinship to all who respond. **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** The answered prayer is therefore both a fulfillment for Abraham’s family and a universal mercy through teaching and purification. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The messenger’s work answers the different needs of a community. Recitation makes revelation heard; teaching explains its meanings and judgments; wisdom guides its application; purification transforms character and practice. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]** The four verbs are not redundant: instruction without purification can leave knowledge unused, while spiritual aspiration without revealed teaching can become self-invented practice. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The phrase “from among them” underscores that the messenger is not distant from the people he guides. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ṭabarī]** Abraham asks for a prophet they can recognize, understand, and follow, and the prophetic message nevertheless extends to all who hear it. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]** The answer “the Mighty, the Wise” closes the petition with trust: God has power to send the messenger and wisdom to appoint him at the right time. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The verse’s fulfillment is thus not only a prediction but a description of the Prophet’s continuing teaching mission. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The ordering of the tasks also helps distinguish the messenger’s public and formative work. Reciting the signs conveys the revelation faithfully; teaching the Book explains what it requires; teaching wisdom demonstrates sound application; purification helps form people capable of living by it. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]** The functions are complementary, and the verse does not reduce prophethood to either recitation alone or moral exhortation detached from scripture. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The closing attributes answer any doubt about the delay between Abraham’s prayer and its fulfillment. **[Ṭabarī]** God is mighty, so no opposition can defeat His purpose, and wise, so the mission arrives according to knowledge and decree. **[Saʿdī]** **[Jalālayn]** The Prophet’s mission thus joins divine power with wisdom rather than being an accidental historical development. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**

The prayer also explains why the Prophet is described as a teacher and purifier, not simply as a bearer of a text. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** A revealed Book needs faithful recitation and explanation; the Wisdom gives a lived example of how revelation guides decisions, while purification makes a community more truthful and just. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** This integrated mission is the answer to Abraham’s request for descendants who worship according to divine instruction rather than inherited custom alone. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** The messenger is a source of guidance, but God remains the one whose might establishes the mission and whose wisdom governs its teaching. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:130
130130
*“And who would turn away from the creed of Abraham except one who has made a fool of himself? We chose him in this world, and in the Hereafter he will surely be among the righteous.”*

**Meaning.** The verse follows Abraham’s prayer and the account of the messenger sent among his descendants with a rhetorical question: who would reject Abraham’s religion after its truth and nobility have been made clear? **[Ṭabarī]** explains *millah Ibrāhīm* as Abraham’s religion of submission to God and pure monotheism. **[Jalālayn]** says that no one turns away from it except a person who wrongs or degrades his own soul. **[Saʿdī]** glosses *safih nafsahu* as ignorance of oneself and acceptance of a low bargain, turning away from a way that leads to good. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the word’s meaning as foolishness and the loss of sound judgment. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects Abraham’s creed with *ḥanīfiyyah*, turning away from idolatry toward worship of God alone. **[Study Quran]** notes the link between *millah* and the religious way or community discussed in 2:120, while emphasizing Abraham’s monotheistic example.

The phrase “we chose him in this world” indicates divine election, not a status he acquired by ancestry. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that God selected Abraham for prophethood and guidance. **[Jalālayn]** says he was chosen for prophethood and friendship with God. **[Saʿdī]** describes his selection as guidance to the deeds that made him among the best of God’s servants. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites other Qur’anic passages praising Abraham as a devoted, upright monotheist and as one who fulfilled his charge. **[Maʿārif]** sees the verse as a direct challenge to communities that claim Abraham while rejecting the messenger who revives his way. **[Study Quran]** notes that other prophets and Mary are likewise described as chosen; election is a divine favor accompanied by responsibility.

The second half looks toward the Hereafter: Abraham will be among the righteous. **[Qurṭubī]** connects this with the reward for his obedience; **[Jalālayn]** explains it as his place among people of elevated ranks. **[Ṭabarī]** reads the statement as confirmation of his lasting honor. **[Saʿdī]** ties his standing in the next world to the faithful actions through which he responded to God’s call. **[Ibn Kathīr]** points to the Qur’an’s repeated praise of Abraham’s truthfulness and worship. The verse thus joins election in this life with righteousness in the next, without making either a mere hereditary possession.

**Context and scope.** The immediate audience includes Jews and Christians who claim to follow Abraham, as well as others who invoke him while practicing what contradicts his monotheism. **[Study Quran]** notes that commentators often identify those turning away as the People of the Book, and that the verse establishes continuity between Abraham’s law and the prophetic guidance culminating in Islam. **[Maʿārif]** makes the same argument in relation to the Kaʿbah and pilgrimage: these practices are linked to Abraham’s worship by divine instruction. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** Yet the rhetorical question is wider than one community; anyone who abandons sincere devotion to God for idolatry or self-made authority is included by the moral description. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]**

**Reflection.** To turn away from Abraham’s creed is called folly because it harms the person who chooses it. **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]** The verse does not praise Abraham merely as an ancestor; it calls readers to the qualities that made him chosen: devotion, obedience, gratitude, and refusal of idolatry. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** The sequence from his tested fulfillment to the House, the prayer for a messenger, and this warning shows that Abrahamic inheritance is lived through faith and practice. **[Maʿārif]** **[Study Quran]** His rank in both worlds is a reminder that divine favor and human response belong together. **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The wording *safih nafsahu* suggests that the person who rejects Abraham’s way has not merely chosen a different label; he has misjudged his own good and accepted a diminished path. **[Saʿdī]** says such a person debases himself and makes a losing bargain. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the phrase as foolishness or ignorance of the soul. **[Jalālayn]** offers both senses: one who does not recognize the soul’s purpose in worshipping God, or one who treats it frivolously. **[Study Quran]** links Abraham’s creed to *ḥanīf*, the turning away from idolatry toward pure monotheism. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites Qur’anic descriptions of Abraham as upright, obedient, grateful, and not among the polytheists. **[Ṭabarī]** The text therefore defines his religion by worship and obedience rather than by ancestry alone. **[Maʿārif]**

The verse also responds to those who claim Abraham but refuse the guidance that confirms his way. **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]** His election in this world and righteousness in the Hereafter are paired with his faithful conduct, not with a claim that his descendants inherit his rank automatically. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** This prepares for the following verses, where Abraham’s submission is shown through his response to God’s command. **[Jalālayn]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

Election is also a charge to carry the divine message, and Abraham’s righteous standing is presented as both an honor and a model. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]** His example calls later believers toward the same orientation: sincere worship, trust in God, and willingness to obey revelation. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** The verse therefore criticizes not a lack of inherited prestige but the folly of turning away from guidance that leads to the soul’s real good. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The description “in this world and in the Hereafter” gives Abraham’s example a horizon beyond the immediate dispute over ancestry. **[Ṭabarī]** His selection is visible in the divine guidance entrusted to him, while his final standing is confirmed as part of the righteous. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** Neither clause makes his descendants righteous by association; rather, the verse invites them to follow the same orientation that made him worthy of praise. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** The appeal to Abraham thus joins a historical memory with a present moral test: will those who invoke him submit to God as he did? **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:131

131131
*“When his Lord said to him, ‘Submit,’ he said, ‘I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds.’”*

**Meaning.** The verse recalls the moment when Abraham received a direct command to submit to God and answered with willing obedience. **[Ṭabarī]** explains *aslim* as making worship sincere for God and humbling oneself through obedience; Abraham’s reply confirms that he has surrendered his worship to the One who owns and governs every creature. **[Jalālayn]** similarly glosses the command as obeying God and devoting religion purely to Him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the response with Abraham’s rejection of all partners in worship and his devotion to God alone. **[Saʿdī]** reads it as complete sincerity, monotheism, and love of God. The verse therefore presents submission as a settled allegiance expressed in action, not simply the repetition of a name for a community. **[Maʿārif]**

The repetition of the root *s-l-m* in the command and reply gives the exchange a concise shape: God commands submission, and Abraham answers that he has submitted. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that the root can carry the senses of yielding, compliance, and following; the verse uses it in its fullest religious sense. He distinguishes outward submission from inward faith in some contexts, but here Abraham’s response is complete and sincere, not coerced speech. **[Ṭabarī]** likewise emphasizes both humility in obedience and exclusive worship. **[Study Quran]** places the exchange within the Qur’an’s broader use of *islām* for primordial devotion to God and also for the revealed religion associated with Muḥammad. The term can therefore name a universal orientation while referring, in later historical contexts, to a specific prophetic community. **[Maʿārif]**

**The Lord of the worlds.** Abraham does not answer merely, “I submit to You”; he names God as “the Lord of the worlds.” **[Maʿārif]** sees in this fuller reply a recognition of God’s authority over all creation and a fitting expression of praise at the honor of being addressed by Him. It also makes clear that obedience is not a service God needs; it is the creature’s proper response to the Creator and is for the servant’s own benefit. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that Abraham submits to the owner and governor of all creatures, not to a local deity or tribal patron. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** The title thus reinforces the universality of the call: the One Abraham worships is Lord of every people and every age. **[Study Quran]**

The verse connects with the preceding praise of Abraham in 2:130 and the family instruction that follows in 2:132. His election and his righteousness are inseparable from his response to God’s command. **[Ibn Kathīr]** describes Abraham’s whole path as rejecting idolatry and directing himself to the Creator; **[Saʿdī]** highlights his readiness to obey and his transmission of this orientation to his children. This connection prevents the reader from treating “chosen” as a status detached from conduct. **[Maʿārif]** Abraham is an example precisely because divine guidance elicited wholehearted submission from him. **[Ṭabarī]**

**Reflection.** The exchange is brief, but it models a central relation between God and the servant. A divine command is not presented as an invitation to bargain over terms; Abraham’s answer is immediate and complete. **[Saʿdī]** Such submission does not erase the person’s agency. It is a chosen act of trust, informed by recognition that God is Lord of all worlds. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]** For later readers, the verse asks whether faith is merely an inherited label or an active readiness to let revelation guide belief and conduct. **[Maʿārif]** It also explains why the next verse describes Abraham and Jacob passing their faith to their children: the response to God becomes a way of life that one hopes to preserve across generations. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]**

The command does not suggest that Abraham was an unbeliever needing conversion: it comes within the account of his already demonstrated monotheism. **[Ṭabarī]** relates it to the trials in which Abraham rejected astral worship, while **[Qurṭubī]** places the command after his tests involving the celestial bodies and glosses it as making his religion sincere in monotheism or submitting in humility. The reply uses the first-person perfect, “I have submitted,” and names God as Lord of all worlds; it makes Abraham’s allegiance explicit without presenting him as its own source. **[Saʿdī]** emphasizes his readiness to obey, and **[Ibn Kathīr]** describes his worship as exclusive. The imperative and reply together show divine guidance answered by willing obedience, not a mere inherited title. **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]**

**Submission and belief.** Because the word *islām* can describe outward yielding as well as inward conviction, commentators distinguish its use according to context. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that a person might outwardly submit under pressure without genuine faith, while true faith necessarily includes surrender to God; in Abraham’s statement, however, the context describes sincere and complete devotion. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** This distinction prevents the verse from reducing religion to a spoken formula. The command concerns worship, obedience, and the orientation of the heart, all of which Abraham embodies. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**

The episode also helps the reader understand why the next verse calls submission a bequest. Abraham’s reply gives a compact description of the way he practiced, while the following instruction makes that way transmissible to his descendants. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** In both verses, the servant’s allegiance is directed to the Lord of all worlds rather than to a people who claim exclusive ownership of Him. **[Study Quran]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:132

132132
*“And Abraham enjoined this upon his children, and Jacob also: ‘O my children, God has chosen the religion for you, so do not die except as Muslims.’”*

**Meaning.** The verse turns from Abraham’s own declaration of submission to the instruction he leaves for his descendants. **[Ṭabarī]** understands “this” as the word and religion of submission expressed in 2:131: worship God alone, obey Him, and do not depart from the path He chose. **[Qurṭubī]** records the same two explanations, identifying the object of the bequest either with Abraham’s religion or more specifically with his words, “I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds.” He regards the latter as especially close to the preceding wording. **[Ibn Kathīr]** says Abraham and Jacob urged their children to remain in Islam. **[Saʿdī]** describes the inheritance as worship, sincere monotheism, and obedience. The verse presents faith as the most valuable legacy a parent can offer. **[Maʿārif]**

The phrase “and Jacob also” has been read in two grammatical ways: Jacob may be joined to Abraham as another person who gave the same counsel, or the wording may begin a separate report that Jacob addressed his own sons. **[Ṭabarī]** discusses the construction and concludes that the substance of both readings is the same: Abraham and Jacob each urged their families to worship God and submit to Him. **[Qurṭubī]** records a related difference and the variant readings *wa-waṣṣā* and *wa-awṣā*, both conveying a bequest or command. **[Study Quran]** treats the two verses as a continuous testament of submission from Abraham through Jacob to their children. The distinction matters for grammar and chronology, but not for the shared religious message. **[Jalālayn]** **[Maʿārif]**

“God has chosen the religion for you” identifies the source of the way being transmitted. **[Ṭabarī]** says the definite “religion” is the path already known through the parents’ instruction. **[Qurṭubī]** similarly understands it as Islam, while **[Maʿārif]** explains that every prophet called people to submission to God, even though particular laws could differ across prophetic eras. The counsel is not that descendants should invent Abraham’s faith for themselves, but that they should hold to the guidance God selected. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the command to Abraham’s own life and to the continuous prophetic call to worship one God. **[Study Quran]** notes the universal dimension of submission alongside its specific place in the later community.

The expression “do not die except as Muslims” cannot mean that a person controls the moment of death. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the intended meaning: remain in Islam throughout life, because no one knows when death will come. **[Qurṭubī]** calls the wording a concise admonition that combines a command to persist with a reminder of mortality; the instruction begins immediately and continues until death. **[Jalālayn]** similarly explains it as remaining in submission so that death finds them in that state. **[Ibn Kathīr]** says a person should live righteously and ask God to let him die upon the path he followed. The verse therefore commands continuous fidelity, not control over the unseen. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]**

**A parent’s bequest.** Abraham’s affection for his children appears not as a request that they inherit worldly standing but as an urgent concern for their faith. **[Maʿārif]** draws a broad lesson for parents: care for children’s spiritual formation is part of family responsibility, just as concern for their worldly welfare is. **[Saʿdī]** emphasizes that the prophets wanted to transmit the lasting wealth of faith and good action. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the counsel with the idea that a person generally dies and is raised upon the path he maintained, while also recognizing that final outcomes remain with God. The verse thus turns family love into religious instruction and a shared responsibility across generations. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]**

**Reflection.** The instruction is both urgent and hopeful. It is urgent because death may come at any time; it is hopeful because a person can maintain the chosen religion through daily obedience and trust in God. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** Abraham and Jacob do not tell their children that ancestry alone will secure salvation. They call them to live in submission themselves. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** This prepares for the following scene, where Jacob asks his sons what they will worship after him and they answer in their own voices. **[Maʿārif]** The passage therefore distinguishes a faithful inheritance from an inherited guarantee: parents can teach and counsel, but each generation must respond. **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The verb translated “enjoined” is reported in variant readings with forms that both convey a bequest or binding instruction. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the doubled form can suggest repeated counsel, while *awṣā* carries the sense of entrusting a command. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that both forms are transmitted and that Jacob may be connected to Abraham in the grammar or may begin a separate clause.
