The variation does not alter the family’s shared testament: hold to God’s chosen religion. **[Maʿārif]**

The phrase “this” looks backward to Abraham’s submission in the previous verse and forward to the instruction of Jacob. **[Ṭabarī]** interprets it as the word “I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds,” while **[Qurṭubī]** says that this is especially likely because it is the nearest antecedent. **[Ibn Kathīr]** allows that it may refer more broadly to Abraham’s whole religion. These readings reinforce rather than compete with one another: the confession of submission summarizes the monotheistic path that the parents want their children to continue. **[Study Quran]**

This interpretation avoids a fatalistic reading of the instruction. Abraham and Jacob do not ask their children to predict the hour of death; they teach them to remain faithful each day so that death does not find them having abandoned the chosen way. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** The verse’s urgency follows from the uncertainty of life, while its hope lies in sustained practice and divine help. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]**

## 2:133

133133
*“Or were you witnesses when death approached Jacob, when he said to his children, ‘What will you worship after me?’ They said, ‘We will worship your God and the God of your fathers, Abraham, Ishmael, and Isaac—one God—and to Him we submit.’”*

**Meaning.** The question challenges those who claim that Jacob belonged to a later religious community. Were the Jews or Christians present at his death and able to testify to what he taught? **[Ṭabarī]** says the address exposes the People of the Book’s attribution of their later identities to Abraham, Jacob, and their descendants without witnessing their instruction. **[Qurṭubī]** calls it a rebuke of those who claim that the patriarchs prescribed Judaism or Christianity. **[Jalālayn]** connects the question with a reported claim that Jacob charged his sons with Judaism. The answer supplied by the verse is not a later label but Jacob’s own question and the sons’ reply: worship the one God and submit to Him. **[Maʿārif]**

The phrase “when death approached Jacob” refers to the approach or circumstances of death, not to a conversation after he had died. **[Qurṭubī]** points out that Jacob could speak because the wording describes the onset of death, its causes, or the final illness. **[Ṭabarī]** understands the repeated “when” as setting the scene for his words to his sons. Jacob asks, “What will you worship after me?”—a question about the object of their worship, not an instruction to choose among later community names. **[Ibn Kathīr]** presents the scene as a final testament urging his children to worship God alone. **[Saʿdī]** likewise reads the question as a test of whether they will preserve monotheism after their father’s death.

The sons answer by naming the God of Jacob and of their forefathers Abraham, Ishmael, and Isaac. **[Ṭabarī]** explains “one God” as exclusive worship and rejection of any partner. **[Jalālayn]** notes that Ishmael is called a father though he was Jacob’s uncle, a usage also discussed by **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The order of the names has been explained by Ishmael’s seniority relative to Isaac. **[Ṭabarī]** records that explanation; the text’s inclusion of the uncle under “fathers” reflects a familiar kinship usage, not a confusion about the family line. **[Maʿārif]** says the wording also illustrates that religious inheritance is transmitted through a family without becoming a guarantee based on ancestry.

The answer concludes, “one God, and to Him we submit.” **[Ṭabarī]** understands the final words either as describing how they worship in the moment or as a continuing statement of allegiance; he prefers the sense that their worship is performed in submission. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects this with the Qur’anic principle that the prophets share one religion even when their legal codes differ. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the grammatical function of “one God” and how it emphasizes divine oneness. **[Study Quran]** places the exchange within the argument that Abraham and Jacob belong to a primordial monotheistic tradition rather than to an exclusive identity imposed later. **[Saʿdī]** highlights the combination of belief and action: they name one God and declare that they submit to Him.

**Family and transmission.** The scene is a family conversation, but it carries a universal theological claim. **[Maʿārif]** notes that prophets are deeply concerned for their children’s faith and that parents should care for their spiritual well-being as well as their worldly needs. Jacob’s question makes the sons state their own allegiance rather than leaving the answer as an assumption. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** Their reply confirms the bequest described in 2:132: the faith passed down from Abraham and Jacob is worship of God alone. **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]** The family bond transmits a testimony, but the testimony itself is about God, not about the family’s right to claim divine favor.

The detail that the sons name Abraham, Ishmael, and Isaac also blocks an attempt to restrict Abrahamic faith to only one later branch. **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]** Jacob’s descendants acknowledge a wider line of prophetic forebears and affirm one God. The phrase “your God” makes their answer relational and direct, while “the God of your fathers” locates their faith in a known prophetic tradition. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Their final “we submit” is not merely historical description; it is a confession that each generation must renew. **[Saʿdī]**

**Reflection.** The verse uses a question about the past to test present claims. Those who were not witnesses cannot honestly manufacture testimony about Jacob’s final words; the Qur’an supplies what he actually asked and what his sons answered. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Qurṭubī]** The challenge is not to deny the value of Jewish or Christian histories, but to reject the retrospective claim that the patriarchs bore later labels before those communities existed. **[Maʿārif]** The enduring example is simpler and more demanding: worship one God, and submit to Him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]**

The opening *am* links the question to the preceding argument while giving it a reproachful force: the audience cannot claim to have witnessed Jacob’s deathbed instruction. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the construction as a fresh question after earlier speech; **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Jalālayn]** read it as a denial of the opponents’ supposed testimony. The point is epistemic as well as theological: people should not attribute a creed to Jacob when they were not present and the recorded words contradict their claim. **[Study Quran]**

Jacob’s choice of “what” rather than “who” has also been discussed. **[Qurṭubī]** says the wording tests the sons and reflects the kinds of objects people might worship, including inanimate idols; it does not imply that Jacob considered God an object. **[Ṭabarī]** reads it as a broad question about what they would serve after his death. Their answer resolves the test by naming the one God of their ancestors and declaring submission. **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:134

134134
*“That was a community that has passed away. They have what they earned, and you have what you earn; and you will not be questioned about what they used to do.”*

**Meaning.** The verse closes the account of Abraham, Jacob, and their children with a rule about moral responsibility. Their generation has passed; its actions belong to it, while the present audience will answer for its own actions. **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the “community” as Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and their descendants, and addresses Jews and Christians who attribute later religious identities to them. The patriarchs’ good deeds cannot be claimed as a substitute for obedience by later generations. **[Ibn Kathīr]** similarly says that kinship with prophets does not benefit a person unless he performs righteous deeds. **[Maʿārif]** applies this warning to anyone who relies on the prestige of ancestors while neglecting personal faith and conduct.

The Arabic *umma* can refer to a community, generation, or people who lived in a certain period. **[Ṭabarī]** explains it as a group and a generation that has gone before. **[Jalālayn]** identifies Abraham and Jacob’s family as that past community. **[Study Quran]** notes that the verse is repeated at 2:141, creating a refrain at the close of the discussion of Abrahamic ancestry. The statement respects the past without keeping it open as a substitute account for the present. **[Qurṭubī]** points out that the structure assigns “what they earned” to the earlier generation and “what you earned” to the addressees. The repeated parallel makes responsibility reciprocal and individual. **[Maʿārif]**

“Earned” includes the deeds for which a person is rewarded or held accountable. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that each generation receives the result of its own good and evil actions. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses *kasaba* as an act genuinely attributable to the servant, while God creates the capacity through which it occurs; human accountability presupposes a meaningful distinction between chosen action and involuntary movement. This is a theological note about responsibility, not a claim that human beings act independently of God. **[Study Quran]** notes that *kasaba* can mean “to commit” or “to perpetrate” depending on context. **[Ibn Kathīr]** draws the practical consequence: lineage cannot make up for deeds that a person has failed to perform.

The final clause, “you will not be questioned about what they used to do,” does not make the ancestors’ conduct irrelevant to history. It denies that descendants will be charged with the ancestors’ account or rescued by it. **[Qurṭubī]** connects it with the Qur’anic principle that no bearer carries another’s burden. **[Maʿārif]** applies the same principle in both directions: children are not punished for their parents’ disbelief, and descendants cannot expect their own sins to be forgiven merely because their forebears were righteous. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the saying that a person slowed by his deeds is not advanced by his lineage. **[Ṭabarī]** directs the original audience away from disputing over the patriarchs and toward the deeds that matter in its own life.

**Ancestry and religious claims.** The verse is placed after Jacob’s sons confess submission to one God because later groups had invoked the patriarchs as proof of their own exclusive status. **[Maʿārif]** explains that the verse refutes confidence in ancestral merit, including the claim that descent from prophets automatically guarantees Paradise. **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes that a person’s relationship to righteous ancestors is not enough without imitating their good deeds. **[Study Quran]** observes that the same statement returns in 2:141, where the argument again turns from the past to the present audience. The passage does not forbid honoring ancestors or learning from their example; it forbids treating their virtue as one’s own earned account. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**

**Reflection.** The verse balances continuity and individuality. A community receives scripture, stories, and examples from earlier generations, but it cannot inherit their obedience in place of practicing its own. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** The same fairness also protects people from inherited blame: each person is assessed for what he or she has done. **[Qurṭubī]** This principle makes the previous family testament meaningful. Abraham and Jacob could teach their children, but every descendant still had to choose submission. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The past is a guide, not a substitute; the present is a responsibility, not a sentence determined by birth. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]**

The warning also speaks to religious communities that take pride in prophetic ancestry while disputing the messenger who renews the prophets’ call. **[Ṭabarī]** says the addressees should stop ascribing their own later creeds to Abraham and his family and instead consider their own deeds. **[Maʿārif]** extends the lesson to Muslims who rely on relation to the Prophet or a saint while neglecting their obligations. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** The verse’s repeated “you” makes its correction direct: no family tree can answer for the individual at judgment. **[Jalālayn]**

The refrain also guards against a misuse of history. The verse does not tell later readers to forget Abraham and Jacob; the passage has just presented their faith as a model. It tells them not to claim the prophets’ deeds as their own or assign the prophets their own later identities. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]** The repeated wording at 2:141 confirms that the point belongs to the larger discussion of ancestral claims. **[Study Quran]** Honoring an ancestor means learning from the ancestor’s obedience, not treating descent as a substitute for it. **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The verse’s balance is important: “they have what they earned” and “you have what you earn” denies both inherited reward and inherited guilt. **[Maʿārif]** **[Qurṭubī]** People remain responsible for their own choices, even though family, teachers, and scripture can influence them. This principle also explains why Jacob questioned his sons and why Abraham gave his children a testament: each generation must affirm the faith for itself. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:135

135135
*“They say, ‘Be Jews or Christians and you will be guided.’ Say, ‘Rather, the creed of Abraham, a ḥanīf; he was not of the idolaters.’”*

**Meaning.** The verse reports rival invitations to adopt a communal identity as the condition of guidance. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the Jews called the Prophet and believers to become Jews, while the Christians made the corresponding call to Christianity; each group claimed that its way alone led to the right path. **[Jalālayn]** places the first claim among the Jews of Madinah and the second among the Christians of Najrān. The command to answer, “Rather, the creed of Abraham,” rejects both exclusive invitations and points to the shared prophetic monotheism from which later communities arose. **[Study Quran]** emphasizes that the groups were not proposing a neutral choice between two religions; each asserted its own community as the sole route to guidance.

The word *millah* means a religious way or creed, and Abraham is named because he was an exemplar of worshipping God without partners. **[Ṭabarī]** calls his way the religion of God, the one to which the different groups can all appeal without arguing over later claims. **[Saʿdī]** glosses *ḥanīf* as turning toward God and away from all else, standing upon monotheism and abandoning idolatry. **[Jalālayn]** explains it as inclining away from every other religion toward the upright way. **[Ibn Kathīr]** presents Abraham as rejecting the deities of his people and directing his face to the Creator. **[Maʿārif]** says the verse identifies the essential religion as submission to God rather than a later group name.

**The sense of ḥanīf.** The term has been explained in several ways: upright, inclining toward truth, devoted solely to God, or in some reports as a pilgrim. **[Ṭabarī]** records these interpretations but argues that *ḥanīf* is not adequately defined as pilgrimage alone, since pagans also performed pilgrimage, nor as circumcision alone, since that would include Jews whom the verse distinguishes from Abraham’s way. He concludes that it is steadfastness in Abraham’s religion and following his monotheism. **[Study Quran]** surveys the word’s lexical history and notes that, in Qur’anic use, it is associated with pure *tawḥīd*, virtue, and freedom from sectarian distortion. **[Qurṭubī]** likewise records the central sense as turning from false religion to the religion of Abraham. These readings are compatible when pilgrimage and bodily practices are treated as expressions of a larger monotheistic orientation, not its whole definition.

The second clause, “he was not of the idolaters,” makes the positive description concrete. Abraham’s *ḥanīf* orientation excludes worship of created beings or participation in *shirk*. **[Ṭabarī]** uses this qualification to reject the identification of his religion with any practice that can coexist with polytheism. **[Ibn Kathīr]** highlights Abraham’s refusal to call upon other deities and his direct challenge to his people. **[Saʿdī]** describes the creed as sincere devotion to God and renunciation of idolatry. **[Study Quran]** says *ḥanīf* often appears alongside a denial of polytheism and can describe an upright orientation beyond narrow communal boundaries.

**Guidance and community.** The verse does not teach that all historical religions are identical or that differences in revelation never mattered. **[Maʿārif]** explains that earlier laws were genuine in their own times, while the later messenger brings the guidance required in the present era. The point is that Jews and Christians cannot make a later communal label the standard by which Abraham himself is reclassified. **[Ṭabarī]** presents the creed of Abraham as a point of agreement that challenges the competing claims. **[Study Quran]** notes that *ḥanīf* does not name a separate organized sect; it describes a monotheistic quality and a universal primordial way. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

**Reflection.** The verse distinguishes belonging from guidance. A name may identify a community, but the test of Abraham’s way is sincere worship of God and refusal to associate partners with Him. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Abraham is therefore not a trophy for later groups to claim while disregarding his faith; his example challenges every community to return to the core of revelation. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]** The answer is not contempt for people who differ, but fidelity to the monotheism that Abraham practiced and the prophetic guidance that confirms it. **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The reply also shifts the discussion from inherited assertions to the character of faith. If a group says that it alone is guided, it must show how its claim accords with the monotheism of Abraham and with the successive messages God sent. **[Ṭabarī]** The Qur’anic response refuses the idea that a label can replace that evidence. **[Study Quran]** **[Saʿdī]** The designation *ḥanīf* also warns against narrowing religion into tribal loyalty: Abraham turns away from every false object of worship, not merely from the practices of one rival community. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The command “Say” gives the Prophet a concise answer to competing invitations: do not accept either group’s claim that guidance depends on joining its later community; return instead to Abraham’s monotheistic way. **[Ṭabarī]** calls this an especially complete and concise argument because the groups can agree that Abraham was a prophet of God, even where they disagree about later institutions. The appeal is not to a neutral third religion but to the source of their own prophetic histories. **[Study Quran]** **[Maʿārif]**

The etymology of *ḥanīf* is debated, and the word can bear senses of turning, uprightness, or inclination. **[Study Quran]** reviews these possibilities and cautions that proposed cognates do not replace the Qur’an’s clear usage. **[Ṭabarī]** prioritizes context: Abraham’s *ḥanīf* faith is straight adherence to God, not merely participation in pilgrimage or circumcision. **[Qurṭubī]** records similar explanations of the term. The verse’s explicit denial of idolatry serves as a safeguard against narrowing the word to an external rite. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:136

136136
*“Say, ‘We believe in God, and in what was sent down to us, and in what was sent down to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Tribes, and in what Moses and Jesus were given, and in what the prophets were given from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we submit.’”*

**Meaning.** The verse supplies a public confession of faith that gathers the messages of the prophets into one commitment. The believers affirm God, the revelation given to Muḥammad ﷺ, and the revelations and guidance associated with Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, the Tribes, Moses, Jesus, and the prophets generally. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the named figures are listed with honor, while the final phrase includes prophets whose names are not given. **[Ibn Kathīr]** similarly says Muslims believe in all revelation and all messengers, whether named in the Qur’an or not. **[Jalālayn]** identifies the Book given to Moses as the Torah and that given to Jesus as the Gospel. The list is therefore both particular and open-ended: it honors specific prophetic histories while affirming the whole chain of divine guidance. **[Maʿārif]**

The “Tribes” (*al-asbāṭ*) are commonly identified as the twelve branches descended from Jacob’s sons. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains that the descendants of each son formed a tribe, and **[Qurṭubī]** compares the term to the tribes among the descendants of Ishmael. **[Study Quran]** notes that *asbāṭ* refers to the Israelite tribal groupings and discusses whether the phrase points to revelation given to the tribes or to prophets arising among them. The verse does not require a detailed list of every prophet in each tribe; it asks the believers to affirm what God sent through them. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]**

**What faith includes.** As-Saʿdī draws attention to the opening command “Say”: the confession is spoken by the tongue while the heart affirms it. **[Saʿdī]** It is not a bare formula, because the closing words “and to Him we submit” joins belief with inward and outward obedience. He describes faith as including conviction, verbal acknowledgment, and deeds, while noting that the words “we believe” make a collective declaration that unites the community. **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]** records a report instructing Muslims not to affirm or deny every claim transmitted from the People of the Book without discernment, but to state their own belief in God and what He revealed. This caution does not weaken the Qur’anic confession; it prevents unverified reports from being mistaken for revelation. **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The phrase “what was sent down to us” includes the Qur’an and, in as-Saʿdī’s explanation, the guidance conveyed through the Prophet’s teaching. **[Saʿdī]** The following clauses affirm that God also sent revelation to earlier prophets. **[Ṭabarī]** treats every listed book and prophet as part of the required belief. **[Maʿārif]** stresses that the divine origin of the earlier revelations is affirmed, while their particular legal dispensations may have been superseded by the final revelation. Belief in an earlier Book does not mean that every later copy or interpretation is automatically accepted as unchanged; it means affirming what God truly revealed and the prophets who conveyed it. **[Study Quran]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

**“We make no distinction.”** The phrase rejects selective acceptance of messengers—believing in Moses while rejecting Jesus, or honoring Abraham while denying Muḥammad. **[Ṭabarī]** reads it as the believers’ refusal to divide the prophets into those they accept and those they deny. **[Jalālayn]** contrasts this with the People of the Book’s rejection of some messengers. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the Qur’anic condemnation of those who believe in some messengers and disbelieve in others. The statement does not mean that all prophets had identical ranks or identical laws; it means that all are accepted as truthful messengers from God. **[Saʿdī]** distinguishes general belief in all prophets from detailed belief in those whose names and teachings are known. **[Study Quran]**

The confession ends by saying “to Him we submit.” **[Saʿdī]** notes that the fronting of “to Him” emphasizes exclusive devotion: worship is directed to God alone, and the believer follows the guidance conveyed by His messengers. **[Qurṭubī]** relates the verse to the danger of declaring “I am truly a believer” as self-praise, while the revealed confession states the content of faith without claiming perfection. **[Maʿārif]** sees the line as a declaration that Muslims obey God alone and follow the final law now in force. The verse thus joins continuity with commitment: the believers honor every prophet and submit to the revelation God has given them. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]**

**Reflection.** The verse offers a model for speaking about religious history with both conviction and fairness. It does not erase differences among communities, but refuses to insult or reject a prophet whom God sent. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** Its list also reminds Muslims that their faith is not a claim to own Abraham, Moses, or Jesus; it is a commitment to the God who sent them all. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** The community’s unity is grounded in shared submission, not in pretending that revelation never unfolded across time. **[Saʿdī]** **[Qurṭubī]** **[Jalālayn]**

The verse begins with the general—belief in God and revelation—and then names particular messengers and communities before returning to the general category of “the prophets.” **[Ṭabarī]** This movement makes the list an illustration of a larger rule rather than an exhaustive register. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]** A Muslim affirms prophets whose stories are not named in the Qur’an as well as those named here, while avoiding invented claims about their biographies. **[Saʿdī]** The specific names carry honor, but the final universal clause guards against treating the unnamed as less truly sent by God. **[Maʿārif]**

The phrase “from their Lord” also gives a criterion for distinguishing prophetic revelation from human speculation. **[Saʿdī]** explains that the prophets call to good and confirm one another because their message comes from the same Lord; the consistency of their call contrasts with the contradictions of false claimants. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The verse’s faith is therefore not an uncritical acceptance of any text claimed to be ancient. It is belief in what God actually revealed through His messengers. **[Study Quran]** **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:137

137137
*“So if they believe in the like of what you believe in, then they are rightly guided; but if they turn away, then they are only in schism. God will suffice you against them, and He is the Hearing, the Knowing.”*

**Meaning.** The verse gives the consequence of the confession in 2:136. If the People of the Book accept the whole body of faith proclaimed by the believers—including God, His revelations, and all His messengers—they will be guided; if they refuse, they remain in opposition. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the likeness is between the two acts of believing, not between God or the objects of faith. The believers affirm the prophets without dividing them, and the invitation is for others to do likewise. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the same reading: they must believe as the Muslims believe and affirm the messengers without distinction. **[Jalālayn]** likewise interprets the phrase as a call to accept the same faith. **[Ibn Kathīr]** emphasizes that guidance requires believing in all God’s Books and messengers.

The expression “in the like of what you believe” could be misunderstood as comparing God to something else. **[Ṭabarī]** reports a reading attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās that paraphrases it as “in that which you believe,” avoiding any implication that God has a likeness. He then explains the canonical wording as comparing one community’s belief with another’s belief, just as one might compare two acts rather than the people who perform them. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the same concern and says the intended likeness is between the confessions or the revealed truths accepted, not between God and a created being. **[Study Quran]** notes another possible emphasis: the People of the Book should believe in their original, undistorted revelations, which would be compatible with the believers’ confession. The verse’s meaning is therefore doctrinally careful, not a claim that God resembles any object of belief. **[Maʿārif]**

The word *shiqāq* describes the condition of those who turn away. **[Ṭabarī]** explains it as separation, disobedience, and hostility toward God, the Messenger, and the believers. **[Qurṭubī]** traces the term to the image of two sides or factions standing apart; he also records the sense of conflict that burdens and harms each side. **[Jalālayn]** reads it as a clear opposition to the believers. **[Saʿdī]** says the person in schism stands on one side while God and His Messenger are on the other, leading to enmity and resistance. This is more than a difference of vocabulary: in context, it is refusal of the shared prophetic faith after the evidence has been stated. **[Ibn Kathīr]**

**God will suffice you.** The promise is addressed first to the Prophet and assures him that hostile rejection cannot defeat the mission. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that God knows both the opponents’ public words and the envy or hostility they hide. **[Jalālayn]** understands the attributes “Hearing” and “Knowing” as encompassing their speech and circumstances. **[Ibn Kathīr]** says God aids the believers against those who persist after proof. **[Qurṭubī]** regards the phrase as a divine promise of protection that was fulfilled through historical events involving hostile groups. The verse’s immediate comfort is God’s sufficiency, not permission for believers to act unjustly or to claim that they control the judgment. **[Maʿārif]**

The names “the Hearing, the Knowing” explain why God can suffice the Prophet: nothing said openly or concealed inwardly escapes Him. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Saʿdī]** The Prophet need not answer every hostile claim on the opponents’ terms, because God knows the evidence and the hidden motives. **[Study Quran]** connects the Qur’anic use of “suffice” with God’s roles as protector, reckoner, witness, and guardian. **[Jalālayn]** The phrase therefore gives reassurance while also warning those who oppose revelation that their conduct is known.

**Reflection.** The verse sets a boundary between a sincere invitation and the acceptance of endless dispute. The believers state their creed openly; those who accept the prophets as a whole are guided, and those who reject them remain responsible for that choice. **[Saʿdī]** **[Maʿārif]** The community can trust God’s protection without claiming control over people’s hearts. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** “He is the Hearing, the Knowing” also comforts those who are misrepresented: God hears what is said and knows what is intended. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Jalālayn]**

The wording *mithla mā āmantum bihi* prompted an early caution attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās: avoid reading it as though God had a likeness, and understand it as faith in what the believers affirm. **[Ṭabarī]** He explains that the comparison lies between the believers’ acts of accepting revelation, not between God and any created object. **[Qurṭubī]** records the same report and discusses the possibility that “like” functions as emphasis in Arabic. The accepted sense remains clear from the context: all prophets and revelations are to be affirmed without division. **[Study Quran]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The promise “God will suffice you” follows the warning of schism, so it reassures the Prophet without authorizing the community to force belief. **[Ṭabarī]** describes the phrase as God’s protection against those who persist in opposition; **[Qurṭubī]** cites historical events in which hostile groups were defeated, expelled, or required to pay tribute. **[Jalālayn]** **[Saʿdī]** The names “Hearing” and “Knowing” ground this sufficiency in God’s knowledge of words and hidden circumstances. The believers are not asked to control the outcome; they are asked to state the truth and trust God’s judgment. **[Maʿārif]**

The conditional is significant: the verse identifies guidance by the truth accepted, not by a community’s inherited name. **[Saʿdī]** A person who sincerely believes in all that God revealed is guided, whereas one who turns away from some messengers cannot make the rejection disappear by affirming others. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Ṭabarī]** This echoes the previous verse’s contrast between submission to God and claims built on lineage. **[Maʿārif]** The statement leaves the final judgment to God, who hears the confession and knows whether it is sincere. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**

## 2:138

138138
*“The coloring of God—and who is better than God in coloring? And we are worshippers of Him.”*

**Meaning.** The verse uses the image of dye or coloring to describe the faith just proclaimed. *Ṣibghah* literally evokes a color that marks cloth, while in the context it means God’s religion, the faith of Abraham, or the primordial disposition toward monotheism. **[Ṭabarī]** identifies it as Islam and reports interpretations as God’s religion or the natural faith with which He created people. **[Qurṭubī]** likewise gives Islam and *fiṭrah* as principal meanings and explains that religion is called a “color” because its practices and character leave a visible mark on the believer. **[Jalālayn]** describes it as God’s religion toward which human nature inclines. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports the interpretation “the religion of God.” **[Study Quran]** surveys the possible meanings, including God’s coloring, religion, purification, and the primordial nature of human beings.

The image also responds to Christian baptism as discussed by several exegetes. **[Ṭabarī]** reports that Christians baptized infants in colored water, treating the ritual as a mark of religious identity; he contrasts it with the “color” of Islam. **[Qurṭubī]** gives a fuller account of this practice and says the verse asserts the superiority of God’s true religion over a rite that cannot itself produce inward faith. **[Maʿārif]** similarly understands the reference as an answer to the Christian baptismal ceremony, while emphasizing that true faith leaves a lasting impression on character. The verse does not require every reader to accept one exact historical reconstruction of the rite; its primary image is the enduring mark of God’s guidance. **[Study Quran]**

The word *ṣibghah* is connected by some commentators to *fiṭrah*, the primordial disposition God placed in human beings. **[Ṭabarī]** records this interpretation and explains that it points to the religion God created people to recognize. **[Study Quran]** says the image can refer to the way a cloth bears its original color, while human beings bear an orientation toward God. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the Qur’anic phrase “the fiṭrah of God” as a related expression. **[Qurṭubī]** says the interpretation of *fiṭrah* returns to Islam, since it is the natural religion of submission. The interpretations of religion and primordial nature overlap: divine guidance restores and completes the human orientation toward its Creator. **[Maʿārif]**

**A mark in character.** As-Saʿdī develops the metaphor as a transformation that should become a stable quality. Just as dye changes the cloth, religion should shape outward deeds, inward beliefs, manners, and choices. **[Saʿdī]** He contrasts the believer whose faith yields truthfulness, patience, courage, mercy, and care for others with the person marked by idolatry, deception, and harm. **[Maʿārif]** similarly says the coloring of true faith should be visible in the believer’s face, conduct, habits, and behavior. The image is not a claim that every believer is flawless; it describes the direction and distinctive imprint of sincere worship. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Jalālayn]**

The rhetorical question “Who is better than God in coloring?” challenges the idea that a human ritual or communal label can provide a more lasting mark than God’s guidance. **[Qurṭubī]** interprets the phrase as a comparison in excellence and records the view that the religion of God is the best “color.” **[Ṭabarī]** connects it with Abraham’s creed and the rejection of associating partners with God. **[Study Quran]** notes that the verb can also mean causing someone to enter into something, allowing the phrase to suggest immersion in divine religion as well as dyeing. The question is therefore both an assertion and an invitation: no religious identity surpasses the way God has revealed. **[Maʿārif]**

The final statement, “we are worshippers of Him,” explains what the coloring consists of. **[Ṭabarī]** reads it as humble obedience and submission to God in following Abraham’s religion. **[Saʿdī]** says worship combines sincerity and conformity to the prophetic way; the fronted “to Him” makes exclusive devotion prominent. **[Qurṭubī]** likewise connects the line with abandoning worship of others. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Thus the divine mark is not a decorative label but a life directed to God. **[Study Quran]**

**Reflection.** The metaphor asks what truly shapes a person. A ritual performed outwardly may identify a group, but the Qur’an points to the lasting imprint of faith on belief, worship, and conduct. **[Maʿārif]** **[Saʿdī]** The verse links back to Abraham’s *ḥanīf* creed and forward to the believers’ declaration that they worship God alone. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** It invites readers to seek the sort of religious mark that survives beyond ceremony: sincerity toward God and good conduct toward His creation. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]** **[Jalālayn]**

The grammar of *ṣibghah* allows the phrase to connect with the preceding confession: the believers’ statement “we believe” is the coloring of God, or the phrase can be an exhortation to follow God’s religion. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** **[Qurṭubī]** records the word as a substitute for “creed” or as an implied command, “follow the coloring of God.” The grammatical options reinforce the same point: the divine mark is the faith just proclaimed and lived. **[Study Quran]**

Some commentators mention a ritual washing when a person entered Islam as a possible secondary interpretation of *ṣibghah*. **[Qurṭubī]** reports this view alongside the principal meaning of religion and *fiṭrah*, and discusses reports concerning converts who were instructed to bathe. It is not necessary to make that legal interpretation the verse’s only meaning. The dye metaphor more broadly contrasts a lasting religious orientation with a temporary ceremonial mark. **[Maʿārif]** Ibn Kathīr reports the meaning “God’s religion” and relates it to *fiṭrah*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The verse’s explicit close—“we are worshippers of Him”—keeps the focus on exclusive worship rather than on the water or the ritual alone. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ṭabarī]**

## 2:139

139139
*“Say, ‘Will you dispute with us concerning God, while He is our Lord and your Lord? To us belong our deeds, and to you your deeds; and we are sincere to Him.’”*

**Meaning.** The verse answers those who claim that their older scriptures or ancestry make them more entitled to God than the believers. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the People of the Book argued that their prophet and Book came earlier, and therefore claimed greater closeness to God. The response is that God is Lord of both communities, and that each will be judged by its own deeds. **[Jalālayn]** similarly reads the question as rejecting the claim that the Jews and Christians are uniquely entitled to divine favor. **[Qurṭubī]** records that some opponents appealed to being “children of God and His beloved,” or to the priority of their ancestors and scriptures. The verse shifts the standard from chronological priority to worship and action. **[Maʿārif]**

The verb *tuḥājjūnanā* means to dispute or contend by argument. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the challenge as asking why the groups quarrel over God’s favor when they share the same Lord. **[Qurṭubī]** discusses the grammar and the sense of arguing over religious closeness or standing with God. **[Saʿdī]** gives an important ethical qualification: disputation should clarify truth, guide someone who is mistaken, or establish proof against stubbornness; when it departs from those aims, it becomes blameworthy quarrelling. The verse does not forbid reasoned theological discussion. It asks whether the debate is grounded in evidence and meaningful differences, rather than in empty claims of inherited superiority. **[Study Quran]**

“He is our Lord and your Lord” undercuts the idea that divine care belongs exclusively to one lineage. **[Ṭabarī]** emphasizes that God controls reward and punishment for both groups. **[Jalālayn]** notes that God chooses whom He wills, so the mere fact that one community received an earlier Book does not dictate His later choice of a messenger. **[Ibn Kathīr]** connects the passage with other Qur’anic accounts in which people dispute over God while He remains Lord of all. The phrase is not a denial of doctrinal disagreement; it is a reminder that both parties stand before the same Creator. **[Maʿārif]**

“To us belong our deeds, and to you your deeds” establishes individual responsibility. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that each group is rewarded or punished for what it earns, not for the age of its scripture or the ancestry it claims. **[Qurṭubī]** connects the statement to accountability for good and evil actions. **[Saʿdī]** reasons that if both groups share the same Lord and each has its own deeds, a claim of superiority requires a real distinguishing quality. In this verse, the relevant quality is sincere worship and obedience, not birth or communal pride. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Study Quran]**

The final clause, “we are sincere to Him,” names that distinguishing orientation. *Ikhlāṣ* means purifying worship from association with others and acting for God rather than for human admiration. **[Ṭabarī]** says the believers worship and obey God without assigning Him partners. **[Qurṭubī]** defines sincerity as purifying an action from regard for created observers and cites spiritual explanations of inward intention. **[Maʿārif]** discusses *ikhlāṣ* as worshipping God alone and performing good deeds to obey Him, not to win people’s praise. This is not a claim that Muslims are automatically sincere in every act; it is the confession and standard to which they are called. **[Saʿdī]**

**A method for dispute.** As-Saʿdī treats the verse as a guide to fair argument: bring forward relevant evidence, seek to return the mistaken to truth, and distinguish genuine differences from claims that merely divide. **[Saʿdī]** The phrase “our deeds and your deeds” avoids pretending that the two communities have no disagreement, while refusing to make ancestry the deciding proof. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Ṭabarī]** The believers are instructed to state their conviction clearly, but the final judgment on deeds belongs to God. **[Maʿārif]**

**Reflection.** Religious confidence is not the same as boasting. The verse answers the claim “we are closer to God” with two questions: Is God not Lord of both groups, and are not deeds what each group brings? **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** The believers’ response is sincerity, not a rival claim to ancestry. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Maʿārif]** The verse encourages honest dialogue grounded in worship and accountable conduct, while warning against using argument as a weapon for communal prestige. **[Qurṭubī]** **[Study Quran]**

The sequence of the clauses is deliberate: first the question about disputing over God, then the reminder that He is Lord of both sides, then the division of deeds, and finally the confession of sincerity. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Jalālayn]** This order answers the claim of inherited superiority with a shared point of reference, an individual standard of accountability, and a positive description of worship. **[Maʿārif]** The verse does not say that every religious claim is equally true; it says that claims of closeness must be judged by what people believe and do before the same Lord. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

Sincerity is also more than a feeling of earnestness. **[Qurṭubī]** defines *ikhlāṣ* as purifying an action from attention to human observers, and **[Maʿārif]** warns against confusing it with following every impulse. The believer seeks God’s approval through the guidance He revealed. **[Saʿdī]** adds that sound debate should clarify truth and guide the mistaken, not become a contest for humiliation. The verse’s confession of exclusive worship thus shapes both private intention and public argument. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Study Quran]**

The wording “to us our deeds and to you your deeds” also guards against attributing another person’s guilt or merit to an entire ancestry. **[Ṭabarī]** Each person is accountable for what they earn, even while communities share histories and obligations. **[Qurṭubī]** This makes the verse a call to examine one’s own worship rather than rest on a revered ancestor. **[Maʿārif]** The believers’ claim is tested by exclusive devotion and the actions that follow it. **[Saʿdī]** **[Ibn Kathīr]**

## 2:140

140140
*“Or do you say that Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Tribes were Jews or Christians? Say, ‘Do you know better, or does God?’ And who does greater wrong than one who conceals a testimony he has from God? God is not unaware of what you do.”*

**Meaning.** The verse continues the challenge to People of the Book who claim the patriarchs as members of later religious communities. It asks whether they say Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Tribes were Jews or Christians. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that the charge is historically impossible: Judaism and Christianity, as later communal identities, arose after the patriarchs. The verse therefore rejects retroactive appropriation, not the value of the patriarchs’ revelations. **[Jalālayn]** interprets the question as a rebuke to those who attribute to Abraham a creed God explicitly denies. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that the wording may continue the previous question about disputation, or begin a fresh rebuke depending on the reading of *am*. **[Study Quran]** connects the point with 3:65–67, which says Abraham lived before the Torah and Gospel and was a *ḥanīf* submitter.

“Do you know better, or does God?” is a rhetorical question whose answer is clear: God knows the prophets and their faith better than later communities can claim. **[Qurṭubī]** calls it both an affirmation and a rebuke. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that God’s testimony is found in revelation, which describes Abraham and his family as Muslims and commands later people to follow their monotheism. **[Saʿdī]** says the question’s obvious answer exposes the absurdity of the claim without needing an extended argument. **[Maʿārif]** likewise says the People of the Book knew that the patriarchs were not Jews or Christians, yet persisted in concealing the truth. The verse invites the audience to submit its claims to divine knowledge rather than assert that inherited identity outranks revelation. **[Ibn Kathīr]**

The accusation of concealing “a testimony from God” has been explained in two related ways. **[Ṭabarī]** prefers the immediate context: the testimony concerns the patriarchs’ monotheism and their freedom from later religious labels. He also records the interpretation that the concealed testimony includes the description of Muḥammad in earlier scripture. **[Qurṭubī]** says the first reading better fits the sequence, while noting the second view reported from Qatādah. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the report that learned People of the Book recognized that the prophets were Muslims and that Muḥammad was the Messenger, but hid this from others. **[Study Quran]** notes that *shahādah* may refer to God’s testimony in revelation or to the testimony that the People of the Book possess. The common point is knowing concealment of evidence, not mere failure to agree with an argument.

“Who does greater wrong?” is a rhetorical condemnation of hiding what one knows to be true. **[Ṭabarī]** says the people addressed conceal that Abraham and his family were Muslims, then assign them a later identity. **[Qurṭubī]** explains the construction as meaning that none is more unjust than such a concealer. **[Maʿārif]** stresses that the wrong consists of two acts together: suppressing testimony and promoting the opposite claim. The line is especially weighty because the testimony is described as being “from God,” not as a human opinion that can be withheld without consequence. **[Saʿdī]**

The closing statement, “God is not unaware of what you do,” turns the accusation into a warning of accountability. **[Ṭabarī]** explains that God knows the concealment and will repay it in this world and the next. **[Qurṭubī]** treats the phrase as a threat that God has not left their affairs unattended. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The name “not unaware” is not a suggestion that God might have overlooked their conduct; it affirms His knowledge and the certainty of judgment. **[Study Quran]** notes that such reminders of divine knowledge often follow a description of human action, joining warning with recompense.

**Reflection.** The verse asks communities to examine how they handle inconvenient knowledge. A claim of loyalty to scripture is tested not by how often it is repeated but by whether one bears truthful witness when the evidence challenges group prestige. **[Maʿārif]** **[Ṭabarī]** The patriarchs cannot be conscripted into a later identity; their own message of submission to one God stands as the measure. **[Ibn Kathīr]** **[Qurṭubī]** The passage also leaves no room for triumphalism: the believers themselves are warned by the principle that God knows what people do. **[Saʿdī]** **[Study Quran]**

The verse’s two questions therefore work together. The first tests whether the audience will accept God’s knowledge over its own claim; the second asks who is more unjust than someone who hides testimony from God. **[Ṭabarī]** **[Maʿārif]** The closing warning then reminds all hearers that concealed motives and public statements alike are known to God. **[Jalālayn]** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Its place at the end of this passage prepares for the repeated reminder in 2:141: ancestors have passed, and each generation must answer for its own response to revelation. **[Study Quran]** **[Qurṭubī]**

The opening has variant readings: one addresses the listeners directly, “or do you say?”, while another reports what “they say.” **[Ṭabarī]** prefers the direct form as a continuation of the previous question, asking which claim the opponents are making. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that the other reading treats it as a separate question. Both preserve the rebuke of attributing the patriarchs to Judaism or Christianity. **[Jalālayn]** **[Study Quran]**

The phrase “a testimony from God” is not limited to an ordinary human witness in a courtroom. **[Ṭabarī]** understands it as testimony preserved in revelation that Abraham and his family followed submission to God; he also records the view that the concealed testimony includes the Prophet’s description. **[Qurṭubī]** prefers the patriarchal context while noting the alternative from Qatādah. **[Ibn Kathīr]** mentions reports that the People of the Book concealed what their scriptures said about the prophets and Muḥammad. The wording allows the passage to expose a broader pattern of concealing revelation, while its immediate focus remains the patriarchs. **[Maʿārif]**
